# ATCS6 Desired Design Model

This document describes how ATCS6 should work after the refactor.
It lists the rules the new code must follow and the behavior that changes between components.
Its job is to guide the engineer doing the refactor, not to be a full spec that replaces the code.
Fields, helper internals, and save/load details that this document does not mention stay the way the current code has them.
When the current code and a changed rule here disagree, this document wins.

## About this document

AI agents (and human reviewers) use this document to write code for a train control simulator. It describes the v1 system as it should be built. It does not record how the design got to this point. Keep these points in mind as you read:

- **`TrainController` stands in for a real train's computer.** A real train computer is one unit made of many modules: ATC, ATO, EB, the cab interface, the mode manager, and operational state. `TrainController` is wide on purpose because it models all of them. Each sub-module is its own separate concern, even when one sentence mentions several at once.
- **A latch models hardware that cannot drop work while the train is moving.** A latch is a command the controller saves and holds in its own state until some test (usually `is_stopped()`) lets it run. A plain flag is not a latch. Before you add a latch, first decide which test must pass before it can run.
- **The runtime uses one thread.** Every API, UI, and admin request runs between ticks, on the same thread as the per-tick phase loop. Every "always recompute live" rule in this document depends on this. If you break the single-thread model, you break several core design choices.
- **v1 means v1.** Anything that is not in v1 must not appear in this document, not even as "removed," "legacy," or "deprecated" text. If a row is missing from a schema table, that absence is the statement that the thing does not exist. Use git for design history.
- **Performance is already checked for the v1 scope.** The compute-live patterns (`lookahead`, the `received_path_plan` projection, derived properties) are there on purpose. They are not performance bugs to optimize away.
- **Tests are out of scope for this document.** This document sets the contracts that generated code must meet. Test plans live elsewhere.
- **This document changes often.** When a design changes, every cross-reference is updated in the same edit. You will never see a half-updated doc. If you find one, that is a bug, not history.

## Authoring Rules

These rules apply to every edit of this document. Reviewers must reject any patch that breaks them.

1. **This document is v1. It contains no "removed in v1," "legacy," "deprecated," or "no longer used" text anywhere.** If a field, method, state, or flag is not part of v1, it must not appear here at all. If a row is missing from a schema table, that absence is the statement that the thing does not exist. Any patch that adds tombstone language ("removed in v1," "no longer used," "kept for compatibility," "deprecated," and the like) is incomplete and must be rejected.

2. **No cross-reference drift.** When any rule changes (ownership, a payload field, a state transition, a callback signature, a latch consumer, a recovery gate), every place in the document that refers to the old rule must be found and updated in the same edit. A patch that updates only one section is incomplete. Reviewers must reject any patch that leaves two sites describing the same rule in different ways.

3. **One canonical home per concept.** Each shared schema, latch, predicate, and helper has exactly one canonical home: the single place where it is defined. Every other site refers to it by name. If two sites describe the same concept in different ways, the patch is incomplete.

4. **Latches are described in the canonical Latch Table.** Every latch in the document must appear in the Latch Table (in the Core Runtime Loop section). Each entry lists the writer phase and entity, the consumer phase and entity, the "consumed by the last entity that uses it in the same 3-phase period" rule, and any conditions that let it survive across ticks. Any latch behavior described elsewhere must point back to the table, not restate its rules.

5. **Phase boundaries are described in the canonical Phase Table.** The Phase Table (in the Core Runtime Loop section) is the only place that says which entity acts in which phase. Every other section refers to it by phase name and entity.

6. **No single-use-case branches in shared contracts.** If a workflow step is written as a branch for one specific case, it must either be rewritten as a general mechanism, or be clearly marked as the one case that the general mechanism cannot cover.

7. **Diagnostic-only distinctions belong on events, not enums.** A distinction that no code acts on must be carried in the `NotificationCenter` event payload, not added as a new enum value on a control-flow type. An enum value written by a genuinely different code path (and that could become functional) may stay. An enum value that exists only so the UI can show a different label must move to the event payload.

8. **Requirement keywords are binding.** `must` and `must not` state required contract behavior. `may` states allowed behavior. `should` is not used in a contract statement: a sentence that states a contract uses `must`, and one that grants permission uses `may`. Readers, including code-generation agents, treat these keywords literally.

## Scope

Primary files reviewed:

- `simulation.py`
- `train.py`
- `train_controller/` (package: `__init__.py` façade plus `atc.py`, `ato.py`, `commands.py`, `eb.py`, `manual.py`, `notifications.py`, `ops.py`, `path_projection.py`, `physics_sync.py`, `route_hooks.py`)
- `train_physics.py`
- `railway_platform.py`
- `station.py`
- `block.py`
- `block_manager.py`
- `block_grouping_manager.py`
- `segment.py`
- `segment_geometry_manager.py`
- `path.py`
- `path_dispatcher.py`
- `route_executor.py`
- `train_route.py`
- `track.py`
- `switch.py`
- `network.py`
- `speed_profile.py`
- `block_power.py`

Terminology:

- `path`: a movement authority that the dispatcher reserves between blocks.
- `route`: a higher-level travel plan. It is a list of `stop`, `waypoint`, `dwell`, and `run` tasks that each resolve to a concrete block target or, for `run`, a direction-seed target.
- `route run`: the running, per-train copy of an assigned route. It points to one shared `Route` definition but holds that train's own changing state: waypoint cursors, the current resolved target status, dwell timing, and which active path it is tied to. The current code may still name the class `RouteExecutionState`, but the canonical concept is `RouteRun`.
- `block`: the power + occupancy + reservation unit. A switch-free connected run of one or more `segment`s that share one power feed (one `track voltage`) and admit one train at a time. It is the unit the dispatcher reserves and the train occupies. The current code carries this concept across two classes: `PowerDistrict` (which groups blocks onto one power feed) plus the occupancy and reservation half of `Block`. The canonical `Block` unifies them — it is that power grouping, now grouping `segment`s through `segment_ids`, and it owns the occupancy and reservation state lifted up out of what the current code calls `Block`.
- `segment`: a group of tiles with one unified geometry — it owns `ordered_tiles`, `length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`, `min_radius_cm`, and `grade_percent`. A segment stores no occupancy, reservation, availability, or power state; those live on its owning `Block`. One `segment` maps to many real-world rail pieces that share its geometry.
- `tile`: one physical rail tile on the grid, identified by its `(x, y)` coordinate and its local 6-bit geometry. A tile is not a group of tiles and is not a block; a `Segment` owns an ordered list of tile entries.
- `platform`: the real, physical place on the track where a train stops. Dispatcher targets are always written against a concrete block traversal frame, never against a logical station group. When a waypoint resolves through station or platform data, the platform supplies the linked blocks and the traversal context used to work out that block target.
- `station`: a logical group of one or more platforms, used only to pick a stop at the route level. When a route targets a station-level stop, `RouteExecutor` must first pick one concrete platform for that attempt, then build the dispatcher request from that platform.

## Out of Scope

This section lists features and concerns that v1 leaves out on purpose. It makes those gaps explicit, so a reader (AI agent or human) can tell that a missing thing is a decision, not an oversight.

An Out of Scope entry is not a wishlist and not a promise to build the thing later. It records that someone asked the question and the answer for v1 was "no." Three rules govern what goes here:

1. **Out of Scope is a clear "no," not a brainstorm.** An entry goes here only when a designer has actively decided to leave a concern out of v1. If no one has raised the question, do not guess.
2. **Out of Scope is not a graveyard.** A concern that was once in scope and then removed does not belong here. It goes nowhere, by the "removed is removed" rule in the Authoring Rules. Only concerns that were never in scope belong here.
3. **Out of Scope does not promise v2.** Listing something here is not a roadmap. v2 will have its own scope discussion.

| Concern | v1 stance | Why Out of Scope for v1 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-threaded runtime | Single thread; all API/UI/admin requests run between ticks on the same thread as the per-tick phase loop | The "always recompute live" projections (`lookahead`, `received_path_plan`, derived properties) work only because changes happen between ticks. Adding threads breaks the read-consistency contract. |
| Persistent / cross-tick lookahead or projection caching | Recompute live on every read across ticks | Performance is fine for v1 train counts. Cross-tick caching adds a place that can go stale, and v1 does not need it. A within-tick memo is allowed as an implementation detail: it is thrown away before the next tick and shadow-checked to match a live recompute (see **Lookahead is a pure derived value**). That memo is not the cross-tick caching this row rules out. |
| Persistence / save-load of runtime state | In-memory only | No use case in v1. |
| Schema migration, backward-compat shims, dual-write transitional fields | None | v1 is the first design; "v1 means v1." |
| Unobservable mechanical outcomes after successful I/O — a signal lamp fails, EB hardware fails to engage, or a point motor accepts a pulse but mechanically sticks | Not inferred, verified, or recovered in v1; the documented commanded state remains the software authority | A point motor reports no position, so software cannot know that the points stuck and must not invent auto-retry, recovery, or a rollback of `Switch.position`. This does not exclude hardware-interface faults: transaction refusal, disconnect, invalid detector/readback data, and failure to reach safe state are in scope. Virtual Hardware injects those observable device/interface failures, and Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware both enter the fail-closed hardware-failure lifecycle defined under **Power**, Hardware interface boundary. |
| Hardware sensors as a fault-detection, verification, or recovery source | Block current detection sets block occupancy (in scope). Using any sensor to detect a mechanical fault, verify a commanded action, or trigger mechanical recovery is out | The block current detector is the authority for block occupancy (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). The commanded `Switch.position` is the authority for switch alignment, and v1 reads no switch-position sensor. The observable interface failures named in the row above are still fatal; what v1 does not do is infer an unobservable mechanical outcome from some unrelated sensor. |
| Collision modeling | Two trains in one block is a fatal runtime failure via `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, not a physics interaction | Physics does not model contact. |
| Reverse motion under cab | Forbidden; wrong-direction cab forces the distance-0 ATC limit to `0` | Physics never produces backward motion. |
| Authorization / RBAC / multi-user model | All API requests trusted | No user accounts in v1. |
| Logging beyond `NotificationCenter` | `NotificationCenter` is the only runtime sink | Component-local logging is forbidden by design. A component may write one last-resort line to the standard library logger when `NotificationCenter.emit(...)` itself raises, because a failed emit must not be silent. That fallback carries no routine trace: it fires only on the emit failure it reports. |
| Idempotency keys, retry/dedup wrappers on API commands | None | Single-threaded between-tick drain makes them unnecessary. |
| Health checks, metrics export, distributed tracing | None | Operators read `NotificationCenter` and live projections. |
| `Path` / `RouteRun` retention caps | Unbounded for v1 | Retention policy will be added when leak becomes measurable. The runtime event stream is capped separately; see **Runtime notifications**, Retention. |
| Per-train safety margin tuning | Two global settings (`TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`, `PLATFORM_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`; see **Runtime settings**) | Per-train tuning is a future calibration concern. |
| Per-tile speed limits beyond `Segment.speed_limit_kmh` | Segment-scoped only | Sub-segment speed restrictions are not modeled. |
| Live editor mutation during operation | Forbidden; Editor and Operation are binary session modes (the split and Operation's three operating environments are defined in **Session mode and operating environment**) | Geometry stability is required by every "always recompute live" rule. |
| Multi-network in one process | One network per `SimulationController` instance | Cross-network interaction has no use case in v1. |
| Re-running the simulation engine (true time-travel of the live model) | None | Visual replay over the recorded **Event log** is in scope (see **Event log**); re-simulating the model forward or backward is not. |
| Schedule adherence / ETA / SLA tracking | None | Only the reporting layer built on top of the stored timing fields is out of scope: ETA projection, SLA dashboards, and lateness alerts. The timestamps under it are not just informational. `RouteTaskRun.started_at` / `finished_at` are inputs to the route-progress cursor, written and read per the **RouteTaskRun schema** and **Route-run state** contracts. The per-walk-position `delay_class` value computed by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` is also in scope. |
| Pluggable physics or speed-profile backends | None | `SpeedProfile` is the single calibration model. |
| Route-level runtime progress summary | None; `RouteRun` is the only runtime progress store | No route-level summary shape has a v1 consumer; UI and API read per-train `RouteRun` progress. |
| Hand-authored config files or env vars for settings | None | Every v1 setting is changed only through the application's own API, never by hand-editing a file or setting an environment variable. **Runtime settings** hold entirely in memory (reseeded from code defaults on restart). **Hardware setup** (**Power**) is written by its own API to an app-managed JSON file so the wiring survives the session rebuild that applies it — persistence the app owns and writes only through the API, the same way it owns the layout save files, which carry a copy of that setup (see **Power**, Saved with the layout). The selected operating environment is session state stored separately from layout wiring. |
| Async/streaming notification protocols (websocket, SSE, push) | None | Frontend reads `NotificationCenter` via polling/projection. |
| Cross-tick latch durability across process restart | None | Process state is volatile. |
| Watchdogs / alert notifications for stuck trains, no-progress | None | Operators read live diagnostics from `lookahead.speed_curves[0].atc_limit_reason_code` and the latest error event for that train (see **Error events**). (The hardware fail-safe watchdog that cuts power to the track and point motors on loss of control is a different mechanism, specified in **Power**, Hardware interface boundary.) |
| Operator removal of an app-controlled train | Not specified in v1; do not implement it | Surface, teardown order, and pending-latch handling are not specified for v1. Retiring an unidentified train is a different thing and is in scope: the runtime built that train for itself, and **Power**, Unidentified trains owns its teardown order. |
| Bulk operations (multi-train commands, batch route assignment) | None | Operators issue per-train commands one at a time. |

## Glossary

This section defines words that get overloaded across the document. Some mean similar but different things in different layers. Others show up in prose with no stored field behind them. Defining each word once here lets every other site point to it by name instead of restating it.

Three rules govern this glossary:

1. **If a word appears in prose but in no schema, it goes in the glossary, or it does not appear in prose at all.** No informal nouns.
2. **If two words mean the same thing across layers, pick one as the main word and mark the other as a synonym.** Do not let the two drift apart over time.
3. **If a word means different things in different contexts, qualify it every time it appears.** Never write a bare `forward`. Always write "block-relative forward" or "train-relative forward."

**Group A — Action verbs.** Each row names the entity acted on, who initiates the action, and the resulting state.

| Verb | Entity / initiator / resulting state |
| --- | --- |
| `cancel` | `Path` (`status: ACTIVE -> CANCELLED`); initiated by `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. Writers and gating per the **Latch Table** (`c832`) and the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. |
| `complete` | `Path` (`status: ACTIVE -> COMPLETED`); initiated by `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` per the **Stop-arrival procedure** |
| `finish` | `RouteRun` (`current_route_status -> FINISHED`); initiated by `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal route completion procedure (`879a`) |
| `deactivate` | `RouteRun` (`current_route_status -> DEACTIVATED`); initiated by `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` from the Phase-3 abort/power-off/deactivate consumers |
| `abort` | `RouteRun`; operator-driven teardown via the `pending_route_abort` latch per the **Latch Table** (`bc5d`) |
| `revoke` | `Path`; system-driven cancel via `pending_path_cancel_request(revoked)` from route reconciliation, admin route deactivation, or a path left with no valid authority after another train takes a block it reserved (see **Path And PathDispatcher**, Authority truncation) |
| `deviate` | `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` with `path_cancel_was_deviation = True` (derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"`); writer and consumer per the **Latch Table** (`75cb`). A non-deviation authority cancellation also sets `path_cancelled` but leaves `path_cancel_was_deviation = False`, so it projects `display_state == "CANCELLED"` instead (see **Route-run state**). |
| `depart` | `TrainController.ops.state -> RUNNING_MOVING`; initiated by `TrainController.depart()` called by `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` when live authority exists |
| `dwell` | `TrainController.ops.state -> RUNNING_AT_STATION`; initiated by `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` called by `RouteExecutor` Phase-1 dwell-entry step |
| `throw` | `Switch` (moves the point motor to a new alignment and writes the commanded `Switch.position`); owned by `SwitchManager` per the **Switch entity** mutation workflow and **Power**, Switch power-controller role |
| `skip` | `RouteRun` (`task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` set, the route-progress cursor advances past a broken task); initiated by Phase 1 step `c481`, or step `592a` for a task a mid-pass cursor move landed on. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `stall` | `RouteRun` (`no_reachable_task = True`, derived `display_state == "STALLED"`, the train holds idle); initiated by Phase 1 step `c481` (or step `592a`) when every task on a `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` walk is broken. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |

**Group B — Geometry terms.** Each row names the frame of reference. Always qualify the term in prose.

| Term | Frame of reference |
| --- | --- |
| `forward` | Block-relative (matches `Block.allowed_travel_directions`; the tile order is the concatenation of the block's constituent `Segment.ordered_tiles`) OR train-relative (the cab end the train is heading toward). Always qualify which. |
| `reverse` | Block-relative or train-relative. Always qualify which. |
| `head` | Train-relative; the physical end of the current span on the authority side, i.e. closer to `authority_end_block_id`. |
| `tail` | Train-relative; the physical end of the current span farther from `authority_end_block_id`. |
| `cab` | Operator-selected control end of the train; `A` or `B` or `None`. The cab is on the head side when the train is moving forward in the authority frame. |
| `frame` | Generic term for a directed coordinate context; always qualify with one of: block-relative, traversal, terminal, or travel. |
| `traversal direction` | Block-relative; `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for one block as walked by a path. |
| `travel direction` | Network-relative; the train's current motion direction in the network frame, derived from cab and span. |
| `terminal frame` | The directed `path_tiles` / `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` of the terminal block in a `Path.resource_sequence`; used for braking and for completion direction match. |

**Group C — Aggregate nouns.** Each row gives the type, or marks the word as informal prose only and points to the real concrete artifact behind it.

| Noun | Type / canonical schema |
| --- | --- |
| `path` | Stored entity (`Path` record); canonical schema in **Path field schema**. |
| `authority` | Informal prose for "the cleared corridor ahead of the train"; not a stored field. Use `Path` or `received_path_plan` when the concrete artifact is needed. |
| `plan` | Informal prose synonym for `received_path_plan`; canonical schema in **received_path_plan schema**. |
| `run` | `RouteRun` runtime instance; canonical schema in the RouteRun section. |
| `walk` | The directed task index sequence of a `RouteRun` (the ordered list of `task_runs[i]` indexes); not a stored field. |
| `task` | `RouteTask` authored entry (with `task_kind`); runtime instance is `RouteTaskRun`. |
| `start mode` | Which stop a fresh `RouteRun` begins at (`FIRST` / `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`). It comes from the route-level default `Route.start_mode` plus a per-assignment one-shot input. Canonical home **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `broken task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose `task_location` ID is not in the network (it resolves with `failure_kind == missing_target`). It is skipped, never waited on. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `unreachable task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose target exists but cannot be served this tick (`failure_kind == target_unavailable`, or a live `PathAcquisitionFailureKind`). It is waited on, not skipped. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `valid task` | Informal prose for a `RouteTask` whose `task_location` ID is in the network, whether or not it can be served right now. It is the opposite of a broken task. Canonical home **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `holding point` | A block a user marks with `Block.is_holding_point = True` as a safe place to wait. The canonical field lives in the **Block** field schema. It has two consumers: the best-effort truncation holding-point preference (item 11 under **Path And PathDispatcher**) and **Meet coordination**'s `meet_ceiling_block_id` selection. |
| `adoption` | The operator turning an **unidentified train** into an ordinary, driveable train by supplying the identity, consist and facing the detector cannot know. The same `Train` record survives, re-keyed to its new `train_id` with `is_unidentified = False`. Canonical home **Power**, Unidentified trains. |
| `unidentified train` | A `Train` the runtime creates by itself when a block reads current and no train the app controls is standing there — current on the rails with no identity behind it. It is tracked like any other train so other trains keep clear of it, and it is never driven while it stays unidentified. The operator ends that by adopting it — saying what the detector found — after which it is an ordinary train. Canonical home **Power**, Unidentified trains. |
| `ghosted` | Said of a train the operator placed whose detected blocks all read clear, so the app holds an identity with no current behind it — the mirror image of an `unidentified train`. It is published as `presence_unknown` on the controller payload. The runtime strips its authority and powers it off, and removes nothing. Canonical home **Power**, Ghosted trains. |
| `configuration` | A category word, not one schema, file, mode, or API surface. Do not use it bare. Name the actual owner: **persistent train data**, **layout geometry**, **Hardware Setup**, or **runtime settings**. These have different lifetimes and activation rules. |

**Group D — Electrical / switch-hardware terms.** These separate the two voltages and the two meanings of switch alignment.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `track voltage` | The DC voltage put on a block to drive the train. Each train uses one magnitude, applied to every block it holds, and every one of those blocks is given its own sign — which way the train runs through that block (see **Power**, A block voltage carries a direction). Because a block shares one feed across its segments, that whole block is powered (see **Block**, one-train-per-Block power domain). This way, when the train crosses a boundary, the next block is already powered instead of dead (see **Power**, Block power-controller role). Canonical home `TrainPhysics` (see **TrainPhysics**). `BlockPowerController` emits it to the block outputs (see **Power**). A switch has no feed of its own; its rails carry the voltage of its `switch feed block`. |
| `throw voltage` | The DC drive put on a switch point motor to move it. Which coil it is put on picks which alignment to move to (see **Power**, Hardware setup). Emitted by `SwitchPowerController` (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role). This is a separate output from `track voltage`: the two are different wires and are never compared. |
| `switch feed block` | The block whose feed also powers a switch's rails. A switch carries no `track voltage` of its own: its rails are wired to the block at its main entrance, so a train standing on the switch is driven by that block's voltage. The wiring is the `switch_track_source` binding in **Hardware setup**, and `SwitchPowerController` owns the relation (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role). |
| `physical channel` | One addressable line on a chip — a PWM channel, a shift-register output bit, a shift-register input bit, or a current-sense channel — that a logical `block_id` or `switch_id` maps to on a given hardware build. A logical entity may take more than one: a block's track takes two PWM channels, a switch takes two coil bits. The physical-channel maps in the binding table in **Hardware setup** (see **Power**) carry it; only the concrete `TrackOutput` / `SwitchOutput` / `OccupancyInput` driver reads them, and no other layer names a channel. |
| `safe state` | Every switch point motor is powered off and every block's `track voltage` is `0`. Real Hardware reaches it by latching every coil bit low and then zeroing every output channel; coils go first because a point motor is the output that destroys itself if left energized. Reaching it is a write, so it needs a live process and a working transport (see **Power**, Safe state / fail-safe). |
| `detected block` | In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware, every layout block is a detected block: activation requires both of its occupancy bindings, and `OccupancyInput.detected_block_ids()` returns the complete layout block set. There is no hardware-backed operating environment with an undetected block. Under `SimulatedOccupancyDriver` the set is empty because Simulation derives occupancy from the model and does not use detector-gated movement. Canonical home **Power**, Hardware interface boundary. |
| `pending-entry block` | The detected block a train's head is being held out of while it waits for that block to confirm occupied. A train has at most one, and only while the cap holds. It carries `track voltage` for that whole time, because the locomotive has to roll onto it to release the cap. Canonical home **Power**, Block occupancy detection. |

**Group E — Session-mode and operating-environment terms.** These name two different layers: the Editor/Operation session-mode split, and the three environments in which Operation may run. Canonical home is **Session mode and operating environment** under **Core Runtime Loop**.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `editor mode` / `operation mode` | The two session modes; a session is in exactly one (binary per session). Editor mode places and wires geometry; operation mode runs the railway — it brings up the runtime (`SimulationController` and its per-tick loop) and drives the trains. |
| `Simulation` | The algorithm-focused operating environment. It needs no Hardware Setup, derives occupancy and train motion from the model, and uses happy-path simulated interfaces whose writes succeed. It does not emulate chips, ports, buses, or wiring. |
| `Virtual Hardware` | The hardware-integration operating environment. It requires the same complete Hardware Setup as Real Hardware and runs the real chip drivers, bindings, HAL, and occupancy pipeline against an in-memory device. Its inputs, responses, disconnects, and failures are controllable. It never opens or energizes the physical controller. |
| `Real Hardware` | The physical-layout operating environment. It requires a complete, validated Hardware Setup. Every required interface is physical; a missing binding, chip, or device blocks activation or faults the session. It never substitutes a simulated interface. |
| `SimulationController` | The runtime engine shared by operation in all three environments. The `Simulation` in its name is historical; it is not a Simulation-environment-only object, and it is not itself a mode. |
| `running` / `stopped` | The two states of an operation session, held in `AppState.simulation_running`. Running advances the whole tick. Stopped runs only the observing steps; stopping drives the outputs to their `safe state`, and starting permits output again. A stopped session is not a frozen railway: in the two hardware environments the layout goes on carrying whatever is on it, and the detectors go on reporting it. Canonical home **Running and stopped** under **Core Runtime Loop**. |
| `authoring step` / `observing step` | The two halves every step of the tick falls into. An authoring step decides what happens next and drives the layout to make it happen; it runs only while the session is running. An observing step reads the detectors and makes the model agree with them; it cannot invent motion, so it runs running or stopped. Canonical home **Running and stopped** under **Core Runtime Loop**. |
| `stopped pass` | One turn of the runtime loop in a stopped Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware session: the observing steps and nothing else. A stopped Simulation session runs no such pass. Canonical home **Running and stopped** under **Core Runtime Loop**. |

## Design Direction

**Ownership split**

- `TrainController` owns live operational state: ATC/ATO/manual mode, handle position, future manual `power_percent`-mode inputs, and the canonical live lookahead data series. "Target speed" and "speed-limit reason" MUST NOT be stored as standalone fields anywhere. They are the value and the reason metadata at position 0 of the lookahead series.
- `TrainController` also owns live power and cab state: the canonical `state` machine (which includes `OFF` for power off), the active cab selection, and any live direction-selection inputs tied to that active cab. The convenience read `power_on` is derived from `state != "OFF"`. Once the runtime pairs each `Train` with one `TrainController` (1:1), these values are not re-synced from `Train` on every tick.
- `Train` owns only persistent train data: identity, consist, capability/profile references, and other non-live train fields. It does not mirror live position or span, live speed, ATO/ATC/path-runtime state, power/cab state, or operational state. Initial live placement comes through `SimulationController.add_train(...)`. That call builds the `Train`, creates or obtains the `TrainController`, works out the initial span from the placement payload, and writes the initial `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before the first simulation tick. The frontend/API input shape stays the same (`id`, optional `position: {x, y}`, `direction`, `max_speed`, `length`, and other persistent train fields). Bootstrap data must enter through `add_train(...)` or explicit controller commands.
- The manual model uses handle plus power-percentage. There is no `manual_target_speed` field.
- Manual control has two controller-owned submodes:
  - `simulation`: the user drives with handle notches. The handle command flows to `TrainPhysics`, which turns it into a speed change over time.
  - `power_percent`: the user drives with a power percentage. The controller passes the raw percentage to `TrainPhysics`. When no braking override is active, `TrainPhysics` converts it right away using train/profile calibration data: `track_voltage` comes from the inverse of `SpeedProfile.voltage_power_curve`, and the calibrated current speed comes from `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve`.
- `TrainPhysics` and `BlockPowerController` MUST NOT decide manual-mode policy. They run whatever command the controller produces, convert command to speed, and emit voltage.
- The percentage-power mapping data lives in `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` as part of train calibration, and physics reads it.

**Errors and diagnostic classification**

- Every error, failure, and cancellation reason in v1 has one of two shapes, decided by one question: does code branch on which one it is?
  - **Branched.** Some caller changes behavior based on the value. It stays a small closed set — an enum or a `bool` — and the branch that reads it must be named where the set is defined. The v1 members of this shape are `PathAcquisitionFailureKind`, `TargetResolutionFailureKind`, the `deviation` / `run_complete` cancellation booleans, and `atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"`.
  - **Not branched.** The value exists so a person can read it in a log, a notification, or the UI. It is carried as one `ErrorRecord` and never as an enum, and no runtime code may switch on its text.
- A new closed-set value requires a caller that actually branches on it. A set whose values all lead to the same behavior is one value plus an `ErrorRecord`, not a taxonomy.
- `ErrorRecord` is the single diagnostic-error object. It carries exactly:
  - `text` — `str`, the human-readable failure detail
  - `origin` — `str`, a free-form label naming what produced the error (`"eb"`, `"out_of_sequence"`, `"switch_intrusion"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"planning_failed"`, `"no_viable_path"`). Diagnostic only: no runtime branch reads it, and writers may use labels beyond the ones this document names. It is the field a reader groups errors by when `operation` alone is too coarse.
- An `ErrorRecord` is always written whole: both fields are present together, or the whole record is `None`. It is never stored on an entity. It rides in the `error` field of one runtime event, and that event owns the time and the level: the event's `event_time` is when the error happened, and the event's `kind` is how bad it is (see **Runtime notifications**). An `ErrorRecord` therefore carries no timestamp and no severity of its own. It is the diagnostic carried by `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, by every latch write that defers a failed or interrupted action, and by the fatal physical-entry conflict payload.
- A plain `reason: Optional[str]` field on a *result* object (what an operation did) is not an error record and stays a free-form string; `ErrorRecord` is for the diagnostic a writer hands forward for a person to read later.

## Layered Architecture

**Layered responsibilities**

- `PathDispatcher` carries out the route plan: it acquires future blocks, locks future switches, and manages movement authority. It plans for the controller by giving it the dispatcher-owned authority geometry and the current authority-end block. The controller then works out the real braking target from that boundary, using its safety-margin policy.
- `PathDispatcher` is the only owner of dispatcher path records. `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` is a read-only property. It returns the dispatcher's current movement-authority projection for that train. It is not controller-owned state, has no setter, and keeps no copy of its own.
- `PathDispatcher` stores each path record by `path_id` in a dispatcher-owned registry such as `paths_by_id[path_id]`.
- `PathDispatcher` exposes a derived read `active_path_id_by_train(train_id) -> Optional[str]`. It scans `paths_by_id` for the one path whose `train_id` matches and whose `status == ACTIVE`. There is no separate registry to keep in sync; this index is just a view of `Path.status`. Each train has at most one `ACTIVE` path at a time. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` enforces this by rejecting a same-train request when one already exists.
- Cancelled or completed paths may stay in `paths_by_id[path_id]` as history. They are not active authority: their `Path.status` is no longer `ACTIVE`, so `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` skips them, and the controller's `received_path_plan` property returns `None` or the next active path. In v1 there is no path-owned deferred-release flag. `PathDispatcher` owns only future authority resources — the blocks and switches the train has not entered yet. When the train head enters a resource, that future reservation or lock is consumed, and physical occupancy becomes resource-local state through dispatcher-mediated writes. Cancellation and completion release only the not-yet-entered dispatcher-owned reservations and locks, and they change `Path.status` in the same step. Because of that status change, the active-path read switches to `None` (or to a different `ACTIVE` path) on its own. Retained path rows do not stop physical motion after completion or cancellation: `TrainPhysics` keeps moving the train using live network geometry and state, and physical `occupied_by` state is cleared only by later tail-vacate release events (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches). `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` rejects a same-train request while that train already has an `ACTIVE` path. Step 1 of the **`create_path()` flow** and the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract** own the rejection rule, its no-side-effect guarantee, and its `failure_kind`. Once the old path is no longer `ACTIVE`, later creation follows only the ordinary live resource conflicts.
- Controller progress reports name the moving train by `train_id` only. They do not carry a caller-supplied `path_id`. `TrainController` owns the stored span and speed. Each tick, `TrainPhysics.tick()` computes the next physical span and speed plus the tile-level delta for that movement. It then calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` once for each resource boundary the train crosses, and once more for the final no-boundary callback that tick. `span_update` carries the new `TrainSpan`, the new `current_speed_kmh`, the ordered `added_tiles`, the ordered `removed_tiles`, and the traversal frame used for each added or removed tile. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` does four things in one step. It applies the new span and speed to controller-owned storage. It runs the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation** synchronously, so any READY↔RUNNING_MOVING transition fires in the same tick that physics changed the speed. It reads the tile delta against `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan.resource_sequence` (when authority exists) plus the canonical lookahead. And it appends one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). Phase 3 is the only place that drains that mailbox: it calls `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report, then clears it. The mailbox lasts only within a tick. It is not kept across ticks, and it is empty before the next per-train transaction begins. `PathDispatcher` finds any active path from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`, decides whether the reported resources belong to that path, and then commits active progress. After authority is cleared, later physical release must not depend on a retained path row still existing. Block release is driven by `block_id` plus `direction` through `BlockManager`. Switch release is driven by the vacated switch resource's train-owned physical occupancy and protection state.
- `TrainController` carries out the path plan: it calculates ATC safety limits, ATO/manual commands, and an active handle or future power command. It plans for physics by producing the active control command for the current mode.
- `TrainPhysics` only executes. It MUST NOT decide manual-mode policy.
- `PathDispatcher` updates the `Path` record in its own registry whenever a path is created or extended. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads a movement-authority view of that record live, so the controller sees any update on its next read. The controller brakes to the current authority boundary using its canonical safety margin. It works out speed restrictions from lookahead over the network and the dispatcher authority structure, not from per-segment speed limits passed in as separate data. The requested target (`requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`) is an argument to the create/extend call and an echo in its result; the `Path` record does not store it. The run's copy lives in `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`, and the **Stop-arrival check** is the only reader that compares live authority against it. The controller reads only the canonical **received_path_plan schema**.
- While a path is active, its dispatcher-owned record is the one source of truth for the controller's authority. To give the controller a clean slate, just cancel or replace the path in `PathDispatcher`.
- External requests (HTTP/API/UI/admin) come in through thin route handlers (`routes/`). The handlers hand off to a service layer (`services/`). The service layer holds the request-side logic that turns an external request into the domain operations above: it enqueues controller commands, writes the controller-owned Phase-3 handoff latches, or calls the `Route`-assignment entry points (`Route.assign_train(...)` / `Route.unassign_train(...)`). Path create and extend belong only to `RouteExecutor` in Phase 1 and are never called from a request handler or service. The service layer is where the "request handlers must not change dispatcher/route/resource state inline" rule is enforced (see **Operator-command processing slot** and the **Procedure / phase ownership table**). Services issue commands and set latches, then let the per-train phase loop commit the actual dispatcher, route, and resource changes. Only a few writes commit directly between ticks: config-level writes (the `disabled` / `manual` availability flags, route assignment) and operator switch-position commands (through `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`, see the **Switch entity** section). The service layer holds no runtime state of its own. It is a stateless adapter between the transport (route handlers) and the domain entities. Services raise typed errors (not-found / validation / conflict), which the route handlers map to HTTP status codes. Bad input shows up as a validation error, not an unhandled exception.

## Core Runtime Loop

**Phase structure**

### Per-train tick transaction

1. Advance `simulation_time`.
2. `RouteExecutor` owns the order trains run in for the tick. `SimulationController.update(...)` advances time and calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state. It then passes the candidate train IDs to `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(...)` and uses the ordered list that call returns. Route-managed trains are ordered first by run phase, then by effective priority (highest first) within each phase tier. A `RouteRun` already running its route (`RouteRun.is_starting == False`) comes ahead of one still finishing its first task (`RouteRun.is_starting == True`). Within each tier, higher effective priority comes first (`RouteRun.effective_priority`, the override-aware read per **RouteRun overrides**; priority is an integer where `9` is highest, `0` is lowest, and the default is `5`). This is the canonical runnable-train ordering rule. Every other site that mentions the ordering cross-references it instead of restating it. A train with no current `RouteRun` is ordered like a running-tier (not-starting) run at effective priority `5` (a one-off route's run is route-managed like any other and uses its own tier and effective priority). A train that is powered off at the start of its transaction still appears in the runnable order — ordered by its current `RouteRun` like any other train — so its Phase-2 command drain can accept `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`; but it skips Phase 1, because route planning cannot run until the command drain has had that chance. Its exact order position carries no behavioral weight, since ordering only governs Phase 1 and a powered-off train skips it. Ties keep the existing stable iteration order. Putting running runs ahead of starting runs keeps trains that are already moving flowing through shared track, and lets a fresh departure fill the gaps in that movement instead of making a running train wait behind one that has not started moving. `SimulationController` still owns running each train transaction in that order. During each train transaction, `SimulationController.update(...)` calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as the whole Phase 1 route-executor tick for that train, before it calls `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)`.
3. For each train in that order, run one full three-phase transaction to completion before starting the next train:
  - **Phase 1 - Setup (top-down planning for that train)**: `RouteExecutor` runs the canonical Planning Kernel steps `9c4f`–`66aa`. The step chain, its order, and its gating rules are defined once in the **Planning Kernel** section, and the **Phase Table** lists the same chain by step ID. After any successful `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` call, `PathDispatcher` has updated its own `Path` registry. The controller's `received_path_plan` property then returns that updated record on its next read, so there is no separate controller write step. Phase 1 may retry or loop within the same tick only while it stays inside Phase-1-owned procedures, such as covered-task index advance, target resolution, candidate selection, and `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` acquisition-and-rollback. Sometimes live-path target re-resolution or a route/controller invariant check decides the current live path must be replaced. In that case Phase 1 must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly, and must not keep a hidden desired-target override attached to the still-live path. Instead it latches `pending_path_cancel_request` for Phase 3, stops planning for that train this tick, and waits for the normal Phase 3 flow: cancel, then callback, then route-deviation cleanup. Operator retargeting and explicit route swap use "abort current run + create new run" instead (see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**), driven by `pending_route_abort` plus its inclusive cancel. After the authority cancellation commits and the train stops, the next eligible Phase 1 replans from current physical progress and requests a brand-new path. Eligibility for this fresh create/replan branch uses the canonical Phase-1 eligibility predicate defined in the **Planning Kernel** section.
  - **Phase 2 - Execute (controller + physics for that train)**: `TrainController.tick(...)` starts by draining that controller's queued operator commands. This command-drain substep runs after Phase 1 has finished for the train, and before any power check, ATC/ATO/manual decision, or physics movement. The controller reads `lookahead` live whenever it needs it, and each read recomputes from current inputs. There is no separate "pre-physics consume" or "post-physics refresh" step. The command-drain substep may set `pending_power_off`, write `pending_path_cancel_request`, and immediately call `controller.ato.stop()` for accepted stopped power/cab/mode/EB commands. It does not commit path completion or dispatcher cancellation directly. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` then computes movement for that same train and must call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` at least once that tick. Phase 2 must not call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, must not read `RouteRun` task-walk state directly, and must not write any `RouteRun` field. `RouteExecutor` owns dwell entry, dwell exit, route-deviation cleanup, and all `RouteTaskRun` timing writes, in Phase 1 and Phase 3. For the dwell lifecycle specifically, only `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` enters `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, and `TrainController.finish_dwell()` returns that state to `READY`; the complete set of other `READY` entries lives in the **Controller state machine** table.
  - **Phase 3 - Feedback and cleanup for that train**: `SimulationController` runs one general feedback and cleanup pass for that train. The canonical step order (a)–(f) is defined in the **Phase ownership in v1 is strict** bullet just below; this prose cross-references that list instead of restating it. A few Phase 3 notes: each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` call consumes at most one task, as defined in the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. A deviation in a local progress report writes `pending_path_cancel_request` (`deviation = True`, carrying the report's `deviation_error`) during the mailbox-drain step (`3e9b`). Like every other cancellation, it commits in the latched-cancel step once the train is stopped. No cancellation ever releases resources under a rolling train. Phase 3 owns `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)`, and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. `RouteExecutor` owns dwell entry, dwell-complete timing, and route-deviation cleanup in Phase 1 (see the Phase 1 description above); Phase 3 does not run a dwell-complete check.
4. After all trains finish their per-train transactions, drain the de-energize queue per **Power**, De-energize against the same source, then update signals from blocks once for the tick.
5. `SimulationController.update(...)` accrues `Block.occupied_seconds` once for the tick, for every block whose `occupied_by` is set (see **Maintenance counters**).

- So the three phases are interleaved per train, not run as three world-wide passes. One train's Phase 1/2/3 transaction fully commits before the next train's Phase 1 begins.
- Phase ownership in v1 is strict:
  - Phase 1 owns downward resolution, the planning-side `RouteRun` writes, and same-phase retries. `RouteExecutor` is the only owner of all `RouteRun` writes. It also writes `RouteRun` outside Phase 1. Three sites define that set and no other site restates it: the Phase Table's Phase-3 row lists every Phase-3 write, the **`current_route_status` write contract** gives the per-writer rules, and **RouteRun overrides** covers the between-tick operator writes through `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(...)` and `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(...)`. Of the Phase-3 writes, the head-advance callback and the stop-arrival step are the two that route through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position via `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`, per the Phase 3 bullet below. The owned steps are the **Planning Kernel** steps `9c4f`–`66aa`, plus route reconciliation and ordinary departure decisions that need no cross-phase cleanup. Every Phase 1 step that needs the resolved next target must call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` again after any step that moves the cursor (deviation cleanup, dwell-complete, covered-task advance). The helper's last result is not reused across cursor changes.
  - Phase 2 owns controller-side observation and execution for that same train: the operator-command drain at the start of `TrainController.tick(...)`, ATC/manual/ATO command generation (each consumer reads `lookahead` live as needed), physics integration, and the controller-owned span/speed update triggered by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`. The **operator-command processing slot** is the first substep of `TrainController.tick(...)`; it is not a separate `SimulationController` drain method. Phase 2 does not call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, does not read `RouteRun` task-walk state directly, does not write any `RouteRun` field, and does not run any dwell-entry, dwell-complete, or route-deviation step.
  - Phase 3 owns the commit and cleanup of dispatcher and physical state, in this canonical order: (a) (`3e9b`) drain and clear the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) for this same transaction — this includes movement-derived deviation detection (which writes `pending_path_cancel_request` with the report's own `ErrorRecord`, `origin = "out_of_sequence"`, per the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**), physical entry and tail-vacate writes, and callback-by-callback route-progress checks against the current task (each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` for the consumed task); (b) (`c6f1`) evaluate normal stop arrival (`PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` releases the authority and hands the arrival to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, which writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` for the just-served stop), and skip this step whenever `pending_path_cancel_request` is set, because a pending cancel outranks completion; (c) (`7b40`) consume an explicit `pending_path_cancel_request` (gated by `TrainController.is_stopped() == True` per the Latch Table; the latch survives across ticks while the train is rolling); (d) (`5d2c`) consume an explicit `pending_route_abort` (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch Table — the inclusive cancel either committed earlier in this same Phase 3 pass, or was never written because no live path existed at write time); (e) (`9a81`) consume an explicit `pending_power_off` (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch Table); (f) (`4f6e`) consume `pending_route_deactivate` (gated by `pending_path_cancel_request is None` per the Latch Table). Terminal route completion is not on this list. When Phase 1 detects the terminal walk, it calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)`, instead of handing off through Phase 3. Latches written *during* this Phase 3 pass by step (c) or later (for example, by the cancellation callback) are not consumed in the same pass; they wait for the next tick's Phase 1 (route-deviation cleanup) or the next tick's Phase 3 (dispatcher cleanup). A latch written by step (a)'s deviation detection counts as an earlier-step write under the Latch-table step-ordering rule: step (c) consumes it in this same pass when its `is_stopped()` gate already holds, and otherwise it survives like any rolling-train cancel latch. Phase 3 must not arm a fresh planning target, create fresh authority, run dwell entry/exit, or do route-deviation cleanup; those wait for the next eligible Phase 1 for that train.

**Running and stopped**

A session is either **running** or **stopped**. `AppState.simulation_running` holds which one, and `POST /api/simulation/control` with `start` / `stop` is its only writer.

Stopped does not mean the railway stops. In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware the layout carries whatever it carried a moment ago: a locomotive stands on a block, a hand pushes a cut of wagons, a train left from the last session sits in a siding. The detectors go on reporting all of it. One rule therefore divides the loop:

> **While stopped, the runtime causes nothing and keeps observing everything.**

Every step of the tick is either **authoring** or **observing**:

- **Authoring** decides what happens next and drives the layout to make it happen: advancing `operating_time` and `simulation_time`, refreshing route scheduling state, building the runnable order and the contention snapshot, Phase 1 planning and switch alignment, the Phase-2 operator-command drain, physics integration, and every latch consumption. Authoring runs only while the session is running.
- **Observing** reads the detectors and makes the model agree with them: the Step-0 global read, debounce, attribution, unidentified-train minting, authority truncation, the forward-only span correction, the Phase-3 occupancy commit, unidentified-train reconciliation, the de-energize drain, the signal refresh, and the `Block.occupied_seconds` accrual. An observing step cannot invent motion — every write it makes is forced by a reading — so it runs whether the session is running or stopped.

**An observing step may remove power and must not apply it.** The de-energize drain is the one observing step that writes to the output boundary, and it belongs there only because it drives toward `0` and never away from it. No observing step may raise a block voltage, throw a point motor, or energize anything else. That constraint is what lets the drain and the signal refresh follow a detector-driven commit in a stopped session without breaking the rule above.

**Stopping is a physical act.** `stop` writes `simulation_running = False` and calls `OutputSafety.enter_safe_state()`, so every block voltage goes to `0` and every point motor de-energizes (see **Power**, Safe state / fail-safe). A train under power coasts to a stand. Every block is then in the **idle** zone, where the digital detector decides (see **Hardware setup**, Occupancy source follows drive state), so a stopped session reads its detectors through the one zone that carries no drive to disturb them.

**The stopped pass.** While a Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware session is stopped, each pass of the runtime loop runs the observing steps and nothing else, in this order:

1. Step 0 in full — the global detector read, debounce, attribution, unidentified-train minting, authority truncation, and the forward-only span correction (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection).
2. For each train Step 0 attributed a confirmed change to, `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` (an observing step) drains `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` into one `ControllerProgressReport` with `current_head_sequence_index = None` and `deviation = False` — the same shape a powered-off train's final Phase-2 callback builds — and clears both sets; Phase-3 step `3e9b` consumes that report as the Phase-3 occupancy commit alone. No latch is consumed, and no other Phase-3 step runs.
3. Unidentified-train reconciliation.
4. The de-energize drain and the signal refresh. Stopping already drove every block to `0`, so on a stopped pass the drain commands nothing new; it stays in the sequence because a detector-driven commit changes block state the same way whether the session is running or stopped.
5. The `Block.occupied_seconds` accrual (see **Maintenance counters**).

A stopped Simulation session runs no such pass. `SimulatedOccupancyDriver` builds its current map from live train spans, so with no span moving the read can only repeat what is already committed. This is one gate applied to both, not a second rule: in Simulation the observing steps take their input from the authoring steps, so gating the authoring half leaves the observing half nothing to find.

**Latches wait for the resume.** A latch is authored work that has not finished, so a stopped pass consumes none of them; the rule and its scope live in the **Latch Table**. Stopping settles nothing a latch is waiting on and undoes nothing: the physical effect of `pending_power_off` has already happened, because the safe state cut the rails, and a train under a latched cancel coasts to a stand during the stop, so that latch's `is_stopped()` gate is already satisfied when the session resumes.

**No deviation verdict is reached while stopped.** A deviation is a statement about live authority, and a stopped session exercises none. A train pushed off its `resource_sequence` while the session is stopped has its blocks committed like any other detected change, and the first *running* tick's Phase-2 `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` callback after the resume finds the head outside the sequence and sets `deviation = true` on that report; Phase-3 step `3e9b` latches the cancellation through the ordinary **Physical progress / deviation procedure** (step `389a`), and step `7b40` commits it once the train is stopped.

**Operator-command processing slot**

- Operator commands targeted at a train (`TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()`, `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_mode(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_handle(...)`, `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent(...)`, `TrainController.eb.activate()`, `TrainController.eb.release()`, `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`) are queued onto the targeted train's `TrainController` as they arrive from the API/UI layer. `TrainController.tick(...)` drains them as its first substep for that train, before any controller decision. For a powered-on train, this substep runs after Phase 1 has finished planning. For a powered-off train, Phase 1 is skipped, but this Phase-2 tick entry still runs so `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted. So when a train is powered, operator intent shows up in the same tick's controller commands and physics step; route planning after power-on waits for a later Phase 1 tick.
- Within that drain slot, queued commands run in the canonical order below, so that higher-urgency commands see the lower-urgency control selections applied earlier in the same tick:
  1. `power_on_cmd()`
  2. `set_mode(...)`
  3. `set_cab(...)`
  4. `set_manual_mode_type(...)`
  5. `set_handle(...)` / `set_manual_power_percent(...)`
  6. `eb.activate()`
  7. `eb.release()`
  8. `power_off_cmd()`

  The drain order enforces three prerequisite pairs:

  - **Mode before cab and manual-mode-type.** `set_mode(...)` (slot 2) always runs before `set_cab(...)` (slot 3) and `set_manual_mode_type(...)` (slot 4). Both of those commands need `control_mode == "manual"` to be accepted (operator cab selection is manual-only in v1, and `set_manual_mode_type(...)` needs manual mode). This way a same-tick "switch to manual, then pick a cab" batch is accepted in one drain.
  - **Cab before handle.** `set_cab(...)` (slot 3) runs before `set_handle(...)` (slot 5). `set_handle(...)` needs a selected cab.
  - **Power percent before EB release.** `set_manual_power_percent(...)` (slot 5) runs before `eb.release()` (slot 7). This lets a driver holding a retained percentage under EB satisfy the **driver-control interlock** and release in one batch, because the release sees the zero the same drain just wrote.
- Each command's preconditions (stopped-only, power-on, EB-not-latched, cab-selected) are re-checked the moment it is drained, against the controller state that earlier commands in the same drain have set. A command that fails its preconditions is rejected (`success=False`) and does not block later commands in the same queue.
- Some commands write Phase-3 latches: `pending_path_cancel_request` (from the operator-issued and ATC-derived EB paths), `pending_route_abort` (from explicit route-abort requests), and `pending_power_off` (from `power_off_cmd()`). They write those latches during this tick-entry command drain, or — for ATC-derived EB activation — from later inside the same `TrainController.tick()` call. The same train's later Phase 3 consumes them in the canonical Phase-3 latch-consumption order. External API/UI/admin operations must follow this same internal workflow: they enqueue controller commands or set the controller-owned Phase-3 handoff payloads, then let the per-train phase loop commit the dispatcher, route, and resource changes. They must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clear controller authority, remove route runs, or release resources inline from the request handler. `TrainController.eb.activate()` is the canonical operator-issued EB command. ATC overspeed escalation can also raise EB from inside `TrainController.tick()` — including the zero-limit branch in step `826a`, where the canonical lookahead has dropped to `atc_limit = 0` with `atc_limit_reason_code = "deviation"` or `"no_authority"`. Both paths perform the **canonical EB activation effects** defined in the **EB workflow state machine**, so Phase 3 commits any resulting cancellation through the same dispatcher cancel-and-release path used by every other cancellation kind.
- Submitting an API/UI command and executing it give separate results. A request handler returns success once the command is appended to the targeted controller's command queue, or once a direct Phase-3 handoff such as `pending_path_cancel_request` is written. It does not block waiting for the later tick-entry drain. The real command result comes when `TrainController.tick(...)` drains the command: each drained command emits one event through the runtime `NotificationCenter` per the **Event schema**, with the command's own function name as `operation`, the train in `entities`, and `{command_id, success, reason}` in `payload`, for UI/API consumers. If a command fails its stopped-only, power, EB, cab, or mode preconditions at drain time, that later result is `success=False` with the diagnostic reason, while the original enqueue response stays an accepted/pending submission. `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` may be accepted with EB latched, as long as the stopped-only shutdown precondition holds; EB release is not required for shutdown.
- `TrainController.eb.release()` is latch-based. Its full gate is defined in the **`TrainController.eb.release()`** contract. On acceptance from `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, it clears `eb.active` and stays in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`; no path was cancelled and the dwell timer continues. On acceptance from any other powered state, it transitions to **`READY`** and ordinary Phase-1 recovery/replanning follows. It never calls `RouteExecutor` or writes `RouteRun` directly.

**Runtime notifications**

- `NotificationCenter` owns the one runtime event stream in v1. It is exposed through `SimulationController.notification_center`. Every component that has something to report — a command result, a failure, a latch write, a state change worth reviewing later — reports it by calling `NotificationCenter.emit(...)`. No component keeps an event queue, a toast buffer, or a log of its own. There is exactly one stream, and every operator-facing "what happened" surface is a filter over it.
- **Event schema.** Every event carries exactly these fields:
  - `notification_id` — `int`, assigned by `NotificationCenter` in emit order. It is the order the stream reads back in.
  - `event_time` — `float`, system-time Unix seconds, sampled by the emitting function at the moment it emits (per **Time sampling**).
  - `source` — `str`, the class that emitted the event (`"PathDispatcher"`, `"RouteExecutor"`, `"TrainController"`, `"BlockManager"`, `"SwitchManager"`, `"SimulationController"`).
  - `operation` — `str`, the name of the function that emitted the event (`"cancel_path"`, `"set_path_cancel_request"`, `"handle_path_completion"`, `"set_handle"`). Every emitting function writes its own name here and never another function's. `source` plus `operation` is the "where did this come from" key a reader filters on.
  - `kind` — `"debug" | "info" | "warning" | "error"`, how serious the event is. This is the only level field on an event, and the only field runtime code may branch on. `debug` is per-tick detail a developer reads; `info` is a normal thing that happened; `warning` is something that did not work but may work on a retry; `error` is something that failed.
  - `entities` — an ordered list of `{entity_type, entity_id}` refs naming everything the event is about: the train, the route run, the walk-position, the path, the blocks and switches involved. One event may name several entities, and every per-entity view is a membership filter over that one list. It is the only entity routing key: a per-train view filters for an `entity_type == "train"` ref rather than reading a separate scalar.
  - `message` — `str`, one human-readable line saying what the operation did. Always present, on every event at every level.
  - The v1 `entity_type` values are `"train"`, `"route_run"`, `"walk_position"`, `"path"`, `"block"`, and `"switch"`. `entity_id` is that entity's own id, except for `"walk_position"`, whose id is the `RouteRun` id and the integer walk index joined by `"#"` (`"<run_id>#<index>"`). An event about a task names both a `"route_run"` ref and a `"walk_position"` ref, so a run-level filter and a task-level filter both find it.
  - `error` — `Optional[ErrorRecord]`, the failure detail (see **Errors and diagnostic classification**). Present when the event reports something that did not work, and `None` otherwise. `message` says what was attempted; `error.text` says what went wrong.
  - `payload` — the structured per-operation data, whatever that operation needs a reader to have. A command result carries `{command_id, success, reason}`; a latch write carries the latch name and its payload; a cancellation carries its two classification booleans.
- **The emit call.** `NotificationCenter.emit(source, operation, kind, message, entities, error=None, payload=None)` is the one way an event enters the stream. The caller supplies those seven fields; `NotificationCenter` assigns `notification_id` and samples `event_time` as it appends. Every field except `error` and `payload` is required, so an event can never reach the stream without a level, a message, or an entity list. A component with many emit sites may wrap this in a thin per-component adapter that fills in its own `source` and its usual `entities` — `TrainController` does, through `controller.notifications` — but the adapter holds no queue and no state, and the stream it writes to is this one.
- **Retention.** `NotificationCenter` keeps the most recent `NOTIFICATION_RETENTION_ROWS` events and drops the oldest beyond that. The v1 default is `10000`, and it lives in `constants.py` as a **Runtime settings** tunable, so an operator running a long session may raise it. `notification_id` keeps counting past a drop, so a gap in the ids means events aged out rather than that the stream lost order. At startup `NotificationCenter` seeds the counter from the highest `notification_id` in the persisted **Event log**, so ids stay strictly increasing across a session restart and `since_notification_id` never straddles two sessions' worth of the same number. `NotificationCenter` is also the only component that decides read/ack state and how the frontend gets events.
- Command execution results are ordinary events: `source` is the class holding the command surface, `operation` is the command's own function name (`"set_handle"`, `"power_on_cmd"`, `"set_cab"`), `kind` is `"info"` on acceptance and `"warning"` on rejection, and `payload` is `{command_id, success, reason}`. Path cancellation, latch writes, fatal runtime failures, route-run errors, and blocked planning are all the same shape. The component that caused the event still owns the underlying state change; `NotificationCenter` owns only the event record and the delivery surface.
- Request handlers must not read controller internals to find out about later command failures. They return accepted/pending for an enqueue or a latch write, and then frontend/API consumers see the later results on this stream.
- Per-entity reads are filters over this one stream, not separate queues. `GET /api/trains/<train_id>/notifications` returns the events whose `entities` include that train, most recent first, and takes optional `kind` and `since_notification_id` filters. It is a read: it acknowledges nothing and empties nothing.
- The persisted, operator-facing view of this stream is the **Event log** (see that section): the same stream kept on disk, read in a viewer, and scrubbed in replay. It is not a second sink.

**Error events**

This is the canonical home for where error state lives in v1.

- No entity stores an error. Every error, failure, and blocked-planning reason is emitted once into the `NotificationCenter` stream described above, and every "what went wrong here" read — the train-panel no-authority banner, the Quick Route toast, the run panel, the event-log viewer — is a filter over that one stream. No `RouteRun`, `RouteTaskRun`, `Path`, `Block`, `Switch`, or controller field holds an `ErrorRecord` as entity state. The one thing that carries one across ticks is a Phase-3 latch payload, which holds the writer's record only until its consumer commits and hands it to the event; that is a deferred message, not stored entity state, and nothing reads it for branching.
- An error event is an ordinary event per the **Event schema**: `kind` of `"warning"` or `"error"`, one whole `ErrorRecord` in its `error` field, and an `entities` list naming what the error is about. What kind of error it is comes from `source` + `operation` (who failed) and `ErrorRecord.origin` (why). The v1 `origin` labels for errors that block a train are `"planning_failed"` (a resolve or acquire attempt produced no authority for a task), `"task_skipped"` (a broken task was skipped past, per **Unreachable and broken task handling**), `"run_stalled"` (every task on the directed walk is broken), `"wedge"` (per **Wedge detection**), and `"meet_hold"` (per **Meet coordination**). Cancellation origins are listed in the **Cancellation** subsection, and fatal-conflict origins under the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**. `kind` and `error` are independent fields. `kind` is the level the writer declared for that event, and nothing derives it from `error`, from `origin`, or from any classification boolean. `error` is present whenever the writer has a diagnostic record to hand forward, whatever the level: a cancellation the operator asked for is `kind = "info"` and still carries the `ErrorRecord` naming what ended the path. A `"debug"` event never carries one, because per-tick detail reports no diagnostic.
- **Emit on change, not per tick.** Phase 1 retries a blocked task on every eligible tick, so an error event is emitted when the condition first appears and not again while the same entity stays blocked with the same `operation`, the same `ErrorRecord.origin`, and the same `payload`. A changed reason — including a changed payload, such as a meet ceiling moving to a different block — is a new event. `NotificationCenter` owns this suppression: `emit(...)` compares an incoming event against the most recent retained event with the same `entities`, `operation`, and `origin`, and drops it when the `payload` also matches. No component keeps its own "last emitted" memory for this, which is why the rule needs no per-caller state. This is the rule **Wedge detection** and **Meet coordination** already state for their own diagnostics, applied to every error.
- **Reading the current reason.** `NotificationCenter.latest_error_for(entity_type, entity_id, operation=None, origin=None)` returns `Optional[Event]`: the most recent event for that entity that has an `ErrorRecord` (`error is not None`) and a `kind` of `"warning"` or `"error"`, narrowed by `operation` and `origin` when given, or `None`. The `error is not None` test is what keeps ordinary `"info"` traffic and levelled-but-diagnostic-free events out of the answer. This derived read is how a surface answers "why is this train not moving" or "what happened at this task". Nothing marks an event resolved: a surface that must not show a stale reason gates on live state instead — the no-authority banner and the Quick Route toast are shown only while the controller payload carries no `received_path_plan`, so a reason from before the train acquired authority is never displayed.
- The read is served from the persisted **Event log**, so an entity blocked for a long time is still answerable across a session restart, and stays answerable for as long as its event sits inside the retention window (see **Runtime notifications**, Retention). An operator who needs a longer memory raises `notification_retention_rows`.
- **Deferred actions are events too.** A latch records an action the runtime intends to take and cannot commit yet (see the **Latch Table**). Setting one emits an event and consuming one emits another, so the operator can see both that the runtime decided to cancel a path and that it later did. Without those two events the intent is invisible whenever a second writer overwrites the latch payload before Phase 3 reaches it. The **Writer-helper rule** owns which function emits which.
- Nothing branches on an error event. The rule in **Errors and diagnostic classification** still holds: `kind` is the one branchable field, and no runtime code reads `message`, `ErrorRecord.text`, or `ErrorRecord.origin` to decide behavior. Filtering a view by `operation` or `origin` is a display choice, not a runtime branch.

**Phase 1 departure-request writes (manual + ATO)**

- In both manual and ATO modes, Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly on the first eligible Phase 1 pass that sees live authority, whether or not the driver has already started rolling manually. This makes route-managed manual operation go through the canonical depart-acceptance path too. Manual pre-depart movement by the driver is allowed (and protected by ATC), but the route-side gate "depart accepted, route progress can complete a stop" (`controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`) is not skipped just because the train moved early. If `TrainController.depart()` rejects (for example: EB latched, no cab in manual mode, `pending_power_off` set; see the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract** for the full precondition list), Phase 1 may retry on the next eligible tick.

**Phase handoff rule**

- A phase may retry immediately only when every operation stays inside that same phase.
