- If logic in one phase needs a later tick or pass to change live runtime state, it must write a per-train retained latch instead of calling across phases directly. The only v1 same-transaction Phase-2-to-Phase-3 data handoff is `ControllerProgressReport`. It uses the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) described below, and it is a within-tick transient, not retained across ticks.
- Step 0 is outside the three per-train phases but has an explicit owned handoff: `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks: Set[str]` and `physics_sync.pending_detected_vacated_blocks: Set[str]`, written in the detector preamble and consumed/cleared by that train's final Phase-2 `on_physics_updated(...)` callback on a running pass, or by `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` on a stopped pass (see **Running and stopped**). Its forward span correction is not deferred: Step 0 calls `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(train, controller)` directly for each affected train, synchronously before Phase 1. These are the only Step-0-to-transaction channels.
- **Same-transaction commit-callback exception.** Within one train's per-tick transaction, Phase 1, 2, and 3 run in order, and Phase 2 does not run again before the next tick. So Phase 3 commit code may synchronously call controller and route reaction hooks that write Phase-2-owned and Phase-1-owned fields, because no live Phase 1 or Phase 2 reader for that train is left to see a partial state. The closed list of allowed same-transaction cross-phase calls is:
  - `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` releasing the authority and handing the arrival to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, which records the finished stop.
  - `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` calling `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`).
  - `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` calling `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` and `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` / `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)` (which fan out to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` / `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` and `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` / `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)`).
  - `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` calling `TrainController.finish_dwell()`.
  - The Phase 3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer (and the Phase 1 dwell-complete step) calling `TrainController.finish_dwell()` per the **`current_route_status` write contract**.
  - The Phase 3 `pending_power_off` consumer calling `TrainController.complete_power_off()` (the controller-owned OFF-cleanup entry point).
  - The Phase 1 authority-acquisition step calling `TrainController.depart()`, the Phase 1 dwell-entry step calling `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`, and Phase 1 run step `6a0d` calling `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` / `TrainController.consume_service_stop()`. `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`, EB activation, and OFF cleanup must call `consume_service_stop()` to clear an interrupted run's `ops.service_stop_active` latch, per the **Latch Table**. These calls own the route-driven movement, dwell, and operational service-stop transitions.
- These last two are Phase-1 forward calls, and they are allowed even though the Phase Table lists those controller fields under Phase 2's canonical writes. Phase 1 runs before Phase 2 in the same per-train transaction, so "Phase-2 canonical writes" means Phase 2 may also write them, not that Phase 1 may not set the route-driven transition. Cross-train and cross-tick writes stay latch-only.
- The per-train Phase 3 retained latch / flag table is controller-owned in v1. Its canonical home is the nested aggregate `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, documented in the **Latch Table** (see the Naming-convention paragraph) in this section.
- The v1 retained latch table contains:
  - `pending_path_cancel_request`
  - `pending_route_abort`
  - `pending_power_off` (controller-owned latch consumed by Phase 3)
  - `pending_route_deactivate` (controller-owned latch written by `route.deactivate()` admin handler per assigned train; consumed by Phase 3)
- `ControllerProgressReport` values are deliberately left out of this retained latch table. Phase 2 appends them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), and that train's Phase 3 pass drains and clears the mailbox right away. The mailbox is a within-tick transient: written in Phase 2, drained and cleared in Phase 3, and never surviving across ticks. So it is not a retained latch.
- `pending_path_cancel_request` is an optional payload, not a bare boolean. It is the **canonical owner** of "cancel my path" — the only latch that ever drives `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`. Every writer that needs a cancel (EB activation, movement-derived deviation, live-path replan revocation, operator/admin path cancellation, route-abort, power-off, route-deactivate, ghosting, and normal run-completion authority release) routes through `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` directly, or through one of the higher-level helpers (`set_route_abort`, `set_power_off` via `_apply_power_off_request`, `set_route_deactivate`) that fans out to it per the **Writer-helper rule**. Once it writes the latch, the writer walks away. Phase 3 commits exactly one cancellation per pass for that train. The payload's canonical fields are: `kind` (the event level the writer declares for this cancellation — `"info"` for a normal one, `"warning"` for something that did not work, `"error"` for a failure; the writer states it outright and nothing derives it); `error: Optional[ErrorRecord]` (present for every cancellation except a `run_complete` release, where it is `None` because nothing went wrong; its `origin` carries a free-form notification label such as `"eb"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"power_off"`, `"out_of_sequence"`, `"revoked"`, `"idle_hold_release"`, or `"contention_release"`); `deviation: bool` (set only by movement-derived deviation); and `run_complete: bool` (set only by Phase 1 step `6a0d`). The two booleans, never anything read out of `error`, drive the only cancellation-callback classifications: `deviation` produces the deviation diagnostic latch, while `run_complete` marks a normal route-progress release that must not set `RouteRun.path_cancelled`. The payload does not decide whether the `RouteRun` must be removed. If several same-tick writers set the request, the most recent complete payload wins; higher-priority teardown writers set `run_complete = False`, so a teardown cannot be misclassified as run completion. Phase 3 resolves the actual live path from `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` and commits at most one explicit latched cancellation for that train that tick. Because v1 allows at most one active path per train, the payload needs no required `path_id` routing key.
- `pending_route_abort` is the Phase-3 bare-boolean latch for intentionally tearing down the current `RouteRun`. API/UI/admin abort, operator retarget, and explicit route-swap requests call `set_route_abort(...)`; it sets the abort latch and fans out to the inclusive path cancel when authority exists. Structural shared-route edits use `pending_route_deactivate` plus held same-route reassignment, not abort. Only same-`RouteRun` live-path target re-resolution, structural extend failure, EB, and ordinary authority revocation write `pending_path_cancel_request` without a route teardown latch. The three workflows must not be conflated.
- Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly when route conditions are met. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` calls `depart()` with no latch in between. The call may fail (for example: EB latched, no cab in manual mode, power off; see the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**), and Phase 1 retries on a later tick. There is no `departure_requested` latch in v1. Dwell entry and dwell exit are also direct calls. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` directly when the dwell-entry preconditions are met (that call samples the dwell row's `started_at`). It calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` directly (after writing `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`) when `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` is satisfied. The controller never reads `RouteRun` to decide either transition.

**Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption: stopped-train gating and no-active-path skip**

- The `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer is the **canonical owner** of the "is there a live path to cancel?" decision. The other latches (`pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`) "assign and forget": their writers already wrote `pending_path_cancel_request` through the inclusive-latch helpers, and Phase 3 commits that cancel before it reaches their consumer steps. They do not check active-path emptiness themselves.
- When Phase 3 reaches the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption step, it first checks `TrainController.is_stopped()`. If the train is still rolling, it keeps the latch and reconsiders it on the next eligible Phase 3 pass. Phase 3 must NOT call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` against a moving train, because that would release future block reservations and switch locks ahead of the still-rolling train. No cancellation commits while a train is rolling — deviation included. The stop is always emergent; the cancel itself never forces it. A train can reach rest before its latched cancel commits in any of these ways:

  - a deviated train brakes under the zero ATC limit with `atc_limit_reason_code = "deviation"`
  - an EB'd train brakes at the emergency rate
  - a train whose route was cleanly cancelled, aborted, revoked, or deactivated while its authority is still valid (head still maps into `resource_sequence`, so it is not a deviation) keeps running under that authority and stops on its own at the current `authority_end_block_id` (`atc_limit_reason_code = "authority_stop"`)
  - a train finishing a timed `run` brakes under the route-owned service-stop override (step `b41e`) while its `run_complete` cancel is latched, and that cancel commits once the brake brings it to rest
  - a ghosted train is already at rest under `complete_power_off()`'s zeroed speed by the time its cancel is written, so its latch is consumed on the very next Phase 3 pass

  So only EB, deviation, and the service-stop override force a stop before the authority end; an orderly operator/admin cancel lets the train run out its current granted authority and halt at that boundary, and only then does the latched cancel commit. (While the cancel is latched, Phase 1 cannot extend the authority — the eligibility precondition excludes `PATH_CANCEL_PENDING` — so the train runs only to its current authority end, not the full route.) Once `is_stopped()` is true, the pending latch is consumed on the next eligible Phase 3 pass. Several writers in the rolling window just overwrite the same single payload slot (most-recent payload wins per the latch table); no extra blocking is needed.
- Sometimes Phase 3 reaches the cancel consumer with the train stopped but no active path. It emits one event per the **Event schema** — `operation` the consuming step's own function name, `kind = "info"`, `error = None`, `entities` naming the train — recording that the latched cancel found no live path, and consumes the latch. Route-run teardown happens only from `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`, or Phase-1 terminal completion. Operator removal of an app-controlled train is out of scope and is not a hidden teardown trigger. Retiring an unidentified train is owned by **Power**, Unidentified trains, and reaches no route-run teardown because such a train never holds a `RouteRun`.
- When the `pending_path_cancel_request` latch survives across ticks because the train was rolling when it was written, no cross-phase coordination is needed: the train protects itself. For a deviation, the live path stays `ACTIVE` and keeps its locks while the canonical lookahead reads `atc_limit = 0` at distance 0 with `atc_limit_reason_code = "deviation"` (the head is unmappable — see the **canonical lookahead payload**). So ATC brakes the train, and the overspeed branch escalates a still-rolling train to EB. Once `is_stopped() == True`, the next eligible Phase 3 explicit-cancel step (`7b40`) consumes the latch. `PathDispatcher` does not reach across phases to clear the latch early.
- Phase 3 may reach the cancel consumer with a `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED` retained path row. `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` is idempotent, so that call is safe: it returns `committed = False` carrying the existing terminal `new_status` and changes nothing, because normal completion on an earlier tick already did what the latch wanted. Phase 3 consumes the latch either way and needs no status pre-check of its own.
- The `pending_route_abort` consumer is an assign-and-forget step (per the first bullet above): it does only its own teardown. It calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` to fully remove the route assignment — that helper writes `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED` and, when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` itself before tearing down the run — then clears `pending_route_abort`.
- The `pending_power_off` consumer is assign-and-forget. Its inclusive cancel commits on the same Phase-3 pass because `_apply_power_off_request()` neutralizes every propulsion input and prevents later command generation from moving the train after stopped acceptance. The consumer then removes the route run and calls `TrainController.complete_power_off()`.
- Terminal route completion is handled by a direct call from Phase 1, not by a Phase 3 latch consumption step. When `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` detects the terminal walk (the final task is finished and `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns `None`), it calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside that same Phase 1 call. By then, the matching live path is already absent: a terminal stop cleared it through authority release at step `bc55`, while a terminal run cleared it through run step `6a0d`'s normal-progress release before finishing its task row. `finish_route_run(...)` therefore needs no new dispatcher cancel. It calls `Route.unassign_train(train_id)`, sets `RouteRun.current_route_status = FINISHED`, and moves the run to `_historical_runs`. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` then returns `None`. If the route is `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and the unassign left it with no assigned trains, it also calls `route.deactivate()`, whose per-train latch walk writes nothing because no assigned trains remain.
- The `pending_route_deactivate` consumer is assign-and-forget too; it does only its own teardown. First it calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` (the helper writes `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED`, unassigns the train from the run's `route_id`, and — when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION` — calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first). Then it resolves any held re-assignment per the **Shared-route edit teardown**, but only when this consumer owned the teardown: if `_pending_reassign[train_id]` is set AND this step's own `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` just cleared a still-`RUNNING` run (`RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` was not `None` when this step began), it applies the re-assignment (`Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the recorded route) and clears the stash. If an earlier Phase-3 consumer (`pending_route_abort` or `pending_power_off`) or a natural finish had already ended the run — so `execution_state_for(train_id)` was already `None` at this step — the operator/admin teardown wins: the consumer discards `_pending_reassign[train_id]` without re-assigning, so a powered-off or aborted train is never silently re-assigned to the edited route. Finally it clears `pending_route_deactivate`. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` returns the re-assigned route after a structural-edit re-assignment, and `None` otherwise.

**Procedure / phase ownership table**

| Procedure | Detect / request in | Commit in | Cross-phase handoff |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| covered-task index advance (for already-covered `waypoint` tasks and covered stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks), run timer/service-stop/release step `6a0d`, next-target resolution via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`, `create_path(...)`, `extend_path(...)`, broken-task skip / no-reachable-task stall (steps `c481` / `592a`, per **Unreachable and broken task handling**), same-phase retry (`d09d`) | Phase 1 | Phase 1, with run authority release committed by the ordinary stopped Phase-3 cancel consumer | run release uses `pending_path_cancel_request(run_complete=True)`; all other listed work is same-phase |
| head-advance task consumption within Phase 3 drain (`39da`) | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; each callback resolves the next non-finished task via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and consumes at most one task per call |
| explicit authority cancel / revoke request (`148a`) | API/UI/admin request handler or Phase 1 reconciliation code | Phase 3 | Set the targeted controller's `pending_path_cancel_request`. Request handlers return accepted/pending once that payload is written, and never call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` inline. This latch cancels authority only; it never removes a `RouteRun`. |
| explicit route abort request (`911d`) | API/UI/admin request handler | Phase 3 | Call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)`, which writes `pending_route_abort` and fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` when a live path exists. Phase 3 moves the `RouteRun` to its historical state (via `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`) only from this abort latch, after any active authority has already been cancelled or found absent. |
| operator retargeting / route swap (`d592`) | API/UI/admin handler | Phase 3 (via `pending_route_abort` consume) + Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation | The workflow is owned by the operator-retargeting bullet under **Route Edit And Reconciliation**. In one step the handler sets the abort latch (with its inclusive cancel) and moves the assignment, then returns. Phase 3 tears the old run down. The next eligible Phase 1 allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the new `route_id` per the **Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation** step. |
| EB activation (operator-issued via `eb.activate()`, or ATC/no-authority escalation inside `TrainController.tick()`) (`4c64`) | tick-entry command drain inside `TrainController.tick(...)` for the operator-issued path; later Phase 2 controller logic for the ATC/no-authority escalation path | Phase 3 | Performs the **canonical EB activation effects** from the **EB workflow state machine**. Phase 3 commits the resulting `pending_path_cancel_request` (`error.origin = "eb"`). |
| stop-arrival check (`8df5`) | Phase 3 | Phase 3 | none; query post-physics controller and route state directly |
| dwell-complete timer check / `finish_dwell()` (`70c3`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` | Phase 1 | none; query route/controller dwell state directly |
| terminal route completion (no remaining tasks on directed walk) (`879a`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` terminal-walk path | Phase 1 | none; Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly to call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)`, set `current_route_status = FINISHED`, archive the run, and (for `ONE_OFF` routes with no remaining assigned trains) trigger `route.deactivate()`. The matching path is already absent through terminal stop completion or terminal run release, so no dispatcher cancel is needed. |
| admin route deactivation (`route.deactivate()` while runs are in flight) (`7f52`) | API/UI/admin request handler | Phase 3 | Per assigned train, call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)`, which sets `pending_route_deactivate = True` and (when a live path exists) fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `error.origin = "revoked"`. Phase 3 of each train sets `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED`, unassigns, and (when applicable) exits dwell. The `RouteRun` is preserved as a historical record. |
| route deviation cleanup (clear `RouteRun.path_cancelled` and `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation`; derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` / `"CANCELLED"` projection clears) (`5fc7`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` | Phase 1 | none; multi-tick latch gated by `controller.is_recovery_clean` |
| `power_off_cmd()` acceptance (`98d3`) | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase 3 | `pending_power_off` |
| operator command acceptance (`power_on_cmd`, `set_cab`, `set_mode`, `set_manual_mode_type`, `set_handle`, `set_manual_power_percent`, `eb.release()`) (`f482`) | Phase-2 operator-command drain | Phase-2 operator-command drain | none |
| route/controller departure acceptance (`990e`) | Phase 1 | Phase 1 | none; `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly whenever live authority exists for the current target, regardless of `control_mode`. Reading depart-acceptance state by other layers uses `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING` (set by any canonical transition into that state in the Controller state machine table — a successful `TrainController.depart()` call or the reactive speed-based evaluation observing motion under live authority). |
| dwell-entry transition after stop completion (`3a37`) | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-entry step | Phase 1 | none; `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` directly when the route's next task is `dwell`, the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true. The dwell row's `started_at` is sampled by that `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` call. |
| physics boundary / no-boundary movement observations (`9b6e`) | Phase 2 | Phase 3 | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient, not retained across ticks) |
| deviation caused by movement (`593a`) | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 (latched) | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient); the drain writes `pending_path_cancel_request(error=..., deviation=True)`, and the explicit-cancel step (`7b40`) commits it once the train is stopped |
| resource entry / tail-vacate release (`7e74`) | Phase 2 report creation | Phase 3 | controller-owned per-train progress mailbox `physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`, drained and cleared in Phase 3 (within-tick transient, not retained across ticks) |
| stop arrival and authority release (`685d`) | Phase 3 stop-arrival check | Phase 3 | none; `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` directly |

**Phase Table**

Each phase is a strict boundary within one train's per-tick transaction. Code must not cross into another phase's entity chain. Retained cross-phase communication uses only the latches defined in the Latch Table below. Same-transaction movement progress uses the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), which is drained and cleared in Phase 3 before the train transaction ends.

**Why this shape.** Phases are interleaved per train, not run as three world-wide passes, so one train's full Phase 1/2/3 transaction commits before the next train's Phase 1 begins. This gives each transaction one coherent view of network state at the moment it runs, and keeps cross-train reads always current. The ownership split is strict: Phase 1 owns route planning and the planning-side `RouteRun` writes; Phase 2 owns the controller and physics; Phase 3 owns the dispatcher and resource commit (plus the `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` writes that `RouteExecutor`'s head-advance callback and Phase-3 stop-arrival step make — `task_runs[i].finished_at`, `delay_class`, and the appended next walk-position). `RouteRun.direction` is not among them: it is a Phase-1 write, set from the projection Phase 1 consumes (see **Route-run state**, the `direction` row). So each field has exactly one owning component. And because one train's Phase 1/2/3 run in order, there is never a concurrent writer. That is what makes "always recompute live" reads safe across the rest of the document.

| Phase | Entry point | Entity chain | Canonical writes | Must not |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Step 0 — Detector preamble** (`0d70`) | `SimulationController.update(...)` before runnable ordering | one global detector read → debounce/attribute → create, extend, hold, or retire unidentified trains for confirmed changes no existing train accounts for (see **Power**, Unidentified trains) → reset then write each train's `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` → call `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)` directly for each affected train | detector debounce state; unidentified-train creation, gap hold, and retirement; every train's `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks`; `physics_sync.current_span` through `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)` | Plan routes, integrate movement, derive switch observations, write `Block.occupied_by` directly for any train, or carry the detected-block sets beyond that train's final Phase-2 callback (or, on a stopped pass, beyond `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()`) |
| **Phase 1 — Setup** (`17ab`) | `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` | (`9c4f`) RouteRun (re)allocation → (`5fc7`) route-deviation cleanup → (`70c3`) dwell-complete timer check (with `TrainController.finish_dwell()`) → (`c481`) helper lookup via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` (terminal-`None` branch is explicit; broken-skip a missing target of any `task_kind`, or stall on `no_reachable_task` when none is valid; route a present `dwell` to `3a37`) → (`6a0d`) run timer/service-stop/release step (when the active task is `run`) → (`a5c6`) covered-task index advance → (`3a37`) dwell-entry transition (with `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`) → (`592a`) re-resolve next target via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` (same broken-skip, for a task a mid-pass cursor move landed on) → (`66aa`) `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` (step `66aa`, and step `592a` only where it feeds acquisition, are gated by the **Planning Kernel** eligibility precondition; step `592a`'s terminal-walk branch runs ungated) | At `9c4f`: new `RouteRun` (`route_id`, `started_at`, `direction = "forward"`, allocated `task_runs` with `run_elapsed_seconds = 0.0` — with the skipped prefix pre-finished per **Run start-mode seeding** when start mode is `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`, `current_route_status = RUNNING`); at later steps: `RouteTaskRun.run_elapsed_seconds` (step `6a0d` only), `RouteRun.direction`, `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` for the `walk_position` returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)` on the successful create/extend (written by step `66aa` only; never written by `_find_next_task_target`), `RouteRun.task_runs[i].finished_at` (written directly by the broken-task skip in steps `c481` / `592a` per **Unreachable and broken task handling**, and otherwise by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` from dwell-complete, run-complete, and covered-task-advance steps — that helper writes `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`; the derived `current_waypoint_index` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` rather than being separate write targets), `RouteRun.path_cancelled` (cleared by deviation cleanup), `RouteRun.current_route_status` for direct `RUNNING` / `FINISHED` writes; `RouteRun.no_reachable_task` (set/cleared by steps `c481` / `592a` per **Unreachable and broken task handling**); `ops.service_stop_active` (set and consumed by step `6a0d` only, through the controller-owned helpers); `pending_path_cancel_request` when reconciliation or run-completion release demands a cancel | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; clear controller authority directly; read Phase-2 or Phase-3 outputs from this same tick |
| **Phase 2 — Execute** (`e59a`) | `TrainController.tick(delta_seconds)` (operator-command drain first), then `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` | Command drain in canonical order → ATC/ATO/manual command generation (each consumer reads `lookahead` live) → physics integration (emitting the train's `track_voltage` to `BlockPowerController` per **Power**, Block power-controller role) → one synchronous `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` callback per crossed resource boundary plus a final no-boundary callback per tick (each callback applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage and appends one `ControllerProgressReport` to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`)) | `TrainController` state fields, `eb.active`, `ato.running`, `pending_path_cancel_request` (EB / ATC escalation), `pending_power_off` (`power_off_cmd()` acceptance), plus same-transaction `ControllerProgressReport` appends to the controller-owned progress mailbox | Call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` directly; mutate `RouteRun` (including any `task_runs[i]` field); call `RouteExecutor` planning methods; call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`; run dwell-entry, dwell-complete, or route-deviation cleanup |
| **Phase 3 — Feedback** (`282a`) | `SimulationController` Phase 3 cleanup pass for that train | Drain and clear the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) via `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` → route callbacks via `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` (writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` for the consumed task) → `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` (releases the authority, then `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`) → `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption → `pending_route_abort` consumption → `pending_power_off` consumption → `pending_route_deactivate` consumption | Block/switch occupancy and lock state via `ResourceCoordinator`, `Path.status`, `Path.current_resource_index`, `RouteTaskRun.finished_at` (only via head-advance callback and Phase-3 stop-arrival step, each routing through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, which writes `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`; `RouteRun.current_waypoint_index` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` rather than being separate write targets); `RouteRun.current_route_status = DEACTIVATED` (via `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invoked from the abort/power-off/deactivate consumers); `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` and `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation` (both via `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` on its ordinary-cancellation branch, per the Latch Table); the four latch clears (`pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate`) plus the step-(a) `pending_path_cancel_request` write for movement-derived deviation (via the writer helper, per the Latch table); controller-local OFF cleanup writes via `TrainController.complete_power_off()` when the power-off consumer fires (`state = OFF`, `cab = None`, `eb.active = False`, and a call to `controller.ato.stop()`). Controller's `received_path_plan` automatically returns `None` once `Path.status` transitions to `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED`. See the **`current_route_status` write contract** for the per-consumer write rules. | Create or extend authority; call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`; arm future targets; write `RouteTaskRun.resolved_target` (Phase 1 only); run dwell entry/exit, dwell-complete timer, or route-deviation cleanup (those are all Phase 1) |

**Latch Table**

**Why latches exist.** Latches model hardware constraints. The underlying real-world action — releasing track resources, cancelling authority, powering off — cannot always commit when requested. The train may still be rolling, hardware may be busy, or another condition may need to clear first. A latch persists the *intent* of the action in controller-owned state and waits for its gating predicate to allow commit. A latch is therefore a deferred command, not a flag. Adding a new latch is only correct when there is a gating predicate that defers the action across one or more ticks. If the action can always commit immediately, do not add a latch — write a flag or call the action directly.

**A stopped session consumes no latch.** Committing a latch is authored work, so no latch in this table is consumed by a `stopped pass` (see **Running and stopped**). Every latch keeps its payload across the stop and is consumed by the first pass of the resumed session whose gating predicate holds. This needs no separate survival rule per latch: `ops.service_stop_active` and `RouteRun.path_cancelled` are already written and consumed inside Phase 1, and the four `phase_latches` entries inside Phase 3, and a stopped pass runs no Phase-1 step and only Phase-3 step `3e9b`, which consumes no latch.

**Naming convention.** The four controller-owned Phase-3 latches live nested under `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, with the field names `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, and `route_deactivate`. The aggregate keeps the four together, so the latch discipline (canonical writers, fan-out, consumers) lives in one class. Two latches sit outside that aggregate: `path_cancelled`, which is `RouteRun`-owned rather than controller-owned and uses the past-tense form, and `ops.service_stop_active`, which is controller-owned but is written and consumed inside Phase 1 rather than handed to Phase 3, so it lives on the operational state machine next to the propulsion state it overrides. New controller-owned Phase-3 latches must be added to `PhaseLatches`. Throughout this document, the flat names `pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` are prose shorthand for these nested fields (`phase_latches.path_cancel_request`, `phase_latches.route_abort`, `phase_latches.power_off`, and `phase_latches.route_deactivate`). They are not separate flat fields on the controller. Every reference, whichever spelling it uses, means the one nested field. It is written only through the `phase_latches.set_*(...)` writer helpers, and read or cleared only through the Phase-3 `consume_*()` accessors.

Rule: each latch is consumed by its canonical consumer in its canonical phase, and the last entity that uses a latch in the same 3-phase period is the one that clears it. Within one phase, steps run in canonical order, so a latch written by an earlier step in that phase may be consumed by a later step in the same phase (that is what step ordering is for). Cross-phase handoffs always wait for the next eligible run of the consumer phase. Gating predicates may defer consumption further, to a later tick's run of the consumer phase. v1 defines no Phase-1-to-Phase-2 retained latch: Phase 1 either commits its work in Phase 1 itself or writes a Phase-3 latch, and Phase 2 reads only operator-command queue entries plus controller-owned state.

**Writer-helper rule.** Every latch has exactly one canonical writer helper. That helper is the only place the latch field is assigned; all other code routes through it and never assigns the field directly. Each helper is also the canonical place that declares its sub-latch fan-out: when a higher-level latch implies a lower-level latch, the helper applies that fan-out from inside itself. Each helper also emits one event through `NotificationCenter` per the **Event schema**, with its own function name as `operation`, the affected train and run in `entities`, the latch name and its payload in `payload`, and the writer's `ErrorRecord` in `error` when it carries one; `kind` is whatever the writer declared for that latch write; the helper records it and derives nothing. A writer states `"info"` for a normal operation, `"warning"` for something that did not work, and `"error"` for a failure. The `"error"` level is reserved for `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`. A fan-out emits one event per helper called, so an inclusive cancel is two events rather than one. Each `consume_*()` accessor, in whichever phase it runs, emits the matching event with `kind = "info"` on a call that actually commits the action, and emits nothing on a no-op call. This is what makes a deferred action reviewable even when a later writer overwrites the latch payload before Phase 3 reaches it (see **Error events**, Deferred actions are events too). The helpers are:

| Helper | Latch written | Sub-latch fan-out applied inside the helper |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` | `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` | none — leaf latch |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)` | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request` | none — leaf latch |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.route_abort = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"operator_cancelled"` when `has_active_path` is true |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_power_off(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.power_off = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"power_off"` when `has_active_path` is true |
| `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(reason, has_active_path=...)` | `phase_latches.route_deactivate = True` | calls `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"revoked"` when `has_active_path` is true |
| `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` | `ops.service_stop_active = True` | none — leaf latch. The helper also calls `controller.ato.stop()` as a cross-field side effect, the same way `_apply_power_off_request(...)` does for `power_off`. |

Callers pass `has_active_path = controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None`, so the data class itself stays free of controller dependencies. The `power_off` setter does not clear `controller.ato.running`. That cross-field side effect lives on `TrainController._apply_power_off_request(...)`, which wraps the latch setter so every power-off path stays consistent.

Public callers route through these helpers — they never assign latch fields directly:

- API/UI/admin handlers call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` directly between ticks. There is no separate `TrainController.request_route_abort()` wrapper in v1; every caller routes through the same writer-helper on `phase_latches`.
- `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` calls `_apply_power_off_request()`, which neutralizes all propulsion, calls `controller.ato.stop()` and clears the `ops.service_stop_active` latch through controller-owned helpers, and forwards to `phase_latches.set_power_off(...)`.
- The ghosted-train rising edge (Step 0, see **Power**, Ghosted trains) calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_power_off(reason, has_active_path=...)` through the same `TrainController._apply_power_off_request(...)` helper `power_off_cmd()` uses.
- The `route.deactivate()` admin handler calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)` per assigned train.
- EB activation calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `error.origin = "eb"` and `kind = "error"` per the **canonical EB activation effects**. This happens only when a live path exists; it is skipped during dwell, where there is no path to cancel (see the **EB workflow state machine**).
- Operator-driven Phase 1 cancel-and-replan calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `error.origin = "operator_cancelled"` and `kind = "info"`, because an operator cancelling their own path is a normal operation.
- System Phase 1 cancel-and-replan (route reconciliation, structural extend failure) calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with `error.origin = "revoked"` and `kind = "info"`. The idle-hold release of a stalled best-effort hold (see the **idle-hold maintenance and release** rule in Planning Kernel step `66aa`) is the same system Phase-1 cancel-and-replan, just with the diagnostic label `error.origin = "idle_hold_release"`. The contention-driven release of that same stalled hold — when a strictly-higher-priority run is blocked on a block this run holds but has not physically entered yet — is also the same system Phase-1 cancel-and-replan, with the diagnostic label `error.origin = "contention_release"`. Both triggers are defined together in that rule.
- Phase 1 run step `6a0d` calls `set_path_cancel_request(error=None, kind="info", run_complete=True)` after its timer elapses and service stopping begins. This is the only writer permitted to pass `run_complete = True`.
- Phase 1 run step `6a0d` calls `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` when the run timer reaches the task's `task_time`. It is the only writer of `ops.service_stop_active`.

Terminal route completion is not a latch and is not part of this rule: when Phase 1 detects the terminal walk, the matching live path has already been cleared by normal stop-completion in the prior Phase 3, so Phase 1's direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` call has no inclusive cancel to write.

| Latch | Owner | Type | Canonical writer helper | Written by (phase + entity) | Consumed by (phase + entity) | Gating state | Multi-tick |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `path_cancelled` (`75cb`) | `RouteRun` | `bool` | `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (leaf — no fan-out) | Phase 3 — `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` invoked from `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` callback | Phase 1 — `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` route-deviation cleanup step | `controller.is_recovery_clean` | Yes — survives across ticks until gating state is satisfied |
| `phase_latches.path_cancel_request` (`c832`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `Optional[PendingPathCancelRequest]` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)` (leaf — no fan-out) | Phase 1 (live-path reconciliation, idle/contention release, or run step `6a0d` with `run_complete=True`), Phase 2 (command drain / ATC escalation), Phase 3 step (a) (movement-derived deviation during the mailbox drain), Step 0 (the ghosted-train rising edge, via `set_power_off(...)`; see **Power**, Ghosted trains), or API/UI handler (between ticks). All callers go through the helper; only step `6a0d` may set `run_complete = True`. | Phase 3 — explicit-cancel step; calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` when an active path exists and forwards both classification booleans; skips dispatcher call and consumes latch when no active path | `TrainController.is_stopped() == True`. While the train is still rolling, the latch survives across ticks and is reconsidered on the next eligible Phase 3 pass; cancellation must not release authority/blocks ahead of a moving train. | Yes — survives across ticks while the train is rolling; consumed in the first Phase 3 pass after the train has fully stopped. Multiple writers within the rolling window overwrite the whole payload (most-recent payload wins); higher-priority teardown writers clear `run_complete` by passing its default `False`. |
| `phase_latches.route_abort` (`bc5d`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(reason, has_active_path=...)` — fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"operator_cancelled"` when `has_active_path` is true | API/UI/admin handler calls the helper. The helper performs the inclusive fan-out per the Writer-helper rule above. | Phase 3 — route-abort step; calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None` (the inclusive cancel has been consumed or was never written because no live path existed at write time). The abort step does not branch on active-path emptiness — that branching is owned by the `path_cancel_request` consumer. | Yes when an inclusive `path_cancel_request` was written: survives across ticks until that cancel has been consumed and `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None`. No when no inclusive cancel was written (no live path existed at write time): the gating predicate is trivially satisfied and the abort step runs in the same Phase 3 pass. |
| `phase_latches.power_off` (`f8d5`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `set_power_off(...)` fans out to the inclusive cancel. `_apply_power_off_request(...)` also calls `controller.ato.stop()`, clears the `ops.service_stop_active` latch, neutralizes `handle_position`, and calls `manual.reset_power_percent_for_safety()`; later Phase-2 command generation must not reintroduce propulsion while the latch remains set. | Phase 2 operator-command drain, accepted only while stopped | Phase 3 power-off step after the inclusive cancel | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None`. Propulsion neutralization preserves the stopped condition through physics, so the cancel gate is satisfied in this same Phase-3 pass. | No — same-tick consumption; the power-off step performs only its own teardown. |
| `phase_latches.route_deactivate` (`2a31`) | `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches` | `bool` | `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(reason, has_active_path=...)` — fans out to `set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"revoked"` when `has_active_path` is true | API/UI/admin handler calls the helper per assigned train when `route.deactivate()` runs — including the shared-route structural-edit teardown handler, which routes through the same helper per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. | Phase 3 — route-deactivate step; sets `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED` and leaves the run as a historical `DEACTIVATED` row in the run history (`RouteExecutor._historical_runs`) | `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None` (the inclusive cancel has been consumed). The deactivate step does not branch on active-path emptiness. | Yes — survives across ticks until the inclusive cancel has been consumed. |
| `ops.service_stop_active` (`4b7e`) | `TrainController.ops` | `bool` | `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` (leaf — no fan-out; the helper also calls `controller.ato.stop()`) | Phase 1 — run step `6a0d`, when `run_elapsed_seconds` reaches the run task's required `task_time` | Phase 1 — the same run step `6a0d` on this or a later tick, through `TrainController.consume_service_stop()` | `TrainController.is_stopped() == True`, `received_path_plan is None`, and `phase_latches.path_cancel_request is None`. While the train is still rolling or the run-completion release is still pending, the latch survives and Controller tick step `b41e` keeps replacing that tick's propulsion command with service braking. | Yes — survives across ticks until the train has stopped and the run-completion release has been consumed. A run that ends before it reaches that point clears the latch through the same `consume_service_stop()` accessor from `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`, `TrainController.complete_power_off()`, and the **canonical EB activation effects**. |
| `pending_de_energize_block_ids` | `SimulationController` | `Set[str]` | No single canonical writer helper — a world-level collection, not a per-train latch; `BlockManager` and `TrainPhysics` add entries directly (see **Power**, De-energize against the same source) | Phase 2, world-level — `BlockManager` on a span-drop or reservation-clear; `TrainPhysics` on an emission-set difference against `TrainPhysics.previous_tick_emission_set_by_train_id` | World-level post-loop drain, after every train's Phase 2 has emitted for the tick | None — the four-term emission-set test at drain time is the only gate (see **Power**, De-energize against the same source) | No — drained and cleared once per tick after the last train's transaction; nothing survives to the next tick. |

**`SimulationController.update()` calls**

- **Step 0 — global block-occupancy detection (named tick preamble, before the per-train loop).** `SimulationController.update(...)` reads every block detector once, debounces, and attributes confirmed changes per train, creating an unidentified train for any confirmed change no existing train accounts for (see **Power**, Unidentified trains). It resets every train's `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` to empty, then writes this tick's attributed block-ID sets onto the affected trains; the train's final Phase-2 `on_physics_updated(...)` call consumes and clears both sets after folding them into the progress report on a running pass, or `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` does the same on a stopped pass (see **Running and stopped**). When detector state leads the calculated span, Step 0 calls `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(train, controller)` directly, for each train with a confirmed change this tick; that method itself computes the corrected span (rule per **Power**, Physical detection outranks the calculated position) and is the committing `current_span` writer — it applies the corrected span synchronously in Step 0, before runnable ordering and any train's Phase 1, so route planning and this tick's physics both start from the reconciled span. Step 0 is a named preamble ownership stage, not an unnamed fourth phase and not a latch: its detected-block sets have a schema, owner, consumer, and same-tick clear point, while its span correction is applied immediately.
- `self.route_executor.update(delta_seconds)` is a pure per-tick scheduling and Phase 1 preparation call. It advances route-executor bookkeeping time and prepares only the route-managed scheduling state that `SimulationController` consumes for this tick. It does not own the outer train loop, and it does not call `SimulationController._update_train(...)` itself.
- `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` is the per-train Phase 2 execute step plus the immediate Phase 3 consume pass for that same train.
- `SimulationController` is the only owner of running the per-tick loop, while `RouteExecutor` owns the train ordering. In one tick, `SimulationController.update(...)`: advances time; calls `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` to refresh route-managed scheduling state; asks `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)` for the ordered runnable-train list per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**; has `RouteExecutor` build that tick's contention snapshot (per **Contention snapshot**), then its wedge report (per **Wedge detection**), then its meet assignment (per **Meet coordination**) before the per-train loop begins; calls `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` as that train's complete Phase 1 route-executor tick, immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction; and then updates signals. `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids: Iterable[str]) -> List[str]` takes only the candidate train IDs; it does not take `delta_seconds` (time advances in `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)`). The runnable list includes ALL trains, powered and powered-off. For a train that is `OFF` at transaction start, route Phase 1 is skipped but Phase 2 still runs: the operator-command drain (so `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` can be accepted), `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integration, and the `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` storage callback. If the controller is still `OFF` after the drain, Phase 3 is skipped for that transaction, unless Step 0 attributed a block entry or vacate to that train this tick. A detected block change is committed whether or not the train is under power, so a still-off transaction carrying one runs Phase 3 to commit it; an unpowered train standing still has no such change, so the skip is the normal case. If the drain turns power on, the normal powered Phase 2 and Phase 3 flow runs in that same transaction, while fresh route planning waits for the next eligible Phase 1 tick. For a train with no current route-managed Phase 1 work, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` is a no-op. `SimulationController` must not forward resource-entry or tail-vacate events itself. A train whose controller reads `presence_unknown` runs this same transaction as any other train while its ghost teardown is in flight, so the pending cancel can commit under the ordinary rolling-train gating (see **Power**, Ghosted trains). Once that teardown has committed and the controller reads `OFF`, `presence_unknown` remaining true additionally suppresses the Phase-2 operator-command drain and `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integration that an ordinary `OFF` train still runs, because there is no detector evidence to accept a command against or to integrate physics from; Phase 3 stays skipped under the same no-detected-change rule as any `OFF` train. Detection returning admits the train back under the ordinary `OFF` rule above. An unidentified train runs the ordinary transaction with Phase 1 skipped, because it holds no `RouteRun` for `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` to act on; Phase 2 and Phase 3 run the normal chain (see **Power**, Unidentified trains).
- `SimulationController` owns one shared `PathDispatcher` instance for all trains. `TrainPhysics.tick(delta_seconds)` calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` for that train on every tick, including ticks where the controller is still `OFF`. What that callback writes and appends — and why an `OFF` transaction appends no dispatcher/route progress reports — is owned by the **Physical progress / deviation procedure** and controller tick step `01f2`. The controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) is empty at the start of each transaction, because the prior tick's Phase 3 cleared it. The Phase 3 pass for a powered train follows the canonical Phase-3 step order (`3e9b`–`4f6e`): drain the mailbox in order and clear it (the only place movement-derived deviation is detected and latched); run the **Stop-arrival check** and, on success, call `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)`, which releases the authority and then has the route record the finished stop (timestamps per **Time sampling**), but skip that arrival step whenever `pending_path_cancel_request` is set, because a pending cancel outranks completion; then consume the four latches in canonical order. The gating, latch-clear-on-no-op, and inclusive higher-level deferral rules are owned by the **Latch Table** and the **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption** subsection above. If a tick advanced the head across several blocks, those block entries arrive batched in that tick's final no-boundary report, ordered by `sequence_index` (per **Power**, Block occupancy detection). The drain fires one head-advance callback per entry, so the entries consume their waypoints in order, one callback at a time.
- After every train's transaction has emitted its `track_voltage` (Phase 2; see the **Phase Table**), `SimulationController` drains the de-energize queue per **Power**, De-energize against the same source. Then `self.network.update_signals_from_blocks()` runs, after dispatcher feedback, so signals reflect all block-state changes, including path completion.

**SimulationController runtime surface**

- `SimulationController` is the registered runtime surface shared by `TrainController`, `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainPhysics` whenever any simulation runtime is active. It is the canonical home for runtime services, and the only owner of the public `add_train(...)` API surface. When a `SimulationController` instance exists for a network, it must finish initializing and registering this surface before any `TrainController` instance is constructed.
- `SimulationController` is optional in editor mode. A pure map-editor session (geometry placement, connectivity wiring, static field edits that do not affect runtime occupancy or locks) runs with no `SimulationController`, no `BlockManager` runtime, no dispatcher, and no controllers. So entities that hold a reference to `SimulationController` MUST type it as `Optional[SimulationController]` and handle `None`. An editor session that wants live track/switch testing MAY create a lightweight `SimulationController` (with `BlockManager` wired but no runtime route/dispatcher trains), so that switch state changes go through the canonical mutation workflow normally. The choice is all-or-nothing at the editor-session level — there is no partial wiring.
- The v1 surface exposes exactly the shared services and registries those components need: `network`, `block_manager`, `path_dispatcher`, `route_executor`, `notification_center`, the controller registry keyed by `train_id`, the physics registry keyed by `train_id`, `get_train(train_id)`, `get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, `get_or_create_physics(train_id)`, the route-event delivery method `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, and the public train-addition API `add_train(...)`. A caller that needs the route's currently armed target calls `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(train_id)` directly through the route-executor surface; there is no separate runtime-services projection for it. `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) -> Optional[str]` is the canonical dispatcher projection for the train's active path id. `PathDispatcher.get_active_path(train_id) -> Optional[Path]` is the canonical accessor that returns the active `Path` record itself (built as `paths_by_id[active_path_id_by_train(train_id)]` when a path is active, else `None`). `add_train(...)` is owned by `SimulationController` because the frontend/client layer calls it to add a new train to the running simulation. It constructs the `Train`, makes sure the controller exists, and writes the initial `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` from placement input before any simulation tick. **Threading model**: every API/UI/admin request that changes runtime state (including `add_train(...)`, controller-command queueing, and latch writes) runs on the same single thread as the per-tick phase loop. The runtime processes API requests strictly between ticks. If a tick is in progress when a request arrives, the request is queued and processed only after the tick's post-tick signal update finishes. (Multi-threaded execution is Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.) Because all API requests run between ticks, the system can rely on always-recompute-live reads: an API action may have changed shared state, so consumers must recompute live (for example, via `_find_next_task_target`) instead of trusting cached values from a prior tick. So `add_train(...)` is synchronous and all-or-nothing inside that single-threaded model: if any sub-step fails (validation, span derivation, controller registration), the partial state must be rolled back and the call must return failure, rather than registering a partially wired train. A request whose `train_id` already exists must fail without changing state.
- External operation surfaces exposed through routes/API/UI/admin code follow the same phase workflow as internal code. A request that wants to cancel or revoke a live path resolves the target train/controller, calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)`, and returns success once that accepted/pending payload is written. A request that wants to abort the whole route run calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` per the **Writer-helper rule** in the **Latch Table** preamble; the abort latch drives route-run removal. A request that sends an operator command returns success once the command is queued. Any later failure is emitted through `NotificationCenter`, by the real command or dispatcher operation, when the command queue or Phase-3 handoff is actually consumed. The later per-train Phase 3 pass is the only place that calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clears authority, releases future resources, removes route runs, and runs route callbacks. Request handlers must not change dispatcher path records, controller authority, route-run state, block reservations, or switch locks directly. A `Switch.position` change is the one resource write handlers do drive between ticks, and only through `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`, which runs the canonical validate → write → notify → throw workflow synchronously and returns its result (see the **Switch entity** section); handlers never write `Switch.position` themselves. Entity availability/config flags (`disabled`, `manual`) and route assignment are config-level writes that handlers may commit directly between ticks.
- When a `SimulationController` instance is created for a runtime session, it is the first runtime object initialized for that network run. `SimulationController` is the canonical owner and registry of `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `PathDispatcher`, and `RouteExecutor` (it constructs `RouteExecutor`, holds the `PathDispatcher` it is built with, and obtains the resource managers from the network). It wires the runtime managers it owns to the same `SimulationController` reference, so cross-entity callbacks — such as the `SwitchManager` → `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` notify — resolve through `self.simulation_controller.block_manager` instead of needing direct injected back-references. Only then does it allow `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` to create a `TrainController`. Any entity created or loaded later must be registered with this same `SimulationController` before it may change cross-entity runtime state. If that surface is not registered yet, `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(...)` must fail rather than build a partially wired controller.
- Every `TrainController` stores only a reference to `SimulationController`, not separate dispatcher or route-executor references. Controller-owned release and removal commands do not call dispatcher or route teardown inline. They only write the right Phase 3 handoff (`pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, and/or `pending_power_off`). That same train's later Phase 3 consume pass then runs `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, any required `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` call, and controller-local cleanup in canonical order. Route-deviation cleanup itself is a Phase 1 step that runs on the next eligible tick after the train reaches the stopped recovery state.
- `PathDispatcher` and `RouteExecutor` also resolve controllers through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` and must not keep private controller registries or construct controllers directly.

**Session mode and operating environment**

A session has exactly one **session mode**: **Editor** or **Operation**. These are binary per session (the ban on editing geometry while operating is recorded under **Out of Scope**). Editor places and wires geometry with no `SimulationController`, no `BlockManager` runtime, no dispatcher, and no controllers (see **SimulationController runtime surface** above). Operation runs the railway: it brings up the runtime — the `SimulationController` and its per-tick loop — and drives the trains. The header names this session mode **Operate** regardless of operating environment. (`SimulationController` keeps its historical name in all three environments.)

An operation session explicitly chooses exactly one **operating environment** when it is built:

- **Simulation** is the algorithm-focused in-memory run. It requires no Hardware Setup and has no chips, ports, buses, or bindings. `SimulatedTrackDriver` and `SimulatedSwitchDriver` accept every command, and `SimulatedOccupancyDriver` derives current-present readings from calculated train spans. Train physics owns movement. Simulation is the environment for ATC, ATO, routing, interlocking, timetable, and multi-train scenarios under happy hardware assumptions.
- **Virtual Hardware** is the hardware-integration run. It requires a complete, valid Hardware Setup and builds the same chips, binding maps, HAL interfaces, occupancy selection, and commissioning behavior as Real Hardware, but the transport is an in-memory device. The operator may control raw detector/current inputs, device responses and failures, or a higher-level virtual train scenario that translates movement into those raw inputs. Virtual Hardware never opens the physical controller and never mutates operational occupancy as a shortcut; the ordinary hardware pipeline must observe and attribute its inputs.
- **Real Hardware** is the physical-layout run. It requires the same complete Hardware Setup, and every required device and binding must be available. Its interfaces talk only to the physical device. Missing or failed hardware blocks activation or enters the latched hardware-failure state; it never falls back to Simulation or substitutes an individual simulated interface.

All three environments share one identical operational core: the per-tick phase loop, train ordering, ATC, ATO, routing, interlocking, notifications, and the occupancy read–debounce–attribute–reconcile pipeline. The environment changes only the physical-world boundary and pacing:

1. **Hardware boundary** — Simulation constructs the three happy-path model interfaces without consulting Hardware Setup. Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware both require the complete setup and construct all required interfaces as one transaction; Virtual uses the in-memory device and Real uses the physical device. A Virtual/Real builder either returns the complete interface set or fails. A `None` slot never means "substitute Simulation" outside the Simulation environment.
2. **Pacing** — Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware advance in real wall-clock time because they exercise hardware timing, so their `simulation_speed` is `1.0`. Simulation is not bound to real time; its tick cadence and speed multiplier are runtime concerns. Stopping a session stops the authoring half of the loop in all three environments, and the two hardware environments go on reading their detectors while stopped (see **Core Runtime Loop**, Running and stopped). No environment replays or time-travels (held **Out of Scope**).
3. **Occupancy authority** — Simulation derives detector readings from model spans. Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware read the configured occupancy interface and apply the two reconciliation rules under **Power**, Block occupancy detection: "Physical detection outranks the calculated position" and "The calculated span never runs ahead of the detector". There is no Real/Virtual environment with model-derived occupancy.

The selected environment is explicit session truth. UI labels, diagnostics, unidentified-train adoption, polling, and safety policy read that environment rather than inferring it from an interface type or from whether one chip happened to initialize. The selected environment is stored separately from Hardware Setup: loading layout wiring does not silently select or activate Real Hardware.

**Fail-closed environment lifecycle.** A requested Real Hardware session that cannot validate or initialize becomes **Real Hardware — blocked** before anything drives. A failure after activation reports to `SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)`, which attempts the full safe state and latches the **hardware failure** halt; the runtime does not keep ticking and retrying the failing I/O. Virtual Hardware follows the same logical fault lifecycle against its fake device so the recovery path is testable. Simulation may report injected happy-interface faults when a test explicitly supplies them, but ordinary Simulation assumes success.

**Canonical runtime procedures**

These named procedures are the canonical home for ordering-sensitive cross-component workflows. Later sections may restate local consequences, but they must cross-reference these procedures rather than inventing a second order.

- **Physical progress / deviation procedure**:
  1. `e3e6` — `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` emits one ordered `PhysicsSpanUpdate` per crossed resource boundary, plus one final no-boundary callback every tick so Phase 3 always has a deterministic consume point.
  2. `35d0` — `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` writes the new controller-owned span/speed, converts the tile delta into one typed `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). It does not call `PathDispatcher` directly.
  3. `908c` — During that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) in FIFO order by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report, then clears the mailbox.
  4. `83a2` — `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` handles entered resources before any path bookkeeping by calling `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)` for each one. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train uses through a switch tile (derived from the controller's `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge`); it is ignored for blocks. The coordinator routes block events to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)` and switch events to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`, which compares the incoming `route` against the current `Switch.physical_occupants`, and only when no conflicting route is held by a different train records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch (the intrusion check defined in the **Switch entity** section: a physical occupant that holds no compatible lock for its route is a fatal intrusion, not a clean entry), and converts applicable same-path switch/flank locks into train-owned physical protection. Two trains using non-conflicting routes (per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`) coexist cleanly. Physical occupancy is the top-priority truth: if a train physically enters a resource and recording that entry would not overwrite another owner's live route or block claim, the resource state must record it even if the software authority state is already wrong.
  5. `018a` — If recording that physical entry would overwrite another train's current physical/reservation owner, `PathDispatcher` enters the canonical fatal physical-entry conflict procedure below immediately, leaves the prior owner intact, and stops processing the report.
  6. `389a` — If `report.deviation == true`, the drain latches cancellation instead of committing it inline: it calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(error=report.deviation_error, kind="warning", deviation=True)` per the **Writer-helper rule**. That `ErrorRecord` rides the latch payload into the `cancel_path` event defined in the **Cancellation** subsection. The deviated path stays `ACTIVE` and keeps its not-yet-entered reservations and switch locks until the latched cancel commits under its `is_stopped()` gate — resources acquired for a train are never released ahead of it while it is still rolling, so a braking train still consumes its own locks cleanly at each entry instead of intruding. The next eligible Phase 1 after the committed cancel reruns ordinary covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from the train's current physical position.
  7. `640a` — Dispatcher applies ordinary active-path head progress for entries that map into the live `resource_sequence` (per the monotonic `current_resource_index` rule) and runs the route head-advance callback for every entered block while the train has an active route run — including entries in a `deviation == true` report and ticks where a cancel latch is pending; a reported block that does not match the next task is a no-op on the route side, and an entry the controller could not map under the **Head-mapping rule** (`sequence_index` is `None`) advances no path index. For each entered block advance, after `Path.current_resource_index` is updated, the route side compares that single reported block/direction with the current next task on the `RouteRun` directed walk. If that task is finished or passed, the callback records it through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances) and returns. If the same physics tick crossed another boundary, the next report in the progress mailbox discovers the next task via a fresh `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call. The callback must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, must not arm a future planning cursor, and must not consume more than one task. Fresh target resolution for a new authority waits for the next eligible Phase 1.
  8. `02bb` — Dispatcher processes `tail_vacated_resources` after all entered-resource handling above.
- **Stop-arrival procedure**:
  1. `e564` — Stop arrival is observed after physics in v1. `TrainController.tick(...)` maintains lookahead and stopped-only command handling, but it does not latch or emit task completion.
  2. `e4ad` — `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` moves the train for that tick, and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` writes the post-move span/speed plus any resource/deviation observations, appending them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`).
  3. `4e0e` — Later in that same train's Phase 3 consume pass, `SimulationController` drains local progress reports first by calling `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each report.
  4. `9d8b` — After local progress has been fully consumed, if an active dispatcher path still exists for that train AND `pending_path_cancel_request` is `None` (a pending cancel — deviation or explicit — outranks arrival and skips this check), `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)`. Plain English: the train has already moved for this tick, so this is the point where the route asks whether the train is now standing at the stop it was sent to. `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` are processed only after this normal arrival chance.
  5. `8df5` — **Stop-arrival check.** This is the canonical home for the predicate. `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` commits an arrival when **all four hold**:
     - the train's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "stop"`
     - the live authority's terminal block is that task's stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id`
     - `TrainController.is_stopped_at_authority_end()` is true — the controller-owned derived read the dispatcher calls, reading no controller internals of its own: it returns `True` exactly when `TrainController.ops.state == RUNNING_MOVING` and `TrainController.is_stopped()` are both true, and `False` otherwise
     - at least one of these two arrival conditions holds:
       - the distance-0 lookahead's dominant restriction is the authority-end stop (`atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"`; under discrete forward-Euler integration `atc_limit` sits at its floor near zero rather than landing exactly on `0`, so the reason code — not the raw value — is the operative test). This condition covers a train that braked to rest at the safety-margin boundary short of a very short or zero-length terminal block.
       - the train head is physically in the terminal block. This condition covers a train held at rest inside its target block by something other than the authority end, such as a latched EB or a manual stop.

     A train stopped anywhere else inside its own authority satisfies neither condition and has not arrived. The check reads no centimetre stop target and runs no second exact-position test. Because the live authority for a `stop` task terminates at that stop's target block (the **Authority termination rule**), a train inside that terminal block has nothing further ahead of it within the authority.
  6. `f8c6` — If the stop-arrival check is false, no task is finished for that train on that tick and the live path stays `ACTIVE`.
  7. `bc55` — If the stop-arrival check is true, `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` gives the spent authority back and then hands the arrival to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, which records the stop as finished through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances). The `stop` task and any following `dwell` task are independent rows. This step does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`; dwell entry belongs to the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`. Timestamps that record this transition (`Path.last_status_updated_time`, the stop's `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at`, and any related notification `event_time`) are each sampled fresh by their own writer at the moment of the write per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**; the route-side and dispatcher-side timestamps are independent samples, not one threaded value.
  8. `09b0` — **Canonical terminal-teardown orders.** Release and cancellation share the same lower-layer prefix and differ in release mode and route-side callback. Release: `ResourceCoordinator.release_path_resources(..., completed=True)` → `Path.status = COMPLETED` → `SimulationController.handle_path_completion(...)`, which is where the route records the arrival for step `bc55`. Cancellation: `ResourceCoordinator.release_path_resources(..., completed=False)` → `Path.status = CANCELLED` → `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(...)`. Neither order makes a controller state-transition callback: the controller reaches `READY` through its reactive stopped/no-authority rule when EB is not latched, or remains held under EB until `eb.release()`.
  9. `d843` — On the next eligible tick, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` runs its dwell-entry step: when the route's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`, the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
