That call is where the dwell row's `started_at` is sampled, so the dwell window begins when the train is actually standing at the platform. While the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (or the next task is `dwell` and the controller is about to enter dwell), Phase 1 must not create or extend authority. The same train's later Phase 1 dwell-complete step checks `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` per **Task time semantics**; when the dwell timer is satisfied, `RouteExecutor` calls `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records the dwell row's `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances), then `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, and continues into target resolution for the next task in the same Phase 1.
- **Power-off procedure**: The `pending_power_off` latch coordinates a four-step teardown across `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and `TrainController`. The dispatcher cancel for an in-flight live path is committed by Phase 3 step `7b40` (the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer that the inclusive-latch fan-out wrote), not by the `pending_power_off` consumer itself.
  1. `98d3` — `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()` is processed in the tick-entry command slot and accepts only while stopped. `_apply_power_off_request()` calls `controller.ato.stop()`, clears the `ops.service_stop_active` latch through `consume_service_stop()`, writes `handle_position = neutral`, calls `manual.reset_power_percent_for_safety()`, sets `pending_power_off`, and writes the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"power_off"` when authority exists. While `pending_power_off` remains set, the rest of `TrainController.tick(...)` may compute diagnostics but must not produce a non-neutral propulsion command. It does not mutate dispatcher or route state inline.
  2. `278a` — Physics therefore receives zero propulsion and preserves the accepted stopped condition. In the same train's Phase 3, step `7b40` consumes the inclusive cancel through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`; its `is_stopped()` gate is satisfied by construction. If no live path existed at acceptance, no inclusive cancel was written and the step is a no-op.
  3. `534f` — After step `7b40` has run (or no-opped), Phase 3 reaches the `pending_power_off` consumer step `9a81`. That step calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` through `SimulationController.route_executor`. The power-off consumer never calls `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` itself.
  4. `886a` — Finally that same Phase 3 cleanup calls `TrainController.complete_power_off()`, the controller-owned OFF-cleanup entry point: it transitions to `OFF`, clears the active cab selection through `controller.commands.clear_cab()`, clears any EB latch through `controller.eb.clear()`, calls `controller.ato.stop()`, and consumes `pending_power_off`. Its full contract — the effect set, the callability rule, and its two other callers — lives in **TrainController**. Note: between `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)` invoking `finish_dwell()` (which transitions the controller to `READY`) and this OFF cleanup step clearing `eb.active`, the controller is briefly in `(state == READY, eb.active == True)` when power-off was requested during an EB-latched dwell. Another case relevant to this cleanup discussion is powering on while EB was latched in `OFF`; the train rests in `READY` under EB until ordinary `eb.release()`. These examples are not an exhaustive enumeration—operator EB activation from `READY` is also legal. In every case `is_recovery_clean` correctly reads false; no consumer needs it between the two power-off-cleanup writes.
- **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**:
  1. `cd5e` — `SimulationController` owns the unrecoverable failed-runtime flag and fatal diagnostic payload.
  2. `16e0` — `BlockManager` (for block conflicts) or `SwitchManager` (for switch conflicts) reports a typed `FatalPhysicalEntryConflict` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` when a block entry encounters another train's `occupied_by` or `reserved_for_train` (see **Fatal-conflict `origin` labels** below), a switch route conflicts with a different train's route in `Switch.physical_occupants`, a train enters a disabled block/switch, or a train enters a switch without a compatible reservation for its route. A physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) resource is not fatal; clean-entry rules apply. Both managers report through the same `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` handoff. The payload carries `resource_type`, `resource_id`, `incoming_train_id`, `prior_owner_train_id: Optional[str]`, `error: ErrorRecord`, and active path IDs when known. `prior_owner_train_id` is populated for conflicting ownership and is `None` for a disabled-resource entry.
  2a. `6e4a` — **Fatal-conflict `origin` labels.** Every fatal physical-entry conflict halts the runtime the same way and is consumed only by `enter_failed_runtime(...)` diagnostics, so no code branches on which conflict it was and v1 defines no conflict enum (per **Errors and diagnostic classification**). The reporting manager builds one `ErrorRecord` with a `text` naming the train, the resource, and the prior owner, and one of these reference `origin` labels; the event that carries it has `kind = "error"`:
    - `"reserved_for_other_train"` (`f3f2`) — entry against another train's future reservation, before physical co-occupancy was recorded
    - `"switch_occupied_by_other_train"` (`c0bf`)
    - `"disabled_resource_entry"` (`b0f5`)
    - `"malformed_route_key"` (`a37e`) — a switch physical-entry event arrived with `route is None`. `PathDispatcher` derives a non-`None` route for every switch tile in the interior of a path (see the **`ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` switch handoff** prose above); this label is the fatal report for the case where that derivation invariant does not hold, so the manager reports it as a fatal conflict instead of writing occupancy state from an unresolved route.
    - `"switch_intrusion"` (`d51c`) — a train physically entered a switch while holding no compatible reservation in `Switch.locks` for the route it occupies (the intrusion check defined in the **Switch entity** section). The physical occupant is not the holder of a route owner / lock for that route.
    - `"occupied_by_other_train"` (`e2c7`) — a train physically drew power in a block whose own `occupied_by` belongs to another train. The single shared feed cannot carry two trains' control, so a second train under power in a held block is fatal (see **Block**, One train per Block).
  3. `b9a2` — `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, not the reporting entity, is the only writer of `failed_runtime = True`. Before freezing, it calls `OutputSafety.enter_safe_state()`, which switches off every point motor and zeroes every block's track voltage (see **Power**, Safe state / fail-safe). The safe-state write touches hardware outputs only; controller/path/route/resource/occupancy state remains exactly as detected for inspection. It then stores the fatal payload, emits one `kind = "error"` event with `operation = "enter_failed_runtime"` carrying that payload, and aborts the transaction. Outputs stay at their safe state until the halt is cleared (step `ee19`).
  4. `525b` — Fatal conflict is not an ordinary cancellation/recovery branch. `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)` must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`, or `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(...)`, because those APIs are recoverable workflow callbacks and would incorrectly clear state or replan while the runtime is failed.
  5. `ee19` — `SimulationController.update(...)` refuses all further advancement while `failed_runtime` is set, and on the same gate while `hardware_failure` is set (see **Power**, Hardware I/O failure): no train ticks run and only `simulation_time` advances.
  6. `7c41` — **Clearing the halt.** The halt is a gate the operator reopens, not the end of the session. A fatal conflict means two trains claim one piece of track; the operator goes and sorts that out on the layout, and the layout state they come back to must still be there. `SimulationController.clear_failed_runtime()` is the only writer of `failed_runtime = False`. It is refused when the runtime is not halted. On success it calls `TrainController.complete_power_off()` on every `TrainController` — that method performs the whole OFF cleanup, including neutralizing the handle and zeroing `physics_sync.current_speed_kmh` (see **TrainController**) — then clears the flag, the stored payload and `failed_runtime_at`, and emits one `kind = "info"` event with `operation = "clear_failed_runtime"`. It calls that method directly rather than queueing `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`, because the command is stopped-only — a frozen train may carry a non-zero speed — and it defers its work to a Phase 3 that cannot run while the gate is shut. Clearing is not a resume: in Simulation outputs re-arm through the ordinary energized-set emission of the next tick, which drives `0` while every train is `OFF`, so nothing moves until the operator powers a train up again. In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware the safe state de-energized the layout, and the next `start` drives it again (see **Power**, Safe state / fail-safe); every train is `OFF`, so what it drives is `0` until the operator powers one up. Path, route, reservation and occupancy state are left exactly as they were, because the conflict never overwrote the prior owner and there is nothing to unwind. v1 still exposes no train-removal recovery and no force-clear of an individual resource; clearing the gate is the whole of the recovery surface.

**Path completion notification flow**

- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` gives a spent authority back and then hands the arrival to the route, which records the arrival that ended it. It runs no acceptance test of its own: the decision belongs to the **Stop-arrival check**, and dispatcher performs only the teardown. Release ordering is exactly step `09b0` of the **Stop-arrival procedure**.
- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` returns a `PathCompletionResult` with this exact shape:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `success` | `bool` | Whether this call performed the `ACTIVE -> COMPLETED` transition. |
  | `path_id` | `str` | The path ID that was released. |
  | `new_status` | `PathStatus` | `PathStatus.COMPLETED` when committed; the unchanged terminal status when the path was already `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED`. |
  | `reason` | `Optional[str]` | `None` when committed. Otherwise a diagnostic string such as `"path already terminal"`. |

  `SimulationController` calls this only from step `bc55`, so `success = True` is the ordinary return. The call is idempotent: a path already `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED` returns `success = False` and mutates nothing. A `path_id` with no `paths_by_id` row is an internal invariant break, not an outcome: it raises rather than returning a result.
- `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, `TrainController`, `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, and any other runtime manager/controller component access cross-component services exclusively through the single `self.simulation_controller` back-reference. (`Switch` is a pure data entity and holds no `simulation_controller` back-reference; its cross-resource `BlockManager` notify is owned by `SwitchManager` — see the **Switch entity** section.) There is no separate "runtime services interface" object; the access shape is direct named-attribute access on the `SimulationController` instance: `self.simulation_controller.block_manager`, `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher`, `self.simulation_controller.route_executor`, `self.simulation_controller.notification_center`, `self.simulation_controller.network`, `self.simulation_controller.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, and `self.simulation_controller.handle_path_cancellation(...)`. `PathDispatcher` calls the route-event methods through this surface, not through a concrete `RouteExecutor` reference; `TrainController` writes latches and calls only controller/`simulation_controller` methods, not route planning helpers directly.
- Wiring pattern: `SimulationController` is initialized first and is the canonical shared runtime owner. It owns `PathDispatcher` and `RouteExecutor` and wires every runtime manager/controller it owns (`BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `PathDispatcher`, `RouteExecutor`, and each `TrainController`) to itself by setting `entity.simulation_controller = self` so all cross-entity callbacks resolve through that single attribute. The `ResourceCoordinator` is a stateless facade that reaches those wired managers (`BlockManager` / `SwitchManager`) directly, so it holds no `simulation_controller` back-reference of its own. `Switch` is NOT wired this way — it holds no back-reference; `SwitchManager` owns the switch-side runtime wiring and the `BlockManager` notify. Components may keep that `simulation_controller` reference, but they must not keep direct concrete references to each other when the same access can go through `self.simulation_controller.<named attribute>`.
- `TrainController` instances are created on demand through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, which runs only after the **SimulationController runtime surface** above is fully registered. Every new `TrainController` is constructed with the same `simulation_controller` reference, and controller-owned release/removal commands (e.g. `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`) write the appropriate Phase 3 handoff latches through controller-owned state rather than reaching directly into `PathDispatcher` or `RouteExecutor`. This keeps `SimulationController` the single registry of shared runtime components and removes any temporal concern about `TrainController` being constructed before `PathDispatcher` / `RouteExecutor`.
- Path completion must only be recognized after the train has already moved for that tick, Phase 3 has already committed any resource entry/deviation/tail-vacate effects, the controller's post-move stop check says the train is fully stopped at the usable authority stop boundary, and the live authority reaches the requested destination block in the requested destination direction. The controller safety margin is a braking/diagnostic target used by lookahead and is higher priority than trying to force the head to the physical edge or physical interior of the requested destination block. No arrival may be recorded while the train is still braking or creeping toward that usable authority stop.
- After authority release, route-level waypoint and dwell logic must follow the **Stop-arrival procedure**: step `bc55` records the finished stop, and the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` call decides whether the train enters dwell (`RUNNING_AT_STATION`) or remains in the **`READY` state** (per the predicate defined in the Controller state machine subsection).

**`SimulationController._update_train(...)` flow**

1. Get or create a `TrainController`.
2. Get or create a `TrainPhysics`.
3. On first pairing only, initialize any controller-owned bootstrap values that still come from persisted train data, then establish the runtime 1:1 `Train` <-> `TrainController` pairing for that `train_id`.
4. Tick controller.
5. Tick physics.
6. Sync only persistent train data back to `Train`. Do not sync live position/span, speed, path authority, power/cab state, or operational state back to `Train`.
7. Do not add a separate simulation-owned dispatcher feedback pass. `TrainPhysics.tick()` must compute a `span_update` and call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, and that controller method must apply the controller-owned span/speed update and append one ordered `ControllerProgressReport` with any typed tail-vacate observations to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). That same train's Phase 3 consume pass later drains the mailbox and delivers those reports to `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`.

**`SimulationController._update_train(...)` calls**

- The per-train calls are `get_or_create_controller(train_id)`, `get_or_create_physics(train_id)`, `controller.tick(delta_seconds)`, and `physics.tick(delta_seconds)`. Both registry accessors are public, train-ID-based `SimulationController` methods; no train-object-keyed private variant exists. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` likewise use the public controller accessor rather than keeping private registries.
- A newly created controller may bind only to the persistent train data needed for that 1:1 pairing. It does NOT seed `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` itself; initial span placement is owned by the application train-addition path. The API `SimulationController.add_train(...)` flow must create or obtain the controller and write `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before any simulation tick. `Train` does not carry live runtime authority or live position/span fields, so this constructor path does not seed `received_path_plan` (it is a read-only property that resolves the dispatcher's active `Path` into the canonical projection whenever read; before any path is created it returns `None`). The derived reads `assigned_path_id`, `ato_path_id`, and `ato_destination_block_id` are never written directly on the controller — they only surface when `PathDispatcher` has an active path. Runtime authority is always route-backed in v1: `RouteExecutor` creates the path in Phase 1 through `PathDispatcher.create_path(train_id=..., ...)`, using either the train's ordinary assigned route or a dedicated one-off operator route for ad hoc/local movement. The controller's `received_path_plan` property automatically reflects that path on the next read; there is no separate `receive_path_plan(...)` setter. In v1 there is no normal standalone non-route dispatcher-authority mode. `RouteExecutor` must already have ensured the controller exists before any Phase 1 path creation; `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` in `SimulationController._update_train(...)` remains only a controller-registry accessor/fallback, not a separate non-route authority path.
- In v1 there is no late-controller recovery hook. Controller existence is a hard precondition for authority assignment: `RouteExecutor` or any other caller must ensure `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` has already established the 1:1 `train_id -> controller` binding before `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` runs (the controller's `received_path_plan` property needs the controller object to exist before any reader can call it). If persisted/bootstrap train data is needed to initialize that controller, `SimulationController` resolves the `Train` entity internally from `train_id`; callers still use the train-ID-based accessor. Controller bootstrap must not recreate authority from train-side runtime mirrors, and `PathDispatcher` does not expose `on_controller_available(train_id)` or any post-bind authority refresh — the property always returns the live dispatcher state.
- Consumers read live target-speed data from controller lookahead at distance 0 and live operational state from `TrainController.ops.state`; the sync step writes no live fields to `Train` (step 6 above).
- Initial power/cab runtime state comes from the v1 controller defaults and later explicit `TrainController` commands only; live power/cab state is never re-synced from `Train`.

**`PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` signature**

- `report: ControllerProgressReport` — payload schema defined immediately below.
- Returns `None`. Side effects only; ordering follows the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**.
- Fatal physical-entry conflicts escalate through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`; the call does not return a failure code.

**Dispatcher progress/completion flow**

**ControllerProgressReport schema**: The canonical field set for `ControllerProgressReport` is defined here. Every producer (`TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`) and consumer (`PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`) refers back to this heading by name. Fields: `train_id` (`str`), `event_time` (`float`, system-time Unix seconds sampled at the write site), `entered_resources` (ordered list of resource-entry events), `tail_vacated_resources` (ordered list of tail-vacate events), `current_head_sequence_index` (`Optional[int]`, the train head's `sequence_index` in the active `resource_sequence`, resolved by the **Head-mapping rule** below), `deviation` (`bool`), `deviation_error` (`Optional[ErrorRecord]`, required when `deviation == true` and `None` otherwise; diagnostic only, must not be used for branching). The report does not carry `path_id`; `PathDispatcher` resolves the active path from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`. Each resource event in `entered_resources` and `tail_vacated_resources` carries `sequence_index` (when known), `resource_type`, and `resource_id`; block tail-vacate events additionally carry `direction` (the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` `N`/`E`/`S`/`W` for the traversal by which the train left that block); switch events (both entry and tail-vacate) additionally carry `entry_edge` and `switch_exit_edge` (the traversal-frame edges `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` copies from the switch tile's `TileTraversal`), from which `PathDispatcher` derives the unordered `route` key — both are required non-`None` on switch events and are `None` on block events.

**Head-mapping rule**: This is the single canonical rule for how `TrainController` turns the train head's physical position into the `sequence_index` values it reports. One `resource_id` may appear at more than one `sequence_index` in a single live `resource_sequence`: the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants** forbids a path from holding one resource twice at once, but it allows an extension to take authority back into a resource the train has already passed and released, so a run that laps through the same block carries that block at two indices. The controller must not resolve the head by taking the first entry whose `resource_id` matches. It resolves the head to the lowest `sequence_index` at or after its own progress anchor. That anchor is the last `sequence_index` this controller reported for the current `received_path_plan.path_id`; it is 0 for a path whose head has not been reported yet, and it resets to 0 whenever `path_id` changes. The resolved index is what the report carries in `current_head_sequence_index` and in that entered event's `sequence_index`. This always picks the occurrence the train is physically in, because an earlier duplicate of the same resource exists only where the train already passed it and the path released it, so every duplicate lies behind the anchor. When the only matching occurrence lies behind the anchor, the controller reports that occurrence's `sequence_index` and the entry is a no-advance — the monotonic `Path.current_resource_index` rule leaves the stored index unchanged — and the controller must not set `deviation = true` for it. A head that matches no entry anywhere in the `resource_sequence` is the mapping failure that sets `deviation = true`, and that is the only thing that makes a report a deviation; its `deviation_error` carries `origin = "out_of_sequence"`.

- `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` is the runtime progress API; the payload is the **ControllerProgressReport schema** above. When live authority exists and the controller cannot map the head to the active `resource_sequence` under the **Head-mapping rule** (no entry anywhere in the sequence matches), the report must set `deviation = true`, a `deviation_error` whose `origin` is `"out_of_sequence"`, and `current_head_sequence_index = None`; dispatcher treats that report as deviation/cancellation input rather than ordinary active-path progress. For a single report, processing order is the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**: entered physical resources are recorded first, deviation latching is handled after clean physical entry, and tail-vacated resources are processed last.
- `PathDispatcher` uses `deviation` to decide whether to latch cancellation of the active path. On a deviation report, the Phase-3 drain writes `pending_path_cancel_request(error=report.deviation_error, kind="warning", deviation=True)` per step `389a`; the explicit-cancel step commits `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, error=..., kind="warning", deviation=True)` once the train is stopped, and that `ErrorRecord` is preserved on the `cancel_path` event payload (defined in the **Cancellation** subsection) for diagnostics. EB recovery timing is gated by controller state (Phase 1 cannot replan until the controller has reached `READY` after `eb.release()`); power-off teardown is driven by `pending_power_off` / the controller transitioning to `OFF`. Planning and routing logic do not read anything inside `error`.
- `deviation = true` has exactly one predicate: live authority exists and the train head is in a resource that appears nowhere in the active `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`. A head that resolves to an occurrence behind the controller's progress anchor is a no-advance under the **Head-mapping rule**, not a deviation. The accompanying `deviation_error` carries `origin = "out_of_sequence"`.

  A head that runs past `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` is covered by that same predicate: the resource it runs into is not in the `resource_sequence`, so the report is a deviation like any other unmappable head. Where the head sits relative to the authorized end is not part of the deviation decision.

- `TrainController` owns progress interpretation and canonical span storage. It applies each `span_update` from `TrainPhysics`; derives only switch entered/tail-vacated observations from ordered `added_tiles` / `removed_tiles`; takes block entered/tail-vacated observations exclusively from `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks`; and compares the updated `current_span` with `received_path_plan.resource_sequence` when live authority exists. Those detected-block sets are folded into the tick's final report and cleared by that same callback on a running pass, or by `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` on a stopped pass. Stop completion is evaluated separately later in Phase 3. `PathDispatcher` still owns path progress/status, resource release, cancellation/completion, and route callbacks.
- For entered resources, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must first record clean physical entry exactly as defined in the **Physical progress / deviation procedure** above. Only after those physical writes succeed, and only when `report.deviation == false` and an active path still exists for `train_id`, may dispatcher resolve the relevant dispatcher-owned path record and update `Path.current_resource_index` from the reported `sequence_index` when the train head enters a resource in that live `Path.resource_sequence`.
- After dispatcher has recorded clean physical entry and updated `Path.current_resource_index` for the entered resource, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must call the single route-facing head-advance callback `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(train_id, path_id, resource_event)` for the path's active route execution, if any. The `path_id` argument is the dispatcher-resolved active path ID. `resource_event` is a structured payload carrying `resource_type`, `resource_id`, and `traversal_direction: Optional`. For block resources, `resource_event.traversal_direction` is the block-relative `"forward"` / `"reverse"` traversal direction computed by the canonical block-direction helper from the entered `resource_sequence` block entry against `Block.ordered_tiles`. For switch resources it is `None`. `RouteExecutor` must not poll `TrainController.get_current_span()` or `TrainController.get_lookahead()` inside `RouteExecutor.update(...)` to discover head movement. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` is a Phase-3 route-progress callback that consumes at most one task per call:
  - If `resource_type != "block"`, return; switches are no-ops on the route side.
  - Call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to live-resolve the next non-finished task on the directed walk. The helper is read-only and does not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`.
  - If the resolved next task's `task_kind == "waypoint"` and its resolved-target block and direction match the callback's reported block and direction, the callback consumes that task: call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `i`); update `direction` if needed; return. The next report in the same local progress drain discovers the next task via the same live recompute.
  - If the resolved next task's `task_kind == "stop"`, the callback is a no-op on the route side regardless of whether the reported block matches: entering the block is not arriving at it. A stop is served by the **Stop-arrival check** in Phase 3 step `8df5`, or by the head-anchored covered-stop case in Phase 1 when no authority exists. The head-advance callback must not record `finished_at` for a `stop` task.
  - If the resolved next task's `task_kind == "run"`, the callback is a no-op on the route side regardless of whether the reported block matches. A run's `task_location` is only its authority direction seed; only Phase 1 run step `6a0d` accumulates its timer and finishes it after the service stop and authority release.
  - If the reported block does not match the next task's target block, the callback is a no-op on the route side. Coverage of an intermediate task that the train physically passed through without entering is reconciled by the next Phase 1's covered-task index advance — Phase 3 does not run that reconciliation.
  - The callback must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, must not arm a future planning cursor, and must not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.

  Head-entry route progress is not gated by depart-acceptance: if the head enters a `waypoint` task's resolved block before departure acceptance, the callback still consumes that task.
- When the entered resource is a block, that same dispatcher-owned entry handoff calls `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry("block", block_id, train_id, path_id, route=None)` (the canonical five-parameter signature; `route` is ignored for blocks per the **ResourceCoordinator** facade), which routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)` — the only normal runtime path that marks a block physically `OCCUPIED`. It consumes any same-train dispatcher reservation for that block because the resource has moved from future authority into train-owned physical occupancy.
- When the entered resource is a switch, the same dispatcher-owned entry handoff calls `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry("switch", switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`, which routes to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`; entry semantics, conflict cases, and flank-lock conversion are owned by the **SwitchManager** section and step `83a2` of the **Physical progress / deviation procedure**. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train uses through the switch tile, derived by `PathDispatcher` from the controller's `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge` (in turn populated from the train's `TileTraversal`). The dispatcher must always pass a non-`None` route for switch physical-entry events: a switch in the middle of a path always has neighbouring tiles on both sides so `_derive_switch_route(...)` always resolves an entry and an exit edge. A `route is None` argument is a v1 contract violation; `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` reports it through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `error.origin = "malformed_route_key"` rather than writing any occupancy state.
- Physical occupancy updates still happen even when no `ACTIVE` path remains for that train. If `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` is already clear because the old authority was completed or cancelled, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` must still apply the resource-typed physical entry update via `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` and simply skip path-progress / completion bookkeeping because there is no active authority row left to update. (Because terminal `Path.status` transitions commit only on a stopped train, such no-path events occur normally only through the detector's one-tick lag — a block entry confirmed on the tick right after completion/cancellation — and they update physical state only.) Clean physical entry records the reported block or switch as occupied by this `train_id` and consumes same-train future claims. A conflicting physical entry is not a normal workflow: it enters the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** — no silent overwrite of the prior owner, no recoverable-cancellation treatment, and no continued route or dispatcher bookkeeping. Every train stays halted until the operator clears the gate (step `7c41` of that procedure).
- A live dispatcher-owned authority path stays `ACTIVE` until it is either `COMPLETED` or `CANCELLED`; entering the final block/resource only updates progress and completion predicates. There is no other lifecycle state between them.
- Tail-vacate release remains resource-local and dispatcher-mediated. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports vacated resources in `tail_vacated_resources`, and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` releases each vacated resource by calling `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` after it has processed any `entered_resources` in that same report. The coordinator routes the release to `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` for blocks and to `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` for switches. `direction` is carried for blocks (the vacated block's forward `exit_edge`, or `None` per its canonical definition under **BlockManager**) and ignored for switches. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train used through the switch tile (derived by `PathDispatcher` from the vacating `ResourceEvent.entry_edge` + `switch_exit_edge`); it is the canonical key into `Switch.physical_occupants` and is ignored for blocks. The dispatcher must always pass a non-`None` route for switch tail-vacate events for the same reason as physical entry above. When the supplied `route` matches a current `Switch.physical_occupants` entry owned by this `train_id`, that single entry is removed; when no remaining routes are owned by this train, train-owned physical protection for that switch is also cleared and the neighbour flank-release walk runs. A supplied `route` that matches no `physical_occupants` entry owned by `train_id` is a contract violation, not a normal case: in v1 the controller emits the same `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` key on tail-vacate that it recorded on entry, so a mismatch means a stale or malformed `ResourceEvent` (the entry-side analogue is the `"malformed_route_key"` fatal contract violation). A release is safe to degrade rather than freeze the runtime, so the manager emits one `kind = "warning"` event naming the train, the switch, and the mismatched route, then — when `train_id` still owns at least one entry on this switch — removes exactly one: the first owned entry under the canonical route-key ordering (route frozensets compared as sorted edge tuples). That keeps the release deterministic and strands no entry (the train's remaining vacate events release the rest), and the same flank-release walk runs after the removal. Active progress continues to resolve through `PathDispatcher`'s registries rather than from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`, but tail-release logic after authority release must resolve from the resource itself so it still works after there is no active dispatcher authority. The single `resource_type` string branch lives inside the coordinator method; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on it.
- `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` is not a deferred-release hook. Once a path is no longer `ACTIVE`, any later physical release is resource-local and triggered by tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches).

**Controller/train sync**

- `TrainController.tick(delta_seconds)` owns ATC, ATO, and manual-control runtime calculations; target speed and speed-limit reason live only in the canonical controller lookahead payload, never as standalone scalars (see **Design Direction**).
- `Train` must keep only persistent train data in sync from the simulation loop.

**Tick-order dependency**

- For one train's transaction, Phase 1 must run before Phase 2 because the controller must read path tiles that already exist.
- For one train's transaction, Phase 2 must run before Phase 3 because the dispatcher must read positions that physics has just produced.
- Physics progress and route-level reactions are synchronous within the same per-train transaction: a boundary crossing reported through `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` is committed by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` and any route callback before the next train's transaction starts.

## Units

**Units contract**

- All deceleration math is expressed in a single unit system. `SpeedProfile.deceleration_rate`, `emergency_brake_rate`, `friction_rate`, and `acceleration_rate` are all in `km/h/s`, and `TrainPhysics` and the controller braking-distance helpers consume them directly in those units (converting to `cm/s` / `cm/s^2` only through the model-scale helpers when integrating distance). There is no second deceleration rate in other units to reconcile: braking is driven entirely by the `SpeedProfile` rates, so no per-layer unit reinterpretation occurs.
- **Canonical stopped predicate.** One stopped predicate is used everywhere: `TrainController.is_stopped()`, comparing controller-owned `current_speed_kmh` against `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH = 0.1`. `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH` is the global "considered stopped" speed threshold, adjustable as `stopped_speed_threshold_kmh` in **Runtime settings**.
  - It is distinct from any ATC zero-speed limit. The "ATC limit" mentioned elsewhere in this document means the ATC-derived speed cap at distance 0 in the canonical lookahead — when that cap is `0` the ATC is granting no clearance distance.
  - Every consumer of a stop condition uses this same helper instead of introducing a layer-specific stop threshold: controller-reported stop completion, `TrainController.commands.power_off_cmd()`, `TrainController.commands.set_cab()`, `TrainController.commands.set_mode()`, and `TrainController.eb.release()`.

**Time sampling**

- v1 timestamps are sampled at the write site, never threaded as method or callback parameters. Whenever this document says `current_time` for a path/route/notification timestamp, it means `time.time()` sampled at the moment the writer commits the write. Every site that records a `last_status_updated_time`, `started_at`, `finished_at`, or event `event_time` calls `time.time()` itself at the moment of the write. Callers do not pass the timestamp in. When one writer needs to record both an event time on a notification and a status timestamp on a related entity in the same step, the writer samples `time.time()` once locally and uses that one value for both writes.
- Every stored timestamp is a wall-clock instant. Durations are a separate question: which clock a duration measures against is decided by **Clocks** below, and no site restates that choice.

**Clocks**

v1 keeps three clocks. Every duration in this document measures against exactly one of them, and every stored instant is written in the first.

| Clock | Advances | Playback multiplier | Reset |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| wall | always | not applied | never; it is system time |
| `AppState.operating_time` | only while the session is running | applied | process start |
| `SimulationController.simulation_time` | only while the session is running | applied | process start, and whenever a session builds a new `SimulationController` |

- **Wall** is `time.time()`, system-time Unix seconds. It measures what the world does whether or not the app is running it, and it is the only clock an absolute instant may be written in. Every stored timestamp in this document — `started_at`, `finished_at`, `created_at`, `last_edited_at`, `target_time`, event `event_time` — is a wall timestamp, sampled at the write site per **Time sampling** above.
- **`operating_time`** is monotonic seconds of operation since process start. Each running pass of the runtime loop adds that pass's wall delta multiplied by `simulation_speed`; a stopped pass adds nothing. It measures how long the app has been conducting, which is what a schedule holds against: a dwell serves its whole hold however long the operator was away, and serves it four times as fast at four times playback. It lives on `AppState` beside `simulation_running` and `simulation_speed`, so loading a layout does not reset it and only a process restart does.
- **`simulation_time`** is the variable physics integrates against. It adds the same wall delta multiplied by `simulation_speed`, capped per pass at `MAX_SIM_DELTA_SECONDS` (lives in `constants.py`) so one stalled pass cannot integrate a single large jump and drive a train past its speed limit. That cap makes it a safe integrator rather than a faithful clock — it discards real time under load, and discards more of it at higher multipliers — so no schedule measures against it. Its consumers are physics integration and the `run` task's window (`RouteTaskRun.run_elapsed_seconds`), so "run 20 s" means 20 s of simulation.
- **An `operating_time` value never leaves the session.** No `operating_time` value is written to a layout save, an **Event log** row, or any other record that outlives the process, because the number means nothing against a later session's clock. Everything persisted is written in wall time.
- **A client derives no durations.** A frontend renders the instants and the elapsed values a payload carries, and never computes an elapsed figure from its own clock (see **Data Flow To UI**, Elapsed values are server-owned).

**Safety margin globals**

**Why this shape.** Two values, not one, because the right standoff distance from a track-end (where overrun has no platform interaction) is different from the right standoff distance at a platform stop (where overrun affects passenger boarding). One global per terminus type captures the operational distinction without forcing each route or block to author its own value. Putting both values inside `TrainController` lookahead computation gives one canonical projection that ATC, ATO, and stop-completion all read. Other layers consume the projected boundary rather than the raw margin number.

- The controller safety margin is two distinct global values, not a single tunable. Both are owned by `TrainController` lookahead computation and are not authored on routes, paths, or per-block fields. Both are adjustable fields in **Runtime settings**.
  - `TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`: applied when the authority-end block is bounded by a track-end / dead-end / non-station authority terminus.
  - `PLATFORM_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM`: applied when the authority-end block is the resolved terminal block of a platform stop.
- `TrainController` lookahead is the single computation site for these margins. When building the authority-boundary projection in `get_lookahead()`, the controller finds the terminal block entry for `authority_end_block_id`, projects the authority-end block's forward boundary in that directed terminal frame, and counts back from that edge by the selected safety margin to build the ATC/ATO speed curve. ATC is intentionally not trying to drive the train all the way to the physical edge of the cleared/authorized block. This projection is a braking and diagnostics calculation only. It is not route-authored stop-position data and is not a route completion tolerance. Other layers (dispatcher, route, physics, signals) must not re-derive or duplicate this margin; they consume the lookahead-projected boundary instead.

## Runtime settings

v1 exposes a fixed set of ATC, ATO, and route-timing calibration values as settings an operator can change from a live session, with no restart and no code change. `RuntimeConfig` (`runtime_config.py`) is the single store of the current value of every tunable field; `GET` / `PUT /api/settings` (`routes/settings.py`) is the only entry point that reads or writes it.

Each field seeds at process start from a fixed code default — for example, `caution_speed_limit_kmh` seeds from `constants.CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH`. A `PUT` writes the accepted value onto the `RuntimeConfig` field and onto every module attribute the consuming code reads that value from, so code that reads the value as a live module attribute observes the change on its next read. The store is in-memory only, per **Out of Scope** (Persistence / save-load of runtime state): a process restart reseeds every field from its code default. Like every admin/API request, a `PUT` is applied between ticks on the runtime's single thread (**Out of Scope**, Multi-threaded runtime), so it never races the per-tick phase loop.

**Settings REST API**

| Endpoint | Body / parameters | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `GET /api/settings` | none | Returns `{"settings": {...}}` with every field's current value. |
| `PUT /api/settings` | `{"settings": {field: value, ...}}`, or a flat `{field: value, ...}` body (a `"settings"` key, if present, is used as the update dict; otherwise the whole body is) | Applies the accepted subset of the request per **Validation** below, then returns `{"status": "success", "settings": {...}}` with every field's current value — the same shape as `GET`, not only the fields the request changed. |

**Validation**

- An unknown field name is ignored: it does not fail the request and is not reported as an error.
- A `null` value for a known field is ignored, leaving that field unchanged.
- An accepted field's incoming value is coerced to that field's own type. A value that cannot be coerced is ignored, leaving that field unchanged; every other field in the same request is still applied. Every v1 tunable is `int` or `float` (see **Tunable fields** below).
- v1 enforces no minimum, maximum, or other range check beyond the type coercion above.

**Tunable fields**

Every field below is a top-level key in the `GET` / `PUT /api/settings` payload, named exactly as its `RuntimeConfig` field (snake_case). Where a field backs a value this document already names elsewhere, the module-global name and its canonical home are given; the operational meaning of that value stays defined at that canonical home, not restated here.

*ATC Computer (`train_controller/atc.py`, `train_controller/__init__.py`):*

| Field | Type | Default | Controls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `overspeed_eb_threshold_kmh` | `float` | `10.0` | Margin, in km/h, above the ATC speed limit at which ATC escalates to EB (`OVERSPEED_EB_THRESHOLD_KMH`; see **ATC Computer**). |
| `overspeed_warn_threshold_kmh` | `float` | `5.0` | Margin, in km/h, above the ATC speed limit at which ATC emits a diagnostic overspeed warning (`OVERSPEED_WARN_THRESHOLD_KMH`). |
| `atc_signal_lookahead_blocks` | `int` | `3` | Scan depth, in blocks, for `controller.atc.signal_lookahead_blocks` (`ATC_SIGNAL_LOOKAHEAD_BLOCKS`). |
| `atc_min_clearance_cm` | `float` | `10.0` | `ATC_MIN_CLEARANCE_CM`. Not read by any consumer in v1. |
| `speed_trend_epsilon` | `float` | `0.1` | `SPEED_TREND_EPSILON`. Not read by any consumer in v1. |

*ATO Computer (`train_controller/ato.py`, `train_controller/speed_curves.py`):*

| Field | Type | Default | Controls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ato_speed_margin_kmh` | `float` | `3.0` | Margin, in km/h, the ATO operational speed limit stays below the ATC speed limit (`ATO_SPEED_MARGIN_KMH`). |
| `ato_speed_tolerance_max_kmh` | `float` | `2.0` | Upper bound of the speed-tracking tolerance band ATO holds around its target speed (`ATO_SPEED_TOLERANCE_MAX`). |
| `ato_speed_tolerance_min_kmh` | `float` | `0.05` | Lower bound of that tolerance band (`ATO_SPEED_TOLERANCE_MIN`). |
| `ato_speed_tolerance_ratio` | `float` | `0.1` | Tolerance band width as a ratio of the reference speed, clamped to the min/max above (`ATO_SPEED_TOLERANCE_RATIO`). |
| `lookahead_headroom` | `float` | `0.5` | Headroom factor in the braking-lookahead distance ATO samples ahead (`LOOKAHEAD_HEADROOM`): `max(min_lookahead_cm, travel_this_tick * (1 + lookahead_headroom))`. |
| `min_lookahead_cm` | `float` | `5.0` | Minimum braking-lookahead distance ATO samples ahead (`MIN_LOOKAHEAD_CM`). |
| `lookahead_sample_count` | `int` | `4` | Number of points ATO samples along the braking-lookahead curve (`LOOKAHEAD_SAMPLE_COUNT`). |
| `brake_deadband_kmh` | `float` | `0.3` | Speed-over-target band inside which ATO holds the current brake notch rather than tightening it (`BRAKE_DEADBAND_KMH`). |

*Stop and safety-margin thresholds:*

| Field | Type | Default | Controls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `stopped_speed_threshold_kmh` | `float` | `0.1` | `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH`, the canonical stopped predicate — see **Units**, Units contract. |
| `track_end_safety_margin_cm` | `float` | `25.0` | `TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM` — see **Units**, Safety margin globals. |
| `platform_safety_margin_cm` | `float` | `5.0` | `PLATFORM_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM` — see **Units**, Safety margin globals. |
| `caution_speed_limit_kmh` | `float` | `40.0` | `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` — see **TrainController**, ATC speed limit. |

*Route timing (`route_executor.py`):*

| Field | Type | Default | Controls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `idle_hold_release_ticks` | `int` | `30` | `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS`, the idle-hold no-progress tick budget — see **Route And RouteExecutor**, Planning Kernel step `66aa`. |
| `idle_hold_cooldown_ticks` | `int` | `20` | `IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS`, the post-release acquisition-skip window — same home. |
| `waypoint_lookahead_blocks` | `int` | `3` | `WAYPOINT_LOOKAHEAD_BLOCKS`, how few block resources may remain ahead of the train head before Phase 1 arms the next target while the current `waypoint` is still unserved — see **Route And RouteExecutor**, Planning Kernel step `66aa`, **Waypoint lookahead**. Raising it makes trains hold more track ahead of themselves and brake less at waypoints; lowering it frees track for other runs sooner. Tune it together with `idle_hold_release_ticks`, which claws back exactly the kind of unentered hold this setting creates. |

*Event stream (`notification_center.py`):*

| Field | Type | Default | Controls |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `notification_retention_rows` | `int` | `10000` | `NOTIFICATION_RETENTION_ROWS`, how many of the most recent events `NotificationCenter` keeps before dropping the oldest — see **Core Runtime Loop**, **Runtime notifications**, Retention. Raising it lets an operator scroll further back through a long session at the cost of memory and event-log file size; lowering it trims both. |

**Why this shape**

- One store and one endpoint pair give v1 a single place to read or change every calibration value, without a separate admin surface per consumer.
- Coercing to the field's existing type, rather than validating against a declared schema, keeps a field's type anchored to the value already seeded from code, so a field can never end up holding a type its consuming code does not expect.
- **Runtime settings**, **Hardware Setup**, and the **selected operating environment** are distinct surfaces changed only through the application's API (the general rule in **Out of Scope**, Hand-authored config files or env vars for settings). Runtime settings reach the running session on the consumer's next read and reseed from code defaults after restart. Hardware Setup is the persisted controller/chip/wiring description stored with its layout; Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware build a session's chips from it at activation, and resolve the per-write values named in **Hardware setup**, **A binding is read per write** on every write. The selected environment is persisted separately from layout wiring so loading a layout cannot silently choose Real Hardware. Changing the environment, or the captured part of the setup, never rewires a running session in place; activation performs the safe stop and session rebuild.

## Canonical Entity Index

**Entity list**

- `Tile`
- `Segment` (geometry group of tiles: `ordered_tiles`, `length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`, `min_radius_cm`, `grade_percent`)
- `TrackResource` (shared identity base of `Block` and `Switch`)
- `Switch` (its route-keyed physical-occupancy field is named `physical_occupants` throughout this document; the current-code implementation may still use the field name `route_owners`, but the canonical concept is the same route-keyed `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge}) -> train_id` map)
- `SwitchManager`
- `Block` (power + occupancy + reservation unit; a switch-free run grouping `segment_ids`)
- `BlockManager`
- `BlockGroupingManager`
- `ResourceCoordinator`
- `Path`
- `PathDispatcher`
- `Route`
- `RouteRun` (current-code implementation may still use the class name `RouteExecutionState`, but the canonical concept is the per-train route run)
- `Platform`
- `Station`
- `Train` (a runtime-created **unidentified train** is a `Train` record too — see **Power**, Unidentified trains)
- `TrainController`
- `TrainPhysics`
- `SpeedProfile`
- `TrainRoster` (catalog of `RosterEntry` records)
- `RosterEntry`
- `SignalRegistry` (storage and lookup for the display-only signals)
- `RailwayPathfinder` (the shared operational pathfinding home; see the **TrainController** canonical-contracts entry)

**Canonical shared contracts**

This list is the complete registry of named canonical homes. Every shared schema, latch, predicate, procedure, and helper contract is defined at exactly one of these sites; all other sites reference the name. A reviewer enforcing Authoring Rule 3 checks this list: if a concept is defined somewhere that is not on this list, either the definition moves or the list gains the entry in the same patch.

- **Design Direction** — **Errors and diagnostic classification** (the branched-vs-diagnostic rule and the `ErrorRecord` schema).
- **Core Runtime Loop** — **Per-train tick transaction** (canonical runnable-train ordering rule); **Running and stopped** (the authoring/observing split, the stopped pass, and the rule that a stopped session causes nothing and keeps observing everything); **Operator-command processing slot** (drain order); **Runtime notifications**; **Error events** (where error state lives) (the `NotificationCenter` **Event schema**, retention, and the one-stream rule); **Session mode and operating environment** (the Editor↔Operation split, the Simulation / Virtual Hardware / Real Hardware contract, and the fail-closed environment lifecycle); **Phase handoff rule** (incl. the Same-transaction commit-callback exception); **Phase ownership in v1 is strict** (per-phase ownership bullets; Phase-3 canonical step order `3e9b`–`4f6e`); **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption: stopped-train gating and no-active-path skip**; **Procedure / phase ownership table** (procedure index by detect/commit phase); **Phase Table** (which entity acts in which phase — Authoring Rule 5); **Latch Table** (every latch's writer/consumer/gating — Authoring Rule 4; incl. the **Writer-helper rule** and naming convention); **Physical progress / deviation procedure**; **Stop-arrival procedure** (incl. the **Stop-arrival check**, step `8df5`, and the terminal-teardown order, step `09b0`); **Power-off procedure**; **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** (incl. **Fatal-conflict `origin` labels**); **ControllerProgressReport schema** (under **Dispatcher progress/completion flow**); the **Head-mapping rule** (how `TrainController` turns head position into `sequence_index` values).
- **Units** — **Units contract**; **Time sampling**; **Clocks** (wall / `AppState.operating_time` / `SimulationController.simulation_time`, and which durations measure against which); the canonical stopped predicate (`TrainController.is_stopped()` / `STOPPED_SPEED_THRESHOLD_KMH`).
- **Runtime settings** — the `RuntimeConfig` tunable-field store and the `GET` / `PUT /api/settings` mechanism: **Settings REST API**, **Validation**, **Tunable fields**.
- **TrackResource** — **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** (shared availability semantics for every resource type).
- **Tile** — **Tile role and traversal**; **Non-empty parent rule** (grouping-entity auto-delete chain).
- **Segment** — segment field schema (`ordered_tiles`, `length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`, `min_radius_cm`, `grade_percent`) and the **canonical block-direction helper** over constituent `Segment.ordered_tiles`; **SegmentGeometryManager** geometry/topology mutation surface.
- **Switch Entity** — switch field schema, the canonical `Switch.position` mutation workflow, `Switch.locks` claim model, the intrusion check, the flank-protection storage contract, and the **Return-contract principle** for `BlockManager` / `Switch` mutators.
- **Flank Protection Computation** — the flank producer rules (route-point exemption, same-path conflict exemption).
- **Platform And Station** — platform/station ownership chain and `RailwayNetwork` reverse-index contracts.
- **Block** — the power + occupancy + reservation unit (a switch-free connected run grouping `segment_ids`): block field schema (incl. `allowed_travel_directions`, the `is_holding_point` holding-point flag, `occupied_by`, `caution`, `reservation`), the computed `state` property (OCCUPIED > CAUTION > RESERVED > FREE), the one-train-per-Block power-domain rule, the shared `track voltage` feed, and the `"occupied_by_other_train"` fatal conflict.
- **BlockManager** — block reservation/occupancy manager contracts; **`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)** (the `CAUTION -> FREE` state machine); `release_train_occupancy(train_id)`; the **Opposing-traversal caution clear**.
- **SwitchManager** — switch lock-manager contracts; the `SwitchLockRequest` and `LockAcquireResult` value types; switch physical-entry and tail-vacate entries; throw emission ownership.
- **ResourceCoordinator** — the `PathDispatcher`-facing resource facade (`reserve_segment` / `rollback_segment` / `release_path_resources` / `release_tail_vacated` / `on_physical_entry`) and the `SegmentReserveResult` value type.
- **Maintenance counters** — `Switch.flip_count`, `Block.occupied_seconds`, `Train.total_distance_cm` / `total_run_time_seconds`, their single writers, and the persistence rule.
- **Block grouping** — the `BlockGroupingManager` create-block / delete-block / add-segment / remove-segment surface, the end-only `segment_ids` edit-validation preserving the switch-free run, the default one-block-per-segment rule, the **One id per record** rule that keeps a block's id distinct from its segments', and the **One name per record** rule that keeps a block's name distinct from its segments'.
- **Path And PathDispatcher** — **Path field schema**; **path_tile entry schema**; **resource_sequence entry schema**; **Path status state machine**; **`create_path()` flow** (incl. the same-train rejection, step 1); **Path extension**; **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**; **`cancel_path()` return contract**; **Acquisition-cap rule** (the Meet-coordination acquisition cap); **Cancellation** (the `cancel_path` event payload); the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**; **Path invariants** (incl. the **repeated-resource rule**); the **Viable-but-unavailable rule**.
- **Route And RouteExecutor** — **Route data** (incl. `timing_basis`, the per-route choice of which clock its task durations measure against); **Route assignment writers**; **Route edit timestamps**; **RouteTask data**; **Task time semantics**; **Route-definition validation**; **Canonical Next-Target Selection** (`_find_next_task_target`; and `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(...)`, the next-move resolver used by the best-effort reversal-trap exclusion and **Run start-mode seeding**, whose canonical definition site is item 11 under **Path And PathDispatcher**); **Unreachable and broken task handling**; **Route-run state** (incl. the **`current_route_status` write contract** and `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`); **RouteTaskRun schema**; **RouteRun overrides**; **Contention snapshot** (the per-tick blocked-waiter view step `66aa`'s contention-driven release reads); **Wedge detection** (the per-tick report-only view of trains stopped waiting on each other); **Meet coordination** (the per-tick meet assignment and advisory ceilings); **Planning Kernel** (Phase-1 step chain `9c4f`–`66aa`, eligibility predicate, **Waypoint lookahead**, **Authority termination rule**, idle-hold maintenance and release, contention-driven release); **Run start-mode seeding**; **Route Edit And Reconciliation** (incl. the **Shared-route edit teardown** and operator retargeting); **route path-event callback schema**; **Arrival detection**; **Dispatcher completion handling**; **Live-path target re-resolution**.
- **TrainController** — **Controller retained field schema**; **`ControllerCondition` set** (`is_recovery_clean`); **Controller state machine** (incl. **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs** and the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation**); **`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle**; **Power/cab/mode command contracts**; **EB workflow state machine** (incl. the **canonical EB activation effects**); **received_path_plan schema**; the **canonical lookahead payload** (**Lookahead is a pure derived value**); **Controller tick order** (steps `01f2`–`44e9`+); **ATC speed limit**; the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**; **route/dispatcher command contracts**; **Read accessors** (`get_lookahead()`, `get_current_span()`); **TrainSpan schema**; **Tunnels**; **Manual mode and movement-authority boundary**; the **Driver-control interlock**; the **Canonical power-percent safety-cap ordering**; the shared operational pathfinding home (`RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)`, `GraphWalkQuery`, the curve-radius / slope / own-length feasibility filters, and the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` candidate comparison order).
- **TrainPhysics** — physics executor contract; the canonical home for `track_voltage`.
- **SpeedProfile** — calibration fields and the two voltage/power/speed mappings.
- **Speed calibration** — the calibration procedure that measures and writes `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve`; **Auto profiling** (the three-block timed run) and **Manual marking** (operator-marked) modes; `SpeedCalibration`, the class that runs a pass and commits its result; the transient `CalibrationRun` working record it holds per train.
- **Lighting-voltage calibration** — the operator-judged procedure that sets a locomotive's `lighting_voltage`; the Speed Profile panel's Calibrate control and its step/save panel; `LightingCalibrationRun`, the transient working record `SpeedCalibration` holds per train.
- **Train Roster** — `TrainRoster` / `RosterEntry` schema and the placement-import rule.
- **Power** — **Hardware interface boundary** (incl. the logical-id -> physical-channel rule); **Safe state / fail-safe** (`OutputSafety`, the one owner of the de-energize sequence: `enter_safe_state()`); **Hardware I/O failure** (`SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)` / `clear_hardware_failure()`, the halt's single writer and single clearer); **Hardware setup** (controller + chip inventory + port bindings, completeness, self-test, REST surface); **Block occupancy detection** (detector authority, debounce, Step-0 read, and the two span-reconciliation rules); **Block power-controller role** (incl. **Only the next reserved block is pre-energized**); **Switch power-controller role** (`SwitchPowerController`: throw emission and the switch's rail feed); **Ghosted trains** (the `presence_unknown` evidence rule and its teardown); **Unidentified trains** (the runtime-created `Train` for unattributed current); **A binding is read per write**; **Occupancy source follows drive state**; **Detector polarity is resolved in the hardware layer**.
- **Data Flow To UI** — API payload ownership split (`Train.to_dict()` / `TrainController.to_dict()`); **Elapsed values are server-owned** (a client renders durations and never derives them); **Signals (display-only)** (incl. the `SignalRegistry` storage and lookup surface).
- **Event log** — the persisted, operator-facing view of the `NotificationCenter` stream: the operational events, the viewer panel, and replay-as-visualization.

**Safety functions index**

The safety case spans four homes. Audit them together when reviewing safety behavior: ATC limits and EB (**TrainController**: **ATC speed limit**, **EB workflow state machine**), flank protection (**Flank Protection Computation** plus the **Switch Entity** storage contract), the fatal physical-entry freeze (**Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** in **Core Runtime Loop**), and the hardware safe state (**Power**, **Hardware interface boundary**).

## TrackResource

**Resource base contract**

- `TrackResource` is the shared base class of `Block` and `Switch`. It carries the common identity surface every dispatcher-facing caller needs: `resource_id` (the entity's primary ID, projected as a `@property` over `block_id` / `switch_id`), `resource_type` (the literal string `"block"` or `"switch"`), `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, and `manual`. All other entity-specific fields stay on the concrete subclass.
- `TrackResource` declares no reserve/release/lock methods. Per the **BlockManager** and **SwitchManager** canonical homes, the manager layer is the sole mutator for reservation, occupancy, CAUTION, and lock fields; `Block` and `Switch` remain data containers in v1.
- `Path.resource_sequence[*].resolve(network)` returns the live `TrackResource` for an entry, or `None` if the resource is not registered. The entry remains the source of truth for path-local geometry (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`); the helper is purely a registry read so callers that want polymorphic access to the underlying entity do not duplicate the `network.blocks.get(...)` / `network.switches.get(...)` branch.

**`disabled` and `manual` availability flags**

This subsection is the canonical home for both flags. `Block`, `Switch`, **Platform And Station** (for logical platforms and stations, which are not `TrackResource` subclasses but follow the same rules), `PathDispatcher`, `BlockManager`, `SwitchManager`, `ResourceCoordinator`, `RailwayPathfinder`, and `RouteExecutor` all refer back to this subsection by name rather than restating its rules. The flag name `manual` here refers only to the resource availability flag and is unrelated to the train controller's manual driving mode; in prose, always qualify as `Block.manual` / `Switch.manual` / `Platform.manual` / `Station.manual`, or as "the `manual` availability flag," to avoid confusion with `TrainController.control_mode == "manual"`.

- `disabled: bool = False` is the physical-absence flag. A disabled resource behaves like missing track / an open circuit: every automatic and operator-initiated code path treats it as unavailable. Pathfinding rejects any candidate that traverses it. `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` rejects it. `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` and manual/API switch-state commands fail against it. Route target resolution rejects it as a `BlockTarget` / `PlatformTarget` / `StationTarget`. And a physical entry is the canonical unrecoverable fatal physical-entry conflict (`error.origin = "disabled_resource_entry"`, see the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**). `disabled` has no override — no operator command can bypass it.
- `manual: bool = False` is the virtual-disable flag. A manual resource is physically present and live, but every automatic system treats it as if it were disabled. The structural differences from `disabled` are:
  - **Operator override.** A `manual_override: bool = False` parameter is carried by `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, and `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`. When `manual_override = True` is set on the call, manual resources participate normally: pathfinding includes them, block reservation accepts them, switch lock acquisition accepts them, and switch-state commands proceed. When `manual_override = False` (or omitted), manual resources are filtered out exactly like disabled ones. The override flows through the entire acquisition chain — `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(...)`, `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`, `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`, `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` — so the eligibility decisions can see it. `manual_override` relaxes the `manual` filter and the `Block.allowed_travel_directions` filter. It never relaxes the `disabled` filter or the pathfinder's curve-radius and slope feasibility filters (physical capability limits, defined in the pathfinding home). When `manual_override = True`, the candidate `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` need not be present in `block.allowed_travel_directions` for pathfinding, reservation, or terminal-direction acceptance, and the block's own `allowed_travel_directions` set is not mutated.
  - **Physical entry is not a fatal conflict.** A physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) resource is treated by `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` / `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` as ordinary clean entry. `occupied_by` is set normally, flank-lock conversion happens normally, and no fatal physical-entry conflict is reported. The `manual` flag filters future planning, not present occupancy.
  - **No topology notification.** Toggling `manual` on a switch does not change connectivity and does not require a `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(...)` notification (toggling `disabled` does, because connectivity does change).
- `manual_override = True` always originates from an explicit operator decision, and the operator surface is route-backed: an operator-initiated route (for example a Quick Route request) stores `Route.manual_override = True`, and `RouteExecutor` passes the assigned route's `manual_override` into every `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call for that run, which forwards it through the acquisition chain. `RouteExecutor` never originates `manual_override = True` itself, and every automatic/scheduled route leaves `Route.manual_override = False`, so automatic plans never touch manual resources. Request handlers never call `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` directly (see **Layered responsibilities**); they carry the operator's override on the route they create.
- When both `disabled` and `manual` are true on the same resource, `disabled` wins everywhere: the resource is unconditionally off-limits and no `manual_override` reaches it. The two flags are independent booleans (both may be true at once); no invariant requires them to be mutually exclusive.
- Operator/admin code may toggle `manual` to either value at any time without preconditions, because `manual` is a future-planning filter and does not disturb already-held reservations, already-set `occupied_by`, or already-acquired locks. Operator/admin code may toggle `disabled = True` only when the resource has no physical occupancy, no live reservation, no live `CAUTION` (blocks only), no future locks (switches only), and no train-owned protection (switches only); the per-entity rules in the **Switch entity** and **Block** sections own those preconditions and `Platform.disabled` / `Station.disabled` toggles in **Platform And Station** are unconditional for the logical entities themselves. v1 has no force-cleanup workflow that bypasses these preconditions.

**Availability flag REST API surface**

This subsection lists the v1 REST surface for reading and writing the `disabled` and `manual` flags on every entity that owns them, plus the `manual_override` operator parameter on path and switch-state commands. The endpoints reuse existing routes; only the request body and parameter list change.

| Endpoint | Body / parameters | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `PATCH /api/blocks/<block_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named block. Either field may be omitted to leave it unchanged. Setting `disabled = True` runs the **Block** admin-reject preconditions (occupancy / reservation / `CAUTION`) and returns HTTP 409 on rejection; setting `manual` to either value always succeeds. Returns the updated block payload including both fields. |
| `PATCH /api/switches/<switch_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Same shape as the block PATCH. Setting `disabled = True` runs the **Switch entity** admin-reject preconditions (occupancy / future locks / train-owned protection) and returns HTTP 409 on rejection; setting `manual` always succeeds. Toggling `disabled` also fires `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`; toggling `manual` does not. |
| `PATCH /api/platform/<platform_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named platform. Both succeed unconditionally — the flags affect only future target resolution per **Platform And Station**. |
| `PATCH /api/station/<station_id>` | `{ "disabled": bool, "manual": bool }` (any subset) | Toggles the two fields on the named station. Both succeed unconditionally. |
| `PUT /api/switches/<switch_id>/state` | `{ "state": int, "manual_override": bool }` (`state` is the commanded `Switch.position` value; `manual_override` defaults to `False`) | Operator-initiated switch position change. The handler calls `SwitchManager.request_position_change(switch_id, position, manual_override=...)` with `position` taken from the body's `state`, which runs the canonical `Switch.position` mutation workflow synchronously (availability/lock/occupancy validation, `Switch.apply_position_change(...)`, the `BlockManager` notify, then the throw per **Power**) and returns the `(success, reason)` result for this request. A manual switch fails unless `manual_override = True`; a disabled switch always fails. The service layer surfaces the result as the operator's command-result event. |
| `POST /api/paths` | existing path-creation body extended with `"manual_override": bool` (defaults to `False`) | Quick Route: operator-initiated movement request. The handler constructs a `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route with a single `task_kind == "stop"` task for the requested destination (per the one-off reposition flow under **Exact departure calls**), stores the body's `manual_override` on that route (`Route.manual_override`), assigns the train, and returns accepted/pending. The handler never calls `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` directly; Phase 1 creates the path and passes the route's `manual_override` down the acquisition chain. When `True`, manual blocks/switches participate in candidate search and acquisition. Disabled resources are never reached. |
| `PUT /api/paths/<path_id>/extend` | existing extend body extended with `"manual_override": bool` (defaults to `False`) | Operator retarget of the train's current movement. The handler runs the operator retargeting flow from **Route Edit And Reconciliation** (abort current run + assign a fresh `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route carrying the body's `manual_override`). It never calls `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` directly; route-driven extension toward the armed target is automatic in Phase 1. |

All four entity PATCH endpoints emit a single canonical command-result event per the **Runtime notifications** contract — `operation` set to the handler's own function name, `kind = "info"` on acceptance and `"warning"` on rejection, payload `{command_id, <entity>_id, success, reason}` — regardless of whether the request mutated `disabled`, `manual`, or both. On 409 rejections the response body carries the reason in the same shape as other admin-reject contracts (no parallel error format).

GET endpoints (`GET /api/blocks/<id>`, `GET /api/switches/<id>`, `GET /api/platform/<id>`, `GET /api/station/<id>`) include both `disabled` and `manual` in their response payload so the editor UI can render the current values without a second round-trip.

**Entity-editor UI controls**

The UI design language for these toggles lives in [`docs/DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md`](docs/DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md), not in this document. The doc-level contract is: every entity editor panel (Block, Switch, Platform, Station) exposes a control that lets the operator read and write the entity's `disabled` and `manual` flags through the PATCH endpoints above. The Route editor exposes the per-field push-policy control for the allowlisted fields (`priority`, `acquisition_mode`); the RouteRun editor exposes per-run override write/clear for the same allowlisted fields. The visual vocabulary (segmented control, toggle, card composition) is owned by `DESIGN_LANGUAGE.md`; this document does not duplicate it.

## Maintenance counters

Maintenance counters record cumulative asset usage so an operator can plan upkeep — switch point-motor replacement, track service intervals, and rolling-stock service intervals. This subsection is the canonical home for the three counters. `Switch.flip_count`, `Block.occupied_seconds`, and `Train.total_distance_cm` / `total_run_time_seconds` are listed as persisted fields in the **Switch entity**, **Block**, and **Train Entity** schemas, each pointing back here; the rules live only here. Each counter has exactly one home entity and exactly one writer:

- `Switch.flip_count: int` is the number of times the switch has actually changed alignment. Its one writer is `Switch.apply_position_change(...)` — the single `Switch.position` mutation site (see the **Switch entity** section) — which increments `flip_count` only when the requested position differs from the current one. Re-aligning a switch to the position it already holds is a no-op and does not count.
- `Block.occupied_seconds: float` is the total time a train has occupied the block. It is an observing measurement — a locomotive standing on a block wears that block whether or not the session is running — so `SimulationController.update(...)` accrues it once per pass of the runtime loop, running or stopped, immediately after the de-energize drain and signal refresh (see **Core Runtime Loop**, Per-train tick transaction), for every block whose `occupied_by` is set. A running pass adds that pass's `delta_seconds`; a stopped pass adds the wall seconds since the previous pass. `Tile` carries no usage field of its own; a tile's usage read is the `occupied_seconds` of its owning `Block`.
