- `Train.total_distance_cm: float` and `Train.total_run_time_seconds: float` are the train's cumulative distance travelled and powered-on time. Their one writer is `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`: `total_distance_cm` adds the distance moved on every tick the train moves, and `total_run_time_seconds` adds the tick `delta` on every tick the controller is powered on (the OFF branch returns before the counter is reached).

`Switch.flip_count` and the two `Train` counters accrue only while the session is running, and not because a gate stops them: a switch changes alignment only when Phase 1 aligns it, and the `Train` counters are written by physics integration, which measures the motion the app drives rather than the motion a detector reports.

Maintenance counters are the one category of per-entity state written during simulation that is also persisted; the **Event log** is the other runtime-written record kept on disk (a notification stream, not per-entity state). Unlike transient runtime state — occupancy, locks, `Switch.position`, the train span — which is in-memory only per **Out of Scope** and repopulated each session, maintenance counters accumulate while the simulation runs and are saved and restored as part of the layout snapshot; they are never reset on load. `Switch.flip_count` and `Block.occupied_seconds` are written by each entity's `to_layout_dict()` and read by its `from_dict()`; the `Train` counters are written by `Train.to_dict()` and read by `Train.from_dict()`. All four counters are also included in the live frontend `to_dict()` payload (see **Data Flow To UI**) so the UI may display current usage.

## Tile

**Path locking**

- Switch locking is owned entirely by the `Switch` entity (multi-owner `Switch.locks`); tile locking is not stored on `Tile`.
- Temporary reservation direction lives at the block/path-plan level: `Block.reserved_direction` is the dispatcher-owned block reservation direction; `Path.resource_sequence[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` plus nested `path_tiles[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` carry the per-resource and per-tile traversal direction.
- `Tile` keeps static geometry, classification, and `switch_id`. If the tile is a switch, callers resolve the active traversal from `Switch.position` in `network.switches` via `switch_id`. `Tile` does not store live switch state.

**Tile role and traversal**

- The retained `Tile` schema in v1 is explicit:
  - persistent fields kept on `Tile`: `x`, `y`, `track_bits`, `dead_end_ports`, `signal_ids`, `switch_id`, `length`
  - computed read kept on `Tile`: `track_type`
  - `Tile` holds no authoritative live switch state. Its `switch_state` is a derived read that resolves through `Switch.position` for the tile's `switch_id` when the tile is attached to a `RailwayNetwork`; a `_switch_state_local` value is only a fallback for a bare detached tile (e.g. a unit test with no network) and is never a second authoritative runtime copy alongside `Switch.position`. `Switch.position` is the one alignment authority everywhere.
  - the following are not stored live runtime fields on `Tile`: `occupied_by`, `reserved_by`, `locked_by_path`, `locked_state`, `path_direction`
  - `Tile` does not store station, platform, or block membership.
- Entity collection ownership in v1 has two orthogonal grouping axes over `Segment`, each level owning the IDs of the level below it:
  - **Operational axis:** `Station.platform_ids: List[str]` is the stored canonical list of `Platform` IDs in that station group. `Platform.ordered_segments: List[str]` is the stored canonical ordered list of `Segment` IDs in that platform. Tile-to-platform reads are derived through the network's tile -> segment -> platform indices.
  - **Power axis:** `Block.segment_ids: List[str]` is the canonical ordered list of `Segment` IDs in that block — the switch-free run of segments it powers; see the **Block** section.
  - **Geometry base (shared by both axes):** `Segment.ordered_tiles: List[SegmentTileEntry]` is the canonical ordered list of `Tile` tiles in that segment, where each `SegmentTileEntry` carries `(x, y, entry_edge)` — see the **Segment** section for the full shape.
  - `Tile` does not store any back-reference for segment/block/platform/station membership.
  All five entities (`Tile`, `Segment`, `Block`, `Platform`, `Station`) are registered with `RailwayNetwork`. Reverse lookups ("which segment owns this tile?", "which block owns this segment?", "which platform owns this segment?") are answered through `RailwayNetwork`-owned indices, not through fields stored on the lower-level entity. A tile-to-block lookup is the two-hop tile -> segment -> block.
- **Non-empty parent rule.** Every grouping entity always owns at least one child: a `Station` keeps at least one `Platform` in `platform_ids`, a `Platform` keeps at least one `Segment` in `ordered_segments`, a `Block` keeps at least one `Segment` in `segment_ids`, and a `Segment` keeps at least one tile in `ordered_tiles`. This holds at create time and edit time — a grouping entity is never created empty, and no edit may leave one empty. When an edit removes a grouping entity's last child — `remove_tile_from_segment` / `remove_tiles_from_segment` taking a segment's last tile, an edit that removes a platform's or a block's last segment, or `remove_platform_from_station_group` taking a station's last platform — the parent is deleted in that same operation instead of being left empty, routing through `delete_segment` / `delete_block` / `delete_platform` / `delete_station`. Because a `Segment` has two parents, its auto-delete fans out to both: deleting a `Segment` first removes it from any owning `Platform.ordered_segments` (deleting that `Platform` if it thereby empties, which cascades to `Station.platform_ids`) and from any owning `Block.segment_ids` (deleting that `Block` if it thereby empties). The two axes are not symmetric on deletion, because block membership is mandatory (exactly one) and platform membership is optional (at most one). Deleting a `Block` (dissolving a power grouping through `BlockGroupingManager`, see **Block grouping**) never deletes its segments, but because every segment must belong to exactly one block, each freed segment is re-homed into its own single-segment block — the default one-block-per-segment rule (see **Block grouping**) — and is never left without a block. Deleting a `Platform` never deletes its segments either; because a segment belongs to at most one platform, those segments are simply left with no platform. In both cases a grouping entity's deletion never deletes the tiles or segments it grouped. The last-child removal and the parent's deletion commit together: if the parent's own delete preconditions reject it, the child removal that triggered it is rejected as a whole, so an empty grouping entity is never stored.
- `Tile` does not carry static or temporary traffic-direction policy in v1. Static allowed travel direction is block-owned, through `Block.allowed_travel_directions`; route/requested travel direction is route-owned, through `RouteExecutor` resolving `RouteTask.task_direction` into `ResolvedRouteTarget.requested_destination_direction`; temporary reservation direction is dispatcher-owned, through `Block.reserved_direction`; and finer-grained traversal frame data lives on `Path.resource_sequence[*]` and nested `path_tiles[*]`.
- Tile-to-segment / segment-to-block / segment-to-platform / platform-to-station lookups go through `RailwayNetwork`-owned reverse indices computed from `Segment.ordered_tiles`, `Block.segment_ids`, `Platform.ordered_segments`, and `Station.platform_ids`. `station_id` is reserved for the logical multi-platform `Station` identifier; `platform_id` is reserved for the concrete `Platform` identifier; the tile-level entity stores neither.
- `Tile` is not the authoritative store for live switch state or switch locks. Switch state lives on `Switch`; lock ownership is expressed through `Switch.locks` (for switches) and `Block.reserved_by_path` / `Block.reserved_for_train` (for blocks).
- If the classification says the tile is a switch, `Tile.switch_id` must point to the authoritative `Switch` entity in the network entity database. Live switch state and switch lock ownership must live only on that `Switch` entity.
- Split planning-time traversal enumeration from live-state traversal reads.
- Live traversal helpers:
  - `Tile.get_active_exits(entry_edge, resolver) -> List[str]`
  - `Tile.get_active_entry_for_exit(exit_edge, resolver) -> Optional[str]`
  where `resolver` is the network or an equivalent switch-state resolver that can read `Switch.position` via `switch_id`.
- If the resolved owning `Switch` exists and `Switch.disabled == True`, the live traversal helpers return no usable traversal for that switch tile. `Switch.manual` does not affect live traversal here, because a manual switch is physically present and connected; the `manual` filter is applied only by the planning/acquisition layer per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- Planning-time helper:
  - `Tile.enumerate_traversals(entry_edge) -> List[(exit_edge, required_switch_state)]`
  This helper enumerates every legal geometric traversal through the tile independent of the switch's current live state. For an ordinary non-switch tile, it yields the one legal `(exit_edge, None)` pair. For a switch tile, it yields the legal `(exit_edge, required_switch_state)` pairs that dispatcher path search may acquire.
- `Tile.enumerate_traversals(...)` remains a pure geometric helper and does not inspect `Switch.disabled` or `Switch.manual`. Callers that resolve the owning switch through the network entity layer must drop all enumerated traversals for that switch tile when `Switch.disabled == True`, so pathfinding/wayfinding treats a disabled switch as absent connectivity. The `Switch.manual` filter is applied higher up at the pathfinder layer (see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home and `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)`), where the `manual_override` parameter can keep or drop the traversal.
- `Tile.get_active_exits(...)` and `Tile.get_active_entry_for_exit(...)` map one edge through the currently active traversal selected by `Switch.position`. `Tile.enumerate_traversals(...)` is the planner-facing geometry contract and must not consult live switch alignment.
- For Y-shape switch geometry in v1, branch-to-branch traversal is invalid. `Tile.enumerate_traversals(...)` must not yield branch-to-branch options.
- `PathDispatcher` path construction/extension and BFS switch derivation use `Tile.enumerate_traversals(...)`. `TrainPhysics._advance_end()`, tail tracing, and other live movement readers use the active-state helpers. Switch traversal never reads track-stored live state.

**Tile invariants**

- Switch-integrity while a train spans a switch: `Switch.physical_occupants` (read diagnostically through `Switch.occupied_by`) plus the still-held `Switch.locks` entries prevent a switch from flipping out from under the spanning train. No global path lock is needed for this guarantee.
- Tail tracing still uses the live-state entry/exit helper, but it now resolves through the live `Switch.position` per the directed resolver contract in **Tile role and traversal** rather than through a separately persisted track-tile field.

## Switch Entity

**Switch entity**

- `Switch` is a `TrackResource` subclass; the shared identity surface (`resource_id`, `resource_type == "switch"`, `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, `manual`) comes from the **TrackResource** canonical home. Switch-only fields below extend that base.
- `Switch` is a first-class resource entity at the same level as a block.
- The retained `Switch` schema in v1 is explicit:
  - identity/location: `switch_id`, `x`, `y` (and the `name` / `occupied_by` / `disabled` / `manual` fields inherited from `TrackResource`)
  - persisted switch fields: `main_length_cm`, `diverge_length_cm`, `main_radius_cm`, `diverge_radius_cm`, `switch_type`, `notes`, `flip_count` (see **Maintenance counters**)
  - new authoritative live/runtime fields: `position`, `locks`, `flank_protection`, `train_protection_claims`
  - `Switch` holds no `simulation_controller` back-reference and never notifies another entity itself. It is a pure data/primitive entity: `Switch.acquire_lock(...)`, `Switch.release_lock(...)`, and `Switch.apply_position_change(...)` mutate only switch-local state. The cross-resource `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(...)` notify that must follow a `Switch.position` write is owned by `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`, the single entry that pairs the write with the notify and the physical throw (see below). The dispatcher-facing entry points for path-driven multi-switch acquisition / release / clean physical entry / tail-vacate live on **SwitchManager** (which calls these `Switch` primitives); `PathDispatcher` reaches `SwitchManager` through the **ResourceCoordinator** facade, and `SwitchManager` reaches `BlockManager` through its own `simulation_controller` reference.
- `Switch.disabled` and `Switch.manual` are the availability flags defined canonically in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** subsection under **TrackResource**. The switch-specific behaviour those rules imply:
  - A disabled switch behaves like missing track / an open circuit. Live traversal helpers return no usable traversal through it (see the **Tile role and traversal** rule), `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` fails, manual/API switch-state commands fail, and pathfinding skips it. Because the switch is treated as physically absent, `TrainPhysics` must stop at the last reachable live edge. If a controller progress report nevertheless says the train entered that disabled switch, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes the entry to `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` → `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`, which must treat it as a fatal physical-entry conflict and report it with `error.origin = "disabled_resource_entry"` to `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`. It must not silently set `Switch.occupied_by`.
  - A manual switch (not also disabled) is physically present and live. Live traversal helpers still see it; pathfinding still skips it; `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` and manual/API switch-state commands still fail — unless the calling operator path carries `manual_override = True`, in which case those operations proceed normally. A controller-reported physical entry into a manual switch is **not** a fatal conflict; `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` treats it as ordinary clean entry, sets `Switch.occupied_by`, and performs the canonical flank-lock conversion.
  - Operator/admin code must reject setting `Switch.disabled = True` while physical occupancy, future locks, or train-owned protection remain. The caller must wait for ordinary tail-vacate or cancel/release the holding path through normal live workflows before retrying. A fatal failed session cannot be repaired in place; it must be restarted. Toggling `Switch.manual` remains unconditional.
  - Toggling `Switch.disabled` changes connectivity and must notify `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` (or an equivalent topology-availability callback) so directional `CAUTION` reevaluation sees the open circuit immediately. Toggling `Switch.manual` does not change connectivity and does not require this notification.
- `position: int` is the authoritative physical switch alignment, with values `0 = MAIN`, `1 = DIVERGE`. `Switch.position` is the single authoritative alignment everywhere — routing, geometry, locks, and traversal all read it. It is the *commanded* alignment, and it is the only alignment the runtime has: a point motor reports no position back, so nothing reads the physical one (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role). When traversal code needs the active switch alignment, it must resolve that alignment from the `Switch` entity rather than keeping a second authoritative live copy on `Tile`.
- `occupied_by: Optional[str]` is a derived read-only projection on `Switch` that returns `next(iter(self.physical_occupants.values()), None)` — any one current occupant, or `None` if no train is on the tile. The `Switch` override has no stored field and no writer (it overrides the `TrackResource` base slot on `Switch` only; `Block.occupied_by` remains a stored field with its own writers). The authoritative store is `physical_occupants: Dict[FrozenSet[str], str]`, a route-keyed map from the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the train is using to its `train_id`. Each entry says "this train is physically traversing this route on the tile right now"; entries are written on switch-resource entry and cleared on tail-vacate. Two route entries may coexist when the routes do not physically conflict per `switch.routes_conflict(...)` — e.g. a scissors crossover in DIVERGE state holds both NW (one train) and SE (another train) simultaneously without collision because those two arcs are in opposite quadrants and share no point. Reservation/lock ownership remains separate. The map's value is a `train_id` (not a `route_run_id` or `path_id`): physical occupancy is a property of the train's body and persists even when the train has no active path or route run, so only `train_id` keys it reliably. Conflict predicate: two routes conflict iff they share any edge midpoint (their edge frozensets intersect) or both pass through tile center (NS and EW straights only). The only geometrically non-conflict pairs are `{NW, SE}` and `{NE, SW}`.
- Physical occupancy (`physical_occupants` / its `occupied_by` read) and reservation ownership (`Switch.locks`) answer different questions and are never merged: `physical_occupants` says which train is bodily on the tile right now; `Switch.locks` says which path/train has *reserved* the switch — and a lock holder need not be the train physically on the tile (a flank-protection lock is sourced from a *neighbour* switch's acquisition, `source_switch_id != None`). `Switch.locks` is the canonical "route owner"/reservation layer.
- **Intrusion check.** A train that is physically on the switch must hold a compatible reservation for the route it occupies. This is enforced at physical entry: `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch; if the entering train holds no lock granting `route`, the entry is a fatal intrusion — the manager reports it through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `error.origin = "switch_intrusion"` and writes no occupancy state. The check fires only on a *newly-entered* switch tile (the physical-entry event produced from `added_tiles`); a switch already in the train's seeded span at `create_path(...)` time is never re-entered and so never reaches this check (and was never newly locked, since seeded already-covered switches are not acquired for fresh locks). This is a general comparison of physical presence against reservation ownership, not a per-workflow branch; once a train has cleanly entered (its lock consumed), its continued presence is protected by `physical_occupants` plus `train_protection_claims`, not by a retained lock.
- **Switch occupancy vs reservation — the three stores at a glance.** These fields are distinct and must never be conflated: `physical_occupants` (and its `occupied_by` read) answer "who is bodily on the tile," while `Switch.locks` answers "who has reserved it." A lock holder need not be physically present, and a physical occupant must hold a lock (or it is an intrusion).

  | Field | Data format | What it represents / does | Written by | Read by (consumers) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `Switch.physical_occupants` | `Dict[FrozenSet[str], str]` — key is the unordered `route = frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})`, value is `train_id` | **Authoritative** physical occupancy: which train is bodily traversing which route on the tile right now. Route-keyed so two non-conflicting routes (e.g. `{NW, SE}`) can coexist. | `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)` records `[route] = train_id` on clean entry; `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)` removes the matching entry on tail-vacate | `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` occupancy eligibility (per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`); the entry conflict gate (`switch.routes_conflict(...)`), the intrusion check, the tail-vacate release procedure, the switch-integrity guard, and the on-read `occupied_by` diagnostic projection |
  | `Switch.occupied_by` | `Optional[str]` — a single `train_id` or `None` (overrides the `TrackResource` base slot on `Switch` only; `Block.occupied_by` stays stored) | **Non-authoritative** single-value diagnostic projection of `physical_occupants` (reflects any one current occupant). Never the gate for an entry/vacate decision. | Derived — has no writer; recomputed on read from `physical_occupants` as `next(iter(self.physical_occupants.values()), None)` | Diagnostics, UI / data-flow projection, and human-readable "is anyone on this switch" reads |
  | `Switch.locks` | Keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`; each claim records `path_id`, `source_switch_id: Optional[str]`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, and the `route` it uses through this switch (non-`None` for a main claim, `None` for a flank claim). The five values are the claim's logical fields; how they are physically laid out (one record vs the train/state pair stored alongside a parallel route map) is an implementation detail. | The **reservation / "route owner"** layer — future authority plus flank protection. A main claim (`source_switch_id is None`) is this path's own use of the switch; a flank claim (`source_switch_id != None`) is protection sourced from a *neighbour* switch's acquisition. A lock holder need not be physically on the tile. | `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` → `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)`; removed by `Switch.release_lock(...)` / `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(...)`; the main claim is *consumed* at head entry and flank claims are *converted* into `train_protection_claims` | `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` eligibility, the cross-train route check (`routes_conflict`), the intrusion check (entrant must hold a compatible lock), and `is_locked()` / `can_lock(state)` |

  (Related: `train_protection_claims` — keyed by `(source_switch_id, train_id)` — is the train-owned physical protection that a consumed flank claim becomes after head entry; it is cleared on the source switch's tail-vacate, not by a path release.)
- Future authority on a switch is held in the multi-owner `locks` collection, keyed canonically by `(path_id, source_switch_id)` where `source_switch_id` is `None` for a main-usage claim and the originating main switch ID for a flank-protection claim. Each future-authority lock claim records `path_id`, `source_switch_id: Optional[str]`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, and `route` — the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through this switch. Those five values are the claim's logical fields; the storage layout is unconstrained (the implementation may keep the train/state pair under the `(path_id, source_switch_id)` key with the route in a parallel map keyed the same way). A main-usage claim (`source_switch_id is None`) always carries a non-`None` `route` so the cross-train co-locking check in `acquire_lock(claim)` has the geometry it needs; a flank-protection claim (`source_switch_id is not None`) carries `route = None`. The convenience read `claim_role` (= `"main"` if `source_switch_id is None` else `"flank"`) may still be referenced by name in this document. Path locks do not carry a deferred-release flag in v1.
- `train_protection_claims` is the train-owned physical protection set used after the train head has entered a switch and consumed the path lock. Each claim stores exactly `train_id`, `source_switch_id`, and `locked_state`; it blocks conflicting switch state changes until the source switch tail-vacate event clears it.

**Return-contract principle for `BlockManager` / `Switch` mutators.** Every method returns either `bool` (single success/failure) or a structured `<Method>Result` object (multiple distinguishable failure modes that callers branch on). When the result's "kind" or "reason" field is purely diagnostic (no caller branches on it), it follows the **Errors and diagnostic classification** rule in **Design Direction**: never a new enum.

- **`Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` payload.** The structured `claim` payload carries `path_id`, `train_id`, `locked_state`, optional `source_switch_id` (`None` for a main-usage claim, originating main switch ID for a flank-protection claim), the `route` the path uses through this switch (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` from the canonical claim shape above — non-`None` for a main-usage claim, `None` for a flank-protection claim), and `manual_override: bool = False`. The input field name `locked_state` matches the stored claim field, so the same name is used everywhere: there is no input-vs-stored renaming inside the helper.
  - `manual_override` is propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)`, so manual switches are reachable only when the operator explicitly chose to override (see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). It is not stored on the resulting recorded lock entry; it is only consulted at the eligibility step inside `acquire_lock(...)`.
- **`Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` eligibility.** The method first checks `Switch.locks`, `Switch.physical_occupants`, and `Switch.train_protection_claims`.
  - **Occupancy.** Occupancy refuses a claim only when the claim's `route` conflicts with a `physical_occupants` entry held by a different `train_id`, per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`. Two non-conflicting routes coexist on one tile, so the single-value `Switch.occupied_by` projection is never consulted here.
  - **Sharing a compatible future lock without a throw.** `acquire_lock(claim)` may record another future lock claim without moving the switch when **all three hold**:
    - `Switch.position` already equals `claim.locked_state`
    - all existing future locks and train-owned protection are compatible with `claim.locked_state`
    - the new claim is not a cross-train primary collision (the rule two bullets below)
  - **Throwing to a new state.** When `Switch.position` differs from `claim.locked_state`, `acquire_lock(claim)` may throw the switch only when **all three hold**:
    - `physical_occupants` is empty
    - no train-owned protection exists
    - no future lock requires another state
  - A switch holding any `physical_occupants` entry is not acquirable for future authority, except for same-train already-covered span entries that are seeded into `Path.resource_sequence` and are not newly locked.
- **`Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` return contract.** It returns a plain `bool` at the entity level (`True` = recorded or idempotently present, `False` = conflict). The specific failure reason here is diagnostic-only and no caller branches on it, so per the **Return-contract principle** above the entity does not return a structured result. `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` wraps that `bool` into the `LockAcquireResult` defined under **SwitchManager** for manager-level callers, synthesizing the diagnostic `reason` (e.g., `"disabled switch"`, `"manual switch (no override)"`, `"locked state conflicts with existing claim"`, `"physical occupancy by other train"`, `"cross-train primary lock conflict"`, `"protection conflict"`).
- `Switch.acquire_lock(...)` must fail immediately when `Switch.disabled == True`. It must also fail when `Switch.manual == True` and `claim.manual_override == False`; when `Switch.manual == True` and `claim.manual_override == True`, the manual filter is bypassed and the remaining eligibility checks (locks, occupancy, protection) run normally.
- **Canonical `Switch.position` mutation workflow.** Every successful `Switch.position` mutation path — dispatcher lock acquisition and manual/API switch-state commands alike — must run the same four stages in order:
  1. **Validate availability** per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home: always reject when `Switch.disabled == True`; reject when `Switch.manual == True` and the caller did not pass `manual_override = True`.
  2. **Validate claims:** no future lock, physical occupancy, or train-owned protection may require a different state.
  3. **Write and notify:** write `Switch.position` (the commanded alignment), then notify `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`.
  4. **Throw:** only when that write actually changed the alignment, drive the physical throw through `SwitchPowerController` (defined in the **Power** section, Switch power-controller role).

  How the stages map onto methods:
  - `Switch.apply_position_change(new_position)` is the validate-and-write step — a pure data operation that does not notify any external entity or drive any motor.
  - `SwitchManager.request_position_change(switch_id, new_position, manual_override=False)` is the single public entry point for position-change requests. It pairs the write with the `BlockManager` notification, runs the whole validate → write → notify → throw workflow synchronously, and returns the `(success, reason)` result for that request; the service layer turns that result into one command-result event per the bullet below. Admin / API / UI handlers route through the manager so the notification is never skipped, and they pass `manual_override = True` only when the operator has explicitly chosen to override a manual switch.
  - Dispatcher lock acquisition that must throw a switch executes synchronously inside Phase 1's acquisition step — it is phase-loop code, not a request handler. It routes its `Switch.position` write through the same `SwitchManager`-owned paired write+notify: the throw inside `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` calls `apply_position_change(...)` and the `BlockManager` notify together, the same pairing `request_position_change(...)` performs. Nothing writes `Switch.position` without that pairing.
  - The commanded `Switch.position` (setter side) and the physical confirmation (getter/feedback side, per **Power**) stay separate.
  - A `Switch.position` change is the one resource write an operator request commits between ticks (it does not enqueue onto the per-train phase loop), because it is a topology-availability edit rather than a movement-authority mutation.
  - `Switch` itself does not hold a back-reference to `SimulationController`; `SwitchManager` owns the runtime wiring and reaches `BlockManager` through its own `simulation_controller` reference.
  - Pure geometric editor writes (placement, connectivity wiring, static field edits that do not affect occupancy or locks) do not invoke runtime methods and remain safe without any runtime surface.
- A non-dispatcher/manual/API switch-state command follows that same canonical workflow and may change `Switch.position` only when the availability check accepts the request and no lock, physical occupancy, or train-owned protection blocks the requested state. If any such claim exists, the state-change request must fail rather than bypassing the workflow. Such a manual/API command is a runtime mutation applied synchronously through `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)`; it therefore requires the **SwitchManager**'s `simulation_controller` to be wired, must raise in pure editor mode, and is available only when the editor session has instantiated a runtime surface.
- Manual/API switch-state command result delivery follows the canonical `NotificationCenter` contract. `SwitchManager.request_position_change(...)` returns the `(success, reason)` result inline, and the service layer surfaces it through `NotificationCenter` as one event with `source = "SwitchManager"`, `operation = "request_position_change"`, `kind = "info"` on success or `"warning"` on failure, payload `{command_id, command_name, switch_id, success, reason}`. A throw the hardware could not be commanded to make (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role) surfaces here with `success = False` and that `reason`; `Switch.position` is not rolled back. This matches the operator-command notification contract under **Runtime notifications**; the command result is delivered through this surface rather than being inferred from a raw transport-level response.
- A disabled switch cannot be targeted by a manual/API switch-state command in v1; the request must fail through that same command-result event rather than mutating the disabled switch. A manual switch can be targeted by a manual/API switch-state command only when the command carries `manual_override = True`; without the override, the request fails through the same command-result event. The `manual_override = True` bypass never applies to a disabled switch. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the override contract.
- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` must be idempotent only for an already-recorded claim on that same canonical claim key with the same `locked_state`, and succeed immediately in that case.
- **Multi-path future locks on one switch.** `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` must allow multiple paths to hold future lock claims on the same switch when those claims all require the same physical `claim.locked_state` and the switch is already in that state.
- **Cross-train primary co-locking.** Two different `train_id`s may both hold a primary claim (`source_switch_id is None`) on the same switch. This is gated by route geometry and requires **both** of these:
  - both claims carry a non-`None` `route` (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through this switch)
  - the new claim's route does not physically conflict with any existing primary claim's route, per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`

  This is what lets a scissors crossover in DIVERGE state hold one path on NW and another on SE simultaneously.
- In v1, primary claims (`source_switch_id is None`) must always carry a non-`None` `route`. `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` rejects with `reason = "missing_route_on_primary_claim"` when any expanded primary request has `route is None`.
- Flank claims (`source_switch_id is not None`) do not carry a route and are not subject to the cross-train route check. Same-train multi-path co-locking is unaffected by the route check.
- When a cross-train claim is blocked, it falls through the ordinary `SWITCH_LOCKED` failure into `PathDispatcher`'s BEST_EFFORT truncation, which lands the live authority on the block before the contested switch. The holding train's tail-vacate then clears its route entry, and the blocked train extends past on a later tick.
- `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` must fail if any existing future lock or train-owned protection on that switch requires a different physical state, or if satisfying the request would require throwing a switch holding any `physical_occupants` entry.
- When the train head enters a switch, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes the entry through `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)` to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`. That manager consumes that path's main switch lock for the entered switch and converts any path-owned flank locks sourced from that main switch into `train_protection_claims`. From that point onward the occupied switch and its active flank protection are train-owned physical state, not path-owned cleanup.
- `release_lock(path_id, source_switch_id=None)` removes that future-authority claim immediately. It must not inspect train physical occupancy or set a pending flag; an occupied switch is protected by `Switch.physical_occupants` and any `train_protection_claims`, not by a retained path lock. Returns `bool`: `True` if a matching claim was found and removed; `False` if no matching claim existed. Idempotent; no exception raised.
- Releasing or rolling back a switch lock claim does **not** restore a previous physical `Switch.position` in v1. `Switch.position` stays at the last successfully written alignment; a later compatible or conflicting path acquisition sets it again if needed.
- `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id, train_id)` performs neighbour-side tail-vacate cleanup. It removes only train-owned physical protection claims where `claim.train_id == train_id` and `claim.source_switch_id == source_switch_id`. This helper manages train-protection claims only; it does not read or mutate `physical_occupants` or `occupied_by`. It is called after the source switch's tail-vacate event has removed that train's `physical_occupants` entry and only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train on the source switch; it is not a path-release trigger. Returns `bool`: `True` if at least one matching claim was removed; `False` if none. Idempotent.
- `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id, train_id)` does **not** inspect `physical_occupants` or `occupied_by`; the caller (`SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)`) is responsible for the ordering check — invoking it only once the releasing train owns no remaining `physical_occupants` entry on the source switch. The helper purely removes train-owned protection claims matching `(source_switch_id, train_id)`.
- Tail-vacate switch release is keyed on the authoritative route-keyed `Switch.physical_occupants`, not on the single-value `Switch.occupied_by` projection (which surfaces only one of possibly several occupants). It removes the `physical_occupants` entry whose key equals the supplied `route` and whose value equals the releasing `train_id`. Only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train does it release neighbour-side train protection sourced from that switch. If the supplied `route` matches no entry but the train still owns at least one entry on this switch, the route-drift fallback defined in the canonical tail-vacate procedure applies; if the train owns no entry at all, emit one `kind = "warning"` event whose `message` reads `switch <switch_id> release for train <train_id> skipped: train holds no physical occupancy here`.
- `Switch.locks` is a multi-owner lock collection. Multiple compatible entries from the same train (different paths, flank claims sourced from different neighbour switches) always coexist. Primary claims (`source_switch_id is None`) from different trains coexist when their routes do not physically conflict per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`. Physical occupancy is route-keyed in `Switch.physical_occupants`, so two trains may hold non-conflicting routes on the same tile. `Switch.occupied_by` is only an on-read, non-authoritative projection of one current occupant for legacy diagnostic display; it has no refresh/write step and is never a conflict gate.
- The switch entity carries `flank_protection: List[Tuple[str, int]]`. In v1 each tuple is exactly `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)`, where `blocking_state` is the neighbour state that would create the flank conflict if that neighbour were set toward this switch.

**Switch invariants**

- `is_locked()` and `can_lock(state)` must check the canonical `(path_id, source_switch_id)`-keyed future lock collection plus train-owned physical protection, not a single field. `can_lock(state)` means "is there a compatible lock/protection state for this switch right now," not "is there only one owner." Train-owned protection continues to block a conflicting acquisition until the later tail-vacate callback removes it.

## Flank Protection Computation

**Flank protection computation**

- `RailwayNetwork` is the single canonical owner that computes flank-protection relationships at the network level and stores them on each switch entity (`Switch.flank_protection`) instead of on individual paths. No other entity writes `Switch.flank_protection`. Two helpers do the work: `RailwayNetwork.compute_flank_protection_for(switch)` returns the `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` list for one switch, and `RailwayNetwork.recompute_all_flank_protection()` rebuilds the stored list for every switch in the network by calling `compute_flank_protection_for(...)` per switch.
- Use one canonical global setting `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM = 5.0` (lives in `constants.py`) when searching for nearby switches that require mutual locking.
- `RailwayNetwork.compute_flank_protection_for(switch)` searches the network graph outward from that switch's tile (within `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM`), determines which neighbour states would create a flank conflict, and returns that switch's `flank_protection` list of `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` entries; `recompute_all_flank_protection()` stores the result on each `Switch`.
- `RailwayNetwork.recompute_all_flank_protection()` runs on every topology change — the editor add/delete-switch and connectivity-edit paths, the bulk `from_dict` network load, and the `Switch.disabled` toggle path (the same topology-availability surface that fires `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)`; see the **Switch entity** disable rule). It rebuilds the whole-network flank table in one pass (cheap relative to the per-tick loop, and topology edits happen only between ticks), which keeps the stored `flank_protection` from going stale and corrupting acquisition.
- In v1, future flank protection uses the same `Switch.locks` compatibility rule as ordinary future switch use: multiple paths may hold compatible same-state lock claims, and no lock or protection set may demand conflicting physical states. Physical occupancy is route-keyed (`Switch.physical_occupants`): two trains on non-conflicting routes may coexist on the tile, while conflicting routes never do. Future flank-protection claims use the same lock-claim shape as main-usage claims (the canonical `(path_id, source_switch_id, train_id, locked_state, route)` claim under **Switch entity**). Flank claims set `source_switch_id` to the main switch whose acquisition created that claim and carry `route = None`, while main-usage claims have `source_switch_id = None` and a non-`None` `route`. When the train head enters the source switch, those future flank claims are consumed into `train_protection_claims` until source-switch tail-vacate clears them. Stored flank-protection data remains `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)`; the acquisition-time `safe_state` is derived from it at the switch-first acquisition step, which is its single canonical definition site (see the route-point exemption under `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`).
- **Route-point exemption.** A switch that the same path traverses as a main-usage claim (a route point on that path) is never additionally flank-locked by that path. It is set and locked by its own main claim, which is its own flank protection. The producer still stores the `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` relationship for these switch pairs, because the relationship is a property of the static topology, not of any one path; the route-point exemption is applied at acquisition time, where path membership is known.
- **Same-path conflict exemption.** A non-route-point neighbour that two of the path's route points would pin to opposite states cannot be flank-protected for that path. It is left unlocked when both route points are acquired in one call, or kept only as the earlier route point set it when `best_effort` splits the path across segments (the switch-triangle apex case). See the route-point exemption and the same-path conflict exemption under the switch-first acquisition step (`SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`).

## Platform And Station

**Platform and station model**

- `Platform` and `Station` are canonical first-class entities in v1. Unchanged local fields, helper internals, and serialization details may continue to follow current code unless this document overrides them; the changed contracts below are the canonical ownership and lookup rules.
- `Station.platform_ids` is the canonical ordered grouping list of concrete `Platform` IDs belonging to that logical station. Route planning that resolves a `StationTarget` task_location reads only through that list; it does not inspect tile-level station membership fields.
- `Platform.ordered_segments` is edited end-only: `RailwayNetwork.extend_platform(...)` may add a `Segment` at either end and removal may take one from either end, and only when the result is still one connected ordered run. Middle insertions, removals, reorders, and an end-add that would require reversing the stored order are rejected by backend edit validation — the same rule `Segment.ordered_tiles` (see **Segment**) and `Block.segment_ids` (see **Block grouping**) follow.
- `Platform.ordered_segments` is the canonical ordered physical extent of that concrete platform. Route target resolution, platform-end selection, and platform availability checks all read platform membership from this ordered segment list rather than from `Tile.station_id`, `Tile.segment_id`, or ad hoc tile scans.
- A `Station` always owns at least one `Platform`, and a `Platform` always owns at least one `Segment`. Removing the last one deletes the now-empty parent instead of leaving it empty, and that delete cascades upward. See the **Non-empty parent rule** under **Tile** for the full create / edit / auto-delete contract.
- `RailwayNetwork` owns the required reverse indices: at minimum segment -> platform, segment -> block, tile -> segment, tile -> block (two-hop), tile -> platform, and tile -> station-group lookups derived from the canonical `Station.platform_ids`, `Platform.ordered_segments`, `Block.segment_ids`, and `Segment.ordered_tiles` ownership chain. Callers that need membership or reverse ownership resolve it through `RailwayNetwork`; lower-level entities do not keep back-reference membership fields in v1.
- Route resolution, platform availability, current-span coverage checks, and UI/export helpers that need platform/station membership use those `RailwayNetwork`-owned indices plus the canonical `Platform` / `Station` ownership fields. They do not consult tile-level membership.
- `RailwayNetwork.add_station / add_platform / delete_station / delete_platform / extend_station / extend_platform / get_station / get_platform / create_station_group / delete_station_group / add_platform_to_station_group / remove_platform_from_station_group / tile_to_platform_id / tile_to_station_id` are doc-canonical method names but are now implemented as forwarders to `StationRegistry`. The actual platform/station CRUD logic lives in `station_registry.py`. The `platforms`, `station_groups`, and `_tile_to_platform` registries remain stored on `RailwayNetwork` itself; `StationRegistry` reads and writes them via a back-reference. External callers (UI, scenario setup, tests) keep using the network surface unchanged.

**Platform and Station availability flags**

`Platform` and `Station` carry the same `disabled` and `manual` availability flags defined canonically in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** subsection under **TrackResource**. They are not `TrackResource` subclasses (`Platform` and `Station` are logical entities with no physical occupancy, no directional locks, and no `occupied_by`), but their availability flags follow the same operator-override and automatic-filter rules.

- `Platform.disabled: bool = False` — the platform cannot be the resolved target of any route task. `StationTarget` candidate enumeration excludes the platform from `Station.platform_ids`-derived candidates; a route task with `PlatformTarget(this)` fails its resolution attempt. There is no operator override — a disabled platform is unconditionally off-target. The constituent blocks remain governed by their own `Block.disabled` / `Block.manual` flags; the platform-level flag does not propagate to its blocks. Per the existing "operator/admin edits to platform/block target-resolution inputs ... do not rewrite an already-successful live segment" rule under **Route And RouteExecutor**, toggling `Platform.disabled` mid-flight does not disturb any live segment that already resolved through this platform; it only affects the next time Phase 1 commits a new successful create/extend attempt.
- `Platform.manual: bool = False` — automatic route target resolution treats the platform as if disabled: `StationTarget` candidate enumeration excludes it; a route task with `PlatformTarget(this)` fails its resolution attempt unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True` (see the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). An operator-initiated route carrying `Route.manual_override = True` (for example a Quick Route request) can still land authority on one of the platform's constituent blocks through Phase 1's create/extend with that flag; the dispatcher-level override is what gates the underlying call. The platform-level `manual` flag does not propagate to its blocks.
- `Station.disabled: bool = False` — no `StationTarget` task can resolve to this station; the station's `platform_ids` are excluded from the station-level candidate enumeration. Individual `Platform` records inside the station remain reachable via `PlatformTarget(...)` subject to each platform's own flags. There is no operator override at the station level.
- `Station.manual: bool = False` — every `StationTarget` auto-resolution treats this station as if disabled (all of its `platform_ids` are excluded from candidate enumeration) unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True`. Individual platforms inside it remain reachable through their own `PlatformTarget` rules subject to each platform's own flags; operator-initiated routes against the station's constituent blocks remain reachable via the route-carried `manual_override` forwarded to the dispatcher for the underlying blocks.
- Operator/admin code may toggle `Platform.disabled`, `Platform.manual`, `Station.disabled`, and `Station.manual` at any time without preconditions. The flags affect only future target resolution and do not disturb any in-flight authority that has already resolved through the platform/station. When both `disabled` and `manual` are true on the same entity, `disabled` wins (same combination rule as **TrackResource**).

## Segment

**Segment model**

- A `Segment` is a group of tiles with one unified geometry — the software unit for a continuous run of real-world track that shares the same curve radius, grade, and speed limit. It is pure geometry and stores no occupancy, reservation, availability, or power state; those live on its owning `Block`. Many tiles map to one segment (`n:1`); one segment maps to many real-world rail pieces that share its geometry (`1:n`).
- A `Segment` is not a `TrackResource` and is never reserved directly. The dispatcher reserves the owning `Block`; the pathfinder reads segment geometry to cost curves, grades, and speed (see the pathfinding home).
- Membership is two orthogonal axes (defined under **Tile role and traversal**, ownership): every segment belongs to exactly one power `Block` (`Block.segment_ids`) and to at most one `Platform` (`Platform.ordered_segments`).

**Segment geometry and traversal**

- `Segment.ordered_tiles` is the canonical segment-local traversal order. The list index is the canonical segment-local traversal index. It is initialized from the tile list supplied to backend segment-creation/edit logic, preserved through segment edits, and never rewritten by runtime switching or path acquisition. Backend validation must verify the supplied list is already one connected switch-free walk in that exact order. It must not reorder, reverse, or silently renormalize the caller-supplied sequence. Segment edits may only insert or remove member geometry at the ends while preserving relative order; middle insertions, reorders, or reversals are rejected by backend edit validation. Removing a segment's last tile does not leave an empty segment: it deletes the segment per the **Non-empty parent rule** under **Tile**.
- `Segment.ordered_tiles[0]` is the canonical local index-0 tile; v1 does not add a second stored "index-0 tile" field. Each entry in `Segment.ordered_tiles` carries `(x, y, entry_edge)`, where `entry_edge` is the external edge through which forward traversal enters that tile. The forward-entry anchor for the segment is therefore `Segment.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge`; no separate field is stored. The forward exit edge is not stored on the entry because it is determined by connectivity to the next tile in the ordered list (or, for the terminal tile, by the segment's external boundary toward the next segment).
- The canonical local-direction anchor table is:

| Field | Type | Required | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ordered_tiles` | ordered list of `(x, y, entry_edge)` entries | yes | Canonical local segment order; `ordered_tiles[0]` is local index `0` and its `entry_edge` is the forward-entry anchor for the segment |

- The **canonical block-direction helper** is defined here. It uses each constituent `Segment.ordered_tiles` adjacency to derive traversal frames and reads `Segment.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge` for single-tile segments and validation; over a block it operates on the concatenation in `Block.segment_ids` order. This imposes a stored-orientation invariant: every member segment's increasing order aligns with the block's increasing forward walk. Block grouping never reverses a segment on read; an opposing end-add is rejected.
- `min_radius_cm: Optional[float] = None` is the segment's tightest curve radius in centimetres, a persisted layout-geometry field. `None` means the segment carries no curve limit. The pathfinder's curve-radius feasibility filter reads it and rejects routing a train through this segment when the value is set and tighter than the train's minimum curve radius capability (see the pathfinding home); `manual_override` does not relax that filter. Like `length_cm` and `speed_limit_kmh` it is mutated through `SegmentGeometryManager`, not runtime state.
- `grade_percent: Optional[float] = None` is the segment's gradient as a signed percent grade -- positive is uphill in the segment's forward (`ordered_tiles`) direction, negative is downhill -- a persisted layout-geometry field. `None` means the segment carries no grade. The pathfinder's slope feasibility filter reads it and rejects routing a train up this segment when the effective uphill gradient in the traversed direction exceeds the train's maximum climbable gradient capability (see the pathfinding home); a descent never rejects, and `manual_override` does not relax the filter. It is layout geometry, not runtime state.
- `length_cm` is the segment's track length in centimetres and `speed_limit_kmh: Optional[float] = None` its static speed limit (`None` means no limit) — both are persisted layout geometry mutated through `SegmentGeometryManager`. `TrainController` lookahead applies the `speed_limit_kmh` of the segment owning each sampled tile.
- `name: str = ""` is the segment's display name, a persisted layout field the operator sets and nothing in the runtime reads. It names this run of geometry only; the block that owns the segment carries its own separate `Block.name` (see **Block grouping**, one name per record).

**SegmentGeometryManager**

- Segment geometry / topology mutation (`length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`, `ordered_tiles`, plus `track.segment_id` writes during segment create / extend / delete / split) lives in `SegmentGeometryManager` (file `segment_geometry_manager.py`). `RailwayNetwork.create_segment / create_segment_with_tracks / extend_segment / delete_segment / assign_track_to_segment / validate_segment_continuity / remove_tile_from_segment / remove_tiles_from_segment` are doc-canonical method names implemented as forwarders to `SegmentGeometryManager`. `create_segment` and `create_segment_with_tracks` conclude their forwarded call by invoking `BlockGroupingManager.create_block(...)` for the new segment, committing the segment and its default single-segment block together; see **Block grouping** for that step and its rejection contract. A segment has no runtime-state manager; occupancy/reservation state lives on the owning `Block` and is mutated only by `BlockManager`.

## Block

**Block model**

- `Block` is a `TrackResource` subclass; the shared identity surface (`resource_id`, `resource_type == "block"`, `name`, `occupied_by`, `disabled`, `manual`) comes from the **TrackResource** canonical home. Block-only fields below extend that base.
- A `Block` is the power + occupancy + reservation unit: a switch-free connected run of one or more segments wired to one power feed, carrying one `track voltage` and admitting one train at a time (see **One train per Block (power domain)** below). `segment_ids: List[str]` is the ordered list of member `Segment` IDs; a segment belongs to exactly one block. The block owns occupancy, reservation, and power state; its member segments own the geometry (see **Segment**).
- `state` is a computed read-only property derived from the following underlying primitive fields:
  - `occupied_by: Optional[str]` — physical train presence (non-None means a train is in the block).
  - `caution: Optional[CautionState]` — physical safety-margin record. `None` when not in caution. `CautionState` carries exactly `exit_edge: "N" | "E" | "S" | "W"` (the departing train's exit edge). The record is written only by `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` and cleared only by `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`, by `occupy_blocks(...)` on clean entry, by `release_path_block_reservations(completed=True)`, or by the **Opposing-traversal caution clear** on the success path of `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` (see **BlockManager**), so storing the flag and the exit edge as one Optional record makes the all-or-nothing rule structural rather than enforced by prose. Convenience reads `_is_caution` (= `caution is not None`) and `caution_exit_edge` (= `caution.exit_edge` or `None`) may still be referenced by name throughout this document for readability.
  - `reservation: Optional[BlockReservation]` — the one active path's dispatcher reservation on this block, or `None` when no path holds it. `BlockReservation` carries exactly `path_id: str` (which active path holds the reservation), `train_id: str` (the train the reservation is intended for; separate from `occupied_by` so that `BlockManager.occupy_blocks()`'s RESERVED-entry safety check stays effective after `occupied_by` is removed from the RESERVED state), and `exit_edge: "N" | "E" | "S" | "W"` (the direction reserved by the dispatcher through the block). The three fields are always written together by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and cleared together by `Block.release_path_reservation()`/`occupy_blocks(...)`, so storing them as one Optional record makes the all-or-nothing rule structural rather than enforced by prose. Convenience reads `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` are derived from `reservation.path_id`, `reservation.train_id`, and `reservation.exit_edge` respectively (each `None` when `reservation is None`); they may still be referenced by name throughout this document for readability.
  - `disabled: bool = False` — physical-absence availability flag inherited from `TrackResource`. A disabled block cannot be used as a direct waypoint/stop target, cannot be reserved, cannot be physically entered without raising the canonical fatal physical-entry conflict, and pathfinding/wayfinding treats it as absent connectivity. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the shared rules.
  - `manual: bool = False` — virtual-disable availability flag inherited from `TrackResource`. Automatic systems treat a manual block exactly like a disabled block (skipped by pathfinding, rejected by reservation, rejected as a route target); a `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call that carries `manual_override = True` (forwarded from an operator-initiated route's `Route.manual_override`) bypasses that filter and may target, traverse, and reserve manual blocks. A physical entry into a manual (but not disabled) block is ordinary clean entry, not a fatal conflict. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
  - `allowed_travel_directions: Set["forward", "reverse"]` — static block-level travel policy for this block. Defaults to both directions allowed. This is not a reservation field and is not stored on `Tile`. `Block.reserved_direction` remains only the temporary dispatcher reservation direction for one path.
    - **Manual override bypass.** Operator-initiated `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` calls that carry `manual_override = True` bypass this filter exactly like the `manual` flag: pathfinding, reservation, and terminal-direction acceptance ignore `allowed_travel_directions` when the override is set, and the field's stored value is not mutated. Routes the operator did not initiate leave `Route.manual_override = False`, so their plans still honor it. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
    - **Effect on best-effort truncation.** A block restricted to a single direction is exempt from the best-effort junction-fouling truncation exclusion (item 11): a one-way block can host no opposing traversal, so a truncated authority may stop on it right after a switch. A two-way block off a switch is held back from, unless it is in `CAUTION`, which item 11 also exempts so a follower can close up behind a leader.
- The computed `state` property must use priority order: OCCUPIED > CAUTION > RESERVED > FREE.
  - Return `OCCUPIED` if `occupied_by is not None`.
  - Return `CAUTION` if `caution is not None`.
  - Return `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path is not None`.
  - Return `FREE` otherwise.
- The intended computed-state table is:
  - physical `occupied_by is not None` -> `OCCUPIED`
  - physically free + `caution is not None` -> `CAUTION`
  - physically free + not caution + `reserved_by_path is not None` -> `RESERVED`
  - physically free + not caution + not reserved -> `FREE`
- This makes composite physical+reservation states directly representable without contradictions:
  - OCCUPIED + `reserved_by_path` set (start-block scenario): `state` reads `OCCUPIED`; reservation is visible and clearable as a plain field write with no state check.
  - CAUTION + `reserved_by_path` set (path holds block through safety margin): `state` reads `CAUTION`; reservation is likewise clearable unconditionally.
- Treat `reserved_by_path` as the dispatcher's resource lock and `occupied_by` as physical train presence only.
- `Block.ordered_tiles` is a derived read-only property: the concatenation of its member `Segment.ordered_tiles` in `segment_ids` order. The segments in a block join end-to-end into one connected switch-free walk (an invariant of `segment_ids`), so this concatenation is a single well-defined block-local tile order. The block stores no tile list of its own, but every `Block.ordered_tiles` reader keeps working against the derived value. `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is the static allowed-direction policy over that order, and that same order is the frame the block's commanded voltage sign is expressed in (see **Power**, A block voltage carries a direction). For a direct block target, `requested_destination_direction == "forward"` means movement in the increasing direction of `Block.ordered_tiles`, and `"reverse"` means the decreasing direction. Before `RouteExecutor` accepts the request with the run's `Route.manual_override = False`, and before `PathDispatcher` accepts a request with `manual_override = False`, the concrete `"forward"` or `"reverse"` value must be present in the target block's `allowed_travel_directions`. Both entry points skip this check when the override is `True`, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- Block direction is computed only by the **canonical block-direction helper** defined under **Segment**, using this derived `Block.ordered_tiles` view. The per-segment tile order, the `(x, y, entry_edge)` `SegmentTileEntry` shape, and the geometry fields (`min_radius_cm`, `grade_percent`, `length_cm`, `speed_limit_kmh`) all live on `Segment`; the pathfinder reads them per constituent segment along a candidate block.
- `occupied_seconds: float = 0.0` is the block's cumulative occupied time, a runtime-written persisted counter; the rules and its single writer live in **Maintenance counters**.
- `is_holding_point: bool = False` is a manually-set persisted layout flag marking this block as a safe place for a train to wait. The operator sets it; like `length_cm` it is saved with the layout and never written by runtime. It does not affect availability, reservation eligibility, pathfinding, the computed `state`, or physical entry — a holding-point block is reserved, traversed, and occupied exactly like any other block. It has two consumers: the best-effort truncation holding-point preference (item 11 under **Path And PathDispatcher**), where a best-effort authority that must truncate short of its requested target prefers to stop the train on a holding-point block over the switch-based scale-back, and **Meet coordination**'s `meet_ceiling_block_id` selection. Marking a block a holding point asserts that a train may wait there without fouling a switch throat or blocking a single-track corridor; that placement is the operator's responsibility.
- Because acquisition decisions need to check all blocks at once, `BlockManager` must be the sole mutator of Block runtime state (`occupied_by`, `caution`, `reservation`); `_is_caution` is only a derived convenience read. `Block` remains a data container. Segment geometry/topology mutation lives in `SegmentGeometryManager`, and block membership (`segment_ids`) is edited by `BlockGroupingManager`; neither writes Block runtime state.
- There is no admin-side direct-mutation surface for Block state. `Block.state` is a read-only computed `@property`, and v1 owns block state through `BlockManager` / `ResourceCoordinator`. Admin endpoints that need to drive block state for testing must drive it through real flows (place a train, create a path, etc.) so the canonical writers run. The `PATCH /api/blocks/<block_id>` endpoint rejects `state` mutations with HTTP 410.

**Block grouping**

- Block membership — creating a block, adding or removing a `Segment` from `segment_ids`, and deleting a block — lives in `BlockGroupingManager` (file `block_grouping_manager.py`). `RailwayNetwork.create_block / delete_block / add_segment_to_block / remove_segment_from_block` are doc-canonical method names implemented as forwarders to it. `BlockGroupingManager` edits only the power-grouping axis (`segment_ids`); it never writes runtime state (that is `BlockManager`) and never writes segment geometry (that is `SegmentGeometryManager`).
- A block is a switch-free connected run of segments joined end-to-end (an invariant of `segment_ids`, see **Block model**), so grouping edits preserve that run: `add_segment_to_block` and `remove_segment_from_block` may add or remove a `Segment` only at either end of `segment_ids`, and only when the result is still one connected switch-free walk whose member `Segment.ordered_tiles` orientations all align with increasing block order. Middle insertions, removals, reorders, and an end-add that would require reversing the stored segment order are rejected by backend edit validation, mirroring the end-only rule for `Segment.ordered_tiles` (see **Segment**) and `Platform.ordered_segments` (see **Platform And Station**).
- **Grouping infers member order, never member geometry.** The member list a caller supplies to `create_block` carries no order: an operator selecting segments on a layout has no canonical order to give. Grouping therefore works the run order out from the members' stored tile geometry and stores `segment_ids` in that order. Ordering is the only thing it infers. When a member is stored running the opposite way round the run, grouping rejects it and names it, rather than reversing that member's `ordered_tiles` — reversing is a geometry write and is only ever done on the segment axis (`SegmentGeometryManager`), so grouping keeps to the power-grouping axis. The operator reverses the named segment first, then groups.
- Grouping never leaves a segment without a block, because every segment belongs to exactly one block (see **Segment**). The invariant is enforced at both ends of a segment's lifetime. At creation, `RailwayNetwork.create_segment(...)` / `create_segment_with_tracks(...)` (see **SegmentGeometryManager**) complete by calling `BlockGroupingManager.create_block(...)` for that one new segment in the same operation: the segment create and the block create commit together, so a rejected block create rejects the segment create as a whole, and `RailwayNetwork` never returns a segment with no block. This is the default one-block-per-segment rule named in the **Canonical shared contracts** entry for Block grouping; the new block's id is derived from the segment's under **One id per record**. After creation, a segment the operator has not grouped into a larger block remains its own single-segment block under that same rule (see **One train per Block (power domain)**). `add_segment_to_block` moves a segment out of its prior block (deleting that block if it thereby empties, through the **Non-empty parent rule** under **Tile**); `remove_segment_from_block` returns the segment to its own single-segment block; and `delete_block` re-homes every member segment the same way. Removing a block's last member segment deletes the block through the same **Non-empty parent rule**.
- **One id per record.** This is the canonical home for how the two axes are identified. A `Block` id and a `Segment` id must never be equal. The two are separate records with separate lifetimes, so one string naming both is ambiguous wherever either is displayed, addressed, or bound — a panel row, an event `entity_id`, a `block_id` key in the **Power** binding table. The default one-block-per-segment rule still pairs them, and the pairing is spelled as a matched prefix pair: a segment `SEG_<suffix>` is owned by block `BLK_<suffix>`, and a platform's segment `<platform_id>_SEG` by its block `<platform_id>_BLOCK`. The pairing must be derived from the id alone and must not change across a save, a load, or a re-bake of the same layout, because the `block_id` keys in the **Power** binding table name blocks that outlive any one session. When one axis already carries the id an outside record names, that id stays put and the other axis takes the derived one: `create_segment(...)` / `create_segment_with_tracks(...)` mint the segment and derive its block, and a caller that must pin an exact block id states it. Splitting a segment whose children each get their own block suffixes the parent BLOCK's id (`PARENT_1`, `PARENT_2`), and each child segment takes the matching derived id.
- **One name per record.** This is the canonical home for how the two axes are named. `Segment.name` and `Block.name` are separate display names, and no create, split, group, or re-home step copies one onto the other. `create_segment(...)` / `create_segment_with_tracks(...)` name the segment they mint and leave the new block unnamed; `create_block(...)` names the block and leaves every member segment's name as it stands; splitting a segment names the child segments after the parent segment and leaves the child blocks unnamed; and a segment re-homed by `remove_segment_from_block` / `delete_block` keeps its name and lands in an unnamed block. Each name says what its own record is: a segment's names a run of track geometry, a block's names a power feed. A record with no name is displayed by its id, so a reader always sees which of the two they are looking at.

**`CAUTION` vs reservation independence**

- `CAUTION` is an independent safety state indicating the block ahead (in the departing train's direction) is still occupied; it is orthogonal to path reservation.
- A block can be simultaneously `CAUTION` and `reserved_by_path` (path still holds it for a future or in-progress train), or `CAUTION` with no reservation (train has passed and path is done). With the computed-state model both combinations are natively representable — the `state` property returns `CAUTION` in both cases, and `reserved_by_path` can be cleared by a plain field write with no state guard.
- `CAUTION` by itself does not make a block unreservable. Its operational effect is that `TrainController` lookahead applies `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` when it samples the block. `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` must still allow a `CAUTION` block when the primitive reservation predicate below is satisfied.
- Runtime physical entry into a disabled block is fatal and never writes occupancy. Entry into a manual-but-enabled block is ordinary clean entry. Setting `Block.disabled = True` is rejected while occupancy, reservation, or `CAUTION` remains; the caller must wait for ordinary tail-vacate/CAUTION clearing or cancel the holding path, then retry. V1 has no force-cleanup or train-removal workflow; a fatal halt is released whole through step `7c41` of the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**, not repaired resource by resource. `Block.manual` remains an unconditional future-planning flag.
- The `OCCUPIED → CAUTION → FREE` lifecycle is driven purely by physical train movement through `BlockManager`; it does not depend on whether a path reservation exists.
- Path reservation (`reserved_by_path`) is consumed by the dispatcher/manager on train-head entry into that block, or released by the dispatcher on path completion/cancellation if the block was never entered. This is separate from the occupancy state machine.
- `CAUTION` is directional: `block.caution.exit_edge` records the compass edge through which the departing train left (the direction it was moving). The `CAUTION → FREE` decision — which forward neighbour is consulted and when the protection chain is stale — is owned by `BlockManager._reevaluate_caution_blocks()`. A block's caution is also cleared, with no reevaluation, when a path reserves that block for a traversal out of its other end (the **Opposing-traversal caution clear** in `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`); a traversal out of the same end keeps it, so a following train still gets the caution speed cap. Both rules live under **BlockManager**; see **`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)** there.

**Block methods**

- Path reservation and physical occupancy are separate concerns with separate `BlockManager` entry points: reservation eligibility is owned by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and physical entry by `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)`, both defined in the **BlockManager** section.
- `Block.reserve_for_path(path_id, train_id, direction)` is an internal helper called only by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`. It writes `block.reservation = BlockReservation(path_id=..., train_id=..., exit_edge=...)` (the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` reads all become defined together). The `direction` argument may be a compass `exit_edge` (`"N" | "E" | "S" | "W"`) or a block-relative `"forward"` / `"reverse"`; the block converts via `Block.ordered_tiles` adjacency (multi-tile) or `Block.ordered_tiles[0].entry_edge` (single-tile) and stores the compass `exit_edge` in `block.reservation.exit_edge`. The reservation never overwrites `occupied_by` (which stays physical-only).
- `Block.release_path_reservation()` is an internal helper called only by `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)`. It clears `block.reservation` (so the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_direction`, and `reserved_for_train` reads all become `None` together) with no state guard. It must NOT touch `block.caution` (that field is the physical-lifecycle owner's, `BlockManager`).

**Block invariants**

- Runtime physical entry is written only through `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(block_ids, train_id)` (full contract in the **BlockManager** section). The single-writer rule, its commit phase (Phase 3, for every train including an unidentified train), and the initial-placement exception are reconciled in **Power**, Block occupancy detection. On clean entry the three primitive fields move together: `occupied_by = train_id`, `caution = None`, and any same-train `reservation = None` (the derived convenience reads `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_direction`, and `reserved_for_train` all become `None` together). `OCCUPIED` overrides `CAUTION` on clean entry; stale caution metadata must not survive an intervening clean entry.

**One train per Block (power domain)**

- A block owns the one `track voltage` its member segments share. Every segment reads its voltage from the block; there is no separate per-segment voltage. The canonical home for the voltage value itself is `TrainPhysics` (see the `track voltage` glossary term and **Power**, Block power-controller role); the block is the set of segments one emitted value lands on. A switch between blocks has no feed of its own and runs on its `switch feed block` (see **Power**, Switch power-controller role). A block the operator has not grouped from a larger run is its own single-segment feed, independently powered — a layout with no grouping applied behaves as one feed per segment, and grouping is the added constraint that models real shared-power wiring.
- **Detection isolation is separate from geometry.** A block is one occupancy section with its own current detection (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). Its member segments are geometry only and are not independently detected.
- A block carries one train's `track voltage` at a time. Two trains cannot be controlled independently on one shared feed: the block has a single voltage, one voltage cannot produce two target speeds, and two locomotives with different speed profiles on one feed close on each other until they collide. So a block is a single-train power domain.
- The one-train-per-block rule is just the ordinary block-exclusion check: a block already occupied or reserved by another train is not eligible for a second train's reservation, so `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` rejects it the same way it rejects any occupied or other-reserved block (see the reservation-eligibility rule in **BlockManager**). A path is therefore never reserved into a block another train holds. A second train that physically draws power in a block already held by another train is the fatal physical-entry conflict `error.origin = "occupied_by_other_train"` (see the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**).
- Running several trains on one layout therefore uses several blocks, one powered train each — the cab / block-control pattern of a real layout. Multi-train spacing on a shared loop divides the loop into blocks so each train's section is independently fed.

## BlockManager

**Block-manager model**

- `BlockManager` is the database-style lock manager for block resources and the single serialization point for atomic block acquisition, whether the dispatcher is attempting a full-route all-or-nothing reservation or a best-effort prefix reservation.
- `BlockManager` holds the network and optional runtime back-reference.
