It uses the runtime only to report fatal physical-entry conflicts. `SimulationController` owns the failed flag, frozen diagnostic payload, hardware safe-state call, and terminal-session policy. Fatal handling performs no recoverable route/controller/path teardown, and v1 exposes no later in-session removal or resource-repair workflow.
- `occupy_blocks(block_ids, train_id)` is the atomic occupy helper and sole physical-entry writer. Clean entry sets `block.occupied_by = train_id`, clears `block.caution`, and consumes any same-train reservation. If a target block is occupied by another train it reports a fatal physical-entry conflict with `error.origin = "occupied_by_other_train"`; if reserved for another train, with `error.origin = "reserved_for_other_train"`; if the block has `disabled == True`, with `error.origin = "disabled_resource_entry"` and `prior_owner_train_id = None`. All three go through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`, and none writes occupancy or overwrites the prior owner. A block whose `occupied_by` already equals `train_id` is `"idempotent_noop"`: no field is written and no conflict is reported. Returns `OccupyResult(outcome: "clean_entry" | "idempotent_noop" | "fatal_conflict", reason: Optional[str])`; the fatal result has already entered failed runtime, and the caller stops processing.
- Define `ReservedBlockTraversal` as the canonical value type for `directions` in `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`. It carries exactly `exit_edge` (`"N" | "E" | "S" | "W"`) and `block_direction` (`"forward" | "reverse"`). `block_direction` is derived canonically from the block's immutable internal tile order: if the directed in-block traversal advances to higher indices in `Block.ordered_tiles`, it is `"forward"`; if it advances to lower indices, it is `"reverse"`; if the traversal stays within one ordered tile, use the chosen entry's explicit directed frame for that tile rather than train-end labels.
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(block_ids, path_id, train_id, directions, manual_override=False)` reserves a path's blocks atomically.
- `directions` in `reserve_blocks_for_path(block_ids, path_id, train_id, directions, manual_override=False)` must be a mapping keyed by `block_id`, where each value is one `ReservedBlockTraversal`. `PathDispatcher` owns building that mapping from the chosen path geometry before calling `BlockManager` and must use the same canonical block-direction helper used by route callbacks. `ReservedBlockTraversal.exit_edge` is written to `block.reservation.exit_edge` (read as `Block.reserved_direction`); `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` is used for `Block.allowed_travel_directions` validation, and it is also the direction the emission layer signs that reserved block's voltage with (see **Power**, A block voltage carries a direction).
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` accepts a `manual_override: bool = False` parameter, propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home. It must first check eligibility for every future block in the candidate block set before writing any fields. Blocks already physically occupied by the train may appear in `Path.resource_sequence` for geometry/progress, but they are not included in the future reservation candidate set.
  - **Block eligibility.** A candidate future block is eligible when **all of these hold**:
    - `block.disabled == False`
    - `block.occupied_by is None`
    - `block.reserved_by_path is None or block.reserved_by_path == path_id`
    - `manual_override == True`, or the candidate `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` is present in `block.allowed_travel_directions`
    - `block.manual == False`, or `manual_override == True`
    - no other train holds the block (the one-train-per-block rule in **Block**)
  - `block.caution is not None` is not an eligibility failure by itself. `CAUTION` affects the following train through `TrainController.CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH`, not by blocking an otherwise legal reservation.
  - When any block in the candidate set fails eligibility, the entire call is rejected and no state changes are made.
  - `PathDispatcher` owns choosing whether that candidate set is the full requested route (`all_or_nothing`) or the longest best-effort prefix it wants to attempt atomically.
- `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` writes the reservation atomically by calling `Block.reserve_for_path(path_id, train_id, reserved_exit_edge)` with the mapping value's `exit_edge` for each future block; the helper sets `block.reservation` in one step (the derived `reserved_by_path`, `reserved_for_train`, and `reserved_direction` reads all become defined together). It must not create path-owned reservations for blocks the train already physically occupies; those resources are train-owned physical occupancy and are represented only in `resource_sequence` / `current_resource_index` for path progress. Returns `ReserveResult(success: bool, failed_block_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str])`. On success all reservations are written atomically; `failed_block_id = None`. On failure no field is mutated, `failed_block_id` is the first block that failed eligibility, and `reason` is a free-form diagnostic string.
- **Opposing-traversal caution clear.** A block is a switch-free connected run with exactly two ends, so a candidate `ReservedBlockTraversal.exit_edge` either equals `block.caution.exit_edge` or is the block's other end. On the success path of `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`, in the same atomic write as the reservations, the manager resolves every future block in the candidate set that has `block.caution is not None`:
  - Same end (`exit_edge == block.caution.exit_edge`): the reserving train traverses the block the way the departing train went, so the protected chain is still ahead of it. `block.caution` is left untouched and the train gets the `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` cap the caution exists for. This is the following-train case, and clearing here would let a follower close on the train ahead at line speed.
  - Other end: the reserving train traverses the block away from the protected chain, so that safety margin sits behind it and can restrict nothing ahead of it. The manager sets `block.caution = None` for that block, and the computed state becomes `RESERVED` under the reservation just written.
- The **Opposing-traversal caution clear** is the only caution write acquisition performs, and it is what keeps a train that reverses out of the block it was protecting from crawling back through its own stale safety margin at `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH`. It needs no `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` cascade: clearing one block's caution changes no block's `occupied_by`, and the reevaluator's keep condition reads only occupancy and connectivity. Cab selection (`TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`) does not reach `BlockManager` — a reversal retires a stale margin only when the train's new path actually reserves that block, and until then the ordinary tail-vacate cascade still clears the caution as soon as the train leaves the block it was protecting.
- **`release_path_block_reservations(block_ids, path_id, completed=False)`.** Releases not-yet-entered reservation ownership during completion and cancellation, without modifying physical occupancy state.
  - It clears only blocks matching **both** of these:
    - the block belongs to that path's dispatcher-owned future reservation set
    - the block currently satisfies `block.reserved_by_path == path_id`
  - A mismatched block is ignored and must emit one `kind = "warning"` event whose `message` reads `release <block_id> but it's not reserved for <path_id>`, rather than being treated as a hard release failure.
  - Because `Block.release_path_reservation()` is a plain field clear with no state guard, this method can call it for each matching owned block regardless of whether the block is currently `CAUTION` or `RESERVED`. Normal head-entry consumption means occupied resources must no longer be path-owned future reservations.
  - When `completed=True` (path completed with the train fully stopped at terminal), it additionally force-clears `block.caution = None` on any still-`CAUTION` matching owned blocks.
  - When `completed=False` (path cancelled mid-flight), physical `CAUTION` state is left untouched, because the train is still moving and the safety margin must be preserved.
  - Returns `bool`: `True` if at least one matching reservation was released; `False` if none.
  - Head-entry consumption of a block reservation happens through the physical-entry path, not this release path.
  - This is the single reservation-release entry point. Path completion and path cancellation are its only v1 callers — v1 exposes no force-cleanup workflow and no admin-side direct-mutation surface for Block state. It never touches `occupied_by` or `caution` outside the `completed=True` exception.
- `on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is the canonical tail-vacate entry and the only normal train-vacated physical transition entry point. Its write set, no-op cases, and cascade are defined in **`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)** below. Returns `bool`: `True` on a clean tail-vacate; `False` on the no-op cases. No exception raised.
- `BlockManager` must read block/switch registries from the owning `RailwayNetwork` entity layer rather than maintaining its own copied block/switch registries. `RailwayNetwork` remains the canonical home of the block registry, switch registry, and topology graph/traversal helpers. `BlockManager` reads `network.blocks`, `network.switches`, and one shared network-topology adjacency helper from there; it must not rescan unrelated entity lists or invent a second topology cache of its own.

**`on_block_tail_vacated()` behaviour (directional CAUTION evaluation)**

- `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports normal tail-vacated block resources through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, which routes the release through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)`; the coordinator is the only normal runtime caller that invokes `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` for block resources. `TrainPhysics` does not call `BlockManager` directly.
- `BlockManager` does not independently decide that a block resource should be released; that instruction comes from `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` after a detector-confirmed tail-vacate observation (debounced no-current; see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). For normal runtime release, `train_id` must still match the current physical owner. If `block_id` is not present in the `BlockManager` registry (e.g. during early bootstrap or after teardown), `on_block_tail_vacated()` is a no-op that returns `False` and emits no event. If the block exists but `block.occupied_by != train_id`, `on_block_tail_vacated()` must leave the block unchanged and emit one `kind = "warning"` event whose `message` reads `block <block_id> release for train <train_id> skipped: currently occupied by <other_train_id>`.
- `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is one-shot: the detector-confirmed tail-vacate fires the single tick the detector confirms the block clear (after the off-debounce window). If `block.occupied_by != train_id`, the diagnostic emits exactly once for that `(train_id, block_id)` and is not retriggered, because the detector reports the clear only once — the block leaves the confirmed-occupied set on that tick and is not re-reported.
- `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` is safe to call once `BlockManager` is constructed, even before any blocks/switches have been registered. Empty-registry calls are a no-op.
- `direction` on `on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)` is `Optional[edge]`. It is the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` (`N`/`E`/`S`/`W`) for the traversal by which the train left that block, or `None` when the train left the detected area and no exit edge is known. A train whose movement the runtime calculates always supplies an edge; `None` arises only when an unidentified train's last held block clears (see **Power**, Unidentified trains).
- If `block.occupied_by == train_id` and `direction` is an edge, `on_block_tail_vacated()` clears `block.occupied_by` and sets `block.caution = CautionState(exit_edge=direction)`. With the computed-state model the block's `state` property now returns `CAUTION`.
- If `block.occupied_by == train_id` and `direction` is `None`, `on_block_tail_vacated()` clears `block.occupied_by` and writes no `caution`, so the block's `state` property returns `FREE`. Caution is directional protection behind a departing train: it is keyed on the edge that train left through, and `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` clears it by looking at the forward neighbour through that edge. With no known edge there is no neighbour to consult and no direction to protect, so the block is free.
- After updating the vacated block, `BlockManager` must call `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` to cascade any blocks that can already advance to `FREE`.
- `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` iterates all blocks currently in `CAUTION`. In the normal reevaluation path, a `CAUTION` block advances to `FREE` unless the forward protection chain is both reachable AND ends in an `OCCUPIED` block. `BlockManager` owns this walk, but it performs it through the shared `RailwayNetwork` topology graph / neighbour-traversal helper rather than by scanning the full entity lists. "Forward block" is defined precisely: start from the vacated block's exit edge given by `block.caution.exit_edge`, inspect the neighbouring resource in network connectivity, and keep following live tile connectivity through any intervening switch tile using the current `Switch.position` until the next block resource is reached. That next reachable block resource is the only block consulted for the `CAUTION -> FREE` decision. Blocks behind the vacated block or off the currently selected switch branch are ignored. When that block is `OCCUPIED`, a train is still standing in the protected chain, so `CAUTION` is kept; when it is anything else, the chain is empty and the block advances to `FREE`. Occupancy of that one forward block is the whole test: the reevaluator reads no train, controller, or `travel_direction` state, so it needs no `SimulationController` back-reference (`BlockManager` keeps one only to report fatal physical-entry conflicts). This shape prevents a following train (entering from behind) from keeping a caution block alive, and lets a later switch throw invalidate the old protected chain. Retiring a margin the protecting train has reversed away from is not this walk's job; that is the **Opposing-traversal caution clear** in `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`.
- If `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()` cannot resolve any reachable forward-adjacent block in `block.caution.exit_edge`, if live connectivity no longer reaches the block that was previously being protected, or if that forward block is not `OCCUPIED`, there is nothing ahead left to protect. In that case it must clear `block.caution = None` immediately, so the computed state becomes `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path` is still set, otherwise `FREE`.
- When a CAUTION block clears, set `block.caution = None`. The block's computed `state` then re-evaluates to `RESERVED` if `reserved_by_path` is still set, or `FREE` if it is not.
- **Reevaluation triggers.** Reevaluation is triggered by manager-owned state changes, not by a periodic scan. The trigger list is closed at **exactly three entries**:
  - `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` must call `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`.
  - Every successful `BlockManager.on_switch_position_changed(switch_id)` call must call it too. A `Switch.disabled` toggle arrives through that same callback, per **Switch entity**.
  - `BlockManager.release_train_occupancy(train_id)` must call it too.

  Those three cover every input to the clear predicate: connectivity comes from switch position and switch availability, and the forward block's `occupied_by` is cleared by `on_block_tail_vacated()` on the ordinary detector-confirmed path, and by `BlockManager.release_train_occupancy(train_id)` on the two teardown paths that have no detector observation to wait for: the ghosted-train teardown (**Power**, Ghosted trains) and the unidentified-train adoption re-key (**Power**, Unidentified trains).
- `release_train_occupancy(train_id)` clears `occupied_by` on every block that train holds, writes no `caution`, and then runs `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`, so a chain protected by that train cannot stay in `CAUTION` forever.
- `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)` is deliberately not a trigger. Writing `occupied_by` can only make the reevaluator keep a caution it would otherwise clear, and this walk never re-adds caution.
- No controller, command, or cab-selection event triggers reevaluation. `Switch` does not independently clear block caution state; it only changes connectivity, and `BlockManager` remains the sole owner of the `CAUTION -> FREE` decision on this path.
- `reserved_by_path` is NOT checked as a condition for `CAUTION → FREE`. The physical safety margin and the dispatcher reservation are independent: a block can become physically clear of `CAUTION` while still holding a `reserved_by_path` value. In that case the computed `state` becomes `RESERVED`, not `FREE`, until the dispatcher separately clears the reservation via `release_path_block_reservations()`.
- Explicit exception: `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)` may force-clear `block.caution = None` during terminal completion release for owned blocks. That is a completion-time release rule, not part of the normal neighbour-based reevaluation path.

## SwitchManager

**Switch-manager model**

- `SwitchManager` is the database-style lock manager for switch resources and the single serialization point for atomic multi-switch lock acquisition, whether the dispatcher is attempting a full-route all-or-nothing acquisition or a best-effort prefix. It mirrors `BlockManager`'s contract on the switch side.
- `SwitchManager` holds the owning `RailwayNetwork` reference and an `Optional[SimulationController]` runtime back-reference. It uses the network for the switch registry; it uses the runtime surface only to report fatal physical-entry conflicts through `SimulationController.enter_failed_runtime(...)`. In editor mode (no `SimulationController`) the manager exists but its runtime entry points are not exercised.
- `SwitchLockRequest` is the canonical value type for one requested switch lock. It carries exactly `switch_id`, `locked_state`, `claim_role` (`"main" | "flank"`, matching the convenience read defined under **Switch entity**: `"main"` when `source_switch_id is None`, else `"flank"`), optional `source_switch_id` (`None` for a main claim; the originating switch_id for a flank claim), and `route: Optional[FrozenSet[str]]` — the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through the switch, required non-`None` when `claim_role == "main"` and `None` for flank requests. `PathDispatcher` populates `route` from the chosen candidate's switch step (`GraphWalkStep.entry_edge` / `exit_edge`); the manager copies it onto each `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` claim and rejects a primary request whose `route` is `None` per the cross-train co-locking rule under **Switch entity**.
- `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(requests, path_id, train_id, manual_override=False)` attempts every requested switch lock atomically. The `manual_override` parameter (default `False`) is propagated from the calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; the manager sets `claim.manual_override = manual_override` on every `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` call it issues so manual switches participate when (and only when) the operator explicitly overrode. On any failure it rolls back the locks recorded in this call and returns `LockAcquireResult(success: bool, failed_switch_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str], locked_switch_ids: List[str])`. Same all-or-nothing rule as `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`.
- `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)` releases every lock owned by `path_id` across every switch. `SwitchManager.release_path_locks_for_switches(switch_ids, path_id)` is the segment-rollback variant scoped to a passed switch_id list.
- `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` is the canonical switch tail-vacate entry, called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for switch tail-vacate events through the `ResourceCoordinator` facade. `route` is required (non-`None`) and is the canonical key into `Switch.physical_occupants`. `SwitchManager` performs the release itself: it removes the `Switch.physical_occupants` entry whose key equals `route` and whose value equals `train_id`, and — only when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train on this switch — it iterates this switch's `flank_protection` neighbours and calls `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id=switch_id, train_id=train_id)` on each, per the **Switch entity** tail-vacate contract. `Switch` holds no back-reference and never walks neighbours itself; the neighbour iteration is manager-owned, consistent with `Switch` remaining a pure data/primitive entity (see **Switch entity**).
- `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` is the canonical clean-physical-entry entry. `route` is required (non-`None`); a `route is None` argument is reported through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** with `error.origin = "malformed_route_key"`. It owns the flank-lock conversion: on clean entry it records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock for that switch, and converts every required flank-protection claim on neighbours into train-owned `train_protection_claims` per the **Switch entity** flank-protection contract. Conflict cases are reported through the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure** and return `False`: a route that conflicts with a route held by a different train (per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`), a disabled switch entry, and the intrusion case (the entering train holds no compatible lock for its route — see the intrusion check in the **Switch entity** section). Physical entry into a manual (but not also disabled) switch is **not** a conflict case — it is treated as ordinary clean entry per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- The actual `Switch.position` write still goes through the canonical mutation workflow on `Switch` per the **Switch entity** section; `SwitchManager` calls into that workflow rather than mutating `Switch.position` directly.
- The physical throw emission is owned by `SwitchManager` as part of that same paired operation: after the `Switch.position` write + `BlockManager` notify, and only when the alignment actually changed, it drives the throw through `SwitchPowerController.set_throw(...)`. `SwitchManager` reaches that controller through its `simulation_controller` reference (with a network fallback), the same way it reaches `BlockManager`, and never calls `SwitchOutput` itself. The throw contract, including how an unissued throw is handled, is defined canonically in the **Power** section (Switch power-controller role); `SwitchManager` does not restate it.

## ResourceCoordinator

**Resource-coordinator model**

- `ResourceCoordinator` is the unified facade `PathDispatcher` calls to reserve, release, and route physical-entry / tail-vacate events for an entire `PathSegment`. It owns no state; it is a pure composition layer over `BlockManager` and `SwitchManager`. The single remaining `resource_type` string branch lives inside `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(...)` and `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(...)`. `PathDispatcher` does not branch on `resource_type` for reserve / release / rollback / tail-vacate / physical-entry.
- `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(future_block_ids, block_traversals, switch_requests, path_id, train_id, manual_override=False)` runs the switch-lock phase first, then the block reservation phase. The parameters are `future_block_ids: List[str]`, `block_traversals: Dict[str, ReservedBlockTraversal]` (keyed by `block_id`, matching `reserve_blocks_for_path`'s `directions` contract in the **BlockManager** section), `switch_requests: List[SwitchLockRequest]`, `path_id: str`, `train_id: str`, and `manual_override: bool = False`. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` pass them positionally. The `manual_override` field (default `False`) is propagated per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home, and forwarded to `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` and `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`, so manual resources are reachable only when the operator explicitly chose to override. On block-reservation failure it rolls back the just-acquired switch locks before returning. Returns `SegmentReserveResult(success: bool, failed_resource_type: Optional[str], failed_resource_id: Optional[str], reason: Optional[str], reserved_block_ids: List[str], locked_switch_ids: List[str])`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.rollback_segment(block_ids, switch_ids, path_id)` releases any partial holds placed on a segment that failed mid-flight. Used by `PathDispatcher._rollback_segment(...)`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.release_path_resources(path_id, block_ids, completed)` composes `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` and `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)`. Returns `(released_block_ids, released_switch_ids)`.
- `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` is the single entry called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each `tail_vacated_resources` event. It routes to `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` for blocks (using `direction`, ignoring `route`) and `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)` for switches (using `route`, ignoring `direction`).
- `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)` is the single entry called by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each `entered_resources` event. `route` is the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` for switch events (required, non-`None`) and is ignored for blocks. It routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([resource_id], train_id)` for blocks and `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)` for switches. It returns `bool`: `True` on clean entry, including an idempotent no-op, and `False` when the routed manager reported a fatal physical-entry conflict. For switches it returns that manager's own `bool` unchanged; for blocks it maps `OccupyResult.outcome` (`"clean_entry"` / `"idempotent_noop"` to `True`, `"fatal_conflict"` to `False`). On `False`, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` stops processing that report per step `018a`.

## Path And PathDispatcher

**Path model**

- The dispatcher path lifecycle has three states: `ACTIVE`, `COMPLETED`, and `CANCELLED`. The lifecycle value is stored on `Path` as `status: PathStatus`. `status` is initialized to `ACTIVE` on successful `create_path(...)` and is the field transitioned by completion and cancellation.
- The core identity / routing fields on `Path` are `path_id`, `status`, `train_id`, and `resource_sequence`. The reads `authority_end_block_id`, `path_tiles`, and `reaches_target(block_id)` are exposed as derived functions (`get_authority_end_block_id()`, `get_path_tiles()`, `reaches_target(...)`) computed from `resource_sequence` on each call; see the **Path field schema** for the canonical contracts.
- `RouteExecutor` owns route scheduling priority through `RouteRun.effective_priority` (the override-aware read of the shared `Route.priority`; see **RouteRun overrides**) once a run exists, and the run-phase scheduling tier through the derived `RouteRun.is_starting`. Dispatcher path records do not carry a second copied `priority` field or any phase-tier field in v1; scheduling stays route-owned rather than becoming path-owned metadata.
- `Path.get_path_tiles()` returns the ordered physical tile list for the authority in train tail -> current authority end order, computed live from `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles`. Every `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` slice uses the canonical **path_tile entry schema** below; shared contracts must not use bare `(x, y)` tuples.
- Resource acquisition is synchronous inside `create_path()`. Terminal-approach behavior is represented by progress/completion predicates, not a separate live status.
- `resource_sequence` is an ordered list of path-step records interleaving blocks and switches in traversal order. The per-entry schema is defined canonically in the **resource_sequence entry schema** subsection immediately below.
- `RouteExecutor` platform-end resolution, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side selection, and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix search must all use the same shared backend pathfinding home/helper. The implementation may extend the existing backend pathfinding code in `PathDispatcher.find_paths()` / `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)`, or extract that code into a `RailwayPathfinder` class, but it must not create a second disconnected pathfinder with different comparison or direction rules. That shared helper must be callable before any live `Path.resource_sequence` exists, and must return the ordered candidate walk it used for comparison. It must be the single source for candidate-length comparison, direction filtering, current-span end selection, and tie-breaking, rather than letting different layers reimplement those rules on their own.
- The dispatcher path-acquisition API surface is exactly `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` for a new authority and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for growing an existing live authority. V1 must not introduce a third acquisition wrapper or route code path with a third acquisition contract.

**Path field schema**

This is the single canonical field set for the dispatcher-owned `Path` entity in v1.

**Why this shape.** `resource_sequence` is the single source of truth for path geometry. `authority_end_block_id` and `path_tiles` are derived functions over it, so any update to live authority is one change to one list, not several stores to keep in sync. `current_resource_index` is the head-progress anchor, and `reaches_target(block_id)` is expressed against it so that "reached" means reached *ahead of the train*, not merely present somewhere in the sequence. The requested target itself is not stored here: it is an argument to `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` and an echo in their result, while the run's own copy lives in `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`. One stored copy of the target, on the side that owns the task, is what keeps the authority record and the run from drifting apart.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `path_id` | `str` | Unique dispatcher path identifier |
| `status` | `PathStatus` | One of `ACTIVE`, `COMPLETED`, `CANCELLED` |
| `train_id` | `str` | Assigned train |
| `path_tiles` | `function (read-only): get_path_tiles() -> List[path_tile]` | Function that returns the full ordered train-tail -> authority-end geometry by extracting and concatenating `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` in `sequence_index` order on each call. Not a stored field; not a cache; no writers; no rebuild step. Shared contracts that previously read `Path.path_tiles` call `Path.get_path_tiles()` instead. |
| `resource_sequence` | `List[resource_sequence entry]` | Ordered interleaved block/switch resources. This is the single source of truth for path geometry. |
| `current_resource_index` | `int` | `sequence_index` of the current path-progress anchor resource: head-based once a true head exists, otherwise initialized to the authority-side resource at the physical train end closer to the path end |
| `reaches_target` | `function (read-only): reaches_target(block_id) -> bool` | Function that returns whether the live authority reaches `block_id`: `True` when `block_id` appears as a `block` entry in `resource_sequence` at a `sequence_index` at or after `current_resource_index`. Not a stored field; no writers. This is the canonical test for "is this authority still short of that target?", and the only one. It is deliberately not an equality test against the terminal block: a target the authority already runs past is reached, not missed. Entries before `current_resource_index` are the train's own covered span behind the head, so they never count as reached. |
| `authority_end_block_id` | `function (read-only): get_authority_end_block_id() -> str` | Function that returns `resource_sequence[-1].resource_id` on each call (the **Path invariants** rule below requires the terminal `resource_sequence` entry to always be a `block` entry). Not a stored field; not a cache; no writers. Updates to live authority are made on `resource_sequence` itself, and the function reflects the new terminal block automatically. Shared contracts that previously read `Path.authority_end_block_id` call `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` instead. |
| `last_status_updated_time` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when `status` last changed, sampled fresh for that transition (see **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` call `time.time()` themselves when they write `Path.status = ACTIVE` / `CANCELLED`. `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` calls `time.time()` itself when it sets `Path.status = COMPLETED`, the same way `create_path(...)` and `cancel_path(...)` do. A call that commits no status transition — an idempotent `cancel_path(...)` or `try_complete_active_path(...)` no-op on an already-terminal path — writes no status and therefore does not resample this field. When that same writer also emits a notification carrying an `event_time` for the same transition, it samples `time.time()` once locally and uses that one value for both the status timestamp and the notification field. |

- `Path` is the entity name for the dispatcher-owned resource-sequence authority record itself. Shared contracts must refer back to this table instead of rediscovering the field set from scattered prose.

**Path status state machine**

This is the single canonical state-machine contract for `Path.status` in v1. `status` is the lifecycle enum. Shared contracts must not invent extra path lifecycle states or path-owned pending cleanup flags.

| From `status` | Trigger | To `status` | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (none) | `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` succeeds | `ACTIVE` | Path record is inserted into `paths_by_id` and indexed as the train's active authority. Writes `last_status_updated_time` to the synchronous create-time system-time Unix timestamp. |
| `ACTIVE` | `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` is called from Phase 3 step `bc55`, and hands the arrival to the route once the authority is released | `COMPLETED` | The **Stop-arrival procedure** owns ordering. Dispatcher runs no acceptance test of its own; the arrival decision belongs to the **Stop-arrival check**. |
| `ACTIVE` | `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)` | `CANCELLED` | Not-yet-entered authority resources are released synchronously; controller authority is cleared; the `Path.status` transition to `CANCELLED` makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return `None` (or skip to a different `ACTIVE` path) by definition; `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` is notified with the structured deviation/normal-progress classifications. |
| `COMPLETED` | any | `COMPLETED` | Terminal. `cancel_path(...)` on a `COMPLETED` path is an idempotent no-op: it returns `committed = False` with `new_status = COMPLETED` and mutates nothing. |
| `CANCELLED` | any | `CANCELLED` | Terminal. `cancel_path(...)` on a `CANCELLED` path is an idempotent no-op: it returns `committed = False` with `new_status = CANCELLED` and mutates nothing. |

- `status` is set once per lifecycle transition by the originating synchronous call. No later stopped cleanup pass re-transitions status or writes a pending field.
- Physical `occupied_by` (block or switch) is outside this state machine. It is cleared only by tail-vacate observations committed through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` — detector-confirmed for blocks (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), controller-reported traversal for switches — and never by `status` transitions.
- A train may have at most one `ACTIVE` path at a time; retained `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED` rows do not block a fresh `create_path(...)`.
- In a stored live path, `resource_sequence[-1]` must always be a `block` entry. A v1 path may contain switch resources internally, but its live authority end is always expressed by `authority_end_block_id` on a block resource, never on a terminal switch.

**path_tile entry schema**

This is the single canonical definition of one tile step inside `Path.path_tiles` and inside each `resource_sequence[*].path_tiles` slice.

- Each `path_tile` entry carries exactly:
  - `x`
  - `y`
  - `entry_edge`
  - `exit_edge`
  - `resource_sequence_index` — the parent `Path.resource_sequence[*].sequence_index`.
- `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` are required on every tile entry; shared contracts must not infer them later from neighboring coordinates.

**resource_sequence entry schema**

This is the single canonical definition of a `resource_sequence` entry. Every site that describes the `received_path_plan` projection, dispatcher/UI authority projections, the `to_dict()` exports, or any other projection of authority geometry refers back to this schema by name rather than restating it.

**Why this shape.** Each entry carries both the resource identity (`resource_type`, `resource_id`) and the directed traversal frame (`entry_edge`, `exit_edge`, plus the nested `path_tiles` slice). Storing the directed frame explicitly — instead of letting consumers infer it from tile coordinates — means every traversal is unambiguous, even where a tile could be walked in either direction, and downstream lookahead/braking math has the geometry it needs without re-walking the tile graph. Storing `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` at both the resource and tile level lets resource-level consumers read the frame without unpacking the tile slice, while still keeping per-tile detail available when needed.

- Each `resource_sequence` entry carries exactly:
  - `sequence_index` — position of the entry in the path's train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
  - `resource_type` — `"block"` or `"switch"`.
  - `resource_id` — stable network resource ID for the block or switch.
  - `path_tiles` — the contiguous ordered slice of canonical `path_tile` entries belonging to that resource, in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order. Every nested tile entry in this slice has `resource_sequence_index == sequence_index`.
  - `entry_edge` — the directed traversal-frame entry edge for that resource in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
  - `exit_edge` — the directed traversal-frame exit edge for that resource in train-tail -> authority-end traversal order.
- No other fields are required in v1.
- Ordered tiles alone are not enough because a single tile can be traversed in either direction and the stop frame changes with the chosen entry side; `entry_edge` and `exit_edge` are required fields on every entry.
- `entry_edge` must equal the first nested `path_tiles[0].entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` must equal the last nested `path_tiles[-1].exit_edge`; they are stored redundantly so resource-level consumers can read the traversal frame without unpacking the whole tile slice.
- For switch resources, the required switch state is not stored as a fixed field on the entry. It is derived at acquisition time from the planner-facing `Tile.enumerate_traversals(entry_edge)` contract on the switch tile, using the `(entry_edge, exit_edge)` pair selected for that resource's `path_tiles` slice. This derivation replaces storing `switch_positions` as a separate dict on the path.
- These per-resource tile slices plus explicit resource/tile entry-exit edges are the geometry contract for controller/UI consumers.
- `current_resource_index` tracks the `sequence_index` of the current path-progress anchor resource, not the first matching raw resource ID; the controller resolves the reported index by the **Head-mapping rule** in the **ControllerProgressReport schema**. Once a cab is selected or live progress updates establish a true head, this index is strictly head-based. Before that, it is initialized to the authority-side resource at the physical train end that is closer to the path end under the same comparison used by automatic cab selection.
- Flank protection lives on the switch entity (acquired automatically when the switch is locked); `Path` does not store its own flank-protection list.
- `Path` does not carry the requested target. `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` are call arguments: dispatcher uses the direction during the call to filter compatible terminal candidates and to choose a legal terminal traversal frame, then stores only the resulting `resource_sequence`. The direction is the caller's already-resolved block-relative constraint and is always `"forward"` or `"reverse"`. No stop-centimetre field is passed or stored.
- `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` returns the live authority end block whose forward boundary the controller brakes toward using its safety-margin policy. The function is defined in the **Path field schema**; it has no setter and no stored backing field.
- The live terminal frame always comes from explicit directed geometry on the relevant terminal `resource_sequence` block entry (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`); the spec must not rely on readers inferring direction from tile coordinates alone or from `requested_destination_direction` alone.
- Whether a live authority reaches a given target block is answered by `Path.reaches_target(block_id)` (defined in the **Path field schema**), never by comparing that block against `authority_end_block_id`. The two differ whenever the authority legitimately runs past the target, and in that case the authority has reached it. Arrival direction is guaranteed at acquisition instead of re-checked later: candidate search rejects any candidate whose terminal traversal does not satisfy the caller's `requested_destination_direction`, so a stored authority that reaches a target reaches it in a legal frame by construction.
- `Path` does not store a path-owned pending cleanup field. Physical `occupied_by` state and train-owned switch protection change only through resource entry/tail-vacate observations committed by `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` — detector-confirmed for blocks (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), controller-reported traversal for switches.
- `PathDispatcher` must treat each `Path` record as the authoritative dispatcher-owned database entry, keyed by `path_id` in `paths_by_id[path_id]`, with `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` deriving the one active authority path for that train. The controller-facing authority is only a read-only projection of that current dispatcher record.
- Dispatcher-owned path records must remain available while active and, after termination, as retained `CANCELLED` or `COMPLETED` records for history/inspection. In v1, retain that history indefinitely rather than aging it out. Update `last_status_updated_time` on every `status` transition for recordkeeping, but authority release must not depend on any controller-side copy surviving.
- `PathDispatcher`'s retained path database must be the authoritative historical store for every path it has created. After `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` has been cleared, controller progress reports resolve active authority only against the new active authority, while delayed release or occupancy reporting for a block or switch that remains under the train resolves from the resource itself (`BlockManager` state for blocks, `Switch.occupied_by` / `Switch.train_protection_claims` for switches). Retained history rows do not by themselves block creation once the old path is no longer active (the same-train rejection while an `ACTIVE` path exists is owned by step 1 of the **`create_path()` flow**); only ordinary live resource conflicts remain.
- `train_id` is mandatory for every dispatcher authority path, including ordinary route-driven paths and one-off operator routes. Frontend/API callers supply it; anonymous authority paths are out of scope. Future authority on reserved resources is tracked through `Block.reserved_by_path` (written by `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`) and `Switch.locks` (keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`, where main claims use `source_switch_id = None`, and each claim stores `path_id`, `source_switch_id`, `train_id`, and `locked_state`).

**Path API migration**

- The canonical write interfaces are `PathDispatcher.create_path(requested_destination_block_id, train_id, requested_destination_direction, acquisition_mode=..., manual_override=False, acquisition_cap_block_id=None)`, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(path_id, requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction, acquisition_mode=..., manual_override=False, acquisition_cap_block_id=None)`, and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)`. `extend_path(...)` is the same request contract as `create_path(...)` plus the leading `path_id` that identifies which live path is being grown in place. `manual_override` is the operator-override flag defined in the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; default `False`, and `True` only when the requesting run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True` (operator-initiated routes such as Quick Route). `RouteExecutor` forwards that route-stored flag; request handlers never call these interfaces directly. `error` is the diagnostic `ErrorRecord` the writer populated for the `cancel_path` event; callers must not branch on anything inside it when deciding planning or routing behavior. `deviation` and `run_complete` are the only structured cancellation classifications, are mutually exclusive, and are forwarded from the consumed latch payload. Create/extend branching uses the structured `appended_resource_count`, `reached_target`, and `failure_kind` fields in the create/extend result object instead of parsing `reason`.
- **Acquisition-cap rule.** `acquisition_cap_block_id` (default `None`, used only by **Meet coordination** — see that subsection) is an optional upper bound on how far authority is acquired on this call. The cap bounds how far authority is stored; it does not change `acquisition_mode`, which independently governs whether an authority short of the requested target is storable at all. When the cap is set, the dispatcher plans and acquires toward `requested_destination_block_id` but never stores authority past the cap block: the live authority truncates at the cap so the derived `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` lands on the cap block (or earlier, if an ordinary live conflict or an item-11 truncation-terminal exclusion forces it further back). The cap never changes the stored `requested_destination_block_id` / `requested_destination_direction` or the returned target fields, and `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` still records the route task's real target, not the cap. A capped authority that is short of the requested target therefore behaves exactly like any other truncated best-effort authority: it returns `reached_target = False` while `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` is false, and it never satisfies the **Stop-arrival check**, so the train holds at the cap without completing. When `extend_path(...)` cannot append because the next forward resource lies beyond the cap, it returns `appended_resource_count = 0` with `reached_target = False` (a valid prefix held back by the cap), the same lane `RouteExecutor` uses for a live-conflict block. Passing `acquisition_cap_block_id == requested_destination_block_id` (or `None`) is the no-cap case and completes normally on arrival. The cap is meaningful in both `create_path(...)` and `extend_path(...)`. A cap short of the requested target and `AcquisitionMode.all_or_nothing` are a contradictory pair: the mode demands the full requested authority and the cap forbids storing it, so the call can never succeed while both hold. That combination is expected rather than exceptional — the route's mode is operator policy and the ceiling is assigned per tick by **Meet coordination** — so it resolves through the ordinary lanes: `extend_path(...)` returns `appended_resource_count = 0` with `reached_target = False`, keeping the live path intact until the meet clears the cap, and `create_path(...)` fails with `blocked_before_target`. Neither tears down authority, and both succeed unchanged once the ceiling lifts. Meet coordination emits the one-time operator warning at cap assignment (see **Meet coordination**, Phase 1 consumption).
- The canonical replacement read interfaces are the controller-facing `received_path_plan` projection for active authority geometry, `TrainController.get_current_span()` for current physical span, `TrainController.get_lookahead()` for live speed/limit diagnostics, and a dispatcher-owned progress projection `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (full field set defined under **Data Flow To UI**) when UI/debug consumers need live `current_resource_index`. That projection is resolved from dispatcher-owned path registries (for example `active_path_id_by_train` plus `paths_by_id`) and returns the currently active path bound to that `train_id`; it does not read train/controller mirror fields.
- Route-managed planning remains point-to-point per call. `RouteExecutor` never asks dispatcher/pathfinder to solve a whole multi-waypoint route in one search. Each `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` request targets exactly one currently resolved waypoint block at a time, and longer waypoint chains are formed by concatenating repeated successful point-to-point segments onto the same live path.

**`create_path()` flow**

1. Before candidate search or any side effect, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` requires that `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` does not already return an `ACTIVE` path. A train can have only one active authority plan at a time. This is a caller precondition, not an outcome: `RouteExecutor` decides between create and extend from that same read before it calls, so a create against a train that already has live authority raises per the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract** rather than returning a failure. Callers grow an existing live path with `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)`, or wait until the old path has transitioned out of `ACTIVE` before calling `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` again.
2. Candidate search must enumerate direction-compatible resource sequences from the train's current occupied span to `requested_destination_block_id`. It rejects any candidate whose own walk would hold one resource twice at once (the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants**); any candidate that traverses a disabled block or disabled switch; any candidate that traverses a manual block or manual switch when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home); any candidate whose traversal violates a block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home); and any candidate that reaches the terminal block in a way that does not satisfy `requested_destination_direction`. The `requested_destination_block_id` itself is subject to the same availability rules: a disabled target block produces no viable candidate, and a manual target block produces no viable candidate when `manual_override == False`. On a single `create_path(...)` call, dispatcher then selects exactly one candidate using the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining start-side choices, and applies `acquisition_mode` to that one candidate. There is no fall-through to a second candidate within the same call. Higher-level alternative-target attempts are owned by `RouteExecutor`: in one eligible Phase 1 pass it may try additional distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` targets, but it must not call `create_path(...)` twice for the same resolved pair in that pass. After every distinct same-tick target has failed without authority creation or growth, planning waits until the next eligible tick.
3. Dispatcher must build and store the exact directed terminal frame for the requested target (`path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, `exit_edge`) as part of the terminal `resource_sequence` block entry. Dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal block traversal. When the requested target was resolved from a multi-block platform, `RouteExecutor` must already have resolved one concrete terminal block for that run before calling dispatcher, and the terminal frame uses that already-resolved terminal block directly; dispatcher does not choose between platform blocks itself.
4. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must take `requested_destination_block_id`, `train_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, `acquisition_mode`, `manual_override`, and the optional `acquisition_cap_block_id` (the Meet-coordination acquisition cap; see the **Acquisition-cap rule**). It must not require a caller-supplied `start_block_id`, a stop-centimeter value, or a second opaque waypoint/platform stop-context payload. Dispatcher derives the planning start side from `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).get_current_span()` plus controller cab state, by calling the same shared network-graph walk query that `RouteExecutor` uses. If a cab is selected, the only start-side candidate is the current head end implied by that cab. If no cab is selected, compare both physical ends of the train against the already resolved terminal block and use the end whose successful graph walk is shorter. If one physical end is currently on a switch resource, map that end to the one occupied block inward from that end of the current span before running the candidate comparison; if no such inward occupied block exists for that end, that end is not a valid start-side candidate. Use the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining start-side choices. Dispatcher must not introduce any other layer-local ad hoc tie-break. Depending on the caller, that shared graph query may return only distance metrics, or the ordered tile/block walk used to build `resource_sequence`.
5. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must also take an explicit `acquisition_mode` parameter of type `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode`, an enum owned by `PathDispatcher` with v1 values `all_or_nothing` and `best_effort`.
6. Candidate search must become direction-aware. The shared ordered-walk entry point (named `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` in this document if the implementation extracts a class from the existing dispatcher search) must reject candidates whose traversal uses a disabled block or disabled switch, reject candidates whose traversal uses a manual block or manual switch when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), reject candidates whose traversal violates `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home), and reject candidates whose terminal traversal does not satisfy `requested_destination_direction`. For a direct `block_id` target, `requested_destination_direction` is interpreted against that block's immutable internal traversal order: `"forward"` means the increasing direction of that block-local order and `"reverse"` means the decreasing direction. `RouteExecutor` must resolve any waypoint-authored `"either"` into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` before calling `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`; dispatcher never receives `"either"` in the v1 path contract. If dispatcher still cannot build one unique terminal frame for the chosen move, path creation must fail rather than guessing.
7. Stop-position centimeter inputs are not part of `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` in v1. The controller computes the braking target from the authority boundary and its canonical safety margin.
8. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` does not build same-head-block repositioning authority. A request whose `requested_destination_block_id` is already the train's current head block is a caller precondition violation and raises per the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**; a covered stop destination is finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance instead (the covered-stop rule — train stopped, no active path, head in the destination block; see the covered-task index advance canonical home), so no dispatcher authority exists for it. A `requested_destination_block_id` the train occupies only with its tail (not its head) is a turnaround target, not a covered stop: it is served normally and resolves through ordinary candidate search as a reverse move. Argument naming is consistent throughout: `requested_destination_block_id` is the caller's current requested target passed into `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)`, and `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` returns the current live-authority end derived from `Path.resource_sequence`.
9. Every stored live authority therefore crosses at least one resource boundary beyond the train's covered span: the forward suffix contains at least one not-yet-covered resource, and the terminal frame always comes from a real forward traversal. Dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal block traversal; it never synthesizes an in-place frame from the train's own covered tiles.
10. In `all_or_nothing` mode, if the full chosen candidate path cannot be acquired, roll back and return failure; no live path is created. Dispatcher does not fall through to a second candidate inside the same call.
11. In `best_effort` mode, if the full chosen candidate cannot be acquired, `PathDispatcher` must truncate the authority to the furthest forward block-terminated resource prefix it can acquire on that candidate and still create one live path. Dispatcher does not fall through to a second candidate inside the same call. If that candidate cannot produce any storable live `resource_sequence` — because the train has no usable occupied-span seed, because no block-terminated prefix can be acquired, or because of the head-on-switch failure below — the call fails, and `RouteExecutor` may then try a different distinct resolved target later in that same eligible Phase 1 pass or on a later eligible tick, but not the same resolved pair twice in one pass. That truncated live path keeps the same `path_id` and stores a `resource_sequence` whose final block is the last block in the current authority (the derived `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` reflects this automatically). The controller does not need a special "you are already at your authority end" branch: it enters the ordinary running flow, and ATC/lookahead handles the geometry using the current authority boundary plus safety margin. A successful create has created a brand-new path record with at least one block resource in its live `resource_sequence`, so it always reports `appended_resource_count > 0`; `reached_target` then says whether that authority reaches the requested target or is still truncated short of it. If no block-terminated live authority can be stored, return `success=False` with a structured `failure_kind` rather than storing a create that appended nothing.
    - Holding-point preference (the higher-priority scale-back target). When the authority must truncate short of the requested target, dispatcher first scans the acquirable forward block-terminated prefix from its furthest acquirable end back toward the train. If any block in that prefix is a holding point (`Block.is_holding_point == True`, canonical home **Block** field schema), dispatcher truncates the live authority to end at that furthest-forward holding point. A holding point is the operator's explicit safe-wait place, so choosing it overrides the switch-based junction-fouling exclusion below — the junction-fouling back-off, and its one-way / `CAUTION` exemptions, apply only when the acquirable prefix has no holding point. The reversal-trap exclusion below stays absolute: a holding point that would trap a reversing train ahead is not an eligible terminal, so dispatcher passes over it to the next-furthest holding point, and to the switch-based fallback when no holding point qualifies.
    - Special case: if the furthest acquirable prefix ends on a `switch` resource, dispatcher must truncate again to the previous `block` resource before storing the live path, so the stored live authority is always block-terminated.
    - The stored live authority may terminate on the requested target block whatever the armed task's kind, including a `task_kind == "waypoint"` target. Dispatcher never grows a prefix past the requested target to avoid landing on it. What it must not do is grow past an armed `stop` or `run` target, per the **Authority termination rule**.
    - **Reversal-trap exclusion.** The stored live authority's terminal block must not be a block that a stopped train ahead needs in order to leave. Truncate further to the previous block-terminated prefix when both hold for the first block past the truncation point:
      - it is occupied by a train that holds no live authority of its own;
      - that occupant's next route move would leave the block by coming back through the candidate terminal block (a reverse / turn-back).

      Parking the follower on that terminal block would trap the two trains nose-to-nose: the follower holds it to go in while the occupant needs it to come out, and neither can move. The follower instead waits one block back and grows its authority once the occupant has left. If no shorter block-terminated prefix can be acquired, the create call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot hold best-effort authority where it would trap a reversing train ahead"`.
      - The check reads only the occupant's immediate exit block. An occupant that leaves forward — an ordinary platform pass-through, or a move that continues out the far side (for example a loop that runs on through a tunnel rather than reversing) — never restricts the follower.
      - A moving occupant that already holds a live path is exempt, since it clears forward under its own authority.
      - The occupant's next move is read through `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(train_id)` (dispatcher reaches the route executor through `simulation_controller`). This is that helper's canonical definition site: it resolves the block of the occupant's next non-`dwell` move from the run's live next-planning position, following `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` wrap and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` end reflection the same way `_find_next_task_target(...)` does. It reads from the occupant's current progress cursor on its currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun`, and stops at the first move task it cannot resolve.
      - **Run start-mode seeding** asks the same question from a hypothetical seed position instead, so it calls the resolver's own anchored helper, which walks on past a move task that cannot resolve rather than stopping. The two share one forward task-walk kernel — the walk order, `CIRCULAR` wrap, and `dwell` skip are defined once — and differ only in that resolve-and-return policy.
      - An occupant with no currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` — a parked train with no assigned route, or one whose run has already finished or deactivated — has no next move, so the helper returns `None` and that occupant is exempt: a train with no route plan never reverses out on its own, so the follower may hold authority up to it. **Route-definition validation** guarantees at least one non-`dwell` task, so a next non-`dwell` move always exists on a valid running walk; a `None` return therefore always means this route-less exempt case.
    - **Junction-fouling exclusion.** Absent any holding point in the acquirable prefix (per the holding-point preference above), the stored live authority's terminal block must not be a two-way block that the path enters straight off a switch. A block is two-way when its `Block.allowed_travel_directions` allows both `forward` and `reverse`. When the furthest acquirable block-terminated prefix would stop the train on such a block, dispatcher must truncate further to the previous block-terminated prefix, before that switch.
      - Holding one block back keeps the switch clear: a train parked on the block past the switch fouls the junction, where an opposing move could still be routed through that switch and meet it head-on, while a train held before the switch leaves the junction free for either train to use.
      - A one-way terminal block (restricted to a single direction) is exempt, since no opposing traversal can be granted on it. Trains may pack tight right up to it — for example along a one-way corridor, where the single permitted direction makes a head-on impossible.
      - A two-way terminal block that is in `CAUTION` is exempt too. A caution block is one a train has just passed forward through this junction, so the corridor through the switch is already committed to one-directional following use; letting the follower close up onto it at the caution speed cap is the close-up that `CAUTION` exists to enable (see the `CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` speed-cap home). The leader still occupies the block ahead in the caution's protect direction, so no opposing move can be routed up to the caution block, and once the follower physically enters it the block is `OCCUPIED`, so no opposing grant onto it is possible either.
      - Apart from this caution carve-out, the exclusion is occupancy-independent: for a non-caution two-way block off a switch, it holds the authority back from the junction before any opposing train has arrived, so there is no window in which both trains have already committed past the switch. (The reversal-trap exclusion above instead keys on a specific stopped occupant, so it cannot hold back until that occupant is already in place.)
      - If no shorter block-terminated prefix can be acquired, the create call fails with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` and a `reason` such as `"cannot hold best-effort authority fouling a switch into a two-way block"`.
      - The exclusion constrains only truncation short of the requested target; when the live authority reaches the requested target itself, its terminal may be any block, switch-adjacent or not.
    - If the train's covered span itself is empty so no non-empty live `resource_sequence` can be formed for the new path, `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` must fail with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target`.
    - If the train head is currently on a switch resource and no forward block beyond that switch can be acquired, `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` must fail with `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` rather than storing a head-on-switch holding authority. A natural-language failure reason such as `"cannot create best-effort authority while head is on a switch"` is sufficient in v1.
12. Allocate the new `path_id` before switch acquisition so `Switch.locks` and flank-protection claims can be keyed by that same path ID. Once the chosen acquisition succeeds, create the dispatcher-owned path record directly in `ACTIVE` state; the controller observes the new movement-authority projection on its next `received_path_plan` read. There is no intermediate persisted lifecycle status.
13. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` seeds `resource_sequence` from the train's already-occupied resource span. Start-side selection uses the canonical rule already defined in the previous step: cab-selected -> head-side occupied block; no cab -> both end blocks compared by the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**. The stored sequence is `[already-occupied resources from the opposite span end through the chosen start side] + [forward candidate from start side to requested target]`. The already-occupied backward portion is not re-reserved; only the forward suffix is subject to `all_or_nothing` vs `best_effort` acquisition. `current_resource_index` is initialized to the head resource (cab selected) or to the authority-side resource at the closer span end (no cab). On each seeded entry, the side toward the path direction is `exit_edge` and the opposite side is `entry_edge`. Covered-task normalization may prevent route-side calls for already-covered `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks. For `task_kind == "stop"`, the covered-task advance step immediately satisfies the stop when the train is already stopped, no active path exists, and the train's head is in the destination block (the covered-stop case — e.g. the route was created while the train was already at the destination platform; see the covered-task index advance canonical home). While the train is rolling or a live path exists, stops are not finished by coverage; `create_path(...)` is still required to produce movement authority.
14. Build `resource_sequence` in traversal order so both blocks and switches are tracked in one sequence.
15. Store only the acquired `resource_sequence` on the `Path`. The requested target is not stored: `authority_end_block_id` and `reaches_target(block_id)` are both derived from that one list, and the run's copy of the target lives in `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`.
16. The active-path guard has already run before acquisition. A retained `CANCELLED` / `COMPLETED` row or retained history row does not by itself block `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`; only ordinary resource conflicts still apply.
17. Store the new dispatcher-owned path record in `paths_by_id[path_id]` with `Path.status = ACTIVE` (which by definition makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return this `path_id`).
18. Do not copy reserved-block state onto `Train`; future reservations remain dispatcher-owned on resources and in the controller-facing authority projection until head-entry consumes them.
19. The movement-authority projection must preserve an explicit traversal frame for every block and switch entry; readers must not try to recover that frame later from tile coordinates alone.

**`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**

- `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` and `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` return the same result object, with exactly `success: bool`, `path_id: Optional[str]`, `resource_sequence: Optional[List[resource_sequence entry]]`, `requested_destination_block_id: Optional[str]`, `requested_destination_direction: Optional[str]`, `authority_end_block_id: Optional[str]`, `appended_resource_count: Optional[int]`, `reached_target: Optional[bool]`, `failure_kind: Optional[PathAcquisitionFailureKind]`, and `reason: Optional[str]`. Both calls answer the same question — grow authority from a start frame toward a requested target — so they share one result shape. The only difference is where the start frame comes from: `create_path(...)` resolves it from the train's covered span (flow item 4), and `extend_path(...)` takes it from the live path's current authority end.
- Two values describe every successful outcome:
  - `appended_resource_count` is how many new resources the call added to the live `resource_sequence`, `0` or more. Callers may branch only on `== 0` versus `> 0`; the number itself is diagnostic. It counts `resource_sequence` entries, which interleave blocks and switches, so it is not a distance.
  - `reached_target` is `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` evaluated after the call. The test is on the block alone, and it is satisfied whether the authority ends on that block or runs past it. It means "there is nothing left to extend", not "the train has arrived" — the **Stop-arrival check** owns the physical arrival test separately.
- The four success shapes are exactly the combinations of those two values:

| `appended_resource_count` | `reached_target` | meaning | what the caller does |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `> 0` | `True` | authority grew and now reaches the requested target | proceed |
| `> 0` | `False` | authority grew but is still short of the requested target | keep the path, retry later |
| `0` | `True` | the live authority already reaches the requested target (`Path.reaches_target(...)` is true), so there was nothing to append | proceed |
| `0` | `False` | the path is still a legal prefix toward the requested target but could not grow this tick | keep the path, retry later |

- `extend_path(...)` may return any of the four. `create_path(...)` has one extra rule: **a successful create must append at least one resource**, so `appended_resource_count == 0` is never a create success. That single rule is what makes every stored create a non-empty block-terminated live path.
- A `0` / `False` result carries a proof obligation. The dispatcher must have established that a legal forward suffix from the current authority end to the requested target still exists. That check walks forward from `resource_sequence[-1]`; it never re-audits the already-held prefix, which is owned by the deviation and cancellation flow instead. That one fact is the whole obligation: whatever stopped the growth on this tick — a live occupancy / reservation / lock conflict, this tick's `acquisition_cap_block_id` (per the **Acquisition-cap rule**), or a truncation-terminal exclusion that bars the only acquirable terminal (flow item 11) — is a fact about now and needs no separate proof. If no legal forward suffix exists, the call must fail with `no_viable_path`. Without this obligation `RouteExecutor` could not tell "busy, retry later" from "hopeless", and would retry forever.
- **Viable-but-unavailable rule.** This bullet is the canonical home for the line between the `0` / `False` retry result and a `no_viable_path` failure. The two answers split on different facts about the world:
  - **Viability** is a fact about topology, traversal direction, and the availability flags: whether any legal walk from the current authority end to the requested target exists at all. Waiting never changes it. When no such walk exists, the call must fail with `no_viable_path`.
  - **Unavailability** is everything else that can stop growth on one tick: the live claims sitting on the resources of a walk that does exist (occupancy, reservations, and locks), this tick's `acquisition_cap_block_id`, and the two truncation-terminal exclusions of flow item 11 (the reversal-trap exclusion and the junction-fouling exclusion). Each is a fact about now rather than about the track — a claim clears when its owner releases it, and a cap or an exclusion is recomputed from the world on every attempt. A viable walk that cannot grow for any of these reasons must not fail with `no_viable_path`: on `extend_path(...)` it returns the `0` / `False` retry result; on `create_path(...)` it returns a truncated `> 0` / `False` success in `best_effort` (per the truncation-terminal exclusions above) or `failure_kind = blocked_before_target` in `all_or_nothing` (see the `blocked_before_target` case below).
  - A resource held by this same path, or occupied by this path's own train, carries a live claim like any other, so it is an availability condition and takes the retry lane on `extend_path(...)`, or the create-side unavailability outcome above on `create_path(...)`. A path that walks back around to a resource this train's own tail still occupies must stay live and retry: the claim clears as the train advances, and the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants** reads holdings live for exactly this reason.
- `PathAcquisitionFailureKind` has v1 values:
  - `no_viable_path` (`062a`): no topology-compatible, direction-compatible suffix to the requested target exists under the availability rules (disabled resources always excluded; manual resources excluded when `manual_override == False`; `Block.allowed_travel_directions` enforced when `manual_override == False`, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). It also covers a target that no legal walk can reach without holding one resource twice at once, which is the permanent case of the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants**; a walk that revisits a resource only because a live claim on it has not cleared yet is viable but unavailable and takes the `0` / `False` retry result instead, per the **viable-but-unavailable rule**. A requested target that would need the train to turn back at the current authority end is this same failure: extension is forward-only, so no forward suffix reaches it. `reason` names the specific case, for example `"extend would reverse at authority end"`.
  - `blocked_before_target` (`d6e3`): a viable suffix exists but `create_path(...)` could not store any authority for it on this tick — in `all_or_nothing` because the full candidate could not be acquired, and in `best_effort` because every acquirable prefix was rejected by a truncation-terminal exclusion (the reversal trap or junction fouling — the holding-point preference only chooses among eligible terminals and never rejects a prefix), because the train head is on a switch and no forward block beyond it can be acquired, or because the train's covered span is empty so no non-empty `resource_sequence` can be seeded. Only `create_path(...)` returns it. The same world state on `extend_path(...)` is the `0` / `False` success above, because there the existing live path is still valid and the right caller behavior is to keep it and retry.
- Some conditions are caller-contract violations rather than outcomes. They raise instead of returning a failure, because no return value would let the caller do anything useful with them:
  - `create_path(...)` called for a `train_id` that already has an `ACTIVE` path. `RouteExecutor` reads `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` and chooses create or extend before it calls (see **Exact `RouteExecutor` tick calls**), so the caller always knows which one applies.
  - `create_path(...)` called with a `requested_destination_block_id` that is already the train's current head block. Covered destinations are finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance, never by dispatcher authority (see flow item 8). A block the train spans only with its tail is a turnaround target, not a covered destination, and is served normally.
  - `extend_path(...)` called with a `path_id` that does not exist or is not `ACTIVE`.
