  - a `requested_destination_direction` of `"either"`, or a missing required argument. `RouteExecutor` resolves `"either"` into a concrete block-relative direction before calling (flow item 6).
- On success, `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, `authority_end_block_id`, `appended_resource_count`, and `reached_target` are required; `failure_kind = None`; `reason = None`; and `resource_sequence` is the full current live `resource_sequence` after that create/extend operation completes. `RouteExecutor` may read those values to see whether authority grew and whether it now reaches the armed target. It does not advance any route waypoint index from this return payload alone.
- On failure, `success = False`, `path_id = None`, `resource_sequence = None`, all target fields are `None`, `appended_resource_count = None`, `reached_target = None`, `failure_kind` is required, and `reason` is required. A failed `extend_path(...)` leaves the existing live path unchanged and valid.
- `reason` is human-readable diagnostic text for create/extend failures. Callers may display or log it, but they must not branch on exact string equality in v1; control flow branches only on `success`, `appended_resource_count == 0` versus `> 0`, `reached_target`, and `failure_kind`.

**`cancel_path()` return contract**

- `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)` returns a small result object with exactly `committed: bool`, `path_id: str`, `new_status: PathStatus`, and `reason: Optional[str]`. `error` is the diagnostic `ErrorRecord` that flows directly into the `cancel_path` event, required for every cancellation except a `run_complete` release, where it is `None`; no planning or routing logic branches on anything inside it. `deviation` and `run_complete` are the two structured classification booleans, and `run_complete` is accepted only from the consumed Phase-1 run-completion request. They are mutually exclusive. Dispatcher forwards both booleans to the route callback and includes the whole `ErrorRecord` in the notification.
- Two values describe the outcome together, the same shape the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract** uses:
  - `committed` is whether this call performed the `ACTIVE -> CANCELLED` transition. It is the only signal that this call ran the resource release, the `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` callback, and the `cancel_path` event.
  - `new_status` is the path's status after the call, and it is always terminal on return. It is what the caller actually asked for: that this path no longer holds authority.
- `cancel_path(...)` is idempotent. A path that is already `CANCELLED` returns `committed = False` with `new_status = CANCELLED`, and a path that is already `COMPLETED` returns `committed = False` with `new_status = COMPLETED` — that path released its future authority through normal completion on an earlier tick, which is what the caller wanted. Neither case re-runs the release, the route callback, or the notification, and neither resamples `Path.last_status_updated_time`. Callers therefore never have to check `Path.status` before cancelling.
- When the path is currently `ACTIVE`, `cancel_path(...)` performs the normal cancellation flow described below and returns `committed = True` with `new_status = CANCELLED`.
- Two conditions are caller-contract violations rather than outcomes, and raise rather than returning a result, per the same rule the create/extend contract applies to its own preconditions: a `path_id` that is not present in `paths_by_id`, and a call carrying both `deviation = True` and `run_complete = True`.
- `reason` is optional plain-text diagnostic context. Callers may display or log it, but they must never branch on exact reason text; control flow branches only on `committed` and `new_status`.

**Authority truncation**

- A block a path reserved may be physically taken by another train before this train reaches it: an unidentified train appears on it, or a train running under its own authority gets there first (see **Power**, Attribution). Authority that runs through a block another train is standing on is not authority, so `PathDispatcher.truncate_path_before_block(path_id, block_id, reason)` pulls the path back to end at the last block in front of the train and behind the taken one. The path stays `ACTIVE`. Everything from the new end onward is dropped: those blocks' reservations and those switches' locks are released, because a lock on a switch the train may no longer reach is held against every other train for nothing.
- The authority end is derived from `resource_sequence` and the controller reads its plan live from the dispatcher, so the shortened authority reaches the canonical lookahead on its next read with no separate republish step. ATC then brakes the train to a stop at the new end exactly as it does for any other authority end. The train keeps every resource it has already entered, and the route layer extends the path again through the ordinary acquisition step once the block clears; truncation itself never retries.
- A path must end on a block (see **Path invariants**), so the cut lands on the last `block` entry before the taken one, and any switches between the two go with the dropped tail. `truncate_path_before_block(...)` returns whether the path survived with a valid remainder. It returns `False` in one situation: nothing valid is left in front of the train — the taken block is the first thing ahead of it, so there is no shorter authority to hold. On `False` the caller releases that one reservation through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations([block_id], path_id)` and writes `pending_path_cancel_request(revoked)` instead, so the whole path comes down through the ordinary Phase-3 cancellation while the train may still be rolling.
- Step 0 is the only caller: after attribution, every block a train has just physically entered that is still reserved for a different train is truncated out of that train's authority. Doing it there means the reservation is released before Phase 3 commits the entry, so a legitimate arrival is never reported as the `reserved_for_other_train` fatal physical-entry conflict (see **BlockManager**, `occupy_blocks(...)`).

**Resource acquisition**

- The caller's `manual_override` flag (default `False`) flows through the entire acquisition chain: `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` pass it to `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(...)`, which forwards it to `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` (used by block-eligibility checks) and `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` (which sets `claim.manual_override` on every `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` call it issues). Pathfinder and candidate-search calls invoked from inside `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` pass the same flag into the `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` request so manual resources are included or excluded consistently with the acquisition step. See the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home for the full override contract.
- Acquire switches first, then blocks, so switch conflicts are discovered before any blocks are reserved. In `best_effort`, dispatcher still uses this switch-first probe to decide the storable prefix: walk the chosen candidate's future switch resources in path order, acquiring main and flank claims until the first switch/flank acquisition failure. If a switch acquisition fails, roll back any switch claims already acquired for that probe, truncate the candidate to the previous block before that failed switch, and then attempt the block reservations for that block-terminated prefix. If every switch in the candidate probe succeeds but block reservation later fails, use the first failing block as the truncation point, roll back, and retry the block-terminated prefix before that block. The final stored prefix is then acquired atomically using the same switch-first, blocks-second order. If that final prefix contains no storable block authority for `create_path(...)`, the create fails; for `extend_path(...)`, appending nothing is still a success when the existing path remains a valid blocked prefix.
- `PathDispatcher` builds the ordered `SwitchLockRequest` list for the candidate and passes it through `ResourceCoordinator.reserve_segment(...)` to `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)`; `PathDispatcher` never calls a `Switch` primitive itself. For each switch in path order, `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` calls `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` with a `claim` built per the canonical `(path_id, source_switch_id, train_id, locked_state, route)` shape under **Switch Entity**, plus `manual_override` — `source_switch_id=None` (the `None` source marks the claim as a main-usage claim) and this switch's non-`None` `route` (the unordered `frozenset({entry_edge, exit_edge})` the path uses through it) — and immediately acquires any `switch.flank_protection` locks. A `flank_protection` entry is skipped when its `neighbour_switch_id` is itself a main-usage switch in the same acquisition request set (a route point this path traverses): that neighbour is already set and locked by its own main claim, so issuing an opposing flank claim on it would make the path self-conflict and `Switch.acquire_lock` would reject the route's own primary. This route-point exemption is the canonical home for the rule. Two switches in series on one route — for example a depot-throat pair where the path runs out of one switch straight onto the lead through the next — each appear in the other's `flank_protection` list, so this exemption is what lets a route through such a throat acquire its switches at all. A `flank_protection` entry is also skipped when two of this path's route points would lock the same `neighbour_switch_id` to opposite `safe_state` values — the switch-triangle case, where that neighbour sits at the apex with one route point on each of its two diverging legs (for example a depot-lead crossover apex whose legs reach the mainline switch on one side and the N-track switch on the other). No single position of that neighbour aims its branch away from both route points, so it cannot flank-protect the path. Locking it to either state would reject one route point's own primary. The two route points need not be acquired in the same call: in `best_effort` the switch-first probe truncates a path into separately-acquired segments, so the earlier route point may already be committed, with its flank claim recorded on the apex — as a future flank lock keyed `(path_id, earlier_route_point)` before the train reaches that route point, or as a `train_protection_claim` owned by this train once the train has entered it. The exemption therefore skips the apex whenever the opposite-state demand is already held there by this same path or train, not only when both route points appear in the current call. This same-path conflict exemption then leaves the apex protected only as the path's earlier route point already set it (and unlocked when both route points are in one call), rather than failing the segment — the path's own block reservations and route-point switch locks still keep a second train from intruding through it. Like the route-point exemption it is applied at acquisition time, where path membership — including route points committed by this path's earlier segments — is known. Each remaining (non-exempt) `flank_protection` entry `(neighbour_switch_id, blocking_state)` is acquired when `SwitchManager.acquire_locks_for_segment(...)` calls `Switch.acquire_lock(claim)` on the neighbour with `source_switch_id=switch.switch_id` (the originating main switch ID marks the claim as a flank-protection claim), `route=None` (a flank-protection claim carries no route, per the canonical claim shape), and `locked_state=safe_state`, where `safe_state` is the neighbour's state whose active branch does **not** point toward the primary switch's track — that is, the state that keeps the neighbour's diverging path aimed away, preventing a head-on approach. `safe_state` is computable from switch geometry: it is the neighbour state whose selected branch exit edge is not the edge facing the primary switch. In v1, because switches are binary, `safe_state` is simply the opposite of the neighbour's `blocking_state` recorded at placement time. Every acquired flank claim stores `source_switch_id = switch.switch_id` so later switch head-entry can convert only the flank claims sourced from that main switch into train-owned protection.
- Acquire blocks atomically through `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)`.
- Resource acquisition writes `Block.reserved_direction` through `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` for every reserved block. Tile-level traversal direction is carried by `resource_sequence[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge` and nested `path_tiles[*].entry_edge` / `exit_edge`; there is no `Tile.path_direction` field.
- Distinguish retained geometry, static allowed travel, and temporary reservation direction: v1 does not define a track-level `traffic_direction_policy` field. Static allowed travel direction lives on `Block.allowed_travel_directions`; `Block.reserved_direction` is only the dispatcher-owned temporary reservation direction for one path; and the path plan geometry carries the finer-grained per-tile traversal frame inside that block.
- On failure, roll back in reverse acquisition order, including switch locks and block reservations. There is no separate tile-level `path_direction` assignment to clear.
- That rollback releases the acquired claims/reservations only; it does not restore an earlier physical `Switch.position`. The live switch alignment remains at the last successfully written value until some later switch command or path acquisition changes it again.
- Completion and cancellation release blocks through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)`, not direct `block.release()` calls.
- In v1, block reservations remain single-train/single-path resources even though `Switch.locks` can have multiple compatible owners. `Block.reserved_direction` therefore has at most one active value per reserved block and must be cleared when that block reservation is released.
- `create_path(..., acquisition_mode: PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode)` and `extend_path(..., acquisition_mode: PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode)` must share the same `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode` enum, with two v1 behaviors:
  - `AcquisitionMode.all_or_nothing`: dispatcher acquires only the full requested authority or returns failure after full rollback
  - `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`: dispatcher may truncate to the furthest forward acquirable resource prefix, but the acquisition of that chosen prefix is still atomic
- In `best_effort` mode, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` must either create a block-terminated live path record or fail; a create never succeeds without appending. `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` carries no such rule, and appending nothing is not by itself a failure. Extend fails in exactly one situation: no legal forward walk from the live authority end to the caller's current requested target exists, which returns `success = False` with `failure_kind = no_viable_path` and leaves the existing path unchanged. (Caller-contract violations raise instead of returning a failure, per the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**.) Every other outcome is `success = True`, because the existing live path is still a legal prefix toward that target and the caller's job is to keep it. A success that appended nothing reports `reached_target = True` when `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` is already true, and `reached_target = False` when the path is still short of it; the caller branches on those two fields and never on why the tick produced no growth. The controller projection reflects any same-path growth on the next read.
- A head-on-switch acquisition remains a failure because v1 does not allow storing a head-on-switch live authority end.
- In all acquisition modes, the stored live authority end must be block-terminated. A same-tick acquisition result that reaches a switch but no following block is not a valid stored live authority in v1; dispatcher must roll that trailing switch/flank acquisition back before storing the path.

**Controller-mediated progress**

- `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` must compute the next `TrainSpan`, next `current_speed_kmh`, and the ordered tile-level delta for that movement, then call `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` at least once every tick. `TrainPhysics` does not call dispatcher progress, completion, or release APIs directly and does not own the stored span.
- `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integrates from the already-reconciled `controller.physics_sync.current_span` that Step 0's `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)` committed before Phase 1. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` then atomically writes each physics-integrated `span_update.new_span` and speed. It derives switch observations from that update's tile delta. Only the final callback for the train's tick folds the named `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` into block observations, appends those observations in the specified order, and clears both sets immediately; earlier boundary callbacks leave them untouched. On a stopped pass, `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` is the sole equivalent producer (see **Running and stopped**). Stop completion remains a separate later Phase-3 check.
- The controller progress report payload follows the canonical **ControllerProgressReport schema** defined in the **Dispatcher progress/completion flow** section. `current_head_sequence_index` is the `sequence_index` of the train head's current position in `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`, resolved by the **Head-mapping rule** in that schema, or `None` when no active resource-sequence mapping is available. When live authority exists and the controller cannot map the head to that active `resource_sequence` under the **Head-mapping rule** (no entry anywhere in the sequence matches), the report must set `deviation = true`, a `deviation_error` whose `origin` is `"out_of_sequence"`, and `current_head_sequence_index = None`; dispatcher treats that report as deviation input rather than as ordinary active-path progress. For a single report, dispatcher processing order is entered resources first, then deviation latching if `report.deviation == true`, then tail-vacated resources.
- Phase 3 is the only normal consumer of these reports. It drains the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) in order, calls `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` for each one, and clears the mailbox. Within that same drain, each route head-advance callback compares its single reported block advance with the current next waypoint; if that callback consumes a waypoint, the following report in the mailbox is what may discover and test the next waypoint. After local progress reports have been consumed, if an active path still exists for that train and `pending_path_cancel_request` is `None` (a pending cancel — deviation or explicit — outranks completion), `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` and, on success, that step releases the authority and hands the arrival to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, which records the finished stop (timestamps per **Time sampling**). `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` are consumed only after that normal arrival chance.
- The controller report carries `deviation_error` as a diagnostic for the dispatcher / route callback chain, but v1 route progression does not branch on it. Once the active path has been cancelled in Phase 3, the next eligible Phase 1 always returns to the ordinary covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` flow from the train's current physical position; already-covered pass-through handling and stop handling follow the normal task-kind rules.
- When physical movement crosses multiple resource boundaries within one simulation tick, `TrainPhysics` must fire multiple sequential `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` calls — one per resource boundary crossed, in physical traversal order — rather than batching them into a single multi-resource report. The per-boundary reports carry the switch observations (calculated traversal, this tick); the detector-confirmed block observations are folded into that tick's final no-boundary report, ordered by `sequence_index` when the entries map into the active `resource_sequence` — each such `sequence_index` resolved by the **Head-mapping rule** — and by physical entry order otherwise (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). Consumers must therefore tolerate the one-tick detector lag between block and switch observations. The monotonic `Path.current_resource_index` rule below absorbs the skew. `TrainPhysics` computes each incremental `span_update` up to the next boundary, calls `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` for that boundary, which appends that step's report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), then computes the next boundary update and repeats. The boundary `event_time` on each returned `ControllerProgressReport` is sampled by `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` itself via `time.time()` at the write site (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**); `TrainPhysics` does not pass a timestamp in. After the last boundary in that tick, or on a tick with no boundary crossing, `TrainPhysics` must still emit one final callback for that tick. When no resource boundary was crossed, that final update carries `added_tiles=[]` and `removed_tiles=[]`; when nothing changed physically, it may be an explicit no-change callback. This guarantees that Phase 3 always runs, that latches such as `pending_power_off` have a deterministic consume point, and that downstream side effects (lock consumption, tail-vacate release, route head-advance callbacks, switch state notifications) are later committed in the same physical order as the local progress reports. Within any single `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` call, `entered_resources` and `tail_vacated_resources` therefore typically carry one entry each at most; if a single call legitimately needs to carry more (for example simultaneous head entry and tail vacate at the same boundary), events within each list must still be ordered by physical traversal / vacate order, and dispatcher still processes the entered list before the vacated list.
- Entry ownership is resource-typed and dispatcher-mediated; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on `resource_type` — it routes every entry through `ResourceCoordinator.on_physical_entry(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, path_id, route)`:
  - on block entry, the coordinator routes to `BlockManager.occupy_blocks([block_id], train_id)`; this is the only normal runtime path that writes `Block.occupied_by`
  - on clean switch entry, the coordinator routes to `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(switch_id, train_id, path_id, route)`, which records `Switch.physical_occupants[route] = train_id`, consumes the entering train's compatible main switch lock, and converts applicable flank locks into train-owned protection. Two trains using non-conflicting routes coexist (per `switch.routes_conflict(...)`); the hard-failure case is an incoming `route` that conflicts with a route held by a different train, or the intrusion case where the entering train holds no compatible lock for its route (see the **Switch entity** intrusion check). A conflict must not overwrite the prior owner
- Dispatcher progress must update `Path.current_resource_index` using the reported path-step `sequence_index`, not by searching for the first matching raw `resource_id`. The producer-side companion is the **Head-mapping rule** in the **ControllerProgressReport schema**: because one `resource_id` may sit at more than one index, `TrainController` resolves each reported `sequence_index` against its own progress anchor, so a lapping path's second visit to a block reports the later index and advances progress instead of being swallowed by the monotonic rule in this bullet. `Path.current_resource_index` is head-based progress only; it advances when the train head enters a new resource and does not wait for the later tail-based release of earlier resources. Because switch observations (calculated, same tick) and block observations (detector-confirmed, one tick later) interleave with that skew, `current_resource_index` is monotonic: an entry whose `sequence_index` is at or behind the current value leaves it unchanged (`current_resource_index = max(current, reported sequence_index)`).
- A live path must remain `ACTIVE` after creation, through intermediate resources, and after entering the final block/resource. Entering the terminal resource must not change lifecycle state by itself.
- Only Phase 3 may finish a `stop` task against a live authority. After local progress reports have been consumed, if an active path still exists for that train and `pending_path_cancel_request` is `None` (a pending cancel outranks arrival), `SimulationController` calls `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)`. That check is the predicate defined in step `8df5` of the **Stop-arrival procedure**. If it is true, step `bc55` releases the authority and then records the finished stop (timestamps per **Time sampling**). Dispatcher runs no acceptance test of its own. The single canonical depart-acceptance signal is `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`, set by any canonical transition into that state listed in the Controller state machine table (either a successful `TrainController.depart()` call or the reactive speed-based evaluation observing motion under live authority). Manual driving input may command motion under live authority before Phase 1's `depart()` call lands; ATC still protects the train. The reactive transition into `RUNNING_MOVING` is treated as equally legitimate to depart-acceptance: `ato.running` setting (when `control_mode == "ato"` and `received_path_plan is not None`) and other readers all key on `state == RUNNING_MOVING` regardless of which transition fired. Head-entry route progress, including `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption through `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, is not state-gated. Explicit latched admin teardown such as `pending_path_cancel_request` and `pending_power_off` is consumed only after this normal arrival chance.
- A distance-0 ATC limit at its floor is not by itself stop completion. No-cab, no-authority, deviation, EB, obstruction/conflict, wrong-direction cab, and truncated best-effort authority all drive the distance-0 limit to zero without being stop completion; only `atc_limit_reason_code == "authority_stop"` (with the controller stopped and the other `8df5` conditions) is.
- For a stop waypoint whose live authority already reaches its requested target block and direction, a post-physics controller stop at the safety-margin-adjusted usable authority stop completes the path normally. The head may be short of the physical requested block when the safety margin/ATC boundary requires that; ATC remains the higher-priority rule and there is no "go back and try again" prompt for a within-block or before-block safety-margin stop.
- EB activation records `pending_path_cancel_request` per the **canonical EB activation effects** (when a live path exists), and that request is committed by the same train's Phase 3 consume pass through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` once the train is stopped. While EB is latched, the **Stop-arrival check** must fail; after EB release any route continuation uses a fresh path request.
- Controller-reported deviation produces a `cancel_path` event carrying the report's own `ErrorRecord` (`origin = "out_of_sequence"`) unchanged on the event's `error` field, with `deviation = true` and a `message` naming the deviation, per the **Cancellation** subsection. Cancellation wins over any same-tick completion attempt. Recovery follows the canonical recovery table. A `task_kind == "waypoint"` target the train has entered, passed, or later covers counts as served and does not produce a separate overshot-waypoint deviation.
- A best-effort truncated path must not complete just because the train has stopped at the current authority boundary while the current authorized target is still short of the requested target. In that case the same path remains `ACTIVE`, the controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` (stopped under zero ATC clearance against the still-live authority), and `RouteExecutor` keeps trying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same `path_id` until `RouteExecutor` releases the truncated authority under the **idle-hold maintenance and release** rule in Planning Kernel step `66aa` — either after the authority makes no progress for `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` consecutive Phase-1 ticks, or as soon as a strictly-higher-priority run is blocked on a block this run holds but has not yet physically entered — and re-plans after a cooldown so the held reservations/locks return to higher-priority runs.
- Completed paths must release only not-yet-entered block reservations through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)`, because reservations for already-entered blocks were consumed on head entry. That reservation release may force-clear terminal `CAUTION` only for matching still-reserved owned blocks, but it must not force-clear any physical `OCCUPIED` block early. On this `ACTIVE -> COMPLETED` transition, dispatcher follows the step `09b0` release order in the **Stop-arrival procedure**. The retained record may remain in `paths_by_id` for history, but physical release of a switch or block still under the train resolves from the resource itself. When a later tail-vacate resource event arrives through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, it clears `Switch.occupied_by` / block occupancy and any train-owned switch protection through the resource-specific release path. `RouteExecutor` records the arrival in step `bc55` from the `handle_path_completion(...)` callback that step `09b0` delivers after this transition, and does not re-check safety-margin projection itself.
- When creating or extending a path, the only stored update is `Path.resource_sequence` (plus `Path.path_id` on a create). `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` and `Path.reaches_target(block_id)` are derived reads of that list and update automatically. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads them live on the next access; there is no separate controller write step. A call that appends nothing therefore stores nothing, and the caller learns the outcome from `appended_resource_count` and `reached_target` in the result rather than from any refreshed field on the record.
- Drive resource release from tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches). `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` routes every vacate through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)` (the single `resource_type` branch lives inside the coordinator; `PathDispatcher` does not branch on it):
  - vacated blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)`
  - vacated switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`. The physical state is cleared first — the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry owned by this train is removed — and only then, when no `physical_occupants` entries remain for that train on this switch, the manager clears train-owned protection sourced from that switch via `Switch.release_train_protection_from(source_switch_id=switch_id, train_id=train_id)` on each protected neighbour. The physical-vacate handler never decides which lock to drop; the manager does, keyed by `(path_id, source_switch_id)`, so a switch that holds both a main lock and a flank lock for the same route releases the correct one (per the **Switch entity** tail-vacate contract)
- All normal block release goes through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays a single-manager state machine. Manual/admin reservation cleanup uses the same `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` entry point as routine completion/cancellation release.

**Path extension**

- The controller has no lookahead cache to invalidate (lookahead is computed live each read; see the lookahead section). Path updates therefore have no per-controller invalidation step.
- A change to `Block.allowed_travel_directions` (static config edit) does **not** invalidate any already-active path's reservations; the live path's `reserved_direction` was set at acquisition time and remains valid for the life of that path. The new direction policy takes effect only at the next `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` call. Operator/admin code must not edit `allowed_travel_directions` to indirectly cancel a live path; cancellation goes through `pending_path_cancel_request`.
- Truncated best-effort authority where the train physically stops at the temporary boundary while no resolved target is armed (the one-tick gap between task-completion and the next Phase 1 target arm) is permissible: the controller stays in `RUNNING_MOVING` against the still-live truncated authority with zero ATC clearance, and the next Phase 1 may extend authority once the next target resolves.
- Extension is forward-only. **`RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` only with the same `requested_destination_block_id` as the currently armed `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`, where `walk_position` is the index returned by the latest `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call for the live planning chain.** Extend identity is the target block alone. Any other target block is a route-side invariant violation and must be expressed via cancel-and-replan, never via extend with a different requested target block. `requested_destination_direction` is not part of that identity either: while the same target block stays armed, Phase 1 passes the stored `resolved_target` pair unchanged and must not resolve the direction again, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. Arming a new target block is a fresh resolve. When the live path already reaches that block, the **Already-reached success** below applies and nothing is appended; the frame that governs arrival stays the one the path acquired, which candidate search already filtered against the direction asked for at the time.
- When the path model switches to `resource_sequence`, append new tiles and new resources while skipping duplicated connection/start entries.
- Reject any extension that would make the path hold one resource twice at once, per the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants**. A resource the train has already passed and released — block or switch — is not held, so an extension may take authority into it again.
- **Already-reached success.** When `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` is already true there is nothing to append: return `success = True` with `appended_resource_count = 0` and `reached_target = True`, and leave `resource_sequence` unchanged. This covers both shapes — the target is the live authority end, and the target sits behind the authority end on the stored walk — because both mean the same thing to the caller: the authority already runs to that block, so no forward growth is owed. It is reached whenever Phase 1 arms a target block the live path already holds, which the **Waypoint lookahead** produces routinely: that step arms the next target while the current one is still unserved, so a later arm of the same block finds the authority already there.
- `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` must take the same argument contract as `create_path(...)`, except that it identifies the existing live path by `path_id`: `path_id`, `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, `acquisition_mode`, `manual_override`, and the optional `acquisition_cap_block_id` (mirroring `create_path(...)`'s full signature; `manual_override` flows through the acquisition chain exactly as for create, and `acquisition_cap_block_id` caps the appended suffix per the **Acquisition-cap rule**). The live path identified by `path_id` is the fixed already-acquired prefix; `extend_path(...)` grows that existing path forward from its current authority end toward the caller-supplied requested target for this extend attempt. Dispatcher does not run a separate shared-walk prefix-compatibility test. When the requested extension cannot continue forward from the existing live path without breaking the ordinary path rules, the outcome splits by the **viable-but-unavailable rule**: an extension that no legal forward walk can serve fails with `no_viable_path`, and an extension whose forward walk exists but whose next forward resource still carries a live claim returns the `0` / `False` retry result. Neither outcome changes the existing path's `resource_sequence`.
- The shared request contract between `extend_path(...)` and `create_path(...)` is the same, but `extend_path(...)` adds extra constraints: it requires a live `path_id`, and the newly appended suffix must continue forward from that live path's current authority-end edge in the same travel direction the existing prefix was built in. The caller-supplied `requested_destination_direction` applies only to the new terminal block `requested_destination_block_id`; it is not a license to reverse at the current authority end. A `requested_destination_direction` that would require the train to reverse 180 degrees at the current authority end (for example, the requested new terminal block lies behind the current authority-end edge on the existing prefix's travel frame, or reaching it would require exiting the authority-end block backward out of its current `exit_edge`) is invalid for `extend_path(...)` and must be rejected with `success = False`, `failure_kind = no_viable_path`, and a diagnostic `reason` such as `"extend would reverse at authority end"`: extension is forward-only, so no forward suffix reaches that target. The existing live path must remain unchanged in that rejection. Callers that actually need the train to turn back at the current terminal must cancel the existing path and issue a fresh `create_path(...)` from the train's current span instead.
- `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` requires a live `ACTIVE` path. A `path_id` that does not exist, or that references a path which is not currently `ACTIVE`, is a caller precondition violation and raises per the **`create_path()` / `extend_path()` return contract**. `RouteExecutor` passes the id it just read from `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`, so it cannot reach this case in normal operation.
- In `all_or_nothing` mode, `extend_path(...)` must either acquire the full requested suffix to `requested_destination_block_id` or leave the existing path unchanged, so a successful all-or-nothing extend always reports `reached_target = True` and `appended_resource_count > 0`. When the full suffix exists but cannot be acquired on this tick — for any of the unavailability causes listed in the **viable-but-unavailable rule** — the call returns `appended_resource_count = 0` with `reached_target = False` and the existing path is left intact for a later retry. When no legal suffix exists, it fails with `failure_kind = no_viable_path`.
- In `best_effort` mode, `extend_path(...)` must keep the same `path_id` and append the furthest forward acquirable suffix it can add to the current path. The `resource_sequence` therefore grows in place on the same live path instead of creating a new path. No outcome stores the caller's requested target on the `Path`; the record holds only the geometry actually acquired.
  - When at least one resource is appended, return `appended_resource_count > 0`, with `reached_target` reporting whether `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` is now true.
  - When no resource can be appended but the dispatcher can prove the existing path is still a legal prefix to the requested target, return `success=True` with `appended_resource_count = 0` and `reached_target = False`, leave `resource_sequence` unchanged (the derived `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` is therefore unchanged too), and let `RouteExecutor` retry later. Why the suffix could not grow does not change this outcome: every unavailability cause in the **viable-but-unavailable rule** lands here. The controller's `received_path_plan` property continues to return the same `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id` projection on its next read; refreshed requested-target fields remain dispatcher-internal.
  - When no legal forward suffix to the requested target exists at all, return `success=False` with `failure_kind = no_viable_path` and leave the existing path unchanged.
- The same three truncation-terminal rules — one terminal preference and two exclusions — that govern `create_path(...)` also govern the appended suffix, and the item-11 canonical rule owns each of them: the holding-point preference (a terminal choice among eligible prefixes), the reversal-trap exclusion, and the junction-fouling exclusion. Where item 11 fails a create because no legal terminal remains, `extend_path(...)` instead appends nothing and returns the `appended_resource_count = 0` / `reached_target = False` success, because the existing live path is still valid and the right caller behavior is to keep it and retry. Growth that would land the new terminal block on an excluded block is not committed; dispatcher truncates to the nearest legal terminal instead, and appends nothing when none is available.
- While the path is still truncated short of the caller's current requested target, the stored `resource_sequence` terminates on the current last acquired block; the derived `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` reflects that terminal automatically.
- Every successful `extend_path(...)` updates the dispatcher's `Path` record in place. A result with `appended_resource_count > 0` carries the grown `resource_sequence`; a result with `appended_resource_count = 0` carries the unchanged geometry and stores nothing. The controller's `received_path_plan` property reads any growth live on the next access. When the live authority finally reaches the requested target, `Path.reaches_target(requested_destination_block_id)` becomes true and the call reports `reached_target = True`.
- Extend outcomes are branchable without parsing `reason`. Every `success=True` result preserves a valid live path: `reached_target = True` means there is nothing left to extend, and `reached_target = False` means `RouteExecutor` keeps the path and retries later, whether or not the call appended anything. `success=False` with `failure_kind = no_viable_path` means no legal walk from the live authority end reaches the requested target, so waiting cannot clear it, and `RouteExecutor` must latch cancellation before a fresh create attempt.

**Cancellation**

- `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(path_id, error, kind, deviation=False, run_complete=False)` is the single canonical cancellation entry point in v1. `error` is the diagnostic `ErrorRecord` that flows directly into the `cancel_path` event's `error` field for logs/UI, and is `None` only on a `run_complete` release; nothing inside it may be used as a condition by planning or routing logic. Recovery classification uses only the two mutually-exclusive booleans: `deviation` for movement deviation and `run_complete` for normal run authority release. All other recovery branching is driven by controller state, runtime latches, current span coverage, and task-kind rules.
- Release owned blocks through `BlockManager.release_path_block_reservations(...)` instead of direct `block.release()` calls. Cancellation release clears future reservation ownership only; it never clears physical occupancy.
- Path cancellation or revocation effects are owned entirely by `PathDispatcher`, and the cancellation commit happens only in Phase 3. `RouteExecutor` or `TrainController` may request cancellation only by recording controller-owned Phase-3 handoff state such as `pending_path_cancel_request`; they must not call `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, clear authority, or release resources directly from Phase 1 or Phase 2. If Phase 1 route logic decides that the current live path must be revoked, it records or refreshes that cancel request and then stops planning for that train for that tick; later eligible Phase 1 passes follow the same latch-only rule while the incompatible live path still exists. A later eligible Phase 1 may resume ordinary create/extend planning only after three things have happened: the same train's later Phase 3 consumes that request, `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` completes, and the ordinary cancellation callback clears route-side live-path correlation. When Phase 3 commits the cancellation through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)`, dispatcher transitions `Path.status` to `CANCELLED`, which by definition makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None` on its next read.
- Power-off uses this same dispatcher cancellation workflow through the inclusive-latch fan-out: see the **Power-off procedure** for the canonical four-step sequence (`98d3` → `278a` → `534f` → `886a`).
- On `ACTIVE -> CANCELLED`, `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` follows the cancellation-specific order in step `09b0`: release future resources with `completed=False`, set status `CANCELLED`, then deliver only the route-side cancellation callback. Cancellation clears future reservation/authority only; physical occupancy and train-owned protection remain until tail-vacate. A same-train new path is allowed once the old path is non-active, subject to ordinary conflicts and controller recovery gates.
- Future switch/flank release for both `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` and `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` goes through `SwitchManager.release_path_locks(path_id)`. The manager scans the stored `Switch.locks` collections and removes every still-future claim whose stored `claim.path_id` matches, including both main and flank claims. It does not recompute flank neighbours from the switch's current persisted fields or infer releases from `Path.resource_sequence`; stored claim identity is authoritative even if topology or flank fields changed after acquisition. The release is idempotent and naturally no-ops for claims already consumed on physical entry. It must not clear `Switch.physical_occupants` or `Switch.train_protection_claims`; those remain physical/train-owned until later controller-reported switch tail-vacate.
- Reservation / physical state ownership in v1 must be read literally:
  - dispatcher-owned future reservation state: `Block.reserved_by_path`, `Block.reserved_for_train`, `Block.reserved_direction`, `Switch.locks`, flank-protection lock ownership for resources the train has not entered yet
  - block physical state owned by `BlockManager` and mutated only through `BlockManager` entry/tail-vacate APIs invoked by `PathDispatcher` on detector-confirmed observations (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection): `Block.occupied_by`, `Block.caution`
  - dispatcher-mediated switch physical state, written by `PathDispatcher` on controller-reported resource-entry / tail-vacate observations rather than directly by `TrainPhysics`: `Switch.physical_occupants` and `Switch.train_protection_claims` (the `Switch.occupied_by` read is a derived projection of `physical_occupants`, not a separately written store)
  - a block has at most one physical `occupied_by` train at a time. Switch occupancy is different: `Switch.physical_occupants` may contain multiple trains on mutually non-conflicting routes, and conflict is decided by `switch.routes_conflict(...)`, not by the single-value diagnostic `Switch.occupied_by` projection. A single train may hold multiple path records layered over physical ownership; path membership stays on dispatcher `Path` records.
  - cancellation / completion release clears only future block reservations and future switch locks that have not already been consumed by head entry
  - only physical entry / tail-vacate observations committed through `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` may clear or set `BlockManager`-owned block physical state or dispatcher-mediated switch physical state (block observations are detector-confirmed per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; switch observations are controller-reported traversal)
  - once a resource is physically occupied, later tail-vacate clears physical occupancy and train-owned protection rather than path-owned residue
- Cancellation commits only on a stopped train (the latch's `is_stopped()` gate), so the controller observes `received_path_plan` turn `None` at rest: the canonical lookahead becomes the no-authority empty payload and ATC holds the train there. When the cancellation was deviation-driven, the stop itself was forced earlier by the zero-limit / EB protocol in step `826a` of the **Controller tick order** (the `"deviation"` reason code while the path was still live). The operator clears EB through `eb.release()` once the cancel has committed and authority is cleared, which transitions the controller to `READY`. Physics keeps integrating live network state during the EB brake; a pending cancellation releases nothing ahead of the rolling train, and the committed cancellation does not unlock a physically occupied switch.
- After a `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` call that returned `committed = True`, the runtime must deliver the canonical cancellation context through `SimulationController.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, error, was_deviation=False, was_run_completion=False)` (which delegates to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)`) only after that call has finished its own lower-layer mutation for that train. A `committed = False` idempotent no-op delivers no callback at all. Dispatcher forwards `was_deviation = deviation` and `was_run_completion = run_complete`; validation rejects a call where both are true. Nothing inside `error` drives either branch. For ordinary cancellation/deviation, `RouteExecutor` records the recovery diagnostic and defers target selection until a later eligible Phase 1. For `was_run_completion == True`, the callback performs no `path_cancelled`, `path_cancel_was_deviation`, or `display_state` write: Phase 1 step `6a0d` owns successful run completion after it observes the stopped no-authority state. The whole `ErrorRecord`, when the cancellation carries one, remains on the mandatory `cancel_path` event and is stored nowhere else.
- **`cancel_path` event.** This is the canonical event surface for cancellation diagnostics. `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` emits one `cancel_path` event through `NotificationCenter` as the last step of the canonical cancellation flow, after the dispatcher mutation has committed and the route-side `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` callback has been invoked. Only a call that returned `committed = True` emits it; an idempotent no-op emits nothing, so a path is never announced as cancelled twice. UI/log consumers use this event as the canonical surface for cancellation diagnostics. The notification carries:
  - `notification_id` — assigned by `NotificationCenter` per the canonical notification rule
  - `event_time` — system-time Unix seconds; sampled by `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` via `time.time()` at the moment it emits this notification (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). The same sampled value is the one the writer also stores into `Path.last_status_updated_time` for the `ACTIVE -> CANCELLED` transition.
  - `source` — `"PathDispatcher"`
  - `operation` — `"cancel_path"`
  - `kind` — the level the writer declared on the `pending_path_cancel_request` payload, passed through unchanged. `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` derives nothing: it does not inspect `error`, `deviation`, or `run_complete` to pick a level. Because the whole most-recent same-tick payload wins, all fields describe the request actually committed.
  - `entities` — refs naming the train, the cancelled path, and the train's `RouteRun` when it has one
  - `message` — one line naming the train, the path this call cancelled, and what ended it in plain words: `deviation` when `deviation` is `true`, `run complete` when `run_complete` is `true`, and otherwise the plain-words form of the writer's `error.origin` (emergency brake, operator cancelled, power off, revoked). This is where a reader sees "deviation"; `error.origin` stays the machine label `"out_of_sequence"` and no code reads either one
  - `error` — the diagnostic `ErrorRecord` the writer committed, carrying its free-form `origin` (`"eb"`, `"operator_cancelled"`, `"power_off"`, `"out_of_sequence"`, `"revoked"`, or another writer-supplied label). `None` on a `run_complete` release, because a completed run had nothing go wrong, so the no-`ErrorRecord` rule for `kind = "info"` events in **Error events** holds here with no exception.
  - `payload`:
    - `deviation: bool` — `True` only for movement-derived deviation
    - `run_complete: bool` — `True` only for Phase 1 run step `6a0d`; mutually exclusive with `deviation`
**Path invariants**

- In v1 the dispatcher path lifecycle is exactly `ACTIVE` / `COMPLETED` / `CANCELLED` (see the **Path status state machine**): acquisition is synchronous inside `create_path()`, so a live path has the single state `ACTIVE`. Whether the train has started moving, entered the terminal resource, or is stopped at the stop point is represented by controller state, `current_resource_index`, and explicit completion predicates, not by separate path lifecycle values.
- `resource_sequence` replaces both `block_sequence` and `switch_positions`, so existing callers iterating those fields need updating.
- `current_resource_index` must track `sequence_index`, not the first occurrence of a raw resource ID.
- A path must not hold the same resource twice at once. Both `create_path()` and `extend_path()` must reject any candidate whose walk would put one block or switch into the path's live holdings a second time. This is the **repeated-resource rule**, and this bullet is its canonical home. For `extend_path(...)`, rejecting a candidate on this rule is an availability outcome rather than a viability failure whenever the claim that makes the resource held can still clear: the call returns the `0` / `False` retry result per the **viable-but-unavailable rule**, and fails with `no_viable_path` only when no legal walk to the requested target avoids the path's live holdings at all. The pathfinder's **own-length feasibility filter** removes the walks that could never satisfy this rule before acquisition is attempted, so the candidates reaching acquisition are the ones whose repeated resources are far enough apart for the train to clear them as it advances. Because a path may still take authority back into a resource the train has already passed and released, one `resource_id` may appear at more than one `sequence_index`; the **Head-mapping rule** in the **ControllerProgressReport schema** is what resolves the train head to the right occurrence.
- What counts as held is the live claim, not the traversal record. `Path.resource_sequence` keeps every resource the path has ever authorized, including ones the train has already rolled through. A block is held while `Block.reserved_by_path` names this path or while this path's train still occupies it; head entry consumes the reservation and tail vacate clears the occupancy (see `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()`), after which the block is free and any path, this one included, may acquire it again. A switch is held while a live claim on the switch names it: a `Switch.locks` claim whose stored `claim.path_id` is this path, or an entry in `Switch.physical_occupants` / `Switch.train_protection_claims` owned by this path's train. Held is computed from the claims stored on the resource; there is no path-owned hold flag, and a path only ever manages the claims it placed. Head entry consumes this path's main switch lock and converts its flank locks into train-owned protection (see `SwitchManager.on_physical_entry(...)`), and switch tail vacate removes the train's `physical_occupants` entry and clears that train-owned protection (see `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(...)`), after which the switch carries no claim from this path and any path, this one included, may acquire it again.
- Reading the rule against live holdings is what lets a `CIRCULAR` route close its lap. The route walk wraps back to the resources the run started from; by then the train has entered and vacated them, so no live claim from this path remains and taking authority into them again is an ordinary acquisition. A seam that falls on a switch closes the same way, since a vacated switch carries no claim either; where the stored authority may end is set separately by the block-terminated live authority rule under **Resource acquisition**.
- Switch resources contribute `0 cm` to forward-distance accumulation. Any helper that sums per-resource physical length must therefore add `0` for `resource_type == "switch"` and continue the cumulative axis across the surrounding block geometry.
- `FLANK_PROTECTION_DISTANCE_CM` must be a global config, not a per-path setting.
- In v1, switch locks are a multi-owner compatibility set: multiple paths may hold compatible same-state lock claims on the same switch, but conflicting required states are not allowed. Physical occupancy is likewise route-keyed and may be multi-train only for mutually non-conflicting routes.
- `PathDispatcher` does not run a behind-train release sweep; block release is fully owned by `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` triggered from physics.

## Route And RouteExecutor

**Route data**

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `route_id` | `str` | Unique route identifier |
| `name` | `str` | Display name |
| `tasks` | `List[RouteTask]` | Ordered route definition — see **RouteTask data** |
| `mode` | `RouteMode` | One of `ONE_OFF`, `CIRCULAR`, `ROUND_TRIP` |
| `assigned_train_ids` | `List[str]` | Stored list of train IDs assigned to the route. Canonical mutators are `Route.assign_train(train_id)` (appends if not already present) and `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` (removes if present); both are idempotent. The field is written from these canonical entry points only — see **Route assignment writers** below. |
| `is_active` | `bool` | Route execution enabled |
| `started_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp when activated |
| `created_at` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when the Route record was constructed. Sampled by `Route.__post_init__` via `time.time()` at the write site (see **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). Written exactly once at construction; never mutated by edit, assignment, activation, deactivation, or any other handler. |
| `last_edited_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp of the most recent route-definition edit. `None` for a freshly-constructed Route that has never been edited after creation. Written only by the canonical route-definition edit writers — see **Route edit timestamps** below. Not touched by `Route.assign_train` / `Route.unassign_train` (assignment) or `Route.activate` / `Route.deactivate` (lifecycle). |
| `priority` | `int` | Route-executor scheduling priority, `0..9` where `9` is highest and `0` is lowest (default `5`) |
| `acquisition_mode` | `PathDispatcher.AcquisitionMode` | Default route path-acquisition policy (default `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`) |
| `timing_basis` | `"operating" \| "realtime"` | Which clock this route's task durations measure against (default `"operating"`). `"operating"` measures them against `AppState.operating_time`, so an operator stop neither consumes a dwell nor makes a train late. `"realtime"` measures them against wall time, for a timetable run to a real clock. It governs `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` and `delay_class` together, and never governs `RouteTask.target_time`, which is an absolute wall instant under either basis (see **Task time semantics**). An edit takes effect on the next evaluation with no run teardown, because every `RouteTaskRun` row already stores both stamps. |
| `manual_override` | `bool` | Operator override for `manual` resources (default `False`). Set `True` only on operator-initiated routes (the Quick Route tool exposes the option when the operator explicitly chooses to use manual resources). `RouteExecutor` passes the assigned route's `manual_override` into every `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` call for that run, which forwards it through the acquisition chain and pathfinder per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` applies the same flag to its `manual` target filtering and to its `Block.allowed_travel_directions` direction filtering. Edits take effect on the next create/extend attempt with no run teardown. |
| `start_mode` | `RouteStartMode` | Default for which stop a fresh run begins at: `FIRST` (default), `NEAREST`, or `SPECIFIC`. Consulted once when Phase 1 allocates a fresh run; not a live override. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `start_task_index` | `Optional[int]` | Stop index used only when `start_mode == SPECIFIC` (the index into `Route.tasks` of the stop to begin at). `None` otherwise. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |

**Route assignment writers**

`Route.assigned_train_ids` is mutated only through these canonical writers:

- `Route.assign_train(train_id)` and `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` are the two helper methods; both are idempotent.
- **The unassign-while-running guard.** Every HTTP/UI/admin writer that calls `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` must first check `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`. When that returns a currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` whose `route_id` is the route being unassigned from, the writer calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` for that train before removing it from `assigned_train_ids`. `set_route_abort(...)` writes `pending_route_abort`, fans out to the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` when a live path exists, and, per the one-shot clearing rule in **Run start-mode seeding**, clears any stashed `RouteExecutor._pending_start_overrides[train_id]`. The train's later Phase 3 then tears the old run down through `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` in the ordinary way. When `execution_state_for(train_id)` returns `None` or a run against a different `route_id` (no run was ever allocated, or an earlier writer already tore it down), the unassign is a bare list removal with nothing to latch.
- HTTP/UI/admin entry points that ultimately call those helpers (existing v1 routes; their exact paths and payload shapes are unchanged from current code unless this document overrides them), each applying the unassign-while-running guard above to every `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` call it makes:
  - `POST /api/train-routes` accepts an `assigned_train_ids` list in its body and calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` for each, unassigning that train's previous route first per the single-assignment rule below.
  - `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` diffs the existing list against the requested list and calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` / `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` per change.
  - `PUT /api/trains/<train_id>/assign-route` calls `Route.assign_train(train_id)` and always calls `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on any previously assigned route first — a train is assigned to at most one route at a time (the single-assignment rule). This endpoint is a plain reassignment entry point, not the operator retargeting/route-swap handler defined in **Route Edit And Reconciliation** — that handler is `PUT /api/paths/<path_id>/extend` (see the **Availability flag REST API surface** endpoint table), which already calls `set_route_abort(...)` as part of its own atomic retarget flow and so needs no separate application of the guard above.
- Internal teardown writers that call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)`:
  - `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` (called by Phase 3 abort, power-off, and deactivate consumers). These already run only once the abort/power-off/deactivate latch has torn the run down, so the guard above does not apply to them.
  - `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` (called by Phase 1 terminal-walk completion). This unassigns the same train whose walk it just finished, atomically with the `FINISHED` write, so there is no `RUNNING` run left for the guard above to catch.
- Operator retargeting / route swap handlers also call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the old route and `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the new route as part of the atomic two-step retarget flow defined in **Route Edit And Reconciliation**; that flow's own `set_route_abort(...)` call satisfies the guard above.

`RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` (a derived read) stays in sync with `Route.assigned_train_ids` (stored) by construction: the only sites that flip a `RouteRun.current_route_status` out of `RUNNING` are also the sites that call `unassign_train(...)` on the matching `Route` — the unassign-while-running guard above is what makes this hold at the HTTP/UI/admin writers, not just the internal teardown writers. A train appears in at most one `Route.assigned_train_ids` at a time (the **single-assignment rule**): every assignment writer unassigns the train's previous route first — the retarget handler does it explicitly, and the assignment endpoints above do it unconditionally. Multi-assignment exists only as the teardown wait it was meant for. A new assignment writing `Route.assign_train(train_id)` creates a fresh `RUNNING` `RouteRun` only at the next eligible Phase 1 RouteRun (re)allocation step. So between the assign call and that allocation, the train shows in the new route's `assigned_train_ids` with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` (its old run, if any, is tearing down through its Phase-3 latches per the guard above). During that window the route is the train's **upcoming route**, and the assigned-with-no-`RUNNING`-run condition is the canonical signal Phase 1 step `9c4f` keys on to allocate a fresh run. A per-assignment start-mode input supplied with the assign call rides this window: it is stashed on `RouteExecutor._pending_start_overrides[train_id]` and consumed by that step `9c4f` allocation (see **Run start-mode seeding**). The handler writes that stash **after** it has applied the unassign-while-running guard, because `set_route_abort(...)` clears the stash as part of the one-shot rule; the stash write is the last step of the assign handler.

**Route edit timestamps**

`Route.created_at` is sampled exactly once via `time.time()` inside `Route.__post_init__`. No other writer touches it.

`Route.activate()` is the canonical route-activation entry point, symmetric with the `route.deactivate()` admin handler. It runs the **Route-definition validation** rules and rejects the call on failure; on success it writes `is_active = True` and samples `started_at` per **Time sampling**. It is idempotent on an already-active route, and it does not bump `last_edited_at` because activation edits no route-definition field. `PUT /api/routes/<route_id>/activate` is the entry point that calls it.

`Route.last_edited_at` is sampled via `time.time()` only by edit handlers that mutate the route definition. The set of fields whose mutation bumps `last_edited_at` is exactly: `name`, `tasks`, `mode`, `priority`, `acquisition_mode`, `manual_override`. Mutations to `is_active`, `started_at`, and `assigned_train_ids` do NOT bump `last_edited_at` — those are lifecycle / assignment events and are timestamped through `Route.started_at` (activation) and the per-train `RouteRun` registry respectively.

The canonical route-definition edit entry points are:

- `POST /api/train-routes` — when the new Route is created with at least one of the five edit fields supplied in the request body, the handler writes `last_edited_at = time.time()` once at the end of the handler before persisting. A bare create with no edit fields leaves `last_edited_at = None`.
- `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` — the handler writes `last_edited_at = time.time()` once at the end of the handler if and only if at least one of the five edit fields was actually written on this request. A request that touches only `assigned_train_ids` does not bump `last_edited_at`.

`Route.priority`, `Route.acquisition_mode`, and `Route.manual_override` edits bump `last_edited_at` even though they do not trigger the structural-edit teardown described in **Route Edit And Reconciliation**: `last_edited_at` records the time of the last definition write, not the time of the last structural change. Of these, `priority` and `acquisition_mode` are the v1 RouteRun override allowlist (`manual_override` is route-level only, not per-run overridable) — see **RouteRun overrides** for the per-field push semantics that distribute later edits to existing runs. UI/log consumers that need to distinguish structural from non-structural edits cannot do so from `last_edited_at` alone in v1; they observe the teardown side effects instead.

**Canonical ownership split**

- `Route` is the shared plan definition only: ordered tasks plus route-level policy such as mode and acquisition mode. It does not own per-train mutable execution cursors.
- A route may be assigned to multiple trains in v1, but each train may run at most one route task at a time. Each assigned train has at most one `RUNNING` `RouteRun` at a time; the live read is `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` (computed by searching the registry for the `RUNNING` `RouteRun`). Historical `RouteRun` rows for the same train (state `FINISHED` or `DEACTIVATED`) are kept indefinitely in `RouteExecutor._historical_runs` as a parallel history; only the active-state predicate is single-instance. The run's `route_id` identifies which shared `Route` definition that train is currently following. That `RouteRun` is the per-train runtime instance of the shared plan, and it owns only per-train mutable execution state: the task cursor, the current traversal direction, per-task timing and resolved-target state (`task_runs`), route-version reconciliation state, and the current route status. Shared route execution state across trains is not allowed.
- `Route` is live shared execution input in v1, not a snapshot source that is copied into each run. Active runs read the current shared `Route` through `RouteRun.route_id`, and later structural route edits immediately latch teardown of every assigned train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` (each committed in that train's later Phase 3 once the train has stopped, per the stopped-train gating in **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption**), followed by fresh `RouteRun` allocation; see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**.
- In helper prose below, when an active-run helper still shows a `route` parameter, read that as the current shared `Route` resolved from `RouteRun.route_id` unless the paragraph explicitly says a new dedicated route assignment is being created.
- Because multi-train assignment is supported, `RouteRun` is the only canonical runtime progress store. `Route` carries no runtime progress fields; consumers read per-train progress from each `RouteRun` (a route-level progress summary is out of scope — see the **Out of Scope** table).
- Per-train route/path errors are emitted as error events with `kind = "warning"` and `ErrorRecord.origin = "planning_failed"` per **Error events**; neither `Route` nor `RouteRun` keeps a stored error summary in v1.
- Departure is not a route-level policy in v1. Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly for every successful create/extend on the first eligible Phase 1 pass; the ATO-vs-manual behavioral difference is owned inside that call per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**. The driver in manual mode sees that depart has been accepted (state == `RUNNING_MOVING`) and starts moving the train; there is no separate operator-confirmation button.
- `acquisition_mode` at route level is the policy for how this route wants dispatcher authority to be acquired (`all_or_nothing` or `best_effort`), with default `AcquisitionMode.best_effort` when the route definition omits it. `RouteExecutor` reads `RouteRun.effective_acquisition_mode` (the override-aware read of the shared `Route.acquisition_mode`; see **RouteRun overrides**) for both `create_path(...)` and later `extend_path(...)` retries on the same planning chain.
- `priority` at route level is the canonical route-executor scheduling priority, with default `5`. Priority values are integers in `0..9`, where `9` is highest and `0` is lowest. `RouteExecutor` sorts runnable route runs by `RouteRun.effective_priority` (the override-aware read; see **RouteRun overrides**); later shared-route edits and per-run overrides may therefore legitimately reshuffle later tick order for active runs. Trains with no current `RouteRun` use effective priority `5` when `SimulationController` merges them into the per-tick runnable-train order.
- `ResolvedRouteTarget` carries exactly `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. In this structure, `requested_destination_direction` is always the block-relative direction for `requested_destination_block_id`. `Platform` has no direction field, no platform-frame direction, and no direction-conversion helper in v1. `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` resolution chooses a concrete platform-end block, then derives the resolved direction from `Block.ordered_tiles`, validates it against `Block.allowed_travel_directions` unless the run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`, and stores that block-relative result. There is no persistent per-task resolved-target cache outside `task_runs`: every route-side attempt that arms a new target block resolves a transient candidate live, from the current shared `Route`, current platform availability, train span, and task policy, via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`. While the same target block stays armed, Phase 1 reuses the stored `resolved_target` pair instead of resolving again, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. That helper is read-only and returns one candidate per call. Phase 1's authority-acquisition step is the only canonical writer of `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that candidate. For a `StationTarget` task, `_find_next_task_target` returns one candidate per call, and Phase 1 retries with successive candidates by re-invoking the helper after a failed `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` attempt within the same tick. A `PlatformTarget` or `BlockTarget` task has only one candidate per tick. If a path request succeeds, the path record's `requested_destination_block_id`, `requested_destination_direction`, and `authority_end_block_id` become the dispatcher-owned stored target contract for that successful live segment, while `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target` records the candidate actually accepted for that segment. Operator/admin edits to platform/block target-resolution inputs (for example platform block membership/order, `Block.disabled` / `Block.manual` toggles, `Platform.disabled` / `Platform.manual` / `Station.disabled` / `Station.manual` toggles, or `Block.allowed_travel_directions` changes) do not rewrite an already-successful live segment; they take effect the next time Phase 1 commits a new successful create/extend attempt.

**Canonical Next-Target Selection**

- **`ResolvedNextTaskProjection` schema.** The return type of `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=None, after_walk_position=None)` is `Optional[ResolvedNextTaskProjection]`. The optional `exclude` argument is a caller-owned set of already-attempted `ResolvedRouteTarget` values for the current planning problem; the helper itself stores no per-tick state. The optional `after_walk_position` argument returns the first non-finished walk-position strictly after that index on the directed walk, appending the next walk-position when the walk has not grown that far yet; it is how the **Waypoint lookahead** in step `66aa` reaches past the task the train is physically working on. `None` (the default) returns the next non-finished walk-position. `None` means no next task on the current directed walk (terminal). Otherwise the projection carries:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `walk_position` | `int` | Walk-position index in `RouteRun.task_runs` of the next non-finished walk-position on the directed walk. The underlying `RouteTask` is `Route.tasks[task_runs[walk_position].task_index]`. |
  | `task_kind` | `"waypoint" \| "stop" \| "dwell" \| "run"` | The task's kind, copied from the underlying `RouteTask.task_kind` for caller convenience. |
  | `direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | The route walk direction at this task (may have flipped at a `ROUND_TRIP` reflection). |
  | `resolved_target` | `Optional[ResolvedRouteTarget]` | The resolved block + direction for `waypoint`, `stop`, and `run` tasks (for `run`, only the direction seed). `None` for `dwell` tasks and for tasks whose target cannot be resolved in the current attempt. |
  | `failure_kind` | `Optional[TargetResolutionFailureKind]` | Why resolution produced no target; set exactly when the task cannot be served. `missing_target` when the `task_location` ID (block, platform, or station) is not present in the network — a **broken** task; this applies to every `task_kind`, including `dwell` (a dwell names a location too, and a dwell whose location is gone is broken exactly like its paired stop). `target_unavailable` when the target is present but cannot resolve to a usable block this tick (`disabled`, or `manual` under the run's `Route.manual_override = False`, or a `StationTarget`'s platforms are all filtered or held); never set for `dwell` tasks (they carry no dispatcher target to acquire). `None` for a `dwell` task whose `task_location` is present and whenever `resolved_target` is present. Drives the broken-vs-unreachable split in **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
  | `failure_reason` | `Optional[str]` | Human-readable diagnostic that accompanies `failure_kind` when `resolved_target is None` for a non-dwell task (e.g. `"Block <block_id> is set to manual"`, `"all platforms in station <station_id> are disabled or manual"`). Phase 1 carries it straight into the `ErrorRecord.text` of the error event it emits (see **Error events**) so the operator sees why the task is stalled instead of an empty diagnostic. `None` for dwell projections and for the same-tick exclude sentinel (all candidates already attempted), neither of which needs a resolver-level error string. It is the prose counterpart to the structured `failure_kind`; consumers branch on `failure_kind`, not on this string. |

  The helper is **read-only**: it does not write `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` and has no other side effect on `RouteRun` state.
