The canonical writer of `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` is Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task. All other callers (head-advance callback, dwell-complete step, UI projection) consume the helper's return value only.

  `TargetResolutionFailureKind` has v1 values `missing_target` (the `task_location` ID — block, platform, or station — is not present in the network; applies to all task kinds, including `dwell`) and `target_unavailable` (the target is present but cannot resolve to a usable block this tick: it is `disabled`, or `manual` under the run's `Route.manual_override = False`, or a `StationTarget`'s platforms are all filtered or held by other trains; never set for `dwell`). It classifies target *resolution* failures only; a target that resolves but whose authority cannot be acquired is classified by `PathAcquisitionFailureKind` instead and is always treated as unreachable, never broken. See **Unreachable and broken task handling**.

- **Index-driven, live-recompute model.** The "armed next task" is reflected by `current_waypoint_index` and `direction`, not by a separate flag. The "armed target" is whatever `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns when called, except that its `requested_destination_direction` half is resolved once per armed target block rather than on every call, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. Whenever any phase needs the current next-task projection — Phase 1's planning step, Phase 1's dwell-complete check, the head-advance callback's next-task pointer, or a UI projection — call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`. The helper computes fresh from current shared `Route` + current physical progress + current span coverage. It does not arm authority, does not write `current_route_status`, does not advance `current_waypoint_index`, does not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`, and does not call `PathDispatcher`.
- `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` is read-only with respect to `RouteRun` storage. It computes the walk direction for the projected walk-position — including the `ROUND_TRIP` reflection — and reports it in the projection, but writes nothing. Phase 1 commits that value to `RouteRun.direction` (see **Route-run state**, the `direction` row).
- For a station task with multiple compatible platform-end candidates on the current tick, `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=...)` picks one candidate per call. The helper itself does not maintain any per-tick state: the caller (Phase 1's per-tick retry loop) owns the `exclude` set of already-attempted resolved targets for this planning problem and passes it on each call. The helper enumerates the current candidate ordering, skips any candidate present in `exclude`, and returns the next untried one (or `None` when exhausted). For a `PlatformTarget` or `BlockTarget` task, only one candidate exists per tick. Callers other than Phase 1's authority-acquisition retry loop (the Phase 1 dwell-complete step and the Phase 3 head-advance callback) pass `exclude=None` and receive the first candidate under the canonical ordering; that candidate is deterministic for the same shared `Route` + physical-progress + platform-availability inputs.
- If `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns no next task on the current directed walk, the current walk is terminal: Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)`, which sets `current_route_status = FINISHED` (`RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` automatically returns `None`) and (for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` routes whose unassign left no assigned trains) calls `route.deactivate()`, which sets `Route.is_active = False` with nothing to latch. The `RouteRun` is preserved as a historical `FINISHED` row in the run history (`RouteExecutor._historical_runs`). Phase 1 then returns for that tick. By the time Phase 1 reaches this step, the matching live path has already been cleared by stop-completion in the prior Phase 3 pass, so no dispatcher cancel is needed. For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, `_find_next_task_target` never returns `None` (it wraps or reflects), so this terminal-walk branch never fires for those modes. When every task on a `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` walk is broken, the run does not finish — it holds in the no-reachable-task stall instead (see **Unreachable and broken task handling**).
- If a next task exists but no legal concrete platform/block target can be resolved under the task's targeting policy on this attempt, the helper returns a `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` whose `walk_position`, `task_kind`, and `direction` describe that walk-position and whose `resolved_target` is `None`. The helper returns overall `None` only for the terminal-walk condition (the `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` terminal case defined in the terminal-walk bullet above). If no live authority exists and Phase 1 has exhausted all same-tick candidates, Phase 1 stops for that train for that tick. If live authority exists, leave the live path and route status unchanged unless a later `extend_path(...)` result proves structural incompatibility.
- Callers must not rewrite `direction` or `task_runs[i].resolved_target` through raw increment/decrement index arithmetic or by indexing `Route.tasks` outside the helper. Next-task selection comes from `_find_next_task_target(...)`; committed progress advances through the head-advance callback recording `finished_at`, through Phase 1 dwell-complete step recording `finished_at`, and through Phase 1's covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks and covered stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks.
- `Route.mode` is consumed inside `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`:
  - `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`: if no next task exists, return `None` (terminal).
  - `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`: wrap from the last task to the first task.
  - `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`: reflect at the route ends and flip `direction` together with the next task.
- The canonical `ROUND_TRIP` truth table for a three-waypoint route `[A, B, C]` is:
  - `714a`: `(-1, forward) -> (0, forward)`
  - `a91b`: `(0, forward) -> (1, forward)`
  - `1f41`: `(1, forward) -> (2, forward)`
  - `c733`: `(2, forward) -> (1, reverse)`
  - `79a9`: `(1, reverse) -> (0, reverse)`
  - `7db6`: `(0, reverse) -> (1, forward)`

**RouteTask data**

Retained current-code enums used by route data:

- `RouteMode`: `ONE_OFF`, `CIRCULAR`, `ROUND_TRIP`

`RouteTask` is the unified route-task schema in v1. Every entry in `Route.tasks` is a `RouteTask`.

| Field | Type | Applies | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `task_kind` | `"waypoint" \| "stop" \| "dwell" \| "run"` | all | Required task behavior discriminator. `waypoint` is a pass-through traversal (no stop, no dwell). `stop` is a platform stop that is served by the **Stop-arrival check**. `dwell` is a hold at a location after a stop. `run` is a timed roll: the train departs and keeps moving for `task_time` simulated seconds, then the controller applies the canonical service-stop command and brings it to a smooth stop wherever it is (position-agnostic) — its `task_location` is only a direction seed, never an arrival target. |
| `task_location` | `BlockTarget \| PlatformTarget \| StationTarget` (tagged union) | all | The concrete target for this task. `BlockTarget(block_id: str)` for direct block targets, `PlatformTarget(platform_id: str)` for fixed concrete platforms, `StationTarget(station_id: str)` for logical stations resolved at runtime. The tagged union enforces that exactly one targeting mode is present. For a `run` task it names the block the train heads toward while rolling; the run ends on its timer, not on reaching it. |
| `task_time` | `Optional[float]` | all | Semantics depend on `task_kind`. For `waypoint`/`stop`: expected travel time in seconds from the previous task's location to this task's location; used to classify arrival as early/on-time/late when the `RouteTaskRun` timing data is available; omit when no timing classification is needed. For `run`: required finite duration greater than `0`, in seconds of **simulated** time, measured by `RouteTaskRun.run_elapsed_seconds` as accumulated per-tick `delta_seconds` while the run is the active task (not wall-clock). For `dwell`: how long in seconds to remain at the location; omit to allow departure immediately (equivalent to `dwell_time_sec = 0` in the old model). |
| `task_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse" \| "either"]` | all | Route-level block-relative traversal preference. `RouteExecutor` resolves `"either"` to a concrete direction before any dispatcher call; the dispatcher never receives `"either"`. Defaults to `"either"` when omitted. |
| `target_time` | `Optional[float]` | `waypoint`, `stop`, `dwell` | Optional absolute wall-clock Unix timestamp. For `waypoint` / `stop` it is the expected arrival time, informational (schedule-adherence / ETA classification) and gates nothing. For `dwell` it is the scheduled **departure** time: the dwell holds until it and departs at it, so an early arrival waits for the schedule and a late arrival departs on arrival (see **Task time semantics**). A `dwell` row carrying a `target_time` does not read its own `task_time`. It is read in wall time whatever the route's `timing_basis` is, because an absolute instant does not move when the session is stopped. A `run` task must omit `target_time`; its only clock is its simulated-time accumulator. |

Route-task authoring rules:

- A typical platform stop with a hold is authored as two consecutive tasks: `stop(task_location=PlatformTarget(platform_X), task_time=expected_travel_seconds)` followed by `dwell(task_location=PlatformTarget(platform_X), task_time=hold_seconds)`. The `stop` is served by the **Stop-arrival check**; the `dwell` holds the train in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` until the dwell timer elapses.
- A terminal stop with no hold is authored as a single `stop` task with no following `dwell` task (or with `task_time` omitted on the implied dwell). There is no `dwell_time_sec = 0` field equivalent; a missing `dwell` task is the correct encoding.
- A pass-through is a single `waypoint` task. It resolves to a concrete `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning, is completed by head entry into the resolved block, and does not invoke dwell logic or `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- `task_kind` is required. Missing, null, or unrecognized `task_kind` is a validation error at task creation time; `RouteExecutor` must never infer a default kind.
- A terminal task must not be `waypoint`. If the train must stop and reverse immediately with no dwell, the terminal task is `stop`. A `run` may be a terminal task: it brings the train to rest at its timed smooth stop.
- For target resolution and path acquisition, `waypoint` and `stop` tasks use the same resolution chain and the same dispatcher target contract. Both resolve to a `ResolvedRouteTarget` for authority planning. The behavioral difference: a `stop` arrival is decided by the **Stop-arrival check**, and the route's `RouteTaskRun` records `finished_at` at that arrival; a `waypoint` is completed by head entry with no stop, no dwell, and no `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`.
- A `dwell` task does not create or require new dispatcher authority. While a `dwell` task is the current active task, Phase 1 does not create or extend authority. The `dwell` task is complete when its `task_time` has elapsed since `task_runs[walk_position].started_at` (the moment `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` sampled, i.e. when the train began dwelling), at which point Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` sets `finished_at`, calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`, and leaves the next task to a later Phase 1.
- A `run` task keeps the train moving for `task_time` simulated seconds and then finishes at a smooth stop. While a `run` is the active task and `RouteTaskRun.run_elapsed_seconds < task_time`, Phase 1 step `6a0d` adds this tick's `delta_seconds` to that task-run field and acquires or extends authority toward the run's `task_location` exactly as for a `waypoint`, so the train keeps rolling; the run never completes on arrival at that block (it is only a direction seed). When the timer elapses, step `6a0d` sets the `ops.service_stop_active` latch through `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` (the controller-level operational deceleration command, never an emergency cut, and valid in both ATO and manual control), then writes `pending_path_cancel_request` with `run_complete = True` (with `error = None` and `kind = "info"`, per the `pending_path_cancel_request` payload contract) when live authority exists. The ordinary stopped-train cancel gate releases that authority without setting `RouteRun.path_cancelled` or projecting `display_state == "CANCELLED"`. Only after the train is stopped, the release request has been consumed, and `received_path_plan is None` does step `6a0d` consume the latch through `TrainController.consume_service_stop()` and call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` to set `finished_at` and advance the walk; it then returns so the next task (typically a `dwell`) starts on a later Phase 1. Where the train comes to rest is not part of the contract; a `run` that must hold after stopping is authored as a `run` followed by a `dwell`.
- Station/platform distinction is explicit:
  - `Station` is a logical grouping of platforms used by route planning and platform selection.
  - `Platform` is the concrete physical stop. When a waypoint's task_location is a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget`, dispatcher path requests, block IDs, and directions are always derived from one resolved concrete platform, never from a station group directly.
  - The current attempt may resolve one concrete `platform_id` when resolution goes through a platform. For a `BlockTarget` waypoint, no platform resolution is needed.
- `Platform.ordered_segments` is the canonical ordered list of segment IDs in the platform, used to identify the two platform-end segments (and, through the segment -> block reverse index, their owning blocks) and preserve edit stability. `Platform` carries no direction, no platform-local forward/reverse semantics, no per-segment alignment mapping, and no user-facing directional option. It is initialized from the segment order supplied to backend platform-creation/edit logic; backend validation must verify the supplied list is one connected switch-free walk in that exact order. Platform edits may only insert or remove linked segments at the ends while preserving relative order; middle insertions, reorders, or reversals are rejected. Removing a platform's last segment deletes the platform rather than leaving it empty, per the **Non-empty parent rule** under **Tile**.
- `Platform` carries no direction or platform-side helper. To make a platform serve only one direction, the user must configure the underlying end block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions` so only that traversal is allowed.
- `requested_destination_direction` is the resolved traversal constraint at the requested target. Task authors express the route-level preference as `forward`, `reverse`, or `either` on `RouteTask.task_direction`, but those values are block-relative preferences, not platform-relative ones. By the time `RouteExecutor` builds `ResolvedRouteTarget`, it must have resolved that preference into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for the chosen `requested_destination_block_id`. For a task that resolves through a concrete platform, the candidate comparison defined in the platform resolution subsection below evaluates terminal traversals through the platform end block(s). `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"` filters out candidates whose terminal traversal does not match that authored block-relative direction, or is not allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. `task_direction == "either"` applies no route-authored direction filter, but every candidate must still satisfy `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and `RouteExecutor` still stores the chosen block-relative concrete direction. When the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, `"forward"` / `"reverse"` are read directly in that block's immutable internal traversal order, and `"either"` means `RouteExecutor` picks whichever block-relative direction is reachable under the same current-span comparison and allowed by `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. When the run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`, `RouteExecutor` skips every `Block.allowed_travel_directions` check named above, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home; the route-authored `task_direction` filter still applies. The dispatcher never receives `"either"` in the v1 path contract; route resolution must always eliminate it before calling `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`. These are deliberately two distinct types: `RouteTask.task_direction` is the route-author input (may be `"either"`), and `ResolvedRouteTarget.requested_destination_direction` is the strict subtype `"forward" | "reverse"` passed to the dispatcher. Implementations must reflect this difference in their static types, so that handing an unresolved `RouteTask.task_direction` to `PathDispatcher.create_path()` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path()` is a type error rather than a runtime branch.
- Define the exact dispatcher terminal frame separately. The terminal frame is the explicit directed geometry used for controller braking and diagnostics: `path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` for the relevant terminal block. When a live path exists, dispatcher derives that frame from the chosen terminal `resource_sequence` block entry. Boundary/safety-margin projection must never infer that frame from `requested_destination_direction` alone.
- Resolve waypoint targets through one canonical chain: `station -> platform -> block`. If the task_location is a `PlatformTarget`, resolution starts at `platform`. If it is a `BlockTarget`, resolution skips station/platform lookup and targets that block directly.
- When a task_location is a `StationTarget` rather than a `PlatformTarget`, `RouteExecutor` must resolve one concrete platform before path creation. In v1, platform availability is evaluated relative to the train for this route attempt. A platform is unavailable when any linked block is held by another train's physical occupancy or another train/path's live reservation. `CAUTION` by itself does not make a platform unavailable: a caution block may still be reserved/acquired when ordinary reservation rules allow it, and the caution state is consumed by `TrainController` lookahead as a lower ATC speed cap. The current train's own physical occupancy or current live authority must not make that platform unavailable to itself; same-train coverage is handled by Phase 1's covered-task index advance and same-train path conflicts are handled by the shared pathfinder/acquisition rules. Deterministic tie-breaking is required and follows the canonical candidate-order rule below.
- Platform/station target selection in v1 is:
  - if the task_location is a `PlatformTarget`, resolve exactly that platform for this tick. Pick its single best compatible platform-end candidate by the canonical graph comparison. If that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than trying another platform or another platform end in the same Phase 1 pass.
  - if the task_location is a `StationTarget`, build the complete ordered transient candidate list of all compatible available platform-end candidates across every platform in `Station.platform_ids`, sorted by the canonical graph comparison defined in the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload** home (estimated travel time first when the run's train max speed is supplied, then `tile_count`, `walk_length_cm`, `candidate_rank`, final `end_a` dead-heat only across current-span end choices). In that same Phase 1 pass, `RouteExecutor` may try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair from that station candidate list at most once, stopping as soon as one create/extend attempt creates or grows authority.
  - if the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, resolve exactly that block/direction for this tick. If the create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, wait until the next eligible tick rather than inventing alternatives.
- A waypoint that wants "try any platform in this station" must use a `StationTarget`; a waypoint that uses a `PlatformTarget` targets that one concrete platform.
- Station candidate enumeration preserves the route-specified `requested_direction` literally as a block-relative traversal preference. If the waypoint specified `forward` or `reverse`, candidate platform ends are compatible only when their terminal traversal satisfies that direction under the candidate block's `Block.allowed_travel_directions`. If the waypoint specified `either` (or omitted the field, which is treated as `either`), any `Block.allowed_travel_directions`-compatible terminal traversal may be used. The `Block.allowed_travel_directions` half of that compatibility test is skipped when the run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home.
- Later retries follow the same route-side selection policy. A concrete platform retry keeps the same platform and recomputes its current best compatible end on the later tick. A station retry may re-enumerate all compatible available platform-end candidates for that station on the later tick. If no concrete platform/block candidate can be resolved for the retry under that policy, `RouteExecutor` fails target resolution for that retry and must not treat it as an `extend_path(...)`-side retarget problem.
- For covered normalization and subsequent task service on the current directed route walk, a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task with `task_kind == "waypoint"` counts as satisfied when the train covers any compatible concrete platform in that task's allowed platform set; a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task with `task_kind == "stop"` counts as satisfied only after the stop has been served by route-committed stop arrival and dwell exit. When either satisfaction rule applies, `RouteExecutor` uses the actually covered or served compatible block/platform only to decide satisfaction and to report diagnostics; it must not force resolution to some other platform, must not write a persistent served-platform choice for later route-control decisions, and must not depend on a persistent per-task cache to remember that choice later.
- For each concrete platform being considered, `RouteExecutor` must gather the terminal candidate block at each end of that platform by calling the same shared network-graph walk query that `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` uses, passing the run's `Route.manual_override` as the query's `manual_override` field. For a multi-segment platform, the candidate end blocks are the blocks owning the first and last segments of `Platform.ordered_segments` (through the segment -> block reverse index); for a platform whose segments all lie in one block, both platform ends collapse to that one block, so only it participates. If a cab is selected, only the current head-side start locator participates in this stop-planning comparison. If no cab is selected, both physical train ends may participate, and a switch-side end is first mapped inward to the occupied block at that end of the current span; if no such inward occupied block exists, that end is dropped from the comparison set. Each candidate graph-query result carries the terminal block-relative direction derived from the canonical block-direction helper through `Block.ordered_tiles`; that direction must pass the task direction filter, and must pass `Block.allowed_travel_directions` unless the run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`. For a `PlatformTarget` task, pick the shortest successful candidate using the canonical candidate comparison defined in **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload**, applied across the remaining current-span end choices. For a `StationTarget` task, keep the full sorted list of compatible successful platform-end candidates as transient same-Phase-1 retry state, and try each distinct resolved `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair at most once in that pass. The chosen or currently attempted block becomes `requested_destination_block_id`, and the chosen block-relative direction becomes `requested_destination_direction`. This is how task-authored `"either"` is converted into one concrete `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` tasks. If no candidate succeeds, fail the task attempt. The chosen/current candidate block is the dispatcher-facing target block for both `stop` and `waypoint` tasks that resolve through a `PlatformTarget` or `StationTarget` task_location.
- For a task-authored `task_direction == "forward"` or `"reverse"`, the same candidate comparison is used, but pairs whose resulting block-relative direction does not match the authored direction are dropped from the comparison before selection. For `task_direction == "either"`, no route-authored direction filter is applied and the shortest allowed candidate wins.
- After resolution, `RouteExecutor` derives the per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` (`requested_destination_block_id`, block-relative `requested_destination_direction`) for the current task and passes only that transient route-owned request contract into `PathDispatcher`. There is no platform-frame direction to convert. Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, after a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task, writes the `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)` pair that call returned into `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` — the dispatcher's return names the authority it actually holds, which is what later stop-completion lookup must match against, where `walk_position` is the index returned by the same Phase 1 pass's `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` projection. `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` itself never writes that field. Callback-time code may call the helper to recompute live without disturbing `task_runs[i].resolved_target`; the stored value remains whatever Phase 1's last successful create/extend wrote and is the authoritative match key for later stop-completion lookup. Later planning retries call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` again whenever Phase 1 needs a fresh resolve, and Phase 1's next successful create/extend overwrites the bookkeeping value.
- A task with `task_kind == "stop"` may therefore use a `task_location` that is:
  - a `PlatformTarget`, or
  - a `StationTarget`, in which case `RouteExecutor` resolves a concrete `platform_id` for the current attempt before path creation, or
  - a `BlockTarget`, in which case no platform resolution is required.
- Direction compatibility depends only on block-level `Block.allowed_travel_directions` (relaxed by the run's `Route.manual_override`, per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home) plus current availability — there is no `required_platform_side` or platform-side direction helper.
- Route arrival for tasks with `task_kind == "stop"` uses explicit stop-policy data:
  - the resolved destination block for the task attempt
  - the block-relative traversal direction for that attempt
- Every per-attempt `ResolvedRouteTarget` carries an explicit route-owned traversal-constraint field, `requested_destination_direction`. Before path creation, `RouteExecutor` must resolve the task-authored `task_direction` into one concrete block-relative `"forward"` or `"reverse"` for `requested_destination_block_id` and store that value on `ResolvedRouteTarget`. Dispatcher still derives the exact terminal frame from explicit terminal geometry, but it does so after route resolution has already eliminated `"either"` from the request contract.
- If the task_location is a `StationTarget` or `PlatformTarget`, `requested_destination_block_id` is resolved only after `RouteExecutor` has picked a concrete platform for that train's current attempt and then applied the canonical shorter-path platform-end selection rule above. If the task_location is a `BlockTarget`, that block becomes `requested_destination_block_id` directly, but a disabled block is never a valid direct target, and a manual block is a valid direct target only when the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True` (the forwarded override lets the dispatcher call accept it; with `manual_override = False` the resolution attempt fails). The same availability rule applies to platform/station resolution: `RouteExecutor` excludes any platform whose own `Platform.disabled` is `True` and every platform belonging to a station whose `Station.disabled` is `True`, and applies the same filter to `Platform.manual` / `Station.manual` unless the run's route carries `Route.manual_override = True`. See the **Platform And Station** availability flags and the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home. In all cases, the stored `requested_destination_direction` is the block-relative direction for that resolved block; there is no raw platform-level direction token in the route definition or resolved runtime state.
- Do not define a separate stop-window tolerance or route-authored stop-position field in v1. The controller safety margin is used by controller lookahead and ATO braking to shape the train's stop, but route completion does not manually verify a centimeter tolerance around any route value.
- For tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`, task arrival is not simply "the train is in the task block", but it is also not gated by an exact-stop tolerance check in v1. A stop task enters dwell only after its arrival is committed — either by the **Stop-arrival check** against the live authority, or, for the head-anchored covered-stop case (train stopped, no active path, head in the destination block), Phase 1's covered-task index advance. The stop's own `finished_at` is recorded at that arrival through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`; the stop and any following `dwell` are independent rows (see **Task time semantics**), and the route as a whole completes only after that `dwell` task's timer elapses (or at the stop itself when no `dwell` follows). `task_kind == "waypoint"` is reached by train-head entry / physical pass of its resolved block. Controller deviation cases produce a `cancel_path` event with `deviation = true` and one `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"out_of_sequence"` on the event payload.
- One shared helper, such as `RailwayNetwork.project_authority_boundary_delta(ordered_walk, head_locator, authority_end, safety_margin_cm) -> delta_cm`, is the single home for boundary projection. `ordered_walk` is the live authority's ordered directed walk, in the same order the controller reads for progress. `head_locator` is a `GraphLocator` carrying `tile`, `edge`, and `distance_cm` for the live train head. `authority_end` carries `block_id` and `terminal_frame: DirectedBlockFrame` (whose `path_tiles`, `entry_edge`, and `exit_edge` describe the directed terminal frame for the authority-end block). The helper measures along `ordered_walk`, forward from the head's resolved occurrence on that walk, and returns the signed centimeter separation between the live train head and the safety-margin-adjusted authority boundary: positive means the train head is past the safety-margin boundary, negative means it is still short of it, and zero is on-target. `ordered_walk` is a required input because a directed walk may enter the same resource twice — a run that laps through a block carries that block at two steps — so a locator on its own does not name one place on the walk, and a closed lap would otherwise have two separations to choose between. The head's occurrence is the one the **Head-mapping rule** resolves for the live path, so this projection and the controller's reported `current_head_sequence_index` always describe the same physical position. In v1 this helper is for braking/diagnostic projection only; it is not the authoritative path-completion gate, and `RouteExecutor` must not compare live train position against it itself.

**Task time semantics**

- Every stored route/task instant is a wall-clock Unix second. A duration measures against the clock the route's `timing_basis` names, and an absolute instant is read in wall time under either basis (see **Clocks** under the **Units contract**).
- `RouteTask.target_time` is the *expected* time for a task: expected arrival for `waypoint` / `stop`, and the scheduled departure for `dwell`. On `waypoint` / `stop` it is informational only and gates nothing.
- `RouteTask.should_depart(now_wall, now_basis, dwell_started_at_basis)` is evaluated only on a `dwell` row. `dwell_started_at_basis` is the dwell row's `started_at_operating` under the `"operating"` basis and its `started_at` under `"realtime"`:
  - with `target_time` set: depart when `now_wall >= target_time`. An early arrival waits for the scheduled departure; a train arriving after it departs on arrival. `task_time` is not read on such a row, because the schedule already names the moment.
  - with `target_time` absent and `task_time` set: depart when `now_basis - dwell_started_at_basis >= task_time`.
  - with neither set: depart immediately.
- **A late train is not held for being late.** The schedule names when the train leaves. A hold served on top of an already-passed departure only adds the delay it was meant to absorb to every task after it.
- A `dwell` that must always stand for a fixed time regardless of the clock is authored with `task_time` and no `target_time`.
- A `stop` task is independent of its neighbors. It is completed at route-committed stop arrival through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` (which writes the stop's `finished_at` and advances the derived route-progress cursor). A `stop`'s own `target_time` is its expected arrival time and is informational only — a bare `stop` with no following `dwell` never holds for a time; a time-gated platform hold must be authored as `stop` followed by a `dwell` carrying `task_time` and/or `target_time`. A following `dwell` task (when present) is a separate row with its own `started_at` / `finished_at` lifecycle. `should_depart(...)` is only evaluated against `dwell` rows.
- A `run` task's timer is not evaluated by `should_depart(...)`. Its window is the stored `RouteTaskRun.run_elapsed_seconds`, incremented by Phase 1 step `6a0d` from the playback-scaled `delta_seconds` while the run is active. When the window elapses, that step sets the `ops.service_stop_active` latch through `TrainController.begin_service_stop()`, waits for the stopped no-authority state, then consumes the latch through `TrainController.consume_service_stop()` and records the run's `finished_at` through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`; where it comes to rest is not part of the contract.

**Route-definition validation**

- A valid route definition must contain at least one non-`dwell` task (`waypoint`, `stop`, or `run`). A non-`CIRCULAR` route (`ONE_OFF` / `ROUND_TRIP`) must in addition contain at least one task that brings the train to rest — a `stop` or a `run` — and end in one (see the terminal rule below). Single-stop routes are valid (e.g. one-off reposition or single-destination delivery); the single stop is both the first and final stop. A `CIRCULAR` route never terminates, so it needs no stop at all: a waypoint-only `CIRCULAR` route runs the loop continuously.
- Consecutive stop tasks that resolve to the same concrete target are valid. Route validation must not reject or collapse them merely because their resolved `requested_destination_block_id` / `requested_destination_direction` pair is identical. At runtime the second stop is finished by Phase 1's covered-task index advance — the train is already stopped with its head in that destination block and no active path, so the covered-stop rule applies — and its following `dwell` row (when present) then runs the ordinary dwell-entry/dwell-exit steps.
- Every task must provide `task_kind` explicitly as `"waypoint"`, `"stop"`, `"dwell"`, or `"run"`. Missing `task_kind`, null `task_kind`, or any other value is a validation error at task creation/update time. A `run` additionally requires a finite `task_time > 0` and must omit `target_time`; route creation/update rejects a run that violates either rule.
- A route made only of `waypoint` tasks is invalid for `ONE_OFF` / `ROUND_TRIP` (they need a task that ends the run at rest). A waypoint-only `CIRCULAR` route is valid and runs the loop with no stop.
- A `CIRCULAR` route must contain at least two non-`dwell` tasks whose `task_location` values differ. A circular walk whose moves all name one location has nothing to travel between: when the walk wraps, the only way to serve the repeated task is authority back into the block the train's head already sits in, and `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` rejects a request whose destination is the train's current head block.
- `Route.tasks[0]` must not be a `dwell` task. A dwell is a hold after a preceding task at that location; with no predecessor on the initial walk there is nothing to hold after, so the route editor/API rejects a leading dwell at creation/update time.
- For a `ONE_OFF` / `ROUND_TRIP` route, the stored `Route.tasks` list must end in a `stop` or a `run` (optionally followed by a `dwell` at that location) before a `RouteRun` may be created, because those modes must have a defined place for the run to come to rest. The route editor/API must validate that terminal requirement before sending the route to route-run activation. A `CIRCULAR` route is exempt: it never terminates, so its authored list may end in any task (including a `waypoint`, or a `run` followed by a `dwell`) and there is nothing to "complete" at a terminal task. For the non-circular modes the terminal task sequence is part of the route: the route is not complete until any terminal `dwell` task's timer has elapsed, or the terminal `stop` / `run` has come to rest when no `dwell` follows it. A terminal `stop` with no following `dwell` is the canonical "stop and end immediately" shape; a terminal `stop` followed by a `dwell` with `task_time > 0` or `target_time` is the canonical "end after holding at the platform" shape. Both are valid.
- A bulb loop or other turnaround that returns to the same stop must be modeled explicitly in the task list (for example `A(stop) -> bulb(waypoint) -> dwell(A) -> A(stop)`). `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` does not infer that geometry from one terminal stop.
- Every `task_kind == "stop"` task must identify a valid stop target through `task_location`: a `BlockTarget`, a `PlatformTarget`, or a `StationTarget` that can be resolved to a platform at runtime.
- `Route.priority` must be an integer in `0..9` (`9` highest, `0` lowest); out-of-range values are a route-definition validation error rejected at activation time.
- `RouteExecutor` must reject or refuse to activate any route definition that violates these rules rather than trying to infer missing stop semantics at runtime.

**Unreachable and broken task handling**

This subsection is the canonical home for what Phase 1 does when the current task on the directed walk cannot be served on a tick. Every Phase 1 step that keys on this behavior refers back here by name. The handling depends on *why* the task could not be served, and the two causes have two different owning steps: a **broken** task is skipped by the target-lookup steps `c481` and `592a` (the two steps that call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`), and an **unreachable** task is waited on by the authority-acquisition step `66aa`. A broken task never reaches step `66aa`: it resolves to no target, so there is nothing for `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` to be called with. The two causes:

- **Broken task (missing target).** `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns a sentinel projection (`resolved_target is None`) with `failure_kind == missing_target`: the task's `task_location` names a `BlockTarget` / `PlatformTarget` / `StationTarget` whose ID is no longer present in the network — for example a `StationTarget` whose station was deleted when its last `Platform` was reassigned to another station (see the **Non-empty parent rule** under **Tile**). Waiting can never resolve a missing target, so a broken task is never waited on.
- **Unreachable task (target present, not usable now).** Either `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns a sentinel projection with `failure_kind == target_unavailable` (the target exists but is `disabled` / `manual`, or — for a `StationTarget` — every platform in `Station.platform_ids` is currently filtered or held by another train), or it returns a concrete `resolved_target` but `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` could not acquire any authority for it on this tick (a live `PathAcquisitionFailureKind`, either `blocked_before_target` or `no_viable_path`). In both cases the target still exists, so the condition may clear on a later tick. No `extend_path(...)` outcome is an unreachable-task wait case: while live authority exists the split is not consulted at all (see the bullet below), and an `extend_path(...)` failure on a live path is governed by the latch-cancellation rule in step `66aa`.
- **When each cause is decided.** The broken half is read straight off the projection `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns, so `failure_kind == missing_target` is known at lookup time and the step that made the lookup acts on it at once. The unreachable half is decided later: a `target_unavailable` sentinel is carried forward to step `66aa`, and an acquisition failure is known only after the canonical same-tick retry loop (the `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=...)` retry defined in **`create_path()` flow** step 2) has tried every distinct candidate for the current planning problem without creating or growing live authority, and no live authority exists for the task. Neither half is consulted while live authority for the task still exists — including every `extend_path(...)` success that did not reach the target, where the path remains a valid prefix — or while planning has not yet run.
- **Unreachable task → wait.** Step `66aa` leaves the task armed, emits one error event with `kind = "warning"` and `origin = "planning_failed"` naming the train and the walk-position (per **Error events**), writes no `finished_at`, and does not move the route-progress cursor. The next eligible Phase 1 tick reruns `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` against current conditions. This is the only behavior for an unreachable task; v1 has no per-route "skip instead of wait" option, and a `disabled` / `manual` target is held rather than skipped because it can be re-enabled.
- **Broken task → skip toward the next valid task.** Phase 1 does not wait on a broken task. A task is **valid** when its `task_location` ID is present in the network (whether or not that target is currently available); a broken task is the only non-valid kind. The skip applies to every `task_kind` — `waypoint`, `stop`, `dwell`, and `run` alike — and is performed by whichever lookup step saw the sentinel: step `c481` for the task it looks up at the head of the pass, step `592a` for a task the cursor moved onto during steps `6a0d` / `a5c6` / `3a37`. Each performs the skip inline and then re-runs its own `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call; neither hands a broken task on to a later step. The skipping step first checks whether the directed walk still contains any valid task: for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` it scans the remaining tasks from the current `task_index` onward; for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` it scans all of `Route.tasks`, because the walk cycles through them.
  - **If a valid task exists**, the skipping step skips the broken task: it sets `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = current_time` (advancing the derived `current_waypoint_index` and triggering `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` per the canonical append rule), and emits one error event with `kind = "warning"` and `origin = "task_skipped"` naming the train and the walk-position, with an `ErrorRecord.text` such as `task <walk_position> skipped: target <id> missing` (per **Error events**). Consecutive broken tasks are skipped this way until a valid task is reached, bounded at one full `len(Route.tasks)` scan per Phase 1 pass, a budget steps `c481` and `592a` share. The same pass then carries on from the skipping step with that valid task in hand — a skip at `c481` runs on into steps `6a0d`–`66aa`, a skip at `592a` runs on into `66aa` — so a broken task costs no tick of its own, and the pass plans the valid task (or waits on it if it is unreachable). A skip writes `finished_at`, so a skipped task is never retried.
  - **If no valid task exists** (every task on the directed walk is broken):
    - For `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`, skipping the remaining tasks leaves the directed walk terminal, so the next eligible Phase 1 sees `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` return `None` and calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal-walk rule; the run ends `FINISHED` having served nothing. Each broken-task skip already emits its own event, so `finish_route_run` needs no special-casing for this case.
    - For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, the walk never goes terminal, so the skipping step must not keep skipping and appending. It instead sets `RouteRun.no_reachable_task = True` (a direct write — not a latch), emits one error event with `kind = "warning"` and `origin = "run_stalled"` naming the train and the run on the `False -> True` transition (per **Error events**), and returns for that tick without arming authority. The train holds with no authority and sits idle, and the derived `display_state` projects `"STALLED"` (see **Route-run state**). The flag is cleared by the next Phase 1 pass whose step `c481` lookup finds any valid task on the walk — for example after the missing station/platform is recreated — at which point ordinary planning resumes. `no_reachable_task` is never set for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`.
- **`dwell` walk-positions.** A `dwell` walk-position is broken (and skipped) when its own `task_location` ID is missing from the network — the normal case alongside its paired stop, since the two share the location, so the same skip loop walks past both. The skip can only happen before dwell entry: a broken stop never reaches arrival, so its dwell is evaluated while still pending; once dwell entry has happened, the dwell-timer rules apply and this handling never interrupts an already-entered dwell. A dwell whose own location is present is valid and follows the ordinary dwell-entry rules.
- Terminal-walk handling is otherwise unchanged: `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returning `None` is the only thing that drives `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`, and for the reasons above that happens only for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`.

**Route-run state**

This is the canonical field set for `RouteRun` in v1. `RouteRun` storage is split by lifecycle state. Each train's currently-live run is held in `RouteExecutor.execution_states: Dict[str, RouteRun]`, one slot keyed by `train_id`. When a run reaches a terminal state, its `current_route_status` flips to `FINISHED` / `DEACTIVATED`, and the row moves into `RouteExecutor._historical_runs: List[RouteRun]`, which keeps every terminal run indefinitely so the live slot is free for that train's next run. A train may therefore have many historical `RouteRun` rows over time. The live invariant is that at most one `RouteRun` per `train_id` is live (its `current_route_status == RUNNING`) at a time. `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` is a derived read, not a raw slot read: it returns the train's live-slot run only when that run's `current_route_status == RUNNING`, and otherwise `None`. The parallel history accessor `RouteExecutor.history_for_train(train_id)` returns every `RouteRun` (any state: `RUNNING`, `FINISHED`, `DEACTIVATED`) for that train across all routes, ordered by `run_number` ascending. Return type: `List[RouteRun]`. The accessor unions `_historical_runs` with the train's live slot, selects only rows where `run.train_id == train_id`, and sorts by `run_number` ascending. The route-keyed counterpart `RouteExecutor.history_for_route(route_id)` unions `_historical_runs` with every live slot, selects rows where `run.route_id == route_id`, and returns them ordered by `(started_at, run_number)`. Throughout the rest of this section, the name `exec_state` is a short alias for the train's currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun` (that is, the result of `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`). When that read returns `None`, no helper that names `exec_state` runs.

`RouteExecutor.route_registry: Dict[str, Route]` is the registry of shared `Route` definitions keyed by `route_id` (not of runs); the run stores above (`execution_states`, `_historical_runs`) are separate instance fields. None of these are persisted across process restart (persistence is Out of Scope for v1 per the Out of Scope table).

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `run_number` | `int` | Composite run-instance number assigned by `RouteExecutor` when this `RouteRun` is created. `RouteExecutor` keeps two parallel per-route bookkeeping maps as instance fields: `_route_registry_index: Dict[str, int]` (append-only registration index per `route_id`, assigned in insertion order starting at `0`, never recycled or compacted on route removal) and `_run_count_for_route: Dict[str, int]` (1-based count of runs created for that `route_id`). `COPY_COUNT_BASE = 1000000` in v1, and `run_number = route_registry_index * COPY_COUNT_BASE + run_count_for_route`. The per-route counter increments on every new run for that `route_id`. A route that is removed and later re-added receives a new `route_registry_index`. `run_number` is assigned at `RouteRun` creation and is never recomputed. A run's `route_id` is established at run creation and never mutated, so `run_number` always identifies the same route registration. It is diagnostic-only and must not be parsed for current `route_id`. |
| `train_id` | `str` | The train this run belongs to. Stored on the run itself; the live run is keyed by `train_id` in `execution_states`, and historical runs carry their `train_id` on the row in `_historical_runs`. |
| `route_id` | `str` | The shared `Route` definition this run is executing. Established at run creation and never mutated, even if the shared route's content later changes. Operator retargeting and route swap do not rewrite `route_id`; they go through "abort current run + create new run" (see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**). |
| `started_at` | `float` | System-time Unix timestamp when this run instance started. Written exactly once at `RouteRun` creation as `current_time` and never mutated afterward. Used as the synthetic predecessor sentinel for `task_runs[0]` in any "elapsed since predecessor" computation. |
| `current_waypoint_index` | `Optional[int]` (read-only derived) | Physical-progress cursor: the largest walk-position index `i` in `task_runs` where `task_runs[i].finished_at is not None`, or `None` when no task has finished yet. Computed each access from `task_runs`; not a stored field; no writers — progress is recorded by `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, now)` (runtime), which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` as one step of advancing the walk position, and the cursor reflects that update on the next read. (At allocation, start-mode seeding may also pre-finish a skipped prefix; see **Run start-mode seeding**. Phase 1's broken-task skip finishes a broken walk-position by the same direct `finished_at` write; see **Unreachable and broken task handling**.) The walk-position order is the directed walk order (each `task_runs[i]` represents one position on the directed walk; new walk-positions are appended on completion of `task_runs[i-1]` per `Route.mode` — see `task_runs` row below). A route-committed stop arrival therefore advances this cursor when it calls `advance_walk_position(...)` for the stop; a following `dwell` row is advanced by its own later `advance_walk_position(...)` call. A run with start mode `FIRST` starts with no `task_runs[*].finished_at` set, so `current_waypoint_index` reads as `None` and the first planning target is `task_runs[0]`. With `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`, allocation pre-finishes the skipped prefix `[0, s)`, so `current_waypoint_index` reads `s - 1` and the first planning target is the seeded start position — see **Run start-mode seeding**. **Naming note:** this field indexes all task kinds (`waypoint`, `stop`, `dwell`, `run`), not only `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks. The name is historical. |
| `direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Effective traversal direction for the current directed route walk. Initialized to `"forward"`; reversible round-trip behavior comes from this field. Its one writer is Phase 1, at steps `592a` / `66aa`, which set it from the `direction` field of the `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` they consume. `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(train_id, ...)` computes the `ROUND_TRIP` reflection into that projection, so the flip reaches the field on the first Phase 1 pass that arms a task past the apex, whatever advanced the walk — a dwell-complete step, a covered-task advance, a terminal `run`, a Phase-3 head advance or stop arrival, or a broken-task skip. This is the one and only direction field on `RouteRun`; all route-walk code reads and writes this field as the literal `"forward"` / `"reverse"` string. |
| `task_runs` | `List[RouteTaskRun]` | Per-run directed-walk table. Each entry represents one walk-position. The list grows over the run's lifetime: at run creation it is initialized with `len(Route.tasks)` entries (one per task in the initial walk; entry at walk-position `i` has `task_index = i`). Start-mode seeding may pre-finish a prefix of these entries at allocation so the run begins at a later stop (see **Run start-mode seeding**). Each completed walk-position is recorded by the canonical advance helper `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, now)`, which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and then calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` per the canonical append rule below. The one walk-position that does not go through that helper is a broken one: Phase 1's broken-task skip writes its `finished_at` directly and appends per the same rule, because a task the train never travelled to has no arrival to grade a `delay_class` against (see **Unreachable and broken task handling**). For `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, the unfinished suffix length stays at `len(Route.tasks)` indefinitely; for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` no append happens, so the unfinished suffix shrinks to zero after the terminal task. Each entry owns per-walk-position `started_at` / `finished_at` timing, resolved target bookkeeping, and per-task error context — see **RouteTaskRun schema** below. The synthetic predecessor for `task_runs[0]` is `RouteRun.started_at`. |
| `path_cancelled` | `bool` | Latch: set by `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` when the matching live path is cancelled; consumed by Phase 1 route-deviation cleanup when `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true. See the Latch Table in the Core Runtime Loop section for the full lifetime rule. |
| `path_cancel_was_deviation` | `bool` | Diagnostic companion to `path_cancelled`, written together with it by the ordinary branch of `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` from the callback's structured `was_deviation` argument and cleared together with it by Phase 1 route-deviation cleanup. `True` only when the consumed request carried `deviation = True`; `False` for another ordinary authority cancellation that keeps the run alive. A `was_run_completion` callback writes neither field. Not a latch — it carries no gating predicate and no Latch-Table entry. Drives the `display_state == "DEVIATED"` vs `"CANCELLED"` split. Default `False`. |
| `no_reachable_task` | `bool` | Set by Phase 1 step `c481` (or by step `592a` when a mid-pass cursor move lands on a broken task) when every task on the current directed walk is **broken** (each task's `task_location` target ID is missing from the network) for a `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` run, so no authority can be planned and the train holds idle. Cleared by the next Phase 1 pass whose step `c481` lookup finds any valid (present-target) task on the walk. Not a latch — it has no gating predicate and no cross-phase handoff, so it carries no Latch-Table entry and no writer helper; Phase 1 maintains it by direct write. Drives the derived `display_state == "STALLED"` projection. Always `False` for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` runs, which finish instead. Default `False`. See **Unreachable and broken task handling**. |
| `current_route_status` | `RouteRunStatus` (`"RUNNING" \| "FINISHED" \| "DEACTIVATED"`) | Always-present scalar route-owned status. Allocated as `"RUNNING"`. It is read by exactly one consumer for control flow: the `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` active-run derived read keys on `current_route_status == RUNNING` to identify the train's currently-active run. Phase 1, Phase 2, Phase 3, and route-callback bodies never read it for control flow — they key on `received_path_plan`, the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table), controller cleanup latches, and current span coverage. See the write-contract table below for writers and triggers. The canonical home for resolved targets is `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`; `current_route_status` does not carry a parallel resolved-target collection or wrapper object. |
| `display_state` | `"RUNNING" \| "DEVIATED" \| "CANCELLED" \| "STALLED" \| "FINISHED" \| "DEACTIVATED"` (read-only derived) | Derived UI projection computed each access, in priority order: (1) when `current_route_status` is terminal, return it; (2) when `path_cancelled`, return `"DEVIATED"` if `path_cancel_was_deviation` else `"CANCELLED"`; (3) when `no_reachable_task`, return `"STALLED"`; (4) otherwise return `current_route_status` (`"RUNNING"`). Not stored; runtime branching never keys on it. |
| `overrides` | `Dict[str, Any]` | Sparse per-run overrides of a small allowlisted set of route-level operational fields. Keys are field names from the v1 override allowlist (`"priority"`, `"acquisition_mode"`); a key being absent means "use the shared `Route` value for that field." Default: empty dict. See **RouteRun overrides** below for the override allowlist, the effective-value read pattern, and the force-push / soft-push edit-distribution rules. |
| `overrides_updated_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix timestamp of the most recent `overrides` write on this run, bumped by `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(...)` and `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(...)` (sampled via `time.time()` at the write site per **Time sampling**). `None` until the first override write. Diagnostic only; no runtime branching reads it. |
| `start_mode` | `Optional[RouteStartMode]` | The resolved start mode this run was seeded with at allocation (`FIRST` / `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`). Written once by the start-mode seed during step `9c4f`; diagnostic only (surfaced read-only in the run-record payload). NOT a live override — start mode has no meaning after the run's starting walk-position is seeded. See **Run start-mode seeding**. |
| `start_walk_position` | `int` | The walk-position (list index in `task_runs`) this run was seeded to plan toward first — its first real movement target. `0` for start mode `FIRST`; otherwise the seed position `s` after start-mode seeding pre-finishes the prefix `[0, s)`, so `next_planning_walk_position == s` immediately after seeding. Written once by the start-mode seed during step `9c4f`. Read only by the derived `is_starting`. |
| `is_starting` | `bool` (read-only derived) | `True` while this run is still completing its first real movement target — `next_planning_walk_position <= start_walk_position` — and `False` once the train completes the task at `start_walk_position`. Monotonic: it flips to `False` exactly once and never back, because `next_planning_walk_position` (the first unfinished walk-position — the lowest `i` with `task_runs[i].finished_at is None`) only advances over the run, including across `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` wrap-around (`current_waypoint_index` is unsuitable here — it is `task_index` space and cycles on wrap). Read by the runnable-train ordering as the primary phase tier (running-phase runs ordered before starting-phase runs) — see the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**. A run re-allocated after a structural edit is seeded afresh, so it reads `is_starting == True` again until it completes its new first target. |
| `idle_hold_ticks` | `int` | Count of consecutive Phase-1 ticks on which this run held a truncated best-effort live authority (the run's armed target block fails `Path.reaches_target(...)`) that neither grew nor saw the head advance. Maintained by the Phase-1 authority-acquisition step (`66aa`) by direct write: incremented on each such no-progress tick, reset to `0` on any authority growth, head advance, full-authority acquisition, or when no live path is held. Not a latch — it has no gating predicate and no cross-phase handoff, so it carries no Latch-Table entry and no writer helper. Drives the timeout branch of the idle-hold release in step `66aa`; the contention-driven release branch in that same step does not depend on this counter and may fire while `idle_hold_ticks` is still below `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS`. Default `0`. |
| `release_cooldown_ticks` | `int` | Post-release acquisition-skip countdown. Set to `IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS` by step `66aa` when it latches an idle-hold release; while `> 0`, step `66aa` decrements it by one and skips authority acquisition for this run so the just-released reservations and switch locks stay free for higher-priority runs to claim, then resumes normal planning at `0`. Not a latch; Phase 1 maintains it by direct write. Default `0`. |

- Operator/manual reposition is modeled through the ordinary one-off `Route` + `RouteRun` flow, with no special run-level flag. Such a route is created with `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` and follows the ordinary route-definition validation rules below. A one-off reposition can be authored as a single-task route whose only `task_kind == "stop"` task is the destination; with a single stop, start-mode seeding (see **Run start-mode seeding**) resolves to that task and `current_waypoint_index` initializes to `None`, so `_find_next_task_target` resolves that stop as the first executable movement task. A destination block where the train is already stopped with its head in it (and no active path) is finished by the covered-stop rule in Phase 1's covered-task index advance (no dispatcher authority is created); any other destination goes through the general Phase 1 / `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` flow.

`ErrorRecord` is defined once in **Design Direction**, **Errors and diagnostic classification**. The run stores none: route and run errors are emitted as events per **Error events**, so the message, its time, its origin, and its level always travel together on one event.

**RouteRun overrides**

This subsection is the canonical home for `RouteRun.overrides`, the per-run effective-value read helpers, and the operator-driven push semantics that distribute later route edits to existing runs.

- **Override allowlist (v1).** Exactly two route-level fields may be overridden per run: `priority` and `acquisition_mode`. Every other route field — `mode`, `tasks`, `assigned_train_ids`, `is_active`, `created_at`, `last_edited_at`, `started_at`, `route_id`, `start_mode`, `start_task_index` — is shared and not overridable. (`start_mode` / `start_task_index` are not live overrides, but they do accept a one-shot per-assignment input consumed at allocation — see **Run start-mode seeding**.) Any attempt to write a key outside the allowlist is a validation error on the override-edit endpoint; storage rejects the request rather than silently ignoring the unknown key.
- **Storage shape.** `RouteRun.overrides` is a sparse `Dict[str, Any]` keyed by the allowlisted field name. A key being absent means "use the shared `Route` value for that field." A key being present means the value at that key is the effective per-run value, regardless of what the shared `Route` currently holds. The stored types match the route-level types exactly (`priority: int`, `acquisition_mode: AcquisitionMode`); validation runs at write time.
- **Effective-value read helpers.** `RouteRun` exposes one read helper per allowlisted field, each returning the per-run effective value: `RouteRun.effective_priority` and `RouteRun.effective_acquisition_mode`. Each `RouteRun` is constructed with a reference to its owning `RouteExecutor` (set once at the step-`9c4f` allocation; the run is registry-owned data, so this owner back-reference is the one allowed access path). Each helper resolves the current shared `Route` through it via `RouteExecutor.get_route(route_id)`, returns `self.overrides[<field>]` if that key is present, and otherwise returns the matching field from the shared `Route`. The helpers are read-only and recompute each access (no cached value), so later operator edits to either the run-level override or the shared `Route` take effect on the next read. Every Phase 1 / Phase 3 / RouteExecutor consumer that needs one of these two fields reads through the corresponding helper, not by reading `Route` or `overrides` directly. The runnable-train ordering at `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(...)` reads `effective_priority` per assigned run (and `RouteRun.is_starting` for the phase tier, per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**), and the create/extend acquisition reads `effective_acquisition_mode`.
- **Override write entry point.** Operator/admin writes to `RouteRun.overrides` route through one canonical handler: `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(train_id, field, value)` sets one allowlisted field on the train's currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun`. The handler validates the field name is in the allowlist and the value's type/range matches the shared route's validation rule for that field; on success it writes `overrides[field] = value` and bumps a single same-run audit timestamp `overrides_updated_at` for diagnostics. A symmetric `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)` removes one key from `overrides`, restoring the shared-route value for that field; clearing a key that is not present is a no-op success. Both helpers are no-op success when the train has no `RUNNING` `RouteRun`. Historical (`FINISHED` / `DEACTIVATED`) runs reject override edits — their `overrides` snapshot is frozen as part of the historical record.
- **Push semantics.** When the operator edits a shared `Route` field (through `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>`), the edit may carry a per-field push policy on each changed allowlisted field, encoded as the `push` parameter on the route-edit payload: `push = "force"`, `push = "soft"`, or `push` omitted (default). The route-edit handler applies one rule per changed allowlisted field, against every currently `RUNNING` `RouteRun` for that route:
  - **`force`** — overwrite any existing `RouteRun.overrides[field]` with the new shared value. Equivalent to calling `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)` on every `RUNNING` run for that route after writing the shared field. The shared route now holds the truth and no per-run override remains on this field.
  - **`soft`** — leave any existing `RouteRun.overrides[field]` intact; runs without an override for this field automatically pick up the new shared value through the effective-value helpers. No per-run write happens.
  - **omitted / default** — equivalent to `soft`; the shared edit lands on the route definition and runs without an override start using the new value on the next read, but no override is cleared.
  - Push policies are per-field; one route edit may carry `priority: {value: 7, push: "force"}` and `acquisition_mode: {value: "all_or_nothing", push: "soft"}` in the same request body. Non-allowlisted fields (e.g. `tasks`) ignore the `push` key — they have no per-run override to push to.
  - Push policies apply only to the route's shared field write; if the same request edits `tasks` or other non-allowlisted fields, those follow the existing **Route Edit And Reconciliation** structural-edit teardown rules unchanged.
- **Historical runs.** Push policies never touch `FINISHED` or `DEACTIVATED` runs. Their `overrides` snapshot is part of the historical row and remains as written when the run was active.
- **API surface.** `PATCH /api/route-runs/<run_number>/overrides` body `{field: value}` (single field) or `{overrides: {field: value, ...}}` (batched) calls `RouteExecutor.set_run_override(train_id, field, value)` per entry. `DELETE /api/route-runs/<run_number>/overrides/<field>` calls `RouteExecutor.clear_run_override(train_id, field)`. Both endpoints reject historical runs with HTTP 409. `PUT /api/train-routes/<route_id>` accepts per-field push policies under the existing edit body; the encoding is `{<field>: {value: ..., push: "force"|"soft"}}` for allowlisted fields and the bare `{<field>: value}` shape continues to work for non-allowlisted fields and for allowlisted fields when no per-field push policy is needed (defaults to `soft`).

`RouteRunStatus` is a scalar enum with exactly `RUNNING`, `FINISHED`, and `DEACTIVATED`; it is not a record with a nested `state` field. `RUNNING` covers ordinary execution, including dwell and unarmed time between legs. `FINISHED` means the directed task walk ended and Phase 1 called `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)`. `DEACTIVATED` means the run ended early through route deactivation, operator abort/retarget/swap, or power-off cleanup; all three teardown consumers call `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`. Both terminal values move the run into `_historical_runs`; Phase 1 does no further work on that row. The run records no separate teardown-cause field, so UI/log consumers use the canonical `cancel_path` and command-result events when they need that distinction.

`RouteRun.current_route_status` has this v1 write contract. Its three Phase-3 teardown consumers below — `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, `pending_route_deactivate` — each run their own teardown only after any inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` they wrote (with the `error.origin` named in the row, and written only when a live path existed at write time) has committed earlier in the same Phase 3 pass, per the Latch Table; none of the three branches on active-path emptiness — the `pending_path_cancel_request` consumer already owns that. Each row states only its own teardown:

| Writer | Trigger | `current_route_status` after write | Other required writes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Phase 1 (after a successful create/extend) (`a436`) | a run resolves a fresh next target via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and Phase 1's authority-acquisition step succeeds in `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that target | `RUNNING` | Phase 1 sets `direction` to the resolved direction and writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)`) to the candidate that the successful create/extend used. |
| `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` (`b0fb`) | the matching live path is cancelled or revoked | unchanged — `display_state` projection follows `path_cancelled` + `path_cancel_was_deviation` | set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` (see Latch Table) and `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation = was_deviation` (the callback's classification argument). The callback does not clear `task_runs[i].resolved_target`; whatever Phase 1's last successful create/extend wrote stays in place. For a non-terminal run the derived `display_state` then reads `"DEVIATED"` when `was_deviation` is true and `"CANCELLED"` otherwise. |
| Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step (`5fc7`) | `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` and `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true | unchanged — `display_state` projection follows `path_cancelled` + `path_cancel_was_deviation` | clear `path_cancelled` and `path_cancel_was_deviation` together; the derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` / `"CANCELLED"` clears with them; the next eligible Phase 1 plans normally |
| Phase 1 terminal-walk detect — direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` (`75fa`) | a terminal `stop` (and optional `dwell`) or terminal `run` has finished and a same-Phase-1 `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns `None` | `FINISHED` | call `Route.unassign_train(train_id)`; move the `RouteRun` into `_historical_runs` as the historical `FINISHED` row (the active-state predicate flips and `assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` returns `None`); if this `ONE_OFF` route now has no assigned trains, call `route.deactivate()` (its latch walk is empty); then return. No dispatcher cancel is needed because terminal stop completion or terminal run step `6a0d` has already cleared the live path. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_route_abort` consumer (`a87c`) | the `pending_route_abort` latch is consumed (operator/admin route-abort, retarget, or swap) | `DEACTIVATED` | The consumer calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`; the helper writes `DEACTIVATED` and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. Clear `pending_route_abort`. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_power_off` consumer (`b7d2`) | the `pending_power_off` latch is consumed (operator-issued `power_off_cmd()`) | `DEACTIVATED` | The consumer calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`; the helper writes `DEACTIVATED` and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. `TrainController.complete_power_off()` (transition controller `state` to `OFF`, clear cab and EB latch, call `controller.ato.stop()`, consume `pending_power_off`) follows the helper return. |
| Phase 3 — `pending_route_deactivate` consumer (`72e2`) | the `pending_route_deactivate` latch is consumed (operator/admin or `route.deactivate()` ran while this run had not yet finished) | `DEACTIVATED` | The consumer calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`; the helper writes `DEACTIVATED`, unassigns the train, and (when the controller is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. When the structural-edit teardown recorded a held re-assignment for this train AND this consumer owned the run teardown (its own `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` cleared a still-`RUNNING` run), the consumer re-applies it (`Route.assign_train(train_id)`) after that clear; if an earlier abort/power-off consumer (or a natural finish) had already ended the run, the consumer instead discards the held re-assignment, per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. Clear `pending_route_deactivate`. |

The teardown cause is not a write. It travels only as the `ErrorRecord.origin` the writer helper put on the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` — `"operator_cancelled"` for abort, `"power_off"` for power-off, `"revoked"` for deactivate, per the **Writer-helper rule** — and reaches consumers on the resulting `cancel_path` event. When no live path existed, those helpers write no inclusive cancel, so the teardown carries no cause and the helper's own latch-write event is the only record of it.

`RouteExecutor` owns these direct status writes and the latch/diagnostic writes that feed the derived `display_state` projection. Other entities such as `BlockManager`, `PathDispatcher`, and switch/resource callbacks may trigger the route callback that causes a status change, but they do not write `RouteRun.current_route_status` directly. The `display_state` priority order is defined once in its field row above. Two things matter for control flow: runtime branching never keys on `display_state`, and the active-run predicate is a separate derived read on `current_route_status == RUNNING`, the only runtime control-flow read of that scalar. The `"DEVIATED"` / `"CANCELLED"` and `"STALLED"` projections clear when their backing flags clear. UI/log consumers that need the precise originating kind subscribe to the canonical `cancel_path` event on `NotificationCenter`.

**RouteTaskRun schema**

`RouteTaskRun` is the per-run task-state entry. `RouteRun.task_runs` is a `List[RouteTaskRun]` parallel to `Route.tasks` — one entry per task index, allocated when the `RouteRun` is created and indexed by the same integer index as `Route.tasks`. `RouteTaskRun` is the canonical owner of per-task timing, resolved dispatcher target, and per-task error context.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `task_index` | `int` | Back-reference into `Route.tasks` for the underlying `RouteTask` this walk-position represents. With the growing-`task_runs` model, walk-position `i` (the index of this entry in `task_runs`) is no longer guaranteed to equal `task_index` — for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`, walk-positions and `task_index` decouple after the first wrap/reflection. Readers needing the underlying task definition use `Route.tasks[task_index]`. |
| `resolved_target` | `Optional[ResolvedRouteTarget]` | Block and direction that Phase 1's most recent successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for this walk-position returned. Written exclusively by Phase 1's authority-acquisition step on success; never written by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` itself, never written by Phase 3 head-advance callback and Phase-3 stop-arrival step, never cleared by any callback. The "what is the next target?" answer for live decisions is recomputed by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and consumed in place. `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the authoritative target record the **Stop-arrival check** compares the live authority terminal against, so for `task_kind == "stop"` the stored value reflects the platform/block the run is actually serving. `None` until Phase 1 successfully creates/extends authority for this walk-position at least once; `None` for `dwell` walk-positions (which carry no dispatcher target). |
| `started_at` | `Optional[float]` | For a `dwell` row this is a stored system-time Unix timestamp, sampled by `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` at the moment the train begins dwelling, and `None` before that. That single sample is its only writer, so the dwell timer measures time standing at the platform rather than time since the previous task finished. For every other row it is a read-only derived value with no writers: for walk-position `i == 0` it equals `RouteRun.started_at`, otherwise `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` (the directly-preceding walk-position's finish), or `None` if that predecessor has not finished yet. Walk-position order is the directed walk order, so `i - 1` is always the directed-walk predecessor regardless of `Route.mode`. Consumers read this field directly for display and for an absolute-instant comparison; a duration reads `started_at_operating` instead when the route's `timing_basis` is `"operating"`. |
