| `finished_at` | `Optional[float]` | System-time Unix seconds when this walk-position ended. `waypoint`: head-entry consumption. `stop`: route-committed stop arrival or the head-anchored covered-stop exception. `dwell`: dwell timer satisfied. `run`: Phase 1 step `6a0d` after its simulated-time window, service stop, and authority release. Successful service writes route through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`. Two explicit exceptions write directly: allocation-time start-mode seeding sets skipped-prefix timestamps to `RouteRun.started_at` without append, and Phase 1 broken-task skip writes the missing-target row and appends; both leave `delay_class = None`. No other writer exists. |
| `started_at_operating` | `Optional[float]` | The `AppState.operating_time` reading at the same moment `started_at` was sampled or derived, carried so a duration can be measured without the operator's stops counting toward it. It is written and derived exactly as `started_at` is, at the same sites, and is `None` whenever `started_at` is. It is never persisted and never displayed (see **Clocks**). |
| `finished_at_operating` | `Optional[float]` | The `AppState.operating_time` reading at the same moment `finished_at` was written, on the same terms as `started_at_operating`. |
| `run_elapsed_seconds` | `float` | Stored simulated-time accumulator used only when the underlying task has `task_kind == "run"`; initialized to `0.0` for every task-run row. Phase 1 run step `6a0d` is its only writer and adds the non-negative playback-scaled `delta_seconds`, capped at the run task's required `task_time`. Head-entry, coverage, dispatcher callbacks, wall-clock reads, and replay never write it. It remains on the historical row after completion for diagnostics. |
| `delay_class` | `Optional["early" \| "on_time" \| "late"]` | Timing classification for `waypoint` and `stop` rows. It is stored run history, not an error classification: the **Errors and diagnostic classification** rules and the `ErrorRecord` shape do not apply to it, and no runtime code branches on it. Its readers are the run panel and the event-log viewer, which display the served walk-position's timing. Computed when `finished_at` is recorded by comparing their expected-travel `Route.tasks[task_index].task_time` with the actual elapsed time since the predecessor walk-position's finish (use `RouteRun.started_at` for walk-position `0`). That elapsed figure is measured against the clock the route's `timing_basis` names — the `finished_at_operating` pair under `"operating"`, the `finished_at` pair under `"realtime"` — so one route classifies its lateness on the same basis its dwells hold on. The comparison uses `DELAY_CLASS_ON_TIME_TOLERANCE_SECONDS`: elapsed below `task_time - tol` is `early`, above `task_time + tol` is `late`, otherwise `on_time`. `None` when expected-travel `task_time` is absent or the row has not finished. Never computed for `dwell` or `run`: dwell time is a hold duration, and run time is a simulated-time movement window tracked separately by `run_elapsed_seconds`. A row pre-finished by start-mode seeding or skipped as broken also keeps `delay_class = None`. |

`ResolvedRouteTarget` is the resolved dispatcher request contract for one task attempt: `requested_destination_block_id: str` and `requested_destination_direction: "forward" | "reverse"`. It supersedes `ResolvedWaypointTarget`.

The "armed next task" is reflected by the directed-walk projection, not by a separate flag: it is the next `task_runs[i]` returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` for the current directed walk. There is no `_arm_next_target(...)` step. `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the historical record of what Phase 1's last successful create/extend used for that task, not a gate that says "this task is armed."

For predecessor timestamp lookup (used by `delay_class` and any other "elapsed since predecessor" computation on a non-`dwell` row): the predecessor is the directly-preceding walk-position. For walk-position `0` on a newly created `RouteRun`, the predecessor is the synthetic route-start sentinel `RouteRun.started_at`. The derived `task_runs[i].started_at` returns `RouteRun.started_at` for `i == 0` and `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` otherwise, **except on a `dwell` row, where `started_at` is the stored sample `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` wrote (per the `RouteTaskRun` schema) and is not derived from the predecessor at all** — the dwell timer therefore reads the dwell row's own stored `started_at`, not its predecessor's `finished_at`. That predecessor `finished_at` always exists by the time walk-position `i` becomes active, because tasks finish in walk-position order and each new walk-position is appended only after its predecessor's `finished_at` is written.

**`RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, now)`** is the canonical helper that advances one successfully served walk-position to completion during runtime. In one call it (1) writes `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at = now`, (2) computes `task_runs[walk_position].delay_class` per the **RouteTaskRun schema** (which leaves it `None` for `dwell` and `run` walk-positions and for any position whose shared task carries no expected-travel `task_time`), and (3) calls `RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()` to grow `task_runs`. The timestamp write is one step inside advancing — it is not itself "the advance" and does not separately trigger anything. Successful progress sites route through this helper: the head-advance callback, the Phase-3 stop-arrival step, the Phase 1 dwell-complete step, Phase 1 run step `6a0d` after stopped authority release, and the Phase 1 covered-task index advance. Two explicit exceptions write `finished_at` without this helper: allocation-time start-mode seeding and Phase 1 broken-task skip; both leave `delay_class = None`, and only the broken-task skip appends the next walk-position. No other writer exists. The derived `current_waypoint_index` and each successor's derived `started_at` reflect the new `finished_at` on their next read.

**`RouteRun._append_next_walk_task()`** is the internal append step that `advance_walk_position(...)` calls after the timestamp / `delay_class` writes commit. It selects the next `task_index` to append per `Route.mode`:

- `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`: do not append. After the terminal task finishes, `task_runs` stops growing.
- `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`: append a new `RouteTaskRun` whose `task_index` is the next-in-cycle index (i.e. `(task_runs[i].task_index + 1) mod len(Route.tasks)`).
- `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP`: append a new `RouteTaskRun` whose `task_index` follows the canonical ROUND_TRIP truth table: forward direction past the last task reflects to the second-to-last task with reverse direction; reverse direction past the first task reflects to the second task with forward direction. The appended walk-position's direction is the reflected `direction` that `_find_next_task_target(...)` returns in its projection and that Phase 1's caller commits to `RouteRun.direction` (per the **`current_route_status` write contract**); `_find_next_task_target(...)` performs no `RouteRun` write. The appended `RouteTaskRun` itself carries no `direction` field — direction lives on the `RouteRun`.

The appended `RouteTaskRun` initializes with `finished_at = None`, `resolved_target = None`, `delay_class = None`. Its `started_at` reads `task_runs[i - 1].finished_at` on a non-`dwell` row, and stays `None` on a `dwell` row until `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` samples it (the value just written by the caller). The helper never re-reads or mutates older walk-positions.

- `RouteRun` is the canonical per-train runtime object for one assigned route. Current-code implementation may still name the class `RouteExecutionState`, but every changed contract in this section refers to that object as the train's `RouteRun`.
- `RouteRun` carries `current_waypoint_index`, `direction`, and `task_runs`.
- `RouteRun.current_route_status` is route-owned temporary per-run status, not a shared route-definition cache. `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` reads this object. `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` also reads it. Both identify the current task by index; whenever they need a fresh `ResolvedRouteTarget`, they call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
- `task_runs[i].resolved_target` is the historical record of Phase 1's last successful create/extend for task `i`. The only writer is Phase 1's authority-acquisition step, on a successful `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that task. `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` is read-only; head-advance, dwell-complete, and UI consumers consume the helper's return value and do not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target`. No callback ever clears it; it is overwritten only by the next successful create/extend that targets the same task.
- `RouteExecutor` owns a scheduling registry over these `RouteRun` entries. On each Phase 1 tick it iterates runnable runs by run phase then descending effective priority (running-phase runs before starting-phase runs per `RouteRun.is_starting`), per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**, rather than arbitrary per-train order. Among equal phase tier and priority, stable existing iteration order is acceptable in v1.
- `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` is the route-owned removal entry point used by Phase 3 abort, power-off, and deactivate consumers (per the **`current_route_status` write contract**). All three Phase-3 latch consumers call it with the same single argument; the originating cause is recorded only on the `cancel_path` and command-result events, not on any helper parameter or any field on the `RouteRun`. Natural terminal completion uses Phase 1's direct `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` call instead and writes `FINISHED`. When `remove_train_route_run(...)` is invoked, it first looks up the train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` via `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`. If no `RUNNING` run exists (e.g. a powered-off train without a route, or a train whose run was already torn down by an earlier consumer in the same Phase 3 pass), the call is a no-op success and returns immediately. Otherwise it performs the active-run teardown sequence:
  - if a live dispatcher path still exists for this train (`active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` is set), the caller must already have cancelled it through `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` before invoking this method (the canonical caller is the same Phase-3 cleanup pass that already runs the inclusive-cancel step, so the latch-cancel-then-callback flow handles this naturally),
  - if the controller is currently in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` (the `RouteRun` is dwelling), the method calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` to exit dwell into the **`READY` state** before clearing route-run state,
  - it calls `TrainController.consume_service_stop()` to clear the `ops.service_stop_active` latch, so an interrupted timed `run` cannot leave service braking armed on the train's next route. The accessor is a no-op when the latch is not set, so this step runs unconditionally,
  - it calls `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the run's `route_id` (idempotent if the train has already been unassigned by an earlier handler step) and flips the run's active-state predicate by setting `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED`, so the derived `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` read no longer returns it. (A structural-edit teardown re-assigns the train to the edited route *after* this unassign: the Phase-3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer applies its held `_pending_reassign` entry once its own clear is done, and only when it owned the teardown — see the **Shared-route edit teardown**. The abort and power-off consumers record no held re-assignment, and if one of them tore the run down first the deactivate consumer discards the held entry, so the train stays unassigned.) Historical rows from finish/deactivate that already exist in `_historical_runs` are NOT touched. The torn-down `RouteRun` is moved from the live `execution_states` slot into `_historical_runs`; only the active-state predicate flips, and the run remains as a historical row (visible to `RouteExecutor.history_for_train(train_id)`).

  An explicit operator/admin "abort route" request — including operator retargeting and explicit route swap — is delivered through the controller-owned Phase-3 latch `pending_route_abort` per the inclusive-latch rule defined in the **Latch Table** preamble. That same Phase 3 cleanup calls `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` only when the abort latch is consumed after the dispatcher cancel step (or the dispatcher-cancel-skipped no-active-path branch) has run. Aborting a dwelling train therefore takes that same path: Phase 3 cancels any live path (or skips the dispatcher cancel when none exists), then `remove_train_route_run(...)` exits dwell via `finish_dwell()` and flips the run's active-state predicate.

- Do not add a synchronous `RouteExecutor.continue_route_run_after_eb(...)` hook. EB release is handled through latches: the EB activation has already written `pending_path_cancel_request` with `error.origin = "eb"`, which Phase 3 commits and which sets `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` via the cancellation callback. The same train's later Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step consumes `path_cancelled` once the controller has reached `READY` with no live path. The next `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` tick reruns the ordinary planning kernel from the current physical position: covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks, `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to resolve the next task, and a brand-new `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`. This flow does not reuse, complete, or preserve the cancelled pre-EB path chain.
- The derived `current_waypoint_index` reads `None` when no task has finished yet (no `task_runs[i].finished_at` is set). There is no "synthetic current-location start stop" branch: a one-off reposition route is authored as a single-task `stop` route. With start mode `FIRST`, `current_waypoint_index is None` means "no task has been completed yet" and `Route.tasks[0]` is the first planning target; `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` seed a later start position instead (see **Run start-mode seeding**).
- `current_waypoint_index` is the route-progress cursor: derived from `task_runs[i].finished_at`, it always marks the last task the train has actually finished. There is no separate stored planning cursor in v1; whenever any phase needs the next-task index, it calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh.
- The initial `RouteRun.direction` is `"forward"` through the stored route order. The route plan remains an ordered list in v1. Reversible round-trip behavior comes only from `RouteRun.direction` flipping between `"forward"` and `"reverse"`, computed by `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(train_id, ...)` and committed by Phase 1, not from building a second linked or reversed route structure.
- When a new target is resolved, `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns the next-task projection and resolved target candidate. Phase 1 sets `direction` to the resolved direction, and after a successful create/extend writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the helper's returned index) to the target pair that call returned, per Planning Kernel step `66aa`. Route planning must not look beyond the resolved task in one pathfinder request.
- An `extend_path(...)` call that did not grow authority while a live path still exists, and a `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` for the matching live path, both leave physical progress unchanged. There is no separate planning-cursor rollback; later Phase 1 calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from the current shared route, current physical progress, and the current live authority end or no-authority stopped position as applicable, and that helper resolves the next task fresh. `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` failure follows the same rule: the next Phase 1 simply re-invokes `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
- `RouteExecutor` reads the live path id via `PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`. A single live path may remain active across multiple task segments. After `waypoint` consumption, `dwell` completion, or `stop` completion, `task_runs[i].finished_at` is recorded for the consumed task (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to that index automatically), and only a later progress callback or later Phase 1 may discover the next task on the same `path_id` when authority is still live. A single `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback never consumes more than one task.
- UI consumers wanting "why does the train have no live authority?" call `NotificationCenter.latest_error_for("train", train_id)` and show the most recent blocking event whatever its `origin` — `"planning_failed"`, `"task_skipped"`, `"run_stalled"`, `"wedge"`, or `"meet_hold"`, per **Error events**, plus the controller's live `lookahead` diagnostics. (Watchdogs and alert notifications are Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.)
- Do not add a separate controller operational sub-state for cancellation recovery in v1. When a deviation or revocation invalidates a live path, the cancel latch holds while the zero-limit / EB protocol per step `826a` stops the train (the `"deviation"` reason code applies while the path is still live). The cancel then commits at rest and clears controller authority. The deferred recovery gate is the `path_cancelled` latch (see Latch Table). `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` sets it and records diagnostic context immediately in Phase 3, but does not choose a new target or create fresh authority there. Once the train reaches the stopped no-authority state (after EB has brought it to rest and `eb.release()` has cleared EB to `READY`), the same train's Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step consumes `path_cancelled`. A later eligible Phase 1 then re-enters ordinary planning: covered-task index advance for already-passed `waypoint` tasks, then `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to resolve the next task.
- Phase 1's covered-task index advance (canonical home for the **covered-waypoint** and **covered-stop** rules): at the start of each Phase 1 tick, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` reads the train's current span via `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id).get_current_span()` and walks the current directed route from `current_waypoint_index` (the derived read of "last finished task"). For each next non-finished task on the directed walk:
  - **covered-waypoint** (`task_kind == "waypoint"`): the task is covered when its resolved target block (resolved live for this purpose) is anywhere inside the current span, and in addition either the walk-position is on the initial walk (`walk_position < len(Route.tasks)`) or the train's head is not in that block. When it is covered, call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances) and continue. A waypoint the train physically passed mid-span is covered even after the head has moved beyond it, so span membership is the right test for whether the train has been there. The head condition is what separates "already passed" from "has not left yet" on the walk-positions a `CIRCULAR` or `ROUND_TRIP` walk appends: a target block the train's head still sits in is one the walk must travel back to, so the train reaches it by running the walk rather than by standing in it. On the initial walk that same overlap means the train started there, so coverage finishes it.
  - **covered-stop** (`task_kind == "stop"`): finish it through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (the same canonical writer) only when the train is stopped, no active path exists, AND the train's **head** is in the destination block (e.g. a route authored while the train already sits at the destination platform, which has no path for the dispatcher to complete). The covered-stop is head-anchored, not span-anchored: a long train at a dead-end can have its tail spill into the next block — which on a turnaround is the very destination it must reverse back to — while its head is still short of it, and a bare span-membership test would wrongly declare that stop complete and finish the route without the train ever turning around. While the train is rolling or a live path exists, coverage never finishes a stop and it is served only by route-committed stop arrival.
  - `task_kind == "dwell"`: return without advancing — dwell is handled by the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`.
  - `task_kind == "run"`: return without advancing — block coverage never finishes a run; Phase 1 run step `6a0d` owns its timer, service stop, authority release, and completion.

  There is no separate `_advance_past_covered_waypoints(...)` helper or `_skip_waypoint(...)` helper in v1; the index advance is just a small loop inside `tick_train_phase1`.

**Canonical Route-Progress Events**

- `current_waypoint_index` advances automatically (it is a derived read) when any of these route-owned events writes `task_runs[i].finished_at` in v1:
  - head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption in `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`, at most one task per head-advance callback
  - `task_kind == "stop"` arrival recorded by Phase 3 step `bc55` of the **Stop-arrival procedure**
  - `task_kind == "dwell"` completion after the dwell timer elapses following a route-committed stop arrival
  - Phase 1 step `6a0d` completing a `task_kind == "run"` after its simulated-time window, service stop, and normal-progress authority release
  - Phase 1's covered-task index advance for already-covered `waypoint` tasks (including post-cancellation reconciliation) and covered stopped-at-destination `stop` tasks (train stopped, no active path, head in the destination block — see the covered-task index advance canonical home)
  - Phase 1 broken-task handling writing a skipped missing-target task directly (the explicit successful-progress-helper exception)
- Head-based `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption is a route-owned in-motion progress event, not a dispatcher stop-completion event. The canonical trigger chain is `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` → `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` → `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` → `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`. The callback contract is fully defined once in the **Dispatcher progress/completion flow** section; consult that section. Summary: each callback consumes at most one task. If the reported block matches the next non-finished task's resolved target, the callback records `task_runs[i].finished_at` (the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances with that write) and returns. Non-matching block reports are no-ops on the route side; intermediate tasks the train physically passed are reconciled by the next Phase 1's covered-task index advance.
- Already-covered `waypoint` normalization is a Phase 1 step: it calls `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (the canonical writer — records `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances) for any next-on-walk `waypoint` task the covered-waypoint test finds covered (see the covered-task index advance canonical home). `task_kind == "stop"` tasks are not skipped this way: they are served only by route-committed stop arrival (except the head-anchored covered-stop case — see the covered-task index advance canonical home). `task_kind == "run"` is never skipped by coverage and is completed only by step `6a0d`. Phase 1 may run the covered-waypoint advance on multiple consecutive tasks in one pass before requesting authority, up to the one-scan-per-pass bound in that same canonical home; post-cancellation covered-task reconciliation uses that same mechanism. V1 has no route-edit or operator-skip request that declares a present-target task finished. There is no separate `_skip_waypoint(...)` helper.
- There is no stored planning cursor in v1. The "next task to plan against" is always the index returned by the latest `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call. `current_waypoint_index` (derived from `task_runs[i].finished_at`) is the only stored route-progress cursor, and it advances only when a task is actually served: waypoint head entry/coverage, stop completion, dwell exit, run completion, or broken-task skip. A successful `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` writes `resolved_target` but does not advance it.

**Route-executor state reads**

- `RouteExecutor` must read live operational state from `TrainController`, not from mirrored runtime fields on `Train`.
- Departure, retry, arrival, dwell, and waiting decisions must use controller state as the operational source of truth.
- `Train` may still expose persisted or UI-facing state, but route-control decisions must not depend on it being a live mirror.
- Manual driving is `control_mode == manual`, not a separate operational state. A route-managed manual train still uses ordinary `READY` / `RUNNING_*` states and dispatcher authority; the difference from ATO is how movement begins and is driven, not whether `RouteExecutor` owns the waypoint plan. In particular, manual `READY` must not block route-side path creation. It changes only what `depart()` means after authority exists.

**Contention snapshot**

- `PathDispatcher.get_waiting_for_block_id(train_id) -> Optional[str]` is the canonical home of the forward-resource acquisition walk used to identify a blocked waiter. It reads the train's active path; when that authority is truncated short of the run's armed target, it reruns the same shared pathfinder/eligibility walk as `extend_path(...)` and returns the block owning the first forward resource that currently fails acquisition because of occupancy/reservation/lock conflict — the same availability test the **viable-but-unavailable rule** defines, including a resource this train's own span still occupies. It returns `None` when authority reaches the target, no active path exists, or no viable suffix exists. The method is a pure live read and stores nothing.
- `RouteExecutor` builds one **contention snapshot** per tick, in the tick preamble, after `order_runnable_trains(...)` returns the runnable order and before the first train's Phase 1 begins. It is `RouteExecutor.contention_snapshot`: a map `block_id -> {train_id, effective_priority}`. For each train whose `PathDispatcher.get_waiting_for_block_id(train_id)` returns a block, the snapshot records the single highest-`RouteRun.effective_priority` waiter (ties broken by the same stable iteration order `order_runnable_trains(...)` uses). A block with no waiter has no entry.
- The snapshot reads tick-start committed state only through `PathDispatcher.get_waiting_for_block_id(...)`, plus the override-aware `RouteRun.effective_priority` per **RouteRun overrides**. `RouteExecutor` reaches that dispatcher helper through its top-level `SimulationController` reference. The snapshot is a read-only tick-start view: recomputed every tick, retained across no ticks, and carrying no gating predicate, so it is not a latch, has no writer helper, and holds no Latch-Table entry. UI/read-model code calls the same helper; neither consumer reimplements the walk.
- Two consumers read it in v1. The contention-driven release branch of Planning Kernel step `66aa` (**idle-hold maintenance and release**) reads it to learn whether a strictly-higher-priority run is blocked on a block this run holds as a not-yet-entered future reservation. **Meet coordination** (below) reads the contending pairs it records to seed meet conflict detection.

**Why this shape.** Each train plans alone in its own Phase 1, so without one shared tick-start view no train can tell that a block it is holding is the block a higher-priority train is stuck behind. The snapshot is the single place that view is assembled. Assembling it once per tick — rather than having every run recompute every other run's `waiting_for_block_id` — keeps each Phase-1 authority step a constant-time read per held block.

**Wedge detection**

- A **wedge** is a set of trains that are each stopped with nowhere to go, where every member is waiting for a resource another member holds. The one-train case counts: a train whose own body holds the resource its authority needs next is a wedge of size one. `RouteExecutor.wedge_report` is the per-tick view of the wedges present, built in the tick preamble alongside the contention snapshot — after `order_runnable_trains(...)` returns the runnable order and before the first train's Phase 1 begins — so it reads tick-start committed state only. Like the contention snapshot it is read-only coordinator state — recomputed every tick, retained across no ticks, carrying no gating predicate — so it is not a latch, has no writer helper, and holds no Latch-Table entry.
- A train takes part only when it is **stopped with nowhere to go**: `TrainController.is_stopped()` is true (the canonical stopped predicate) and it is standing at its live authority boundary with no clearance left to advance, which is the zero-clearance hold `lookahead` reports against that live authority. A train that is still rolling is never a member, because the resource it waits for may clear before it gets there — and when the waited-for resource is held by its own tail, advancing is exactly what clears it.
- Each participating train contributes one edge: from that train to the train holding the block `PathDispatcher.get_waiting_for_block_id(train_id)` names. The holder is read from the block's live claim — `Block.reserved_by_path` resolved to that path's train, or the block's occupant. A cycle in that graph is a wedge, and an edge that points back at the train that drew it is a one-train wedge.
- Detection covers trains that hold live authority, which is what `get_waiting_for_block_id(train_id)` reads. A train with no live authority is not wedged: it holds nothing it acquired, and why its task could not be served is already recorded by **Unreachable and broken task handling**, which emits its own error event and projects `"STALLED"`.
- `RouteExecutor` emits one event per wedge, with `operation = "build_wedge_report"`, `kind = "warning"`, and `ErrorRecord.origin = "wedge"`, naming the trains in the cycle and the blocks the cycle runs through, on the tick that wedge first appears, and not again until its payload changes, per **Emit on change, not per tick** in **Error events**. It carries the ordinary fields defined in the **Event schema**, and the **Event log** records it like any other event.
- The report changes no runtime state. It gates no planning step, caps no acquisition, and cancels no path. The only release path for a stalled hold remains the idle-hold maintenance and release rule in Planning Kernel step `66aa`.

**Why this shape.** Clearing a wedge takes a train moving, moving takes authority, and the missing authority is what makes it a wedge — so the resolution is physical, and an operator taking manual control is the one actor who can carry it out. What the operator needs from the software is therefore the diagnosis: which trains are involved and which blocks the cycle runs through, said once, rather than several trains that each separately project `"STALLED"` with nothing to show that they are waiting on each other. Cancelling a path returns only reservations a train has not yet entered, so for trains already standing on the contested blocks there is no cancellation the detector could order that would free anything.

**Meet coordination**

- After the contention snapshot (above), `RouteExecutor` computes a per-tick **meet assignment**: a map `train_id -> meet_ceiling_block_id` that caps how far a train may reserve authority this tick when a higher-priority train's route needs the same track. Like the contention snapshot it is read-only tick-start coordinator state — recomputed every tick, retained across no ticks, carrying no gating predicate — so it is not a latch, has no writer helper, and holds no Latch-Table entry. v1 stores no persistent cross-train meet plan; the assignment is re-derived each tick.
- **Conflict detection.** Starting from the contending pairs the contention snapshot records, `RouteExecutor` reads each contender's candidate route toward its current next target (from the shared pathfinder — the same walk Phase 1 would run) and looks for a **contested zone**: a maximal run of resources both routes traverse with conflicting frames — opposing block traversal direction, or a shared switch whose two routes `routes_conflict` (per the **Switch Entity** section). Routes that share only resources their frames do not conflict on — distinct parallel blocks, or a switch set to routes that do not `routes_conflict`, such as a scissors holding NW and SE — have no contested zone and produce no assignment.
- **Capacity.** A contested zone has sufficient capacity when the contenders can be given non-conflicting routes through it (distinct blocks, every shared switch on routes that do not `routes_conflict`); otherwise capacity is insufficient — a single bidirectional track, or a crossover/throat whose required routes `routes_conflict`.
- **Assignment** is a deterministic function of `RouteRun.effective_priority` (per **RouteRun overrides**) and the stable tie-break order `order_runnable_trains(...)` uses:
  - Sufficient capacity: each contender keeps a distinct non-conflicting route, and its `meet_ceiling_block_id` is the far entry of the contested zone on its route, so neither reserves the other's exit track before reaching the zone. Both proceed and pass within the zone; a contender's ceiling lifts on the first tick its route no longer conflicts with the others.
  - Insufficient capacity: the highest-priority contender is admitted with no ceiling — it reserves to its normal route target through the zone — and each lower-priority contender's `meet_ceiling_block_id` is the furthest holding point (`Block.is_holding_point`, per the **Block** field schema) on its route before the contested zone. A lower-priority contender that has no holding point before the zone receives no ceiling and falls back to ordinary best-effort truncation; `RouteExecutor` emits one `kind = "warning"` event with `ErrorRecord.origin = "meet_hold"` recording that the layout offers no holding place to meet at.
- **Phase 1 consumption.** The meet event names `operation = "compute_meet_assignment"`. When the meet assignment carries a `meet_ceiling_block_id` for a train, that train's Planning Kernel step `66aa` passes it as the `acquisition_cap_block_id` on `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` while keeping `requested_destination_block_id` (and the `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` written on success) equal to the route task's real target, per the **Acquisition-cap rule**. The dispatcher caps the acquired authority at the ceiling, so the derived `Path.get_authority_end_block_id()` lands on the ceiling while the requested target stays the farther route stop; because `Path.get_authority_end_block_id() != requested_destination_block_id`, the capped authority does not complete and the train holds at the ceiling. The route target is unchanged; the first tick the train carries no ceiling, step `66aa` passes no cap and requests it. A train aimed at a holding-point ceiling therefore receives clean authority to that holding point and waits there, instead of relying on best-effort truncation to land it there. When the assignment writes a ceiling short of the train's requested target while that run's `RouteRun.effective_acquisition_mode` is `AcquisitionMode.all_or_nothing`, the two are contradictory and the train will hold without acquiring anything until the ceiling lifts (per the **Acquisition-cap rule**). Meet coordination emits one `kind = "warning"` event with `operation = "compute_meet_assignment"` and `ErrorRecord.origin = "meet_hold"` at the moment it writes that ceiling, carrying `train_id`, the assigned `meet_ceiling_block_id`, the run's `requested_destination_block_id`, and the effective acquisition mode, so the operator can see why the train is stopped. It follows **Emit on change, not per tick** in **Error events**, so a ceiling that moves to a different block is a changed payload and a new event: the acquisition calls themselves stay silent and are retried every eligible Phase 1 tick for as long as the meet holds.
- **Stability.** Because the assignment is a deterministic function of effective priority, the stable tie-break, and current world state, it does not flip between ticks: the admitted contender stays admitted until it clears the zone, and each held contender keeps the same ceiling until then. When the admitted train's reservations clear the contested zone, the next tick's recompute finds no conflict there and writes no ceiling, so the held trains resume toward their route targets.
- **Composition.** The meet assignment only caps acquired authority (through `acquisition_cap_block_id` per the **Acquisition-cap rule**); it never changes a route target and never reserves a resource. All acquisition still runs through the interlocked `BlockManager` / `SwitchManager` path with flank protection and the item-11 truncation exclusions intact, so a stale or wrong ceiling costs at most one tick of a train aiming one block short. Meet coordination is the proactive layer above the reactive guards: it reduces how often best-effort truncation, the contention-driven release (step `66aa`), and the reversal-trap exclusion fire, but those stay the safety floor — including for trains the assignment never governs, such as manual or unrouted trains.

**Why this shape.** A meet is a global decision — which train takes the shared track and where the other waits — that no single train's Phase 1 can make from its own view, so it is computed once per tick in the preamble where the global view exists. Re-deriving it each tick from a deterministic priority rule keeps it consistent with the "always recompute live" model and avoids storing the first retained cross-train state in v1; the decision is stable without persistence because priority and the tie-break do not change mid-meet. Writing only an advisory ceiling — never a reservation — leaves the single owner of each resource lock unchanged (`BlockManager` / `SwitchManager`), so the coordinator can never deadlock the interlocking: the worst a bad ceiling does is hold a train one tick longer.

**Planning Kernel**

- Phase 1 is a state-machine: each canonical step has explicit preconditions and is a no-op when those preconditions are not met. The kernel never creates fresh authority while the train has not yet reached a stopped recovery point, while any teardown/recovery latch is still dirty, while the controller is `OFF`, or while the `RouteRun` is pending/inside a dwell step.
- Eligibility precondition for the planning kernel's authority-acquisition step (step `66aa`) and the re-resolve step (step `592a` when it would lead to acquisition): `RouteExecutor` may run those steps only when one of the following is true:
  - the train has live authority (`PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) is not None`) AND `controller.active_conditions ∩ {PATH_CANCEL_PENDING, ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING, ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING} == ∅`, in which case only step `66aa`'s `extend_path(...)` branch is allowed. The latch exclusion prevents Phase 1 from extending a live path that Phase 3 is about to cancel; or
  - `RouteExecutor.is_clean_for_create(train_id)` is true per the **`ControllerCondition` set** canonical home, in which case the create branch is allowed.
- Steps `5fc7`, `70c3`, `c481`, `a5c6`, and `3a37` (deviation cleanup, dwell-complete, helper lookup, covered-task advance, dwell-entry) run unconditionally on every Phase 1 entry. They make no `PathDispatcher` mutation and only mutate `RouteRun` state per their own gating predicates. Run step `6a0d` is the exception: a pending path cancel, route abort, power-off, or route deactivation outranks normal run progress, so that step performs no timer/service-stop/release write while any of those latches is set. When `pending_route_deactivate` is set, the other listed steps still run unconditionally. The interactions with Phase 3's deactivation teardown are safe by construction. (a) If step `70c3` calls `finish_dwell()` on the deactivation tick, Phase 1 runs before Phase 3, so the controller has already left `RUNNING_AT_STATION` by the time Phase 3's deactivate consumer runs `remove_train_route_run(...)`; that helper checks `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION` before calling `finish_dwell()` and correctly skips it. (b) If step `3a37` calls `enter_station_dwell()` on the deactivation tick, the same-tick Phase 3 deactivate consumer calls `finish_dwell()` through `remove_train_route_run(...)` before archiving the run. Do not add a guard to skip steps `70c3` or `3a37` when `pending_route_deactivate` is set; doing so would leave the controller stuck in `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, because the deactivation teardown relies on the Phase-1-first ordering.
- The derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` / `"CANCELLED"` projection is not itself a gate; it clears automatically when `path_cancelled` (and its `path_cancel_was_deviation` companion) is consumed by the same Phase 1 route-deviation-cleanup step (step `5fc7`).
- If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 is skipped. If eligibility preconditions are otherwise unmet (still braking, dirty handoff), Phase 1 returns and waits for a later tick.
- For the EB case, the same eligibility rule applies: while `eb.active == True`, `controller.is_recovery_clean` is false per the **`ControllerCondition` set** canonical home and Phase 1 produces no authority. After `eb.release()` returns the controller to `READY`, the next eligible Phase 1 runs the planning kernel normally.
- One Phase 1 pass owns the planning-side `RouteRun` mutation in this canonical order (for the `RouteRun` writes made outside Phase 1, see the Phase Table's Phase-3 row, the **`current_route_status` write contract**, and **RouteRun overrides**). `RouteExecutor` is the canonical owner of every step; `SimulationController` invokes the kernel through `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` and `RouteExecutor` reaches the controller through its top-level `SimulationController` reference (`self.simulation_controller.get_or_create_controller(train_id)`). Steps:
  0. `9c4f` — **RouteRun (re)allocation.** Look up the train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` (`RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)`). If one exists, continue to step `5fc7`.
     - Otherwise, scan the route registry for the `Route` with `train_id ∈ Route.assigned_train_ids` and `Route.is_active == True`. At most one exists, per the single-assignment rule in **Route assignment writers** — this is the train's upcoming route. If none is found, Phase 1 returns (no work).
     - If one is found, allocate a new `RouteRun` against that `route_id`:
       - write `route_id`;
       - sample `RouteRun.started_at = time.time()`;
       - set `direction = "forward"`;
       - allocate `task_runs` with `len(Route.tasks)` entries — one per task with `task_index = 0..N-1`, `run_elapsed_seconds = 0.0`, and all Optional fields `None`;
       - set `current_route_status = "RUNNING"`;
       - seed the starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding** (for `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` this pre-finishes the skipped prefix so the run begins at the chosen stop; `FIRST` leaves the run at `task_runs[0]`).
     - The newly-allocated run is the canonical home of all subsequent Phase 1 mutation for that train this tick. Continue to step `5fc7`.
  1. `5fc7` — **Route-deviation cleanup** (multi-tick latch). When `RouteRun.path_cancelled == True` AND `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, clear `path_cancelled` and `path_cancel_was_deviation` (the derived `display_state == "DEVIATED"` / `"CANCELLED"` projection clears with them).
  2. `70c3` — **Dwell-complete timer check.** Finish the dwell when all three hold:
     - `TrainController.ops.state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`;
     - `controller.eb.active == False`;
     - `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` is satisfied per **Task time semantics**.

     Finishing the dwell means: call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)` (which writes the dwell row's `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position` and the next task's derived `started_at` reflects this `finished_at`); then call `TrainController.finish_dwell()` (resolved through the top-level reference); then continue into step `c481`.
     - Dwell completion is suppressed while EB is latched per the **EB workflow state machine** row `0a8e`: even once `should_depart(...)` is satisfied the train holds in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` until `TrainController.eb.release()` (row `1bbc`) clears `eb.active`, after which the next eligible Phase 1 pass finishes the dwell.
  3. `c481` — **Look up the next non-finished task** on the directed walk by calling `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
     - If it returns `None`, continue directly to step `592a`, which owns terminal-walk completion.
     - When the projection's `failure_kind == missing_target` — the task's own `task_location` is gone, whatever its `task_kind` — broken-skip it per **Unreachable and broken task handling** and re-run the lookup. Repeat until the lookup returns a valid task, the walk goes terminal, or the pass's `len(Route.tasks)` scan budget is spent; this step and step `592a` share that one budget.
     - When the scan finds no valid task at all on a `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` walk, this step sets `RouteRun.no_reachable_task = True` per that same canonical home and returns for the tick. Any lookup that finds a valid task clears the flag.
     - Then branch on the valid projection: `task_kind == "dwell"` goes to step `3a37` (dwell-entry); every other kind continues to step `6a0d`.
     - Step `c481` reads the next task only to run the broken-skip and pick the branch here (terminal walk, no-reachable-task stall, dwell-entry, run timer, or covered-task advance). It does not carry the resolved target past a cursor-moving step, so step `592a` still re-resolves the acquisition target.
  4. `6a0d` — **Run timer, service stop, and normal-progress authority release.** This step applies when the projection from `c481` is a present-target `task_kind == "run"` task and no higher-priority teardown latch is set.
     - **Timer.** Add this tick's non-negative `delta_seconds` to `task_runs[walk_position].run_elapsed_seconds`, capped at the task's required `task_time`. While the stored elapsed value remains below `task_time`, continue to step `a5c6` and ordinary authority acquisition.
     - **Service stop.** Once it reaches `task_time`, set the `ops.service_stop_active` latch through the idempotent `TrainController.begin_service_stop()`. If live authority exists and no run-completion release request is already pending, call `set_path_cancel_request(error=None, kind="info", run_complete=True)`. Then return while any of these holds:
       - the train is moving;
       - that release request remains pending;
       - `received_path_plan is not None`.
     - **Finish.** Once the train is stopped with no live authority and no pending release request, call `TrainController.consume_service_stop()`, then `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)`, and return for the tick. The gating predicate above belongs to this step; the accessor itself only clears the latch.
     - This step is the only writer of `run_elapsed_seconds`; head entry, coverage, stop arrival, and the cancellation callback never finish a run. A missing/unavailable run target is handled later by the ordinary broken/unreachable path and does not accrue timer time on that tick.
  5. `a5c6` — **Covered-task index advance.** While the next non-finished task is `task_kind == "waypoint"` and the covered-waypoint test in the covered-task index advance canonical home finds it covered, call `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(i, current_time)` (which writes `task_runs[i].finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances and the next task's derived `started_at` reflects this `finished_at`). The advance stops when the next task is:
     - `task_kind == "stop"` — except finish it in the covered-stop case (train stopped, no active path, and the train's head in the destination block, per the covered-task index advance canonical home); while the train is rolling or a live path exists, block coverage never finishes a stop;
     - `task_kind == "dwell"` — continue to step `3a37`;
     - `task_kind == "run"` — run step `6a0d` is its only completion owner;
     - or no further task fits coverage.

     This advance covers at most one full `len(Route.tasks)` scan within a single Phase 1 pass — its own budget, the same size as the one steps `c481` and `592a` share for the broken-task skip in **Unreachable and broken task handling**. `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` and `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` keep appending walk-positions as each one finishes, so the scan bound is what ends the pass and hands the tick back.
  6. `3a37` — **Dwell-entry transition.** Call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` when all three hold:
     - the next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`;
     - the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set;
     - `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true.

     That call samples the dwell row's `started_at`, so the dwell window begins when the train is actually standing at the platform. Return for that tick — no path creation while dwell is pending or active. If the controller is already `RUNNING_AT_STATION` at step `3a37`, also return.
  7. `592a` — **Re-resolve the next target.** Call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh. The `c481` lookup picked the Phase-1 branch but predates any cursor move from steps `6a0d` / `a5c6` / `3a37`, so this resolve is the one that feeds authority acquisition.
     - If it returns `None`, the directed walk is terminal: call `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly and return for that tick, per the terminal-walk rule in **Canonical Next-Target Selection**. This fires only for `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`; `RouteMode.CIRCULAR` / `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` never resolve to `None`.
     - When the fresh projection's `failure_kind == missing_target`, broken-skip it per **Unreachable and broken task handling** and re-run this resolve, drawing on the same per-pass scan budget as step `c481` and setting `RouteRun.no_reachable_task` on the same no-valid-task condition. This branch exists because steps `6a0d` / `a5c6` / `3a37` can move the cursor onto a task step `c481` never looked up.
     - Otherwise use the returned projection's `walk_position` and set `direction` from that projection.
     - When the live path already holds the projection's target block as its armed target, the request contract for this attempt is the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`; otherwise this arms a new target block and the projection's `resolved_target` is the candidate, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. Do not write `task_runs[i].resolved_target` yet.
  8. `66aa` — **Authority acquisition.**
     - **Waypoint lookahead.** Before acquiring, decide which target this tick arms. Arm the next unfinished task's target instead of re-arming the served one when both hold:
       - the currently armed task has `task_kind == "waypoint"` and the live authority already reaches it;
       - the count of `block` resources in `resource_sequence` strictly after the train head's `current_resource_index` is below `WAYPOINT_LOOKAHEAD_BLOCKS` (**Runtime settings**).

       This way the extend attempt happens before the train has to brake for the current terminal. Phase 1 reaches that task by calling `RouteExecutor._find_next_task_target(train_id, after_walk_position=<the served waypoint's index>)`; it never indexes `task_runs` or `Route.tasks` directly.
     - While a lookahead target is armed, `walk_position` is the returned lookahead index, not the served waypoint's, at all three use sites: the `resolved_target` write below, the `extend_path(...)` retries in **Exact `RouteExecutor` tick calls**, and the `Path.reaches_target(...)` idle-hold predicate.
     - Arming stays one target at a time: the newly armed target becomes the authority terminal, and the next eligible tick may repeat the step for the target after it. The walk-forward stops at the first `stop` or `run` target, which terminates the authority per the **Authority termination rule**.
     - A blocked extend is not a failure of this step — the authority simply stays where it is and the train brakes toward it.
     - **Unreachable-task wait.** When the projection carries `resolved_target is None` (`failure_kind == target_unavailable`), per **Unreachable and broken task handling**: make no `PathDispatcher` call, emit one `kind = "warning"` event with `ErrorRecord.origin = "planning_failed"` and `ErrorRecord.text` taken from the projection's `failure_reason`, and return for that tick.
     - **The create/extend call.** Otherwise call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that one armed target only, passing `manual_override` from the run's route (`Route.manual_override` resolved through `RouteRun.route_id`).
     - **Meet ceiling.** When **Meet coordination** has assigned this train a `meet_ceiling_block_id` for this tick, pass it as `acquisition_cap_block_id` and keep `requested_destination_block_id` equal to the route task's real target, per the **Acquisition-cap rule**. The dispatcher caps the acquired authority: `authority_end_block_id` lands on the ceiling while the requested target stays the route stop, and the capped authority holds there without completing. On the first tick the train carries no ceiling, step `66aa` passes no cap and requests the full route target.
     - **On a successful create/extend**, write `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the index the helper returned for the target this tick armed) to the target pair the create/extend call returned — the dispatcher's own report of what it planned toward, never the meet `acquisition_cap_block_id`, and never a fresh re-resolve of the task on this tick. This is the only canonical writer of that field in v1; under the **Waypoint lookahead** it may write a row ahead of the one the train is physically working on. When this Phase 1 has live authority for the current target, also call `TrainController.depart()` directly for the current authority.
     - **On a failed create/extend**, emit one error event with `origin = "planning_failed"` naming the train and the walk-position per **Error events**.
     - **Idle-hold maintenance and release (best-effort hoarding guard).** This step is the canonical home for releasing a stalled best-effort hold; it maintains `RouteRun.idle_hold_ticks` and `RouteRun.release_cooldown_ticks`. An idle hold is a run keeping its reserved blocks and switch locks while making no progress toward its target.
       - **Cooldown window.** While `release_cooldown_ticks > 0`, this step does no acquisition: it decrements `release_cooldown_ticks` by one and returns for that train this tick. The just-released reservations and switch locks stay free for higher-priority runs through the cooldown window.
       - **Counting.** Otherwise, after the create/extend attempt, increment `idle_hold_ticks` by one when all three hold:
         - the live authority is a truncated best-effort prefix — the run's armed target block fails `Path.reaches_target(...)`;
         - the authority did not grow this tick — the create/extend call reported `appended_resource_count == 0`;
         - the head did not advance.

         Reset `idle_hold_ticks` to `0` when any of these holds:
         - the authority grew;
         - the head advanced;
         - the acquisition reached the full target;
         - a meet ceiling capped this tick's acquisition;
         - there is no live path.

         A meet-capped run is deliberately parked at the meet point, not hoarding, so it must not time out and release.
       - **Timed release.** When `idle_hold_ticks` reaches `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS`, release the held truncated authority the system-replan way: call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"idle_hold_release"` per the **Writer-helper rule**, stop planning for that train this tick, set `release_cooldown_ticks = IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS`, and reset `idle_hold_ticks = 0`.
     - **Contention-driven release.** Independently of the no-progress count: when this run holds a truncated best-effort authority, and the tick's contention snapshot (per **Contention snapshot**) records a waiting train whose `RouteRun.effective_priority` is strictly greater than this run's on any block reserved for this run's live path (`Block.reserved_by_path == path_id`) that the train does not yet physically occupy (`Block.occupied_by is None`), then this run gives up the held truncated authority the same system-replan way: call `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_path_cancel_request(...)` with an `ErrorRecord` whose `origin` is `"contention_release"` per the **Writer-helper rule**, stop planning for that train this tick, set `release_cooldown_ticks = IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS`, and reset `idle_hold_ticks = 0`.
       - This release does not wait for `idle_hold_ticks` to reach `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS`: when the higher-priority run is blocked on one of the held-but-unentered reservations, this run gives them up at once.
       - Equal-priority contention does not trigger it. The strictly-greater test stops two same-priority runs from releasing in turn; the no-progress timeout above stays their backstop.
       - Phase 3 then commits that cancel (the train is already stopped under zero ATC clearance), freeing the not-yet-entered reservations and switch locks. The ordinary post-cancel replan reruns from the train's real position on a later eligible Phase 1 tick once the cooldown has elapsed.
     - A full authority — one whose `Path.reaches_target(...)` is true for the armed target — is never truncated, so `idle_hold_ticks` stays `0` and neither release fires for a train that already holds authority to its real target.
     - `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` (the consecutive-no-progress tick budget) and `IDLE_HOLD_COOLDOWN_TICKS` (the post-release acquisition-skip window) are adjustable fields in **Runtime settings**.
- A single Phase 1 pass may loop locally through these steps. For one planning problem, Phase 1 may try multiple distinct same-tick resolved targets only when the current task is a `StationTarget` task. Phase 1 owns a local `tried` set of already-attempted resolved targets for this planning problem, and calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id, exclude=tried)` repeatedly, appending each returned `resolved_target` to `tried` after a failed `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` attempt. The helper itself stores no per-tick state; Phase 1 has all the information needed to decide which candidates have been attempted. The helper returns the next compatible candidate each time (or `None` when exhausted). A `PlatformTarget` task, `BlockTarget` task, or explicit local-reposition task has only one resolved pair for that tick; if that create/extend attempt fails without creating or growing authority, the run waits until a later eligible tick rather than trying alternatives in the same pass. If every same-tick candidate is exhausted without authority creation or growth and this was a create attempt (so no live path exists), `RouteExecutor` stops Phase 1 for that train for that tick. If an existing live path remains and `extend_path(...)` returns `success=True` with `reached_target = False`, keep `current_route_status = RUNNING`, preserve that live path unchanged, and retry on later eligible ticks. Step `66aa`'s idle-hold maintenance applies when that retained authority satisfies its separately stated truncated-best-effort predicate. Only a `success=False` extend failure — `no_viable_path` in v1 — latches cancellation before a fresh create attempt.
- `RouteExecutor` never asks dispatcher/pathfinder to solve a whole multi-task route in one search. Longer authorities are formed only by repeated point-to-point segments appended onto the same live path identified by `path_id`.
- In normal route-managed execution, v1 arms exactly one target per Phase 1 pass and never concatenates two targets on the same tick. Across ticks the armed target may run ahead of the train's task progress: the **Waypoint lookahead** arms the next target while the current `waypoint` is still unserved, so more than one unfinished `waypoint` row can carry a `resolved_target` at once. Those are separate cursors and always have been — `Path.current_resource_index` is authority progress and `RouteRun.current_waypoint_index` is physical task progress. A `waypoint` is still consumed only by the head-entry callback chain, whatever the authority terminal is at the time.
- **Authority termination rule.** This is the canonical home. A live authority terminates at the currently armed target block, whatever that task's kind. Dispatcher must not grow it past the resolved target of a `stop` or `run` task before that task is served: authority beyond an unserved `stop` would let the train roll through the place it was sent to stop, and a `run` is finished by its own Phase 1 timer rather than by arrival, so authority past it would run the train on under an already-satisfied plan. A `waypoint` target is an ordinary terminal — the train is meant to pass through it, so Phase 1's **Waypoint lookahead** arms the next target and grows the authority forward through consecutive waypoints, one armed target at a time. Between arming steps the authority legitimately ends on a waypoint block, and the train brakes toward it if the next arm cannot acquire; that braking is correct, because an authority that cannot grow is exactly as long as the train is allowed to run.
- `task_kind == "stop"` versus `task_kind == "waypoint"` changes only what happens when the resolved target is reached: `stop` enters dwell (or the `dwell` task that follows it) through the **Stop-arrival check** — or, when the route is authored with the train already stopped at the destination, through Phase 1's head-anchored covered-stop finish — while `waypoint` advances immediately without dwell. It does not change the dispatcher path API shape.

**Run start-mode seeding**

This subsection is the canonical home for which stop a fresh `RouteRun` begins at. It is consulted once, when Phase 1 step `9c4f` allocates a fresh run, and seeds the run's starting walk-position.

- **Start mode.** `RouteStartMode` has three values:
  - `FIRST` (default) — start at `task_runs[0]`. The covered-task index advance then walks past any already-covered `waypoint` tasks exactly as before; stops are never skipped by coverage. This is the v1 behavior when no start mode is set.
  - `NEAREST` — seed the run at the best `task_kind == "stop"` task for the train to begin at. A stop is eligible only when the train has a viable path to its resolved block in the block-relative direction that stop's task requires — the same directed graph-walk the stop-planning rule under **RouteTask data** runs to plan a real move there, which already filters on the task's `task_direction` and on `Block.allowed_travel_directions` (the latter relaxed when the run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`). So a stop the tracks merely touch, but that can only be reached against its task's direction or against block travel-direction, is not eligible, and the ranking falls through to the next viable stop. Eligibility and track distance both come from that one shared directed graph-walk query (the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload** comparison, run with the stop's required direction), so this subsection does not restate the metric or the direction filter. Among the eligible stops, rank each by four things in order, each one breaking ties left by the ones before it:
    1. **No reversal first.** Prefer a stop the train can drive straight on from. A stop is a *reverse stop* when, after arriving there, the train's next route move would have to leave it back through the same block the train arrived through — it has to turn around in place. A drive-on stop is preferred even when a reverse stop is a little closer. (The next move is the first `stop` / `waypoint` task after this one, skipping `dwell` tasks, wrapping for `RouteMode.CIRCULAR`, and reflecting at `RouteMode.ROUND_TRIP` ends — resolved from this candidate stop's walk-position by the resolver's anchored next-move helper, which shares its forward task-walk kernel with `RouteExecutor.find_next_move_target(train_id)` — the same next-move rule the **reversal-trap exclusion** under best-effort `create_path()` applies from the occupant's current cursor.)
    2. **Free first.** Prefer a stop no other train has taken. A stop is *taken* when its resolved block is either occupied by another train or is the live next-move target of another train already running this route. Reading other runs' current next move is what spreads a yard-full of trains across distinct stops instead of all of them picking the same nearest one; because it reads each run's *current* move, a train that has already driven past its own seed no longer holds a claim on it.
    3. **Nearest.** Prefer the shorter track-distance walk.
    4. **Lowest task index** breaks any remaining tie.

    If no stop has a viable directed path, fall back to FIRST.
  - `SPECIFIC` — start at the stop named by the effective `start_task_index`. The index must be in range and name a `task_kind == "stop"` task; otherwise fall back to FIRST.
- **Where it is set.** The route-level default is `Route.start_mode` / `Route.start_task_index`. A single assignment may override it with a per-assignment input (`start_mode` / `start_task_index` on `PUT /api/trains/<id>/assign-route`), stashed on `RouteExecutor._pending_start_overrides[train_id]` and consumed by the next fresh allocation. The effective value is: the per-assignment input if present, else the route default, else `FIRST`. The per-assignment input is **one-shot** — it is cleared at the top of every assign / retarget / unassign and removed when the fresh run consumes it, so it never leaks onto a different route's run and a re-allocation after a structural edit falls back to the route default. It is NOT a live `RouteRun` override (it is not in the override allowlist): start mode has no meaning once the run's starting walk-position is seeded, so it is consulted only at allocation.
- **Seeding mechanism.** At allocation (step `9c4f`), after `task_runs` is initialized with `len(Route.tasks)` entries, let `s` be the chosen start task index. For `FIRST` (and any fallback to FIRST), `s = 0` and nothing is seeded. For `s > 0`, the run is allocated with the skipped prefix already finished: `task_runs[i].finished_at = RouteRun.started_at` for `i` in `[0, s)`, with `delay_class` left `None` (those positions were not travelled). `next_planning_walk_position` then lands on `s` and `current_waypoint_index` reads `s - 1`. This prefix-finish is an allocation-time initialization, distinct from the runtime `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` progress writer, and it does not append walk-positions. Because the initial walk holds only the forward leg (`task_index == walk_position` for `0..N-1`) and `s ≤ len(Route.tasks) - 1`, the apex is never pre-finished and `RouteRun.direction` stays `"forward"`; ROUND_TRIP reflection is handled normally by `_append_next_walk_task()` when the genuine apex later finishes. The resolved mode is recorded on `RouteRun.start_mode` for diagnostics, and the chosen start index `s` on `RouteRun.start_walk_position` (the seeded first planning target that the derived `RouteRun.is_starting` phase tier compares the progress cursor against).

**Route Edit And Reconciliation**

- Active runs reference the current shared `Route` through `RouteRun.route_id`. Any edit to `Route.tasks` that changes the waypoint set, ordering, or task-kind composition immediately latches teardown of every assigned train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` for that `route_id` — the per-train `pending_route_deactivate` latch plus its inclusive cancel, each committed in that train's later Phase 3 once the train has stopped (a moving train with still-valid authority first runs out to its `authority_end_block_id`, per the stopped-train gating in **Phase-3 latched-cancel consumption**) — followed by fresh `RouteRun` allocation against the edited `Route` on the next eligible Phase 1. There is no in-place reconciliation in v1 and no version stamp on the run. The fresh run picks its starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding**: because the per-assignment start input is one-shot (already consumed by the original allocation), a re-allocation after a structural edit uses the route-level `Route.start_mode` default. `Route.priority`, `Route.acquisition_mode`, and `Route.manual_override` edits are the exception — they take effect live without a teardown because they do not change the walk structure. Allowlisted-field edits may carry per-field `push = "force"` / `push = "soft"` policies that distribute the new value across existing `RouteRun.overrides` per the **RouteRun overrides** subsection. All edits in this paragraph (structural and non-structural) bump `Route.last_edited_at` per the **Route edit timestamps** subsection.
- A `RouteRun`'s `route_id` is established at run creation and never mutated.
- **Shared-route edit teardown.** When the shared-route edit handler accepts a structural edit, it walks `Route.assigned_train_ids` and per assigned train calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_deactivate(...)`, which sets `pending_route_deactivate = True` and writes the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` (`error.origin = "revoked"`) when a live path exists. Each train's later eligible Phase 3 commits the inclusive cancel and the deactivate teardown through the standard latch consumption flow: the deactivate consumer calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`, which writes `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED` on the old run and (when `state == RUNNING_AT_STATION`) calls `finish_dwell()` first. The historical row stays in `_historical_runs`. Because `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` unassigns the train from the run's `route_id` (the edited route) as part of that clear, a same-route re-assignment cannot be applied up front — it would be undone by that unassign. The new assignment is therefore **held until the clear is done, then applied**: immediately after writing the deactivate latches, the handler records a one-shot pending re-assignment for each train (`RouteExecutor._pending_reassign[train_id] = route_id`, the edited route) instead of re-adding to `Route.assigned_train_ids` up front. The same train's Phase-3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer, **after** its own `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` has torn the old run down and unassigned the train, applies the held re-assignment by calling `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the recorded route and clearing the stash — but only when that consumer actually owned the teardown (its `remove_train_route_run(train_id)` cleared a still-`RUNNING` run, i.e. `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id)` was not `None` when the deactivate step began). The Phase-3 consumer order is `pending_route_abort` → `pending_power_off` → `pending_route_deactivate`, so an operator abort or power-off for the same train runs first; if it (or a natural finish) already ended the run, the deactivate consumer finds `execution_state_for(train_id) is None`, **discards** `_pending_reassign[train_id]` without re-assigning, and the operator/admin teardown wins — a powered-off or aborted train is never silently re-assigned to the edited route. The next eligible Phase 1 then sees the train assigned to the edited route with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` and allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the edited `Route.tasks` per Phase 1's RouteRun (re)allocation step. (Operator retargeting / route swap differs: it assigns the train to a *different* route up front — see the next bullet — so its assignment is not undone by the old run's same-route unassign and needs no held re-assignment. `_pending_reassign` is consumed only by the `pending_route_deactivate` consumer; the abort and power-off consumers, and admin `route.deactivate()`, never record it, so they tear down without re-assigning.)
- The fresh `RouteRun` starts with `direction == "forward"` and `task_runs` initialized from the edited `Route.tasks`, then seeds its starting walk-position per **Run start-mode seeding** (re-allocation after a structural edit uses the route-level `Route.start_mode` default, since the per-assignment input is one-shot). For start mode `FIRST` this leaves `current_waypoint_index == None`; `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC` pre-finish a skipped prefix. Phase 1 then runs the ordinary planning kernel: the covered-task index advance walks past any `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks the train already covers, and `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` returns the first non-finished walk-position for the next planning step. Stops are not skipped by coverage, so the fresh run lands on the first non-covered stop or waypoint at or after its seeded start position automatically.
- Operator retargeting and explicit route swap follow the same teardown workflow with one variation: the API/UI/admin handler for the retarget/swap request atomically (a) calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_route_abort(...)` on the targeted train (which fans out to inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` (`error.origin = "operator_cancelled"`) when a live path exists) and (b) updates assignment — `Route.unassign_train(train_id)` on the old route and `Route.assign_train(train_id)` on the new (or newly-created one-off `RouteMode.ONE_OFF`) route. The handler returns success immediately. The same train's later Phase 3 consumes the inclusive cancel and then the abort latch through `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`; the next eligible Phase 1 sees the train assigned to the new route with no `RUNNING` `RouteRun` and allocates a fresh `RouteRun` against the new `route_id`.

**Exact `RouteExecutor` tick calls**

- `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` refreshes route-managed scheduling inputs for this tick. `delta_seconds` is the per-tick movement-window length (the gap between consecutive ticks at the simulation's current playback rate). It is used by physics integration and by callers that need a per-tick delta, but `RouteExecutor` does NOT maintain a separate `simulation_time` accumulator. All stored route/run timestamps use system-time Unix seconds (`time.time()`); there is no v1 `RouteExecutor.simulation_time` field. `RouteExecutor` owns the final runnable-train ordering through `RouteExecutor.order_runnable_trains(candidate_train_ids)`. Every candidate train resolves to one phase tier (running-phase vs starting-phase per `RouteRun.is_starting`; trains without a current `RouteRun` are running-tier) and one effective priority (route-managed trains use `RouteRun.effective_priority`, the override-aware read per **RouteRun overrides**; trains without a current `RouteRun` use effective priority `5`). The merged set is sorted first by phase tier (running before starting), then by effective priority descending (`9` highest, `0` lowest), with stable existing iteration order as the only tie-break, per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**. Route-managed and non-route trains at the same phase tier and effective priority interleave in stable iteration order; route management does not bump priority within a tier. `SimulationController.update(...)` owns executing the returned train IDs, but it must not apply a second priority sort. `RouteExecutor.update(delta_seconds)` prepares the route-managed Phase 1 work that `SimulationController` will invoke by train; it must not itself call `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` for every train in a world-wide pass. Trains without a current `RouteRun` still remain in the `SimulationController` loop and simply have no route-managed Phase 1 work for that tick. Stored route/run timestamps such as `Route.started_at`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].started_at`, `RouteRun.task_runs[i].finished_at`, and `RouteTask.target_time` use system-time Unix seconds; so does the `event_time` on every event (see **Error events**).
- `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` is the canonical public Phase-3 route surface for a served stop: `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` calls it once the authority is released, and it owns the step `bc55` commit. It is one of the route-executor entry points outside Phase 1, alongside the `handle_train_advanced(...)` / `handle_path_cancellation(...)` callbacks.
- `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(train_id, delta_seconds)` is the canonical public Phase-1 surface and is the entire Phase 1 route-executor tick for one train. `SimulationController.update(...)` calls it immediately before that same train's `SimulationController._update_train(train, delta_seconds)` transaction. Dwell handling, covered-task index advance, live target resolution, station candidate iteration, and `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` / `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` attempts all live here. `tick_train_phase1` creates or reuses the train's `RouteRun` (current-code class may still be named `RouteExecutionState`). If the controller is `OFF`, Phase 1 route work is skipped and power-on is handled by the Phase-2 command slot. If the next non-finished task on the directed walk has `task_kind == "dwell"`, dwell is the active route step: Phase 1 returns for that tick (Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` handles the transition into `RUNNING_AT_STATION`).
- Before any create/extend decision, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` and receive a legal `ResolvedNextTaskProjection` for the current shared route and current physical progress. The returned `resolved_target` is the transient request candidate for this attempt. If the helper cannot currently resolve a legal concrete platform/block target under the task's fixed/flexible platform policy, the retry fails at target resolution and `RouteExecutor` must not call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` for that tick.
- Before any create/extend decision, the current attempt's resolved target is the route-owned request contract for the `walk_position` returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`: the task target block and the route-owned `requested_destination_direction` constraint. While the live path already holds that target block as its requested target, the contract is the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` rather than a fresh resolve, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. It must not resolve or pass any stop-position centimeter field. Dispatcher and controller use the chosen terminal frame plus controller safety margin for braking/boundary projection.
