- Route-managed path building is always segment-by-segment along the current directed route walk. On each create/extend attempt, the resolved target represents exactly one resolved task target, never a bundled multi-task search request. After one segment is successfully created or appended, route planning stops for that train on that tick; if the armed target is `task_kind == "waypoint"`, the next task is not armed until that `waypoint` is physically consumed and the next planning tick runs.
- `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must ensure the train's `TrainController` exists before it reads controller state or calls `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`. A route-managed train already has a controller by the time authority is assigned in Phase 1. `SimulationController` owns only the 1:1 binding between `train_id` and `TrainController`; it does not construct or write authority projections.
- Before resolving a new task or retrying the current one, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` runs the covered-task index advance (a small loop inside the method) so the route does not keep targeting a task the train already physically covers.
- The operational state checks inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must read controller state rather than `train.state`.
- If controller state is `OFF`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` is not invoked for normal Phase 1 planning. A powered-off train must not create, extend, or rely on dispatcher authority, and the system must not auto-power-on it. `TrainController.commands.power_on_cmd()` is accepted only through the Phase-2 command slot; route planning resumes on a later tick after the controller is powered and in an eligible state. The canonical power-off release/removal order follows the standard phase workflow per the **Power-off procedure**.
- If the next route task is `task_kind == "dwell"`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must treat dwell as the active route step before any departure/path planning and return for that tick. The Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` transitions the controller to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` automatically when its preconditions are met (`controller.is_recovery_clean` is true and the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set). Phase 1 must not advance past the dwell task or request authority until the Phase 1 dwell-complete step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` marks the `dwell` task finished.
- If controller state is `READY` and there is no pending/active dwell task, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must run its authority-acquisition step (**Planning Kernel** step `66aa`). Path creation/retry is allowed from `READY` regardless of cab selection. The behavioral difference between ATO and manual is only departure execution after authority exists: ATO may auto-select/start, while manual mode leaves actual movement to the driver.
- If controller state is `RUNNING_MOVING`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must not poll for `stop` task arrival; normal stop arrival comes only from the **Stop-arrival check** in Phase 3. `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption is also not polled inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`; it is driven by the head-advance callback chain `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` -> `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` -> `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` -> `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)`. Each `RouteExecutor.handle_train_advanced(...)` callback compares one reported block advance with the current route task pointer and may consume at most that one task; it must not arm the next planning target or create/extend authority. `waypoint` consumption is head-based route progress only; the later physical release of earlier path resources still waits for tail-vacate callbacks. `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` in `RUNNING_MOVING` is therefore limited to best-effort `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` retries toward the currently armed target; it does not re-implement head-entry detection.
- If controller state is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must run its Phase 1 dwell-complete step (which may call a helper such as `RouteExecutor._check_dwell_complete(train, route, exec_state)`) before any covered-task or planning work; if dwell does not complete on this pass, the run is dwelling/on hold and the rest of Phase 1 returns without new path or departure planning. The dwell-complete helper is owned by Phase 1 only and is never invoked from Phase 3 or from `RouteExecutor.update(...)`.
- If a still-live path's `authority_end_block_id` is still short of the route-owned `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` for the live planning chain), `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` must keep retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same path using the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` as the armed requested target. If a fresh resolve for that same `walk_position` would produce a different target **block** than the row records, Phase 1 must latch cancel-and-replan per **Live-path target re-resolution** instead of extending toward the changed target. Arming the *next* task's target on a live path is ordinary progress and uses `extend_path(...)` normally. Each retry passes that stored pair unchanged; Phase 1 must not resolve the direction again while the same block stays armed, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. It must not request a brand-new path while `received_path_plan` is still present.
- More generally, dynamic best-effort extension is allowed in any controller state except `OFF`. The common zero-clearance retry case is when ATC is holding the train at the live-authority boundary, but if a still-live path remains short of its requested target while the train is still moving or otherwise stopped under authority, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` may proactively retry `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on later ticks without waiting for a state change, as long as it does not create a second live path.
- `RouteExecutor` does not call any stop-position resolver and does not pass stop centimeters into dispatcher. Route callers pass only the resolved block/direction request contract.
- The authority-acquisition step (**Planning Kernel** step `66aa`) is the route executor's normal "make progress toward the current task" entry point. The same function is used for the initial departure attempt and for retries after `PathDispatcher` has cancelled/revoked the previous path once the train has reached a fresh stopped no-authority state. If the cancellation stop escalated into EB, that retry point occurs only after `TrainController.eb.release()` has returned the controller to `READY` according to cab selection; active-path completion still comes back from `PathDispatcher`, not from `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` polling for end-of-path.
- `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly whenever live authority exists for the current route target, regardless of `control_mode`. There is no route-level `departure_mode` policy and no `departure_requested` latch in v1; the ATO-vs-manual behavioral difference is owned inside `TrainController.depart()` itself per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- After EB release, the EB-interrupted path has already been cancelled by the EB activation flow. `RouteExecutor` must wait for EB release, then make a fresh `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)` decision from the current route state; it must not try to reuse that cancelled path. Once fresh authority exists, the next eligible Phase 1 calls `TrainController.depart()` directly per the same rule.

- **Departure-to-waypoint flow**

- The canonical step ordering and gating for one Phase 1 pass live in the **Planning Kernel** subsection (steps `9c4f`–`66aa`). This subsection only states the manual-vs-ATO behaviour at depart-acceptance time and the per-controller-state guards on the authority-acquisition step; it does not redefine the kernel's step order, eligibility precondition, latch checks, or per-step writes. Where this subsection's prose appears to differ from the kernel, the kernel wins.
- `RouteExecutor` owns task targeting; `PathDispatcher` owns authority acquisition and terminal authority geometry; `TrainController` owns stop/braking behavior from that authority geometry using the controller safety margin. The departure flow is: resolve task block/direction, request or extend dispatcher authority as needed, and call `TrainController.depart()` directly once live authority exists.
- The stop, covered-stop, and dwell-entry rules in this subsection apply only to tasks with `task_kind == "stop"`. Tasks with `task_kind == "waypoint"` do not call arrival/dwell helpers; they constrain route geometry only.
- A `task_kind == "waypoint"` task uses the same block/direction resolution rules and the same point-to-point create/extend contract as a `stop` task. The difference is route behavior only: when that task's resolved target is reached, `RouteExecutor` advances immediately without dwell and leaves the next target to the next planning tick.
- A longer multi-task route is implemented as repeated point-to-point segments across later planning ticks. A pathfinder call never receives "go through all remaining tasks"; after one task target has been resolved and physical route progress actually consumes that target, a later `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` request is made from the then-current live authority end to the next resolved task. Physical route progress still waits for `waypoint` head entry or `stop` arrival.
- `RouteExecutor` owns task-chain progression on the route side. `PathDispatcher` grows authority geometry; it does not own task progression. A `waypoint` task is consumed only when the train head first enters that task's resolved block. Live `resource_sequence` coverage may be inspected for diagnostics or extension planning, but coverage alone does not advance `current_waypoint_index` and does not retarget dispatcher authority away from the currently armed task. Tail-based resource release is a separate physical-lifecycle concern owned by tail-vacate observations (detector-confirmed for blocks per **Power**, Block occupancy detection; controller-reported traversal for switches) and dispatcher-mediated resource release.
- For a train departing from rest under route control, `RouteExecutor` does not try to grow authority past the currently armed task inside the same departure call. Departure uses the current live authority only; later ticks may extend or recreate authority after physical route progress advances and the next task becomes the armed target.
- The route-side already-covered handling has two shapes, both in Phase 1's covered-task index advance. For `task_kind == "waypoint"` targets it is a skip: when the covered-waypoint test in that canonical home finds the current `waypoint` task covered, `RouteExecutor` treats that task as already missed/skipped, does not request a `create_path(...)` for it, and immediately advances route planning toward the next task on the current directed route walk. For `task_kind == "stop"`, coverage finishes the task only in the covered-stop case (train stopped, no active path, head in the destination block — recorded by Phase 1's covered-task index advance, its canonical home); while the train is rolling or a live path exists, a stop is served only by route-committed stop arrival. Operator/manual reposition uses the ordinary one-off route flow as a `stop` task and follows this same rule.
- `RouteExecutor` does not resolve or compare stop-position targets at all. For any `stop` task that still needs authority, it passes only `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction`. Dispatcher stores authority geometry and the controller owns safety-margin braking from that geometry. The route/task resolution step must still provide `requested_destination_direction` as a route-level constraint; dispatcher must not guess it from local geometry, and must not treat it as the exact terminal frame by itself.
- `RouteExecutor` uses `RouteRun.effective_acquisition_mode` for both `create_path(...)` and any later `extend_path(...)` retries while it is still pursuing that same planning chain. `AcquisitionMode.best_effort` is therefore a route/run policy choice, not a one-off branch invented mid-chain.
- For a `waypoint` task, the create/extend chain is still block-based: the current per-attempt resolved target still carries `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` just like a `stop` task. The difference is not the path API shape; the difference is that once the train head enters that task's resolved block, `RouteExecutor` consumes the `waypoint` immediately, advances the route cursor, and leaves the next create/extend step to a later planning tick.
- The canonical create-vs-extend decision for `RouteExecutor` is driven by `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` presence, not by any route-side re-evaluation of whether the current waypoint is reachable on an empty map. For the currently armed waypoint:
  - if `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is None`, the train has no live authority, so `RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.create_path(train_id=..., ...)`
  - if `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None`, the train is already on a live path, so `RouteExecutor` must call `PathDispatcher.extend_path(path_id=controller.path_projection.received_path_plan.path_id, ...)` on that same `path_id` rather than synthesizing a second path
  Because `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` is derived from the dispatcher-owned active path, this rule is well-defined in v1: a cancelled/completed path transitions out of `ACTIVE`, so the next controller read returns no live plan and the next planning tick naturally falls into the `create_path(...)` branch. A pass-through waypoint at which the train happens to be stopped with no live plan is therefore a `create_path(...)` case, not an `extend_path(...)` case.
- When live authority exists for this train (`PathDispatcher.active_path_id_by_train(train_id) is not None`), the authority-acquisition step must treat that live authority as the current route path and must not request a second `create_path(...)`. It calls `extend_path(active_path_id, ...)`. Any `success=True` result leaves the existing path valid, so Phase 1 keeps it, and a result with `reached_target = False` is retried later. A `success=False` structural failure (`no_viable_path`) instead latches `pending_path_cancel_request`, stops planning, and waits for the ordinary Phase-3 cancel/callback/recovery flow before any fresh path request.
- If `PathDispatcher.create_path(..., acquisition_mode=best_effort)` returns a live path whose current authorized target is still short of the route's current planning target, that is not a failure. It is a degraded but valid live authority: let the controller treat it as normal movement authority toward the current authorized end, and let `RouteExecutor` keep retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on the same `path_id` toward that same pending waypoint, passing the stored `resolved_target` request contract unchanged on each retry (per **Live-path target re-resolution**), on later ticks in any controller state except `OFF`. A best-effort create request counts as success whenever it creates one new path record with a non-empty block-terminated live `resource_sequence`; a best-effort extend request counts as success whenever the live path is still a valid authority toward the requested target afterwards — whether it grew, was already at the target, or is temporarily held back by live resource ownership.
- Once such a live path exists, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly when this target is otherwise route-ready. Any immediate zero-clearance hold is controller-owned and comes from lookahead/ATC against that live authority rather than from a special route-side suppression rule.
- On successful path, do not write train-side path cache fields from `RouteExecutor`. `PathDispatcher` owns the authoritative path-progress view; any controller/UI-facing path tiles or path-progress reads must be read as a projection of dispatcher state rather than as a train mirror.
- Do not set destination-block fields directly on `Train` or `TrainController` from `RouteExecutor`; controller authority comes only from the live `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` projection.
- `PathDispatcher` owns the `Path` record underlying `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan`; `controller.assigned_path_id` must be only a derived read of `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan.path_id`, so `RouteExecutor` does not write that field.
- On any successful `create_path(...)` / `extend_path(...)` call, `RouteExecutor` must update route-owned state for the immediate live task carried by that path: set/keep `current_route_status = RUNNING`, set `direction` from the accepted projection, and write `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (where `walk_position` is the index returned by `_find_next_task_target(...)` for this attempt) to the target pair that call returned, per the **`current_route_status` write contract**. Later planning retries against the same live path call `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` again and use the returned `walk_position` plus its stored `resolved_target`. If a fresh resolve for the same walk-position would change that row's target **block** while authority is live, Phase 1 uses cancel-and-replan rather than rewriting the live target under the existing path; while the same block stays armed, Phase 1 passes the stored pair into the next `extend_path(...)` call and must not resolve the direction again, per **Live-path target re-resolution**. Returned `resource_sequence` geometry alone does not advance `current_waypoint_index`, which remains physical route progress, and v1 does not arm a second waypoint target on the same tick.
- After any successful `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` that reports `appended_resource_count > 0`, `RouteExecutor` keeps the same currently armed waypoint/stop because `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` keeps returning the same `walk_position` until physical progress consumes that task. If the grown path is still short of that target, later retries continue toward the stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target`. If the grown path now reaches that target, the next call to `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` still returns that same `walk_position` until physical route progress consumes the waypoint through head-entry callback, route-committed stop arrival, or explicit skip. The result payload by itself does not advance physical route progress; `current_waypoint_index` still advances only when the train actually reaches/consumes that waypoint.
- `TrainController.depart()` is the controller's departure-acceptance command once a valid path plan exists. `RouteExecutor` calls it directly on every eligible Phase 1 pass with live authority, regardless of `control_mode`. ATO vs manual effects on acceptance are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- When `TrainController.depart()` returns `success == False`, the caller must treat that as a failed departure attempt, not as movement. The controller leaves itself in its current stopped state, records or emits the diagnostic `reason` through `NotificationCenter`, and the next planning tick re-evaluates from current controller state and `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan` presence. If authority was cleared before the call, the normal create-path branch handles recovery on that later tick; if EB or power/state made departure invalid, the existing EB/power/stopped-state rules decide when retry is allowed.

**Exact departure calls**

- Every `RouteExecutor` access to a train's controller goes through `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train.train_id)` (the shared public accessor) rather than a private `_get_controller` helper or any direct dictionary lookup.
- If the covered-waypoint test in Phase 1's covered-task index advance finds a `task_kind == "waypoint"` task covered, the authority-acquisition step must not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` for that `waypoint` task. Phase 1's covered-task index advance has already advanced past it by recording `task_runs[i].finished_at` (which makes the derived `current_waypoint_index` advance) before the depart helper is reached, so the helper continues planning toward the next task on the current directed route walk. If the resolved target block for a `task_kind == "stop"` task is the block the train's head already sits in, Phase 1's covered-task index advance has already finished it whenever the covered-stop conditions held (train stopped, no active path, head in the destination block, per the covered-task index advance canonical home); the authority-acquisition step therefore only ever sees such a covered stop while the train is rolling or a live path exists, and in that case it makes no create/extend call for it — the stop is served by the normal **Stop-arrival check** and the release of the live path that follows it.
- For any `stop` task that still needs authority, `RouteExecutor` does not compute or compare a centimeter-level local stopping target. It passes only the task's `requested_destination_block_id` and `requested_destination_direction` into `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)`.
- Explicit operator repositions are route-backed in v1 through the ordinary one-off `Route` flow with no special run-level flag. A direct operator/API local-reposition request (the Quick Route tool) constructs a `RouteMode.ONE_OFF` route with a single `task_kind == "stop"` task whose `task_location` is a `BlockTarget`, `PlatformTarget`, or `StationTarget` for the destination, and stores the request's `manual_override` (default `False`) on that route per the **Route data** table. Because a single-task reposition route has only one stop, start-mode seeding (see **Run start-mode seeding**) resolves to that task and `Route.tasks[0]` is the first executable target. `RouteTask.task_direction` for the destination follows the same task-authoring rule as any other route; the operator/API construction step is responsible for resolving "current cab/running direction" into a concrete `"forward"` / `"reverse"` value before the route is stored, because dispatcher never receives `"either"`. If that direction cannot be derived at construction time, the operator/API request fails before any route is stored. The run is executed through the ordinary Phase 1 / `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` flow. If the destination stop block is already covered when planning runs, the covered-stop rule applies: once the train is stopped with no active path, Phase 1's covered-task index advance finishes the stop in place and the run completes without creating authority. These routes carry no separate target-position field; the controller uses the standard safety-margin stop logic.
- When the current attempt uses `AcquisitionMode.best_effort`, test the live path against the run's own armed target each tick with `Path.reaches_target(task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target.requested_destination_block_id)`. If that is false, keep calling `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on that same `path_id` using the stored `resolved_target` request contract for the same pending waypoint; do not synthesize a new path.
- If the authority-acquisition step sees current live authority already present on the controller (`TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan is not None`), do not call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` again. First perform any eligible `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` retry for the currently armed target; after that create/extend decision is complete for this Phase 1 pass, call `TrainController.depart()` directly for that current live authority. ATO vs manual effects are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.
- `RouteExecutor` does not write `current_path`, `path_index`, `train.assigned_path_id`, `controller.ato_path_id`, or any destination field. `PathDispatcher` owns path-progress; `controller.assigned_path_id` is derived from the live `received_path_plan` projection.
- Route-driven movement requires a dispatcher path plan and reaches departure acceptance only through a direct `TrainController.depart()` call by `RouteExecutor`. The controller infers "no path authority" from an empty `received_path_plan`.
- If initial path creation fails before departure in `all_or_nothing` mode, `RouteExecutor` must emit one error event with `kind = "warning"` and `origin = "planning_failed"` carrying that route/path error per **Error events** and keep the train in `READY`; the train never had authority, so no special controller waiting command is needed.
- In `best_effort` mode, only "no authority at all could be created" is a creation failure. A truncated but live path is not a failure path; the train may run under that current authority and later wait at the current authority boundary while `RouteExecutor` keeps retrying `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` on the same `path_id`.
- On cancel/revoke, the latched cancel commits once the train is stopped: `PathDispatcher` transitions `Path.status` to `CANCELLED` (the controller's `received_path_plan` property then returns `None` on its next read; a still-rolling train is first stopped by the zero-limit / EB protocol in step `826a`), and recovery follows the canonical recovery table. Phase 1 is gated by `controller.is_recovery_clean`; it never reads the cancellation `error`. If the next planning attempt fails, fall through to the ordinary pre-departure route/path error path — no recovery-only state machine.

**Arrival detection**

- The **Stop-arrival check** owns arrival observation for a `stop` task, and `RouteExecutor` owns the commit. There is no separate platform-position arrival gate.
- Step `bc55` records arrival by finishing the matched stop's walk-position through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, sampling its own write timestamp (per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**). That sets `task_runs[walk_position].finished_at` and the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position`. It does not call `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`; that transition happens in the next eligible `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-entry step.
- `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()` is the controller-side dwell-entry transition called by `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` (Phase 1). The controller does not detect dwell entry on its own; it never reads `RouteRun`. The transition is valid only from the **`READY` state**. This call is the single writer of the dwell row's `started_at`, which it samples per **Time sampling** under the **Units contract**.
- On the next eligible tick after `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` records the stop's `finished_at`, `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` performs the dwell-entry step: when `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true, the route's next non-finished task has `task_kind == "dwell"`, and the predecessor task's `finished_at` is set, `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. That `enter_station_dwell()` call samples the dwell row's `started_at`, per the **RouteTaskRun schema**, so the dwell window measures time standing at the platform rather than time since the predecessor task finished. The train does not plan a new target while dwell is pending or active.
- The dwell-complete check is a Phase 1 step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)`, invoked before covered-task index advance. It runs only while controller state is `RUNNING_AT_STATION`. It calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to live-resolve `walk_position` (the walk-position of the next non-finished task on the directed walk), then calls `RouteTask.should_depart(...)` with the arguments **Task time semantics** defines, sampling each clock it names at the moment of the check.
- Stop arrival sets `finished_at` for that stop directly through `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, which makes the derived `current_waypoint_index` advance. The stop task is independent of its neighbors: a following `dwell` task (when present) is a separate row with its own `started_at` / `finished_at` lifecycle; a `stop` with no following `dwell` simply has no dwell-entry / dwell-complete steps to run, and the next eligible Phase 1 either plans the next task or calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` for terminal completion.
- A route is complete only after the terminal task sequence (stop + any following dwell) has finished. Route completion handling never fires before the terminal dwell (if any) exits.
- If dwell/timing says depart, the Phase 1 dwell-complete step calls `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(walk_position, current_time)` (the canonical writer — it records the dwell row's `finished_at`, computes `delay_class`, and appends the next walk-position; the derived `current_waypoint_index` advances to `walk_position`, and the next task's derived `started_at` automatically reflects this `finished_at`), sets `exec_state.direction` from the route walk, and calls `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train.train_id).finish_dwell()`. The same Phase 1 then continues into target resolution for the next task via a fresh `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` call.
- If no task remains after the completed stop+dwell pair, the same Phase 1 pass continues to step `c481`, whose `None` branch reaches step `592a` — the owner of terminal-walk completion — and that step calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` directly inside `tick_train_phase1(...)` and returns for that tick. `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` performs the route-completion teardown (set `current_route_status = FINISHED`, possibly trigger `route.deactivate()` for one-off routes with no remaining assigned trains) per the **`current_route_status` write contract**. `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)` automatically returns `None`. Dwell exit (`finish_dwell()`) has already run before this call; the `RouteRun` is preserved with `current_route_status = FINISHED` indefinitely as a historical record.
- `route.deactivate()` is the admin-driven entry point for switching off a shared `Route` while one or more assigned `RouteRun`s have not yet finished. It sets `Route.is_active = False` and walks every assigned train, writing `pending_route_deactivate` on each train's controller per the inclusive-latch rule defined in the **Latch Table** preamble. `route.deactivate()` is idempotent; calling it on an already-inactive route with no assigned trains is a no-op. The actual per-train teardown happens in each train's later Phase 3 `pending_route_deactivate` consumer, which calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)` after the inclusive cancel has committed; the helper writes `current_route_status = DEACTIVATED` and (when applicable) calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()` first. There is no separate `RouteRun.deactivate()` method, and `route.deactivate()` does not delete any `RouteRun` — the runs are kept as historical `DEACTIVATED` records.
- `finish_dwell()` is a **dwell-exit state-transition command only**, named `finish_dwell` rather than `depart_from_station` because it does not start movement. It transitions the controller out of `RUNNING_AT_STATION` to the **`READY` state**. The normal caller is `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` dwell-complete step. `RouteExecutor` is still responsible for deciding whether more route work remains, clearing route assignment when complete, and removing the execution-state entry; those are route-level bookkeeping, not controller state.
  `TrainController.finish_dwell()` must not touch `received_path_plan` (the property is derived from the dispatcher-owned active path; authority for the served target already transitioned out of `ACTIVE` in `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(...)` before dwell began), must not call `TrainController.depart()` / `TrainController.ato.go()`, and must not initiate physics movement. The next target's route-side restart happens in the same Phase 1 (after dwell exit) or on a later tick through the authority-acquisition step -> `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` and then `RouteExecutor` calls `TrainController.depart()` directly once live authority exists. ATO vs manual effects are owned per the **`TrainController.depart()` canonical contract**.

**Dispatcher completion handling**

**route path-event callback schema**

- This is the single canonical callback contract for `PathDispatcher -> RouteExecutor` path-terminal notifications. Dispatcher notifies the route on both terminal transitions: `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` calls `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)` after release, and `PathDispatcher.cancel_path(...)` calls `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` after a committed cancellation.
- The canonical callback signatures are exactly:
  - `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(train_id, path_id, walk_position)`
  - `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, error, was_deviation=False, was_run_completion=False)`
  Parameters are listed positionally above; callers may pass them by name. No other parameter is accepted in v1. Dispatcher derives the two mutually-exclusive booleans from the structured cancel-request fields, never from anything inside `error`. `was_deviation` feeds `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation`; `was_run_completion` suppresses the ordinary `path_cancelled` diagnostic writes because step `6a0d` owns that successful progress event. Neither callback carries a timestamp parameter: every route-side timestamp this chain records is sampled fresh with `time.time()` at the write site per **Time sampling**. `PathDispatcher` independently samples its own status timestamp.
- The callback payloads are exactly:
  - cancellation context: `train_id`, `path_id`, `error`, `was_deviation`, `was_run_completion`
  This callback is invoked synchronously by `PathDispatcher` immediately after committing the path-status transition, so its `path_id` always identifies the just-terminated path. There is no path-id correlation step.
- In that cancellation context:
  - `error` is diagnostic context only and must not be used for branching. UI/log consumers that need the originating kind subscribe to the `cancel_path` event on `NotificationCenter` (its `error.origin` field carries the writer-supplied label). v1 route-side recovery does not branch on the originating kind. EB recovery timing is gated by controller state (Phase 1 cannot run until `eb.release()` has returned the controller to `READY`); power-off teardown is driven by `pending_power_off` and the controller transitioning to `OFF`; every other case shares the same recovery shell.
- The single v1 recovery table for cancellation / mismatch cases is:

  | Case | Detection point | Immediate effect | Next eligible Phase 1 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | any cancellation (movement-derived deviation, or explicit operator cancel / revoke / EB) (`7fa0`) | `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(...)` | preserve only confirmed physical progress, set `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` and `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation` from the callback's `was_deviation` argument (the derived `display_state` then reads `"DEVIATED"` for a movement deviation and `"CANCELLED"` for operator cancel / revoke / EB) | rerun covered-task index advance plus `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` from current physical position once `controller.is_recovery_clean` is true; the next task's behavior follows normal task-kind rules |
  | live-path target re-resolution invalidates a live path — a fresh resolve for the walk-position the live authority was acquired for would yield a different target **block** than that row records (`162a`); advancing to the next task on the walk is not this case, and neither is a direction-only change for the same block — see the live-path target re-resolution paragraph below for why | Phase 1 latch + Phase 3 cancel / replan flow | let the cancel callback set `path_cancelled` per the cancellation row of the **`current_route_status` write contract** | rerun ordinary planning under the same `route_id` (the `RouteRun` is kept, never deactivated); later Phase 1 calls `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` afresh |
  | EB interruption (`eddc`) | EB cancellation plus release flow | latch `pending_path_cancel_request` (`error.origin = "eb"`); the live path stays `ACTIVE` until Phase 3 commits the cancel once the train is stopped | the `RUNNING_MOVING` → stop → `eb.release()` chain naturally returns the controller to `READY`, at which point Phase 1 replans normally |
  | power off (`dc91`) | power-off cleanup flow | latch `pending_power_off` (plus the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` when a live path exists); Phase 3 commits the cancel once stopped, then removes the `RouteRun` and performs OFF cleanup | the `RouteRun` no longer exists and the controller is `OFF`; Phase 1 has nothing to do |

- `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` must follow the step `09b0` release order in the **Stop-arrival procedure**: release not-yet-entered block reservations, release not-yet-entered switch/flank locks, transition the `Path` to `COMPLETED` (which by definition makes `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)` return `None` for this train and makes the controller's `received_path_plan` property return `None` on its next read), then call `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, which performs the step `bc55` commit.
- Every live authority path has a matching `RouteRun` in v1 (including one-off operator routes). The callback can rely on `RouteRun` existing.
- The callback does not write `Train` fields — authority residue lives on dispatcher records and controller-facing projections.
- `PathDispatcher` clears controller authority by transitioning `Path.status` to `COMPLETED`. The controller's `received_path_plan` property then returns `None` on its next read, and the derived reads `controller.assigned_path_id`, `controller.ato_path_id`, and `controller.ato_destination_block_id` disappear automatically.
- Controller destination-block/path release must remain dispatcher-owned: dispatcher transitions `Path.status` and the controller's `received_path_plan` property automatically reads the new state. The controller reaches the **`READY` state** through its reactive stopped/no-authority rule rather than through a push callback. `RouteExecutor` remains responsible for entering dwell (`RUNNING_AT_STATION`) on the next eligible Phase 1 and for marking the dwell task finished in the same Phase 1 dwell-complete step.
- The **Stop-arrival check** identifies the stop being served as the train's next non-finished task, so no search over remaining task indexes is required. `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` remains the authoritative record of which platform/block that stop was targeted at.
- `RouteExecutor.handle_path_cancellation(train_id, path_id, error, was_deviation=False, was_run_completion=False)` is the cancellation-side counterpart. The lower-to-upper ordering is canonical: dispatcher finishes its path/resource mutation first, then hands the mutually-exclusive classifications upward. For `was_run_completion == True`, the callback returns after correlation/diagnostic validation and writes no `path_cancelled`, `path_cancel_was_deviation`, progress cursor, or task timing field; successful run progress remains owned by Phase 1 step `6a0d`, which finishes the task only after observing stopped/no-authority state. For every ordinary cancellation, the callback sets `RouteRun.path_cancelled = True` and `RouteRun.path_cancel_was_deviation = was_deviation`, and never chooses a fresh target or creates authority. The derived display reads `"DEVIATED"` for a movement deviation and `"CANCELLED"` for another ordinary authority cancellation until Phase 1 cleanup consumes the diagnostic latch. The callback never advances a task or synthesizes arrival.
- Every ordinary cancellation source (movement-derived deviation, operator cancel, revoke, EB, power-off) shares that ordinary `handle_path_cancellation(...)` branch; runtime gates produce their recovery timing differences. Normal run-completion release is the one explicit non-recovery branch, selected only by `was_run_completion`, not by anything inside the `ErrorRecord`:
  - the Phase 1 eligibility precondition only allows replanning when the controller is in `READY`, which naturally defers EB recovery until `eb.release()` has run;
  - the power-off Phase 3 cleanup separately calls `RouteExecutor.remove_train_route_run(train_id)`, which flips the run to its historical `DEACTIVATED` row so no later Phase 1 has anything to replan; `handle_path_cancellation(...)` still records the same diagnostic state, but the run is unassigned moments later by the same Phase 3 cleanup.
  Planning and routing logic rely on these runtime gates (controller state, latches, presence of `RouteRun`, current span coverage, and task-kind rules).
- **Live-path target re-resolution** uses this same temporary-status-clear model only after the current live path has already left `ACTIVE`. The trigger is a resolve that changes under a live path, not ordinary forward movement along the walk. Concretely: when a fresh resolve for the walk-position the live authority was acquired for would yield a different target **block** than that row's stored `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` records — a platform turned `disabled` or `manual`, station membership changed, the task was retargeted — the live path must be treated as potentially stale, because the system cannot prove the already-acquired authority still serves the task. Advancing along the walk is not this case: finishing a task and arming the next one yields a different `walk_position` and a different target block every time, and the **Waypoint lookahead** arms a target ahead of the train's current task by design. Neither latches a cancel. Phase 1 therefore latches `pending_path_cancel_request` with `error.origin = "revoked"` (system/non-operator-driven), stops planning for that train, and waits for the ordinary Phase 3 cancel/callback/route-deviation-cleanup flow. This keeps the same `RouteRun`; it is NOT a structural `Route.tasks` edit, which always tears the run down to `DEACTIVATED` via `set_route_deactivate(...)` per the **Shared-route edit teardown**. Operator-driven retargeting and explicit route swap take a different path entirely (abort current run + create new run, see **Route Edit And Reconciliation**); the inclusive cancel for that workflow uses `error.origin = "operator_cancelled"` per the **Latch Table** preamble. Only after the live path is no longer active may `RouteExecutor` clear or rewrite the affected route-owned temporary target state and restart ordinary planning from the train's current position under the reconciled route.
- **Direction is resolved once per armed target block.** Phase 1 resolves `requested_destination_direction` only when it arms a target block the live path does not already hold as its requested target. While the same block stays armed, every `extend_path(...)` retry passes the stored `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` pair unchanged, and Phase 1 must not resolve the direction again for it. This is the canonical rule; the extend-identity rule under **Path extension** and the Phase 1 steps `592a` / `66aa` refer back here.
- **Why this shape.** A `task_direction == "either"` task resolves its block-relative direction against the train's current span (the `requested_destination_direction` rule under **RouteTask data**), and that span moves every tick, so resolving a live target again lets the direction drift away from the terminal frame the path already acquired. The stored pair is what the **Stop-arrival check** compares the live authority's terminal block against, and it is also what every extend retry passes back in. Resolving once per armed block keeps the run's stored pair and the frame the path acquired the same value for the life of that target.
- A same-block direction change is therefore not something Phase 1 can produce against a live path, and it does not trigger the live-path invalidation above. Arming a **new** target block is a fresh resolve, and it is the only route-side direction resolution that runs while a path is live. When that new block is one the live path already holds, `extend_path(...)` succeeds with `appended_resource_count = 0` / `reached_target = True` and reports the frame it holds rather than the direction Phase 1 passed (per **Path extension**), which is why Phase 1 writes `task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` from what the call returned rather than what it passed, per Planning Kernel step `66aa`. `extend_path(...)` still rejects a direction that would require the train to reverse at the current authority end (`failure_kind = no_viable_path`, per **Path extension**), and that rejection is itself a structural extend failure: `RouteExecutor` latches the same `error.origin = "revoked"` cancel for it (per the structural-`failure_kind` rule under **Exact `RouteExecutor` tick calls**), so a new target whose direction is incompatible with the live authority still ends in cancel-and-replan.
- Completion handling may observe a train still physically occupying the terminal block or switch footprint. That is not a contradiction: `RouteExecutor` records arrival at the **Stop-arrival check**, while physical resource release still waits for the later tail-vacate callbacks.

## Train Entity

**Train ownership**

- `Train` is the persistent train-data entity. In v1 it keeps these fields: identity, consist (including length), `max_speed`, `total_distance_cm` and `total_run_time_seconds` (runtime-written persisted counters; rules and single writers in **Maintenance counters**), `min_radius_cm` (the train's minimum curve radius in centimetres, read by the pathfinder's curve-radius feasibility filter — see the pathfinding home), `max_slope` (the train's maximum climbable grade as a percent, `0.0` meaning unspecified, read by the pathfinder's slope feasibility filter), `consist_length_cm` (the train's physical length in centimetres, used to build its span when it is placed and read by the pathfinder's own-length feasibility filter), `is_unidentified` (`False` on every train the operator places, `True` only on a runtime-created **unidentified train**; the runtime sets it at creation, and the one thing that clears it is `SimulationController.adopt_unidentified_train(...)`. It is the predicate every rule about unidentified trains tests — see **Power**, Unidentified trains), and other non-live persistent train data already on the entity. Route assignment is not one of them. Consumers ask `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)`. That call prefers the live `RouteExecutor.execution_state_for(train_id).route_id` (the derived RUNNING-`RouteRun` read, not a stored dict — see **Route-run state**), and falls back to scanning `Route.assigned_train_ids` in the route registry (at most one route holds the train, per the single-assignment rule in **Route assignment writers**). The active route is structurally encoded by the live `RouteRun` in `execution_states`, so no per-train sync is needed.
- All live runtime state lives on `TrainController`: `current_span` (live physical span, including end locators and ordered occupied tiles), `current_speed_kmh`, operational `state`, power/cab, ATO/path projections, and operational diagnostics. `TrainPhysics` computes movement and sends `span_update` deltas to the controller, and the controller applies those updates to its own storage. Live target speed and speed-limit reason are reads from controller lookahead at distance 0. There is no `manual_target_speed`, `train.state`, `train.assigned_path_id`, `train.ato_destination_block_id`, `train.current_path`, `train.path_index`, or `train.reserved_blocks` field.
- Initial placement of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` is owned by `SimulationController.add_train(...)`. It constructs the train, builds the train's initial physical span from the placement input (reusing the repo's placement-to-position derivation, which produces precise `end_a` / `end_b` locators plus `occupied_tiles`), obtains the controller, and writes the span directly into `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before any simulation tick runs. When the placement input names a `RosterEntry` (see **Train Roster**), `add_train(...)` seeds the new `Train`'s persistent fields and its `SpeedProfile` from that entry; without one it builds the train from blank persistent defaults and seeds the default `SpeedProfile` described in **SpeedProfile**. `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` does NOT seed the span; it only makes sure the controller object exists and is registered.
- Persistence-loading code also calls `add_train(...)` with the saved placement payload for each restored train; there is no parallel `seed_train_span(...)` helper.
- An **unidentified train** is the one `Train` the runtime builds for itself rather than through `SimulationController.add_train(...)`: the Step-0 detector preamble creates it when a block reads current that no existing train accounts for. It is built from blank persistent train defaults with the default `SpeedProfile`, and that profile is never used because an unidentified train is never powered. Its full contract is in **Power**, Unidentified trains.
- After initial placement, `TrainPhysics` is the only normal producer of movement `span_update` values, and `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` is the only normal writer of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`. Live movement updates never write back to `Train`.


## TrainController

`TrainController` is a multi-module train computer that mirrors a real cab and on-board control system. There is one per train. Inside, it is made of 10 separate sub-modules organized into three layers: input, compute, and output. Code that touches one sub-module must not need to load the others.

**Sub-modules and where to find them in this chapter.**

*Input layer — data feeds into the controller:*

- **Operator command surface** — queue and drain order plus the setter / queue methods themselves: `controller.commands.set_cab(...)`, `controller.commands.set_mode(...)`, `controller.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`, `controller.commands.set_handle(...)`, `controller.commands.set_manual_power_percent(...)`, `controller.commands.power_on_cmd()`, `controller.commands.power_off_cmd()`, `controller.commands.enqueue_command(...)`, and `controller.commands.clear_cab()` (the cab-owned ungated clear used only by `TrainController.complete_power_off()` during OFF cleanup — it sets `cab = None` with no gate, is not an operator command, and is never queued, distinct from the precondition-gated `set_cab(...)`), plus `controller.eb.activate()` and `controller.eb.release()`. Lives in `train_controller/commands.py` (class `OperatorCommands`); the operator-command queue and drain order are stored on this sub-module and accessed externally as `controller.commands.command_inbox` and `controller.commands.drain_order`. The cab and mode selections it sets are also stored on this sub-module and accessed externally as `controller.commands.cab` and `controller.commands.control_mode` per the **Controller retained field schema** (serialized under the keys `"cab"` and `"control_mode"`). The setter / queue methods orchestrate writes across multiple sub-modules (eb, ato, manual, route_hooks, physics_sync, ops) through the controller back-reference. See **Power/cab/mode command contracts** and **Driver/operator inputs** below.
- **Path plan projection** — live read-only projection of the dispatcher-owned `Path` into `received_path_plan`. Lives in `train_controller/path_projection.py` (class `PathProjection`). No owned fields — every read recomputes from `path_dispatcher.get_active_path(train_id)` (or other live sources) and auto-flips to `None` when the dispatcher transitions the path away from `ACTIVE`. Accessed externally as `controller.path_projection.received_path_plan`, `controller.path_projection.assigned_path_id`, `controller.path_projection.ato_path_id`, `controller.path_projection.ato_destination_block_id`, and `controller.path_projection.get_live_path_data()`. See **received_path_plan schema**, **Dispatcher/route-executor inputs**, and **route/dispatcher command contracts** below.
- **Physics sync** — `current_span` and `current_speed_kmh` storage; `on_physics_updated()` handler; `ControllerProgressReport` generation. Lives in `train_controller/physics_sync.py` (class `PhysicsSync`). It owns the progress mailbox, the named within-tick `pending_detected_entered_blocks: Set[str]` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks: Set[str]` written in Step 0 and consumed/cleared by the final Phase-2 callback on a running pass or by `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` on a stopped pass, and `pending_block_exit_edges`. `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(train, controller)` writes the Step-0 synchronous span correction directly onto this sub-module's `current_span` before Phase 1; there is no separate forward-snap buffer and no `TrainController`-owned intermediary method. The orchestrator methods stay flat on the façade because the runtime and physics invoke them directly.
- **Route hooks** — external entry points called by other components: `enter_station_dwell()` and `finish_dwell()` (from `RouteExecutor`). Lives in `train_controller/route_hooks.py` (class `RouteHooks`); owns the four Phase-3 retained latches bundled under `PhaseLatches`. Accessed externally as `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches` (and the `set_*` writer helpers on that aggregate per the **Latch Table**). The orchestrator entry-point methods (`enter_station_dwell`, `finish_dwell`) stay flat on the façade because they are direct external callbacks; they delegate to this sub-module when latch writes are needed. See **route/dispatcher command contracts** and **Completion ownership and timing** below.

*Compute layer — the on-board computers:*

- **Operational state machine** — `state` field and transitions (`OFF` / `READY` / `RUNNING_MOVING` / `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) plus the controller-owned `service_stop_active` latch. Lives in `train_controller/ops.py` (class `OperationalStateMachine`); accessed externally as `controller.ops.state`, `controller.ops.service_stop_active`, `controller.ops.handle_position`, and `controller.ops.handle_reason` (diagnostic). State-transition methods, `TrainController.begin_service_stop()` / `consume_service_stop()`, the canonical stopped predicate `is_stopped()`, and related predicates stay on the façade because they orchestrate writes across multiple sub-modules. See **Controller state machine**, **Controller tick order**, the **Latch Table**, and the **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs** table.
- **ATC Computer** — live `lookahead` computation, ATC speed-limit application, overspeed-to-EB escalation. Lives in `train_controller/atc.py` (class `ATCComputer`); accessed externally as `controller.atc.safety_margin_cm` (the `track_end_safety_margin_cm` field in **Runtime settings**) and `controller.atc.signal_lookahead_blocks` (the `atc_signal_lookahead_blocks` field in **Runtime settings**; scan depth). `controller.atc.clearance_ahead()`, `controller.atc.speed_limit_now()`, `controller.atc.speed_limit_with_code()`, `controller.atc.is_caution_ahead()`, `controller.atc.is_approaching_yellow_signal()`, `controller.atc.is_signal_red_for_block(block_id)`, and `controller.atc.effective_safety_margin()` are ATC-internal recompute helpers used to build the lookahead payload; they are not an external read surface, and no consumer outside `train_controller/atc.py` calls them for a target speed or reason code — the **canonical lookahead payload** is the single source of truth for that. The canonical lookahead payload is built by `TrainController.get_lookahead()` (`_compute_canonical_lookahead_impl`); `LookaheadBuilder` in `lookahead_builder.py` builds the per-block `LookaheadPoint` list projection. See **Lookahead is a pure derived value**, **canonical lookahead payload**, **ATC speed limit**, and **Speed curves** below.
- **ATO Computer** — handle-selection / curve-tracking / braking-notch logic, `ato.running` lifecycle, and depart-in-ATO acceptance. Lives in `train_controller/ato.py` (class `ATOComputer`); accessed externally as `controller.ato.running` (run-active flag), `controller.ato.info` (destination snapshot), `controller.ato.debug` (per-tick debug payload), `controller.ato.go()` / `controller.ato.stop()` (run lifecycle), `controller.ato.compute_handle(delta_seconds)` (per-tick handle selection), `controller.ato.compute_info()`, `controller.ato.sample_curve_at(curve, distance_cm, key)`, `controller.ato.simulate_forward(speed, handle, distance_cm, dt)`, `controller.ato.calculate_power_notch(...)`, and `controller.ato.calculate_braking_notch_for_speed(...)`. The module-level `rate_limit_handle(...)` helper lives in the same file. See **`ato.running` meaning and lifecycle** and **ATO handle calculation** below.
- **EB Workflow** — `eb.active`, EB activation effects, release contract, dwell exception. Lives in `train_controller/eb.py`; accessed as `controller.eb.active` (latch), `controller.eb.activate()` (operator-issued EB), `controller.eb.release()` (the gated operator clear), and `controller.eb.clear()` (the EB-owned ungated clear used only by `TrainController.complete_power_off()` during OFF cleanup — it sets `eb.active = False` with no gate, distinct from the precondition-gated `eb.release()`). These three methods are the only sites that assign `eb.active`; the full single-writer rule is Module boundaries rule 2 below. The canonical home is the **EB workflow state machine** table below in this section; controller-side tick handling appears in **Controller tick order** below.
- **Manual driving** — `simulation` and `power_percent` submode behavior; `handle_position`, `manual.manual_power_percent`. Lives in `train_controller/manual.py` (class `ManualDriving`); accessed externally as `controller.manual.manual_mode_type` and `controller.manual.manual_power_percent`. The operator-facing setters `set_manual_mode_type(...)` / `set_manual_power_percent(...)` live on the operator command surface and write through to these fields. See **Manual mode and movement-authority boundary** below.

*Output layer — what the computer writes:*

- **Notification emission** — the controller's adapter onto the one runtime event stream. Lives in `train_controller/notifications.py` (class `Notifications`, accessed as `controller.notifications`). It holds no queue and no state. `controller.notifications.emit(operation, kind, message, error=None, payload=None)` fills in `source = "TrainController"` and an `entities` list naming this train, then calls `NotificationCenter.emit(...)` per the **Event schema**; the caller passes its own function name as `operation`. Every controller-side report — each drained command's result, each acceptance/transition command's result (`depart()`, `enter_station_dwell()`, `finish_dwell()`), EB activation and release, latch writes through the **Writer-helper rule**, and the ATC reason-code change of **Controller tick order** substep `4d7c` — goes through this one adapter. Each of those emits on both outcomes: `kind = "info"` on acceptance and `"warning"` on rejection, with `{success, reason}` in `payload`. Those acceptance events are what the **Event log** records as "departed" and "entered station dwell". The frontend reads them back through `GET /api/trains/<train_id>/notifications`, which is a filter over the stream per **Runtime notifications** and empties nothing.

The Phase-3 latches owned by `TrainController` live nested under `TrainController.route_hooks.phase_latches: PhaseLatches` (fields: `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, `route_deactivate`) and are documented in the **Latch Table** in the Core Runtime Loop section. `TrainController` owns the in-memory storage; the Latch Table is the single canonical home for writers, helpers, consumers, gating predicates, and lifecycle.

**Module boundaries.** These rules consolidate the "only X writes Y" constraints that apply across the chapter:

1. The Operational state machine is the only writer of `state`.
2. The EB Workflow is the only writer of `eb.active`: `eb.activate()` sets it `True`, and `eb.release()` / `eb.clear()` set it `False`. The ATC overspeed and no-authority escalation inside `TrainController.tick(...)` raises EB by calling `controller.eb.activate()`, so it applies the **canonical EB activation effects** through that one routine instead of assigning the field. The activate gate (`eb.active == False`) can never reject that call, because tick-order step `d483` early-exits the tick whenever EB is already latched, before the ATC overspeed step `914f` runs. The Phase-3 OFF cleanup clears the flag through the EB-owned `controller.eb.clear()` helper rather than assigning the field itself. No site outside `train_controller/eb.py` assigns `eb.active`.
3. The ATC Computer is the only computation site for `lookahead`.
4. Phase-3 latches are written only through the writer-helpers listed in the **Latch Table** preamble; no sub-module assigns latch fields directly.
5. `handle_position` is written only through the Operational-state sub-module. Three sites produce a driving command, matching its dual role (the active handle command for both ATO and manual `simulation` mode): in manual `simulation` mode the Manual driving sub-module writes it; in ATO mode the ATO Computer writes it every tick from `controller.ato.compute_handle(...)` while a run is active (and writes the braking-to-stop notch otherwise); and the EB Workflow writes `handle_position = EB` as part of the canonical EB activation effects. `manual.manual_power_percent` storage and mutation stay encapsulated by the Manual driving sub-module: the operator setter writes through `manual.set_power_percent(...)`, while ATC, service-stop, power-off, and control-mode-change orchestration may call the sanctioned `manual.reset_power_percent_for_safety()` helper. The EB Workflow does not call it: EB holds the train through `handle_position = EB` plus the zero ATC limit, and the retained percentage is cleared by the driver to satisfy the **driver-control interlock** on `TrainController.eb.release()`. Every other write to `handle_position` is a neutralize, and goes through the sanctioned `ops.reset_handle_for_safety()` helper: `_apply_power_off_request()`, `set_manual_mode_type(...)` entering `power_percent`, tick step `b41e` service stopping, and `TrainController.complete_power_off()`. Tick step `d483` re-writing `handle_position = EB` each tick while the latch is set is the EB Workflow re-applying its own write, not a separate writer. Those safety workflows never assign the field directly, and no other writer exists.
6. The Operator command surface is the only entry point for external operator commands. `controller.eb.activate()` and `controller.eb.release()` belong to that surface even though they are called on `controller.eb`: they enqueue into the same command inbox and run at slots 6 and 7 of the canonical drain order. Apart from those two, no external caller invokes ATC, ATO, EB, or manual driving sub-modules directly.

**Data flow per tick.**

- *Input:* the Operator command surface drains queued commands at the start of `TrainController.tick(...)`; the Path plan projection refreshes on every read of `received_path_plan`; Physics sync absorbs `PhysicsSpanUpdate` from `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`; Route hooks fire when `RouteExecutor` or `PathDispatcher` calls them.
- *Compute:* the Operational state machine evaluates reactive transitions; the ATC Computer computes `lookahead`; the ATO Computer (in ATO mode) or Manual driving (in manual mode) produces the active command; the EB Workflow can override the result at any step.
- *Output:* Notification emission flows to `NotificationCenter`; Phase-3 latches are written when accepted commands or in-tick escalations need deferred dispatcher cancellation; controller-state writes land on the canonical retained fields.

**Controller state**

- `TrainController` owns live power/cab state: `state` (canonical operational state, including `OFF`), active cab selection, and the live direction selection implied by that cab. The convenience read `power_on` is derived from `state != "OFF"`; it has no separate writer. These are not re-synced from `Train` on every tick.
- `TrainController` owns the train's live physical span and speed. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` computes movement and emits `PhysicsSpanUpdate`; `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` is the normal writer that applies the new span/speed to controller-owned storage. `Train` itself does not store live position/span.
- `manual.manual_mode_type` holds the manual submode: `simulation` or `power_percent`.
- `TrainController.travel_direction` is a **read-only derived value**, computed each time it is read from `(self.cab, self.current_span)`. There is no stored field and there are no writers. The mapping rule works like this. The cab's physical end (`end_a` or `end_b` per `TrainSpan`) is the head. `travel_direction` is the network-frame direction that points from the opposite physical end, through the cab end, and onward into the next span resource ahead. When there is no next span resource ahead of the cab end (for example, the cab end is at a track-end, dead-end, or disabled-resource boundary, or the consist sits on a single tile with no live forward connectivity), `travel_direction` is the network-frame direction outward through the cab edge of the cab end's tile. This rule depends only on consist-and-cab geometry, not on `received_path_plan`. When `self.cab is None`, the value is `None`. A successful write to `cab` (`TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, or `TrainController.depart()` auto-cab-selection) writes only `cab`. It does not "atomically update" `travel_direction`, because there is no field to update; the next read of `travel_direction` recomputes from the new cab.
- `manual.manual_power_percent` is the `power_percent`-mode manual control input: an integer `0..100` voltage-style command rather than a signed traction/brake command.
- `phase_latches.power_off: bool` is the stopped-only shutdown latch — row `f8d5` in the **Latch Table**, which owns its writer, consumer, and gating rules. It is the canonical handoff that moves power-off from command acceptance into the standard per-train phase workflow.
- `TrainController` owns the per-train Phase-3 retained latch / flag aggregate in v1. The aggregate is `phase_latches: PhaseLatches`, with four fields: `path_cancel_request`, `route_abort`, `power_off`, and `route_deactivate`. `ControllerProgressReport` values are not retained controller fields. Phase 2 appends them to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`), which Phase 3 drains and clears before the train transaction ends. The retained-field schema below lists `phase_latches` as a single field; the four nested fields are documented inside the **Latch Table**. Dwell entry is NOT a flag. It is a state-machine transition driven by route state and controller state inside `TrainController.tick(...)`.
- The `READY` trigger, stated explicitly: `READY` is the controller's state when the train is powered on and stopped, no matter the cab selection. A controller enters `READY` from `OFF` when power is turned on. Selecting or deselecting a cab while stopped keeps the controller in `READY`. Route authority may still be assigned while a cab is unselected, and `RouteExecutor` must not treat a missing cab as a blocker for `create_path(...)`. `TrainController.depart()` accepts authority per its canonical contract; auto-cab selection on success is part of that contract and is not restated here. Manual input may move the train even before `depart()` is accepted (controller still in `READY`), but only when `received_path_plan is not None`, so the canonical lookahead in step `22a9` exposes a path-bound ATC limit at distance 0. Without a live path, the no-authority empty payload sets `atc_limit = 0` at distance 0, and ATC holds the train at rest until a route assigns authority. Pre-depart manual movement cannot satisfy path stop-completion until `controller.state == RUNNING_MOVING`. That state is reached EITHER by a successful `TrainController.depart()` acceptance OR by the reactive speed-based `READY → RUNNING_MOVING` transition inside `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` (see the **Reactive speed-based state evaluation** bullets and the `RUNNING_MOVING` state row); both are equally valid. Such pre-depart manual movement may still produce head-entry route progress, such as `task_kind == "waypoint"` consumption; only stop-completion is gated on `state == RUNNING_MOVING`.
- Manual driving is `control_mode == manual`; there is no separate `MANUAL` operational state.
- `assigned_path_id: Optional[str]` is a computed read-only property derived from `received_path_plan.path_id`; it is the controller-facing runtime read and is not stored or written independently.
- `received_path_plan` is the controller's read-only live movement-authority projection over the active dispatcher-owned path.
- `received_path_plan` is not stored controller state and has no writer. `PathDispatcher` owns the underlying `Path`; `TrainController` reads the projection and must not mutate path ownership or lifecycle.
- `TrainController` is also the canonical query and storage home for the train's current occupied span. The live span is produced by `TrainPhysics` and written into `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`. Block and switch resources still carry their own physical `occupied_by` ownership, but tile-level ends and ordered occupied tiles live on the controller span, not on `Train`. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` ask the controller for one cab-independent physical span read, instead of deriving it on their own from `Train`. That span read must expose the ordered occupied span in physical order (`occupied_blocks` and `occupied_tiles`), plus the two explicit physical end locators needed for later ordering comparisons. The occupancy list is the block-level ownership view; the end locators are the tile-level precise geometry view. The read does not depend on a currently selected cab.

**Controller retained field schema**

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `state` | controller state enum | Canonical operational state (`OFF`, `READY`, `RUNNING_MOVING`, `RUNNING_AT_STATION`) |
| `power_on` | `bool` (read-only derived: `state != "OFF"`) | Convenience read for "is the controller powered on?" Computed each access from `state`; not a stored field; no writers. Power transitions are owned by the canonical `state` machine, not by a parallel boolean. |
| `commands.cab` | `Optional[str]` | Active cab selection (`"A"`, `"B"`, or `None`). Stored on the `OperatorCommands` sub-module; serialized under the key `"cab"`. |
| `commands.control_mode` | `"ato" \| "manual"` | High-level driving mode selector. Stored on the `OperatorCommands` sub-module; serialized under the key `"control_mode"`. |
| `manual.manual_mode_type` | `"simulation" \| "power_percent"` | Manual-mode submode selector. Construction default is `simulation`. Mutated only through `TrainController.commands.set_manual_mode_type(...)`. |
| `travel_direction` | `Optional[str]` (read-only property) | Read-only derived value computed each read from `(self.cab, self.current_span)`. Not a stored field; no writers. See the `travel_direction` derivation rule above. |
| `handle_position` | controller handle enum/value | Active handle command for ATO and manual `simulation` mode |
| `manual.manual_power_percent` | `int` | Active manual driver command for manual `power_percent` mode (`0..100`) |
| `eb.active` | `bool` | Emergency-brake latch |
| `ato.running` | `bool` | Whether an ATO run is presently active (the train has been commanded to run under ATO and has not yet been halted, dwelled, EB'd, or otherwise stopped). This is a run-active flag, not a mode flag; the mode flag is `control_mode`. |
| `ops.service_stop_active` | `bool` | Latch that arms service braking for Phase 1 timed-`run` completion; the **Latch Table** is its canonical home and defines the writer, the consumer, and the gating state. Default `False`. While it is set, Controller tick step `b41e` replaces that tick's ATO/manual propulsion command with service stopping. |
| `phase_latches` | `PhaseLatches` | Aggregate of the four Phase-3 latches the controller owns: `path_cancel_request: Optional[PendingPathCancelRequest]`, `route_abort: bool`, `power_off: bool`, `route_deactivate: bool`. Writers go through `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_*(...)`; consumers in Phase 3 call `consume_*()`. The full lifecycle for each field — gating predicates, multi-tick survival, fan-out — lives in the **Latch Table** in the Core Runtime Loop section. |
| `received_path_plan` | `Optional[ReceivedPathPlan]` (read-only property) | Live read of the controller's active movement authority. Computed each time it is read by projecting `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher.get_active_path(self.train_id)` into the canonical **received_path_plan schema**. Not a stored field; there is no `receive_path_plan(...)` setter. The projection exposes only controller-needed authority fields such as `path_id`, `resource_sequence`, and `authority_end_block_id`; dispatcher-only requested-target and lifecycle fields remain on `Path`. `None` when the train has no `ACTIVE` path. |
| `physics_sync.current_span` | `TrainSpan` | Controller-owned live physical span, including ordered occupied resources/blocks/tiles and both physical end locators. Lives on the `PhysicsSync` sub-module. |
| `physics_sync.current_speed_kmh` | `float` | Controller-owned live physical speed written by `TrainPhysics`, and set to `0` by `TrainController.complete_power_off()` as part of OFF cleanup. Lives on the `PhysicsSync` sub-module. |
| `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` | `Set[str]` each | Named within-tick Step-0 → Phase-2 handoff (or, on a stopped pass, Step-0 → the stopped-pass occupancy commit). Step 0 resets both to empty for every train, then writes this tick's attributed block IDs onto each affected train. The final `on_physics_updated(...)` callback folds them into that tick's block observations and clears both immediately on a running pass; `TrainController.build_detected_progress_report()` does the same on a stopped pass (see **Running and stopped**). Neither survives the train transaction and neither is a latch. |
| `physics_sync.pending_block_exit_edges` | `Dict[str, edge]` | Per-block held tail-vacate direction: when the calculated tail leaves a block, `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` records that block's forward `exit_edge` here and consumes the entry when the debounced detector vacate fires (per **Power**, Block occupancy detection), so `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` receives the same caution `direction` despite the detector lag. Step 0 writes the same field for an unidentified train, whose edge comes from its detected blocks and is consumed on that same tick (see **Power**, Unidentified trains). The one `PhysicsSync` buffer that may hold an entry across ticks. |
| `presence_unknown` | `bool` (read-only derived) | Computed on each read from the train's evidence set — its span's `occupied_blocks` plus the blocks it holds through `Block.occupied_by`, intersected with `OccupancyInput.detected_block_ids()` — per **Power**, Ghosted trains. Not a stored field; no writers. Exported on the controller payload. |
| `atc.last_emitted_reason_code` | `Optional[str]` | Last distance-0 `atc_limit_reason_code` this controller emitted a change event for. Written only by tick substep `4d7c`, immediately after that substep emits. Starts `None`, and no command or state transition resets it. Lives on the ATC sub-module. |
| `lookahead` | canonical lookahead payload (read-only property) | Computed each time it is read from `(current_span, cab, received_path_plan, network_state, signals, current_speed_kmh, eb.active)`. Pure derived value; not a stored field; no cache; no invalidation rules. See **canonical lookahead payload** below for the schema returned by each read. |

- The table above is the canonical retained controller field set for v1. If a field is not in this table or in a later canonical payload schema, it must not remain as an independently written live controller field.

- **`ControllerCondition` set** (canonical home; referenced elsewhere by name). `ControllerCondition` is the canonical enum of currently-active controller-state conditions used to express recovery and create-eligibility predicates. v1 values:

  - `HAS_AUTHORITY` — `received_path_plan is not None`
  - `EB_LATCHED` — `eb.active == True`
  - `POWER_OFF_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.power_off == True`
  - `PATH_CANCEL_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.path_cancel_request is not None`
  - `ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.route_abort == True`
  - `ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING` — `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.route_deactivate == True`

  `TrainController.active_conditions` is a derived `FrozenSet[ControllerCondition]` computed each read from the controller's stored fields per the mapping above. Not a stored field; no writers.

  `TrainController.is_recovery_clean` (derived `bool`): `state == READY` AND `active_conditions` contains none of `{HAS_AUTHORITY, EB_LATCHED, POWER_OFF_PENDING}`. This is the canonical "controller has reached the stopped recovery state" predicate. Other sites cross-reference it by name and must not restate the conjuncts.

  `RouteExecutor.is_clean_for_create(train_id)` (derived `bool`): `controller.is_recovery_clean` AND `active_conditions` contains none of `{PATH_CANCEL_PENDING, ROUTE_ABORT_PENDING, ROUTE_DEACTIVATE_PENDING}` AND `run.path_cancelled is False`. This is the canonical "Phase 1 may run the create branch" predicate.

  Adding a new latch in a future revision means adding one `ControllerCondition` value plus one mapping line — every existing predicate site automatically picks up the new condition.

- **Allowed `(state, cab)` pairs.** The relationship between `state` and `cab` is structurally constrained but enforced by its three cab writers — `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)`, the ATO cab selection inside `TrainController.depart()`, and `TrainController.commands.clear_cab()` during OFF cleanup — plus the cab precondition on `TrainController.enter_station_dwell()`. The legal combinations are:

  | `state` | `cab` |
  | --- | --- |
  | `OFF` | `None` |
  | `READY` | `None` or `"A"` or `"B"` |
  | `RUNNING_MOVING` | `"A"` or `"B"` |
  | `RUNNING_AT_STATION` | `"A"` or `"B"` |

  Any other combination is a contract violation. Readers needing cab membership read `cab` directly; the state alone does not encode it.

**Lookahead is a pure derived value**
