- `get_current_span()` is the canonical controller accessor for occupied-span queries. It reads `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`, the v1 storage home for live physical occupancy and end locators. The payload is general and geometry-first: at minimum it must expose `occupied_blocks` and `occupied_tiles` in physical span order, from one physical end of the train to the other, plus explicit end locators for both physical ends. The ordered occupancy is the block-level ownership view; the end locators are the tile-level precise geometry view. `end_a` and `end_b` are the train's two **physical** ends, and they are cab-independent: they are stable geometric labels for the two ends of the consist and do NOT imply head/tail. The "head" end is whichever physical end matches the currently selected cab in the controller's travel frame. Without a selected cab, `current_span` still carries both physical ends but no head/tail labeling. `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` consumers that need head-side reasoning derive it from controller cab/travel state plus the span; they must not assume `end_a == head`. `TrainPhysics` is the only normal producer of movement `span_update` values, and `TrainController` is the only normal writer of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span`. `RouteExecutor` / `PathDispatcher` must use `TrainController.get_current_span()` whenever they need the train's current span or physical ends.

**TrainSpan schema**

This is the canonical v1 shape of `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` and the payload returned by `TrainController.get_current_span()`.

| Field | Type | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `end_a` | `SpanEndLocator` | Stable physical end A of the consist. It is cab-independent and does not imply head/tail. |
| `end_b` | `SpanEndLocator` | Stable physical end B of the consist. It is cab-independent and does not imply head/tail. |
| `occupied_tiles` | `List[Tuple[int, int]]` | Tile-level coverage in physical order from `end_a` to `end_b`. |
| `occupied_blocks` | `List[str]` | Unique block IDs currently covered by the train, in first-seen `end_a` -> `end_b` order. |

`SpanEndLocator` carries exactly:

- `tile` — current tile coordinate for that physical end
- `entry_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` edge used as the end's current in-tile distance frame
- `distance_cm` — precise in-tile distance from `entry_edge`, in centimeters

Consumers that need the forward/head side derive it from selected cab/travel state plus `end_a` / `end_b`; `TrainSpan` itself never relabels `end_a` or `end_b` as head/tail. The two locators' in-tile frames are always expressed in the current head-forward travel direction: each end's `entry_edge` sits on its rear side for the current head selection, so `distance_cm` grows the way the train travels. The head selection is the cab (`"B"` selects `end_b`; any other selection, `None` included, selects `end_a`). Whenever an accepted cab change moves the head to the other physical end, `TrainController.commands.set_cab(...)` re-expresses both locators in the reversed frame — `entry_edge` flips to the tile's opposite exit and `distance_cm` becomes `tile_length - distance_cm` — so the ends keep describing the same physical points. A frame left un-flipped would make physics advance the head opposite to the direction the cab commands and draw the head marker pointing into the body.

`PhysicsSpanUpdate` is the canonical payload from `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` into `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`. It carries exactly:

- `new_span: TrainSpan`
- `current_speed_kmh: float`
- `added_tiles: List[TileTraversal]` in physical traversal order for tiles newly covered by the updated span
- `removed_tiles: List[TileTraversal]` in physical vacate order for tiles no longer covered by the updated span

`TileTraversal` carries exactly:

- `tile_xy` — tile coordinate
- `entry_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` traversal-frame entry edge
- `exit_edge` — `"N"`/`"E"`/`"S"`/`"W"` traversal-frame exit edge; for tail-vacated block tiles this is the vacated block's forward exit edge for the traversal by which the train left that block, which `TrainController` reads as the block tail-vacate `direction`
- `resource_type` — `"block"` or `"switch"` when the tile belongs to a known resource, otherwise `None`
- `resource_id` — block or switch ID for that resource, otherwise `None`
- `block_id` — owning block ID when applicable, otherwise `None`

`TrainPhysics` computes this delta by comparing the previous controller-owned span it read at the start of the movement step with the newly integrated span. `TrainController` is the only normal writer of the stored `current_span`: it applies `span_update.new_span`, derives resource entry/release observations from `added_tiles` / `removed_tiles`, packages them into one `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) for later delivery to `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` in Phase 3.

`TrainPhysics.tick(...)` must emit a `PhysicsSpanUpdate` on every tick, even when `added_tiles` and `removed_tiles` are both empty and the integrated `new_span` / `current_speed_kmh` are unchanged. When nothing changed physically, this is an explicit no-change callback. The always-callback rule keeps `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` / `current_speed_kmh` canonical and gives Phase 3 a deterministic consume point for local progress consumption, the post-physics stop check, and latches such as `pending_power_off`.

- One shared neighbor/edge helper, such as `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(tile_a, tile_b) -> Optional[Tuple[str, str]]`, answers two questions at once: are the two tiles track-connected, and if so, which edge on each tile connects to the other. The helper resolves both ordinary grid adjacency (the two tiles share a cardinal edge whose `track_bits` connect) and tunnel adjacency (the two tiles are linked through `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` — see **Tunnels** below). `TrainPhysics` and traversal helpers use this instead of re-deriving neighbor-edge pairs ad hoc. The lower-level neighbor lookup `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(x, y, edge) -> Optional[Tile]` follows the same two-step resolution: it returns the grid neighbour first, then falls through to `RailwayNetwork.tunnels.get((x, y, edge))` when the grid cell is empty.

**Tunnels**

- `RailwayNetwork.tunnels: Dict[Tuple[int, int, str], Tuple[int, int, str]]` pairs two dead-end edges so they behave as a single tile-to-tile connection. The map is symmetric: every pair is stored in both directions so lookups are O(1).
- The sole writer is the helper pair `RailwayNetwork.add_tunnel(a, b)` and `RailwayNetwork.remove_tunnel(endpoint)`; no other site assigns to `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` directly. `add_tunnel` enforces the tunnel invariants:
  - Endpoints are distinct.
  - Each endpoint references an existing tile whose `track_bits` include the named edge.
  - Each endpoint is a dead-end at write time — no grid neighbour connects back on the opposite edge. A tunnel fills a dead-end; it does not override real grid geometry.
  - Neither endpoint already participates in another tunnel.
  - The two edges may be any pair, opposite or not (W↔E, N↔S, N↔W, …). A non-opposite pair is needed in layouts such as zig-zag siding loops where, for example, a north-facing dead-end on one side connects to a west-facing dead-end on the other. `Tile._update_dead_ends` uses `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(...)` rather than the cardinal opposite, so the connectivity check follows the actual recorded pair on each tile.
- `RailwayNetwork.propagate_from_tile(...)` runs `_sweep_stale_tunnels()` at the top of every propagation. Any tunnel whose endpoint loses its track, loses the named edge from `track_bits`, or gains a real grid neighbour that connects back, is removed through `RailwayNetwork.remove_tunnel(endpoint)`, so pair symmetry stays owned by the one writer. The propagation that triggered the sweep then sees the cleaned table and recomputes dead-end ports through it. `RailwayNetwork.remove_track(x, y)` calls the sweep explicitly because the removed-tile path does not always touch a grid neighbour propagation.
- `Tile.dead_end_ports` is computed through `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(...)`, so a tunneled edge is automatically excluded. Other consumers of dead-end status (the `TRACK_END_SAFETY_MARGIN_CM` trigger, `TrainController.travel_direction` end-of-line fall-through) read through the same `dead_end_ports` field and inherit tunnel awareness without their own table lookups.
- `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` is serialised by `to_dict()` and `to_layout_dict()` as a `tunnels` list of `{"a": {"x", "y", "edge"}, "b": {"x", "y", "edge"}}` records, one per pair, and reloaded by `from_dict()` after all tracks are in place. Malformed entries on load are logged and skipped without aborting the load.
- Blocks remain grid-contiguous: a tunneled edge is always a block boundary, and `Block.ordered_tiles` does not walk through a tunnel.
- One shared backend network-graph candidate-query / walk-metrics home lives in the common network/pathfinding layer, such as `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...) -> List[GraphWalkCandidate]`. Thin adapter methods such as `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` or `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(...)` may still exist, but they must all delegate to this same operational graph home. The candidate query takes tile-precise locators, the caller's `manual_override` flag (default `False`; `True` only when the requesting run's route stores `Route.manual_override = True`), always skips disabled blocks/switches, also skips manual blocks/switches when `manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home), and returns zero or more directed walk candidates with whatever ordered tile/block walk payload the caller requested.
- `GraphWalkCandidate` carries exactly this payload. **This subsection is the canonical home for the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` candidate comparison order. All other sites that compare candidates must cross-reference this subsection by name rather than restating the comparison rule.**

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `ordered_walk` | `List[GraphWalkStep]` | Canonical directed walk over graph steps in traversal order. Each step contains enough resource/tile/frame data for dispatcher acquisition, route target comparison, and later distance projection. |
  | `ordered_tiles` | `Optional[List[Tuple[int, int]]]` | Ordered tile IDs for callers that requested tile payload. `None` when `return_tiles=False`. |
  | `ordered_blocks` | `Optional[List[str]]` | Ordered block IDs visited by the walk, with consecutive duplicate block steps collapsed only when they represent the same directed block traversal. `None` when `return_blocks=False`. |
  | `start_locator` | `GraphLocator` | The resolved tile-precise start locator used by the graph search, including block/tile membership and optional in-tile position when supplied by the caller. |
  | `terminal_locator` | `GraphLocator` | The resolved tile-precise terminal locator reached by the candidate. |
  | `terminal_block_id` | `str` | Block ID of the terminal block reached by the candidate. |
  | `terminal_block_direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Terminal block-relative traversal direction derived from the candidate's directed frame against `Block.ordered_tiles`. |
  | `terminal_frame` | `DirectedBlockFrame` | Terminal directed block frame, including path tiles plus `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` metadata needed by dispatcher/controller projection helpers. |
  | `tile_count` | `int` | Number of tile steps in the directed walk; first comparison key. |
  | `walk_length_cm` | `float` | Centimeter length along the directed walk; second comparison key. |
  | `candidate_rank` | `int` | Stable deterministic tie-break assigned by `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` after `tile_count` and `walk_length_cm`; third comparison key. |

  Every caller that compares candidates must choose by `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank)`. `walk_length_cm` is the common physical-distance metric regardless of caller input type: a waypoint/station/platform input is first resolved into one or more concrete terminal `GraphLocator` values and compared by the same walk distance, while a direct block/tile input already supplies the terminal locator used for the same distance calculation. `candidate_rank` is only for exact ties after those graph metrics. It is assigned by the shared graph home using one deterministic ordering of the directed walk identity: ordered resource IDs, ordered tile coordinates, entry/exit edge sequence, terminal locator identity, and then any remaining stable insertion order from the graph query. Callers must not replace `candidate_rank` with waypoint-specific, UI-order, or layer-local heuristics. If those metrics still remain exactly tied across the remaining current-span end choices, use `end_a` as the final deterministic dead-heat tie-break outside `GraphWalkCandidate`; no other layer-local tie-break is allowed.

  When the originating `GraphWalkQuery` carries the routed train's max speed (`train_max_speed_kmh`), `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` first ranks the surviving candidates by **estimated travel time** -- the sum over the walk of each segment's length divided by its effective speed (the posted `Segment.speed_limit_kmh` capped at the train's max speed, or the train's max speed where no limit is posted) -- and uses the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` order above only to break ties. A faster route therefore wins over an equal-or-shorter-distance slower one. Geometry-only queries (no `train_max_speed_kmh`) rank by the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` order alone. The travel-time key is a query-time ranking computed from live segment speed limits, not a stored `GraphWalkCandidate` field.

- `GraphLocator` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `tile` | `Tuple[int, int]` | Tile coordinate anchoring the locator |
  | `resource_type` | `Optional["block" \| "switch"]` | Resource type containing the locator, when known |
  | `resource_id` | `Optional[str]` | Resource ID containing the locator, when known |
  | `block_id` | `Optional[str]` | Convenience block ID when the locator is inside a block; `None` for switch-only locators |
  | `edge` | `Optional["N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"]` | Tile edge when the locator is anchored at a graph boundary or caller knows the relevant edge |
  | `distance_cm` | `Optional[float]` | In-tile distance from `edge` along the directed traversal when the caller has a precise position; `None` for tile-only searches |
  | `physical_end` | `Optional["end_a" \| "end_b"]` | Set only for current-train-span start locators so the caller can apply the final `end_a` dead-heat tie-break outside the graph query |

- `DirectedBlockFrame` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `block_id` | `str` | Block whose directed traversal is represented |
  | `block_direction` | `"forward" \| "reverse"` | Direction relative to `Block.ordered_tiles` |
  | `path_tiles` | `List[path_tile entry]` | Ordered tile slice through the block using the canonical path-tile entry schema |
  | `entry_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge by which the traversal enters the block frame |
  | `exit_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge by which the traversal exits the block frame |

- `GraphWalkStep` carries exactly this payload:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `step_index` | `int` | Zero-based step index inside `ordered_walk` |
  | `resource_type` | `"block" \| "switch"` | Resource traversed by this step |
  | `resource_id` | `str` | Block or switch ID for the traversed resource |
  | `tile` | `Tuple[int, int]` | Tile coordinate for this graph step |
  | `entry_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge used to enter this tile/resource step |
  | `exit_edge` | `"N" \| "E" \| "S" \| "W"` | Edge used to exit this tile/resource step |
  | `block_id` | `Optional[str]` | Block ID when `resource_type == "block"` |
  | `block_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse"]` | Block-relative traversal direction when the step is inside a block |
  | `switch_required_state` | `Optional[int]` | Required switch state for switch traversal when topology search determines one |
  | `length_cm` | `float` | Physical length contribution of this step |

- Operational candidate comparison, filtering, and multi-candidate enumeration live only at this backend home. The frontend editor pathfinders in `static/js/pathfinders.js` (`ConstructionPathfinder.findPath(...)` and `ExistingTrackPathfinder.findPathOnExistingTracks(...)`) answer connectivity for the track editor and return a single path. They never sort by `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)`, never apply the disabled-block / disabled-switch / manual-block / manual-switch filters, and never apply `Block.allowed_travel_directions` or `requested_destination_direction`. The comparison order has exactly one implementation site and each operational filter has exactly one application site. Callers that need ranked candidates or filter-aware paths — including UI features — route through `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` rather than re-implementing comparison or filtering on the client. The frontend and backend must agree on one shared primitive: entering a tile on one segment of a switch limits the valid exits to that segment's paired edge. The frontend encodes this in `ConstructionPathfinder.getExitsForEntry(trackBits, entry)`; the backend encodes it in `Tile.get_exits(entry, use_switch_state=False)`.

**Pathfinding-home migration**

- The current operational backend path search lives in `PathDispatcher.find_paths(start_block_id, end_block_id, train_id=None) -> List[PathSegment]` / `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)`. That contract is insufficient for the v1 contracts above because it is block-to-block and distance-sorted only; it does not support direction-aware terminal rejection, locator-level (tile-precise) start positions, explicit switch-handling mode, the canonical `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, final end_a dead-heat tie-break)` comparison rule and its estimated-travel-time primary ranking when a train max speed is supplied, or measure-along-walk projection. The refactor must extend or extract that existing backend pathfinding home so it exposes at least:
  - `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(request: GraphWalkQuery) -> List[GraphWalkCandidate]` — a direction-aware network-graph search that accepts a `GraphWalkQuery` request payload (defined below). It accepts tile-precise start and end locators, not just block IDs. It always skips disabled blocks/switches, and also skips manual blocks/switches when `request.manual_override == False` (per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home). The search is topology-only: it plans through switches whatever their current position, because acquisition throws them into the state the walk needs afterwards. It rejects candidates whose terminal traversal does not satisfy `request.requested_destination_direction`. It rejects candidates whose traversal violates `Block.allowed_travel_directions` when `request.manual_override == False` (per the same canonical home). It rejects candidates that traverse a block or switch leg whose curve radius is tighter than `request.train_min_radius_cm`, when that capability is set (the curve-radius feasibility filter defined below). It rejects candidates that climb a block steeper than `request.train_max_slope_percent` in the traversed direction, when that capability is set (the slope feasibility filter defined below). It rejects candidates whose directed walk re-enters a resource with less than `request.train_length_cm` of walk since it last left that resource, when that capability is set (the own-length feasibility filter defined below). It ranks the surviving candidates by estimated travel time when `request.train_max_speed_kmh` is set, and by the `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` comparison order otherwise (per the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload** canonical home). Finally, it returns the comparable walk candidates plus whichever ordered tile/block walk payload the request flags asked for.

  `GraphWalkQuery` carries exactly:

  | Field | Type | Meaning |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `start_locator` | `GraphLocator` | Tile-precise start locator. |
  | `end_locator` | `GraphLocator` | Tile-precise end locator. |
  | `requested_destination_direction` | `Optional["forward" \| "reverse"]` | Optional terminal direction filter; candidates whose terminal traversal does not match are rejected. |
  | `exclude_resources` | `Optional[Set[str]]` | Optional set of resource IDs to exclude from the search (used by retry / extension logic). |
  | `manual_override` | `bool = False` | Operator-override flag per the **`disabled` and `manual` availability flags** canonical home. When `False`, manual blocks/switches are filtered out exactly like disabled ones and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is enforced. When `True` (forwarded from a run whose route stores `Route.manual_override = True` — operator-initiated routes), manual resources participate normally and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` is not enforced; disabled resources are still filtered. |
  | `train_min_radius_cm` | `Optional[float] = None` | The routed train's minimum curve radius capability in centimetres, or `None` for geometry-only callers with no train context. When set, candidates that traverse a segment (`Segment.min_radius_cm`) or switch leg (`Switch.main_radius_cm` / `Switch.diverge_radius_cm` for the leg the walk uses) whose radius is tighter than this value are rejected by the curve-radius feasibility filter defined below. `manual_override` does not relax this filter. |
  | `train_max_speed_kmh` | `Optional[float] = None` | The routed train's maximum speed in km/h, or `None` for geometry-only callers with no train context. When set, surviving candidates are ranked by estimated travel time (posted `Segment.speed_limit_kmh` capped at this max, or this max where unposted), with `(tile_count, walk_length_cm, candidate_rank, end_a)` as the tie-break; see the **`GraphWalkCandidate` payload** canonical home. `None` ranks by that distance order alone. |
  | `train_max_slope_percent` | `Optional[float] = None` | The routed train's maximum climbable gradient (percent grade), or `None` for geometry-only callers, or `0.0` (the `Train.max_slope` "unspecified" sentinel) for no limit. When set above zero, candidates that climb a segment (`Segment.grade_percent`, sign-flipped for a reverse traversal) steeper than this value are rejected by the slope feasibility filter defined below. `manual_override` does not relax it. |
  | `train_length_cm` | `Optional[float] = None` | The routed train's physical consist length in centimetres, or `None` for geometry-only callers with no train context. When set, candidates whose directed walk turns back into a resource sooner than this length are rejected by the own-length feasibility filter defined below. `manual_override` does not relax this filter. |
  | `return_tiles` | `bool = True` | Whether to populate `GraphWalkCandidate.ordered_tiles`. |
  | `return_blocks` | `bool = True` | Whether to populate `GraphWalkCandidate.ordered_blocks`. |

  **Curve-radius feasibility filter.** A train must not be routed through a curve tighter than it can physically take. `query_graph_walks(...)` rejects any candidate that traverses a segment whose `Segment.min_radius_cm` is set and smaller than `request.train_min_radius_cm`, or a switch leg whose active radius (`Switch.main_radius_cm` for the main leg, `Switch.diverge_radius_cm` for the diverge leg) is set and smaller than `request.train_min_radius_cm`. The leg is the one the walk's `GraphWalkStep.switch_required_state` names; when that field is `None` the walk names no leg for that switch, so that step carries no curve limit and never rejects. A `None` block/switch radius carries no curve limit and never rejects; a `None` `request.train_min_radius_cm` (geometry-only callers with no train context) skips the filter entirely. This is a physical capability limit, so `manual_override` does not relax it — unlike the `manual` and `Block.allowed_travel_directions` filters. The capability flows in through the dispatcher: `PathDispatcher` resolves the routed train's `Train.min_radius_cm` and passes it as `request.train_min_radius_cm`; automatic and operator-initiated routes both carry it.

  **Slope feasibility filter.** A train stalls on a climb steeper than it can manage, so it must not be routed up such a grade. `query_graph_walks(...)` rejects any candidate that climbs a segment whose effective uphill gradient exceeds `request.train_max_slope_percent`. `Segment.grade_percent` is signed in that segment's own increasing `ordered_tiles` direction. The effective gradient is therefore `Segment.grade_percent` when the candidate traverses the segment in increasing order and `-Segment.grade_percent` in decreasing order. Because block grouping enforces the stored-orientation invariant above, this is also respectively the block's forward and reverse direction; callers must not maintain a second grade frame. The candidate is rejected when the effective gradient exceeds the train capability. Only a climb rejects; a descent never does. `None` grade, `None`/`0.0` train capability, or an unclassified traversal skips the filter. Like the curve-radius filter, `manual_override` does not relax it. `PathDispatcher` resolves `Train.max_slope` and passes it as `request.train_max_slope_percent`.

  **Own-length feasibility filter.** A train cannot enter track its own body is still standing on, so it must not be routed onto a walk that turns back into itself sooner than its own length. `query_graph_walks(...)` rejects any candidate whose directed walk visits the same resource twice with less than the train's length of walk between the two visits. Taking `b1` as the along-walk distance at which the walk leaves that resource and `a2` as the distance at which it re-enters, the candidate is rejected when `a2 - b1` is not greater than `request.train_length_cm`: the head would re-enter the resource while the tail is still inside it, so acquisition there can never succeed no matter how long the train waits. A resource the walk visits once never rejects, and a `None` `request.train_length_cm` (geometry-only callers with no train context) skips the filter entirely. Like the curve-radius and slope filters this is a physical capability limit, so `manual_override` does not relax it. `PathDispatcher` resolves the routed train's `Train.consist_length_cm` and passes it as `request.train_length_cm`.

  This filter is what makes a permanently self-fouling walk a viability failure instead of a runtime stall. A candidate it rejects is not a legal walk, so when it removes every candidate the create/extend call fails with `no_viable_path` per the **viable-but-unavailable rule**, and the operator learns at planning time that this train cannot run this walk. A candidate it accepts may still find a repeated resource held by the train's own tail on the tick it is planned; that is an availability condition and takes the `0` / `False` retry lane on `extend_path(...)`, or the create-side unavailability outcome under the same rule on `create_path(...)`, because the tail clears as the train advances. A loop with more than the train's length of track between leaving a resource and returning to it therefore passes the filter and closes normally (see the **repeated-resource rule** under **Path invariants**).

  - `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...) -> List[GraphWalkCandidate]` — thin adapter over `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` returning the ranked ordered-walk candidates (best first per the canonical `GraphWalkCandidate` comparison); a caller that needs a single chosen walk takes the first element.
  - `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(ordered_walk, locator_a, locator_b) -> float` — in-walk centimeter distance projection between two locators on an already-chosen walk, used by lookahead and platform-end pair comparison.
  - `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints(start_block_id, include_stations=True) -> List[str]` — topological reachability enumeration that returns every block (and optionally every station) reachable from `start_block_id`. The walk steps tile-by-tile, so switch tiles do not act as graph barriers. The walk does not consult current switch state, does not apply `Block.allowed_travel_directions`, and knows nothing about any train; callers that need direction filtering use `find_ordered_walk` / `query_graph_walks` instead. Used by the route editor's block-to-block feasibility highlighting, where there is no placed train and any travel direction is allowed.
  - `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints_for_span(head_seeds, occupied_tiles, occupied_blocks, include_stations=True) -> List[str]` — span-aware reachability for one placed train. `head_seeds` is the list of `(tile, outward_edge)` pairs for the train's two physical span ends, where `outward_edge` is the network edge pointing away from the body at that end. The walk starts only from those seeds, in those outward directions. It steps tile-by-tile through switch tiles exactly like `get_reachable_endpoints`, and it never steps onto a tile in `occupied_tiles`; `occupied_blocks` are removed from the result. So it returns exactly the blocks (and stations) the train can reach driving forward out of its head or in reverse out of its tail — never back through its own body, or through a switch its body is sitting on. `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints_for_train(train_id, include_stations=True)` resolves the train's current span and its two end-outward edges and delegates here; the Quick Route tool's destination highlighting calls it. Each end's outward edge is a purely geometric value — the network edge of that end's tile pointing away from the consist body — computed from the span geometry (`end_a` / `end_b` tile + `entry_edge`) alone, independent of cab selection. It is not derived from `travel_direction` (which is cab-dependent, describes only the cab/head end, and is `None` when no cab is selected). Both span ends always have a well-defined outward edge, so the tool works whether or not a cab is selected.
  - the `GraphWalkCandidate` table above as the only candidate payload contract.
- Every layer that needs pathfinding shares this single home: `RouteExecutor` platform-end selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side and forward candidate selection, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix selection, and the boundary-projection helpers all route through it.
- The implementation must use the following function table as the canonical operational pathfinding surface. If the shared graph home is implemented as methods on `PathDispatcher` rather than an extracted `RailwayPathfinder` class, keep the inputs/outputs and single-home rule identical.

  | Function | Inputs | Output | Required callers / need | Current nearby implementation |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `RailwayNetwork.get_connection_edges(tile_a, tile_b)` | tile coordinates that are either grid-adjacent or linked through `RailwayNetwork.tunnels` | `Optional[Tuple[str, str]]` edge on `tile_a`, edge on `tile_b` | `TrainPhysics.tick(...)`, graph traversal, directed frame construction | Implemented; consolidates the grid-adjacency check and the tunnel fallback used by `RailwayNetwork.get_neighbor(...)`. See the **Tunnels** canonical home above. |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(request)` | `request: GraphWalkQuery` | `List[GraphWalkCandidate]` sorted/comparable by candidate metrics | `RouteExecutor` platform-end selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` start-side selection, `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` candidate selection, `PathDispatcher.extend_path(...)` suffix selection | Closest backend code is `PathDispatcher.find_paths(...)`, `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_paths(...)`, `PathDispatcher._find_extension_paths(...)`, and `PathDispatcher._bfs_find_extension_paths(...)`; these must be extended/extracted. `static/js/pathfinders.js` has `ConstructionPathfinder.findPath(...)` and `ExistingTrackPathfinder.findPathOnExistingTracks(...)`, but those are editor/construction helpers and are not the runtime operational home. |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.find_ordered_walk(...)` | Same as `RailwayPathfinder.query_graph_walks(...)` plus selection policy when needed | `Optional[GraphWalkCandidate]` chosen by canonical comparison | Thin convenience adapter for callers that need one chosen walk rather than all candidates | No exact current equivalent; closest is `PathDispatcher.find_paths(...)[0]` after sorting, but that lacks new frame semantics |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.measure_along_walk(ordered_walk, locator_a, locator_b)` | chosen `ordered_walk` and two `GraphLocator` values on that walk | signed or absolute centimeter distance as specified by caller | lookahead projection, platform-end pair comparison, graph-nearest route reconciliation | Current `PathSegment.distance_cm` is whole-path only and is not sufficient for locator-to-locator projection |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints(start_block_id, include_stations=True)` | starting block id, station-inclusion flag | `List[str]` of reachable block ids (plus station ids when included) | route-editor block-to-block feasibility highlighting (no placed train, any direction) | Lives at the pathfinder home; `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints(...)` is a thin wrapper that delegates here so existing callers keep their entry point |
  | `RailwayPathfinder.get_reachable_endpoints_for_span(head_seeds, occupied_tiles, occupied_blocks, include_stations=True)` | the two span ends as `(tile, outward_edge)` seeds, the train's occupied tiles and blocks, station-inclusion flag | `List[str]` of reachable block ids (plus station ids when included), excluding the train's own occupied blocks | Quick Route tool destination highlighting, via `PathDispatcher.get_reachable_endpoints_for_train(train_id, include_stations=True)` | Span-aware companion to `get_reachable_endpoints`: same tile-by-tile switch traversal, but seeded from the train's head/tail outward edges (resolved through its `TrainController`) and forbidden from entering the body's occupied tiles |
  | `RailwayNetwork.project_authority_boundary_delta(ordered_walk, head_locator, authority_end, safety_margin_cm)` | the live authority's ordered directed walk, `head_locator` (a `GraphLocator` carrying `tile`, `edge`, `distance_cm`), plus `authority_end` (carrying `block_id` and `terminal_frame: DirectedBlockFrame`) | signed centimeter delta measured along the walk, forward from the head's resolved occurrence; positive means head is past safety-margin boundary, negative means still short | controller braking diagnostics and stop-boundary projection | No exact current equivalent; the geometry comes from shared graph/geometry helpers and the ordering from the live authority walk, so a lapping route has one answer |

**Manual mode and movement-authority boundary**

- In `simulation` submode, the driver must call `set_handle()`, the controller must store `handle_position`, and `TrainPhysics` must integrate it into speed while ATC can still override with braking.
- In `power_percent` submode, the driver must call `TrainController.commands.set_manual_power_percent()`, the controller must store `manual.manual_power_percent`, and `TrainPhysics` must convert it through `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve` in the same tick whenever no braking override is active.
- In that same `power_percent` submode, the converted power-percent command is the absolute voltage/speed command and overrides the normal acceleration/deceleration-rate fields while no safety braking override is active. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` must set the `span_update.current_speed_kmh` for that tick to the calibrated effective speed; the model assumes negligible momentum under direct voltage control, so there is no service-rate ramp between settings. When ATC, service stopping, or EB intervenes, use the **Canonical power-percent safety-cap ordering** in the controller tick: sample the retained driver command and compute the current tick's capped operating point. ATC and service stopping then reset the retained percentage through `manual.reset_power_percent_for_safety()`; EB leaves it retained and blocks `TrainController.eb.release()` until the driver zeroes it. There is no signed negative power-percent command.
- In manual mode with a received path plan, the controller lookahead brakes the train to the current authority end boundary using the controller safety margin — the same authority-boundary stop the lookahead builds in ATO mode.
- Without a received path plan, manual mode receives the same no-authority empty lookahead defined in step `22a9` (`atc_limit = 0` at distance 0, `atc_limit_reason_code = no_authority`). There is no fallback to the current-block boundary; a stopped train with no plan is held at rest, and a deviated rolling train is stopped through the zero-limit / EB protocol in step `826a`.

**Completion ownership and timing**

- `TrainController` is the sole owner of the post-physics stopped/authority-boundary state, `RouteExecutor` the sole owner of the arrival decision, and `PathDispatcher` the sole owner of the release commit. The controller exposes whether the train is now stopped at the usable end of live authority; the route decides whether that stop serves the armed task and records it; dispatcher then mutates path and resource state.
- `TrainController` reaches the **`READY` state** through its reactive stopped/no-authority rule when live authority disappears; it does not decide independently that a path-backed run is complete.
- `PathDispatcher` is the sole owner of the `Path` record underlying `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`; the controller reads that projection and never mutates authority.
- Path completion is emitted only after two things hold: the train has already moved for that tick, and the **Stop-arrival check** says the train is standing at the stop it was sent to. `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` releases the authority and then hands the arrival to `RouteExecutor.handle_path_completion(...)`, so the recorded arrival always follows the release that made it final.

**Controller invariants**

- There is no `self.target_speed` field. Lookahead at distance 0 is the canonical source of target speed.
- Controller-owned live control and physical state must stay cleanly separated from persistent train data.
- Controller ATO destination state comes only from `received_path_plan`. There is no fallback read against `Train`.
- `SimulationController.get_or_create_controller(train_id)` must keep the 1:1 train/controller pairing and must not recreate authority from train-side runtime mirrors. Its only job is to make sure a controller object exists and is registered for this `train_id`. In v1, the controller must exist before any dispatcher path creation or extension: callers must make sure of this before `create_path(...)` or `extend_path(...)`. `SimulationController` must not build or write a path payload itself. There is no late-bind recovery hook such as `PathDispatcher.on_controller_available(train_id)`, because `received_path_plan` is a property that resolves live, so no late controller write is needed.

## TrainPhysics

**Movement-authority input in `TrainPhysics`**

- `TrainPhysics` runs physical integration: the speed update, the span and end-locator update, occupancy tracing, and tail tracing. But the canonical live storage is `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` and `TrainController.physics_sync.current_speed_kmh`. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` computes each `span_update` (`new_span`, `current_speed_kmh`, ordered `added_tiles`, ordered `removed_tiles`, and traversal frame metadata) and passes it to `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, which performs the controller-owned storage write. `TrainPhysics` must not own path identity, dispatcher authority state, progress interpretation, completion, or a separate canonical position store on `Train`.
- Each tick `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` caps the distance it moves the train so the calculated span stays inside what the block detector confirms (canonical home **Power**, "The calculated span never runs ahead of the detector"). The cap limits distance only. It does not change the speed solution, it never moves the train backwards, and every consumer — the controller's braking and stopping logic, the voltage emission, the progress reports — reads the capped span as that tick's position with no branch for having been capped.
- To choose exits on ambiguous geometry, `TrainPhysics` normally needs a read-only authority-geometry projection from the controller, derived from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan.resource_sequence`. The least it needs is the ordered `resource_sequence` / `path_tiles` geometry from that active plan, plus enough local direction context to pick the correct next edge through the current tile.
- `TrainPhysics` does **not** need dispatcher ownership fields such as `path_id` to step the train, and it does not need requested or authorized centimeter stop targets for physical integration. Centimeter stop-target fields are not part of the v1 path/controller contract. Controller braking logic uses authority-boundary geometry plus the controller safety margin.
- `TrainPhysics` obtains that geometry projection from `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan`; it is the only path-geometry movement input.
- `TrainPhysics` must read the current live position through the controller-owned span. It must write movement only by sending `span_update` to `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)`, never through a train-entity position field. Initial placement is set by the API `SimulationController.add_train(...)` flow, which writes `TrainController.physics_sync.current_span` before the first tick. (Persistence and save-data migration are Out of Scope; see the **Out of Scope** section.)
- Exception for cancellation/revocation braking: if the dispatcher-owned path has already left `ACTIVE` while the train is still rolling, `TrainPhysics` must keep moving the train according to its speed and the current live network state. That post-revocation movement relies on the physical `Switch.position` and live track geometry in the network at that moment, not on retained authority locks or a second copy of path ownership kept in the controller.
- Because live switch state is owned by `Switch`, `TrainPhysics._advance_end()` and tail tracing must call the `Tile` traversal helpers using the resolver input defined by the `Tile` helper contract above.

**Block occupancy**

- Route normal tail-vacated transitions through `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`. That chain routes block releases through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()`, so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays in one state machine.
- `TrainPhysics` must not write `Block` fields directly, and must not call block-state mutators for normal occupancy, CAUTION, or release. It computes movement `span_update` values, and the controller/dispatcher feedback chain does all block writes through `BlockManager`.
- Block tail-vacate observations come from the Step-0 detector (debounced no-current; see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), never from tile-level span arithmetic. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` includes a block in `tail_vacated_resources` only when the detector slice has confirmed that block is clear, so `PathDispatcher` calls `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated()` only on detector-confirmed clears.
- Normal CAUTION clearing happens inside `on_block_tail_vacated()` via `_reevaluate_caution_blocks()`, plus the **Opposing-traversal caution clear** inside `BlockManager.reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` when a path reserves a caution block for a traversal out of its other end. Both are `BlockManager` writes; there is no separate `clear_block()` / `release_block()` step.

**Tail-vacate flow in `TrainPhysics`**

- `TrainPhysics` does not call `BlockManager` mutators directly; CAUTION transitions go through `on_block_tail_vacated()` via the detector-confirmed tail-vacate path (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection).
- On tail-vacate, `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` includes a typed resource-release observation in `tail_vacated_resources`, packages it into the same `ControllerProgressReport`, and appends that report to the controller-owned per-train progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`) for the train's Phase 3 consume pass. For blocks, the controller reports the vacate only when the Step-0 detector slice has confirmed the block is clear (debounced no-current per **Power**, Block occupancy detection); the calculated span is not the block-vacate authority. For switches, a tile-level vacate of the switch resource in the calculated span is enough. Block IDs arrive directly on the detector slice. Switch IDs come from `Tile.switch_id` on switch tiles, via the same tile-lookup mechanism used for entry detection. The controller report carries the block tail-vacate `direction` as the vacated block's forward `exit_edge` for the traversal by which the train left that block. During Phase 3, `PathDispatcher` routes the release through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(resource_type, resource_id, train_id, direction, route)`. Blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(block_id, train_id, direction)`, so `OCCUPIED -> CAUTION -> FREE` stays in one state machine. Switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`, which removes the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry first, and then, only when no entries remain for that train, clears train-owned protection sourced from that vacated switch.
- `TrainPhysics` does not track its own `_caution_blocks`; `BlockManager` owns the full release lifecycle.

**Voltage behavior**

- `track_voltage`, `power_percent`, and `speed_kmh` are three different values. **Canonical home for `track_voltage` is `TrainPhysics`.** It is computed each tick from the authoritative source for the active mode. In ATO and manual-simulation mode, that source is the controller/physics speed solution. In manual `power_percent` mode, `manual.manual_power_percent` is mapped to `track_voltage` through the inverse of `SpeedProfile.voltage_power_curve` (the stage-1 `(track_voltage, power_percent)` curve), while the calibrated `speed_kmh` for that same operating point comes from `SpeedProfile.power_speed_curve`. `track_voltage` is always reached through `voltage_power_curve`, never through `power_speed_curve` (which has no voltage axis). `track_voltage` is a magnitude: it says how hard to drive a block, not which way. Direction is added per block when the value is emitted (see **Power**, A block voltage carries a direction), so every mapping on this page runs from `0` up to the profile's `max_voltage` and no calibration curve carries a sign. `track_voltage` is not stored on `TrainController` and not persisted on `Train`. It is per-tick derived state, owned by `TrainPhysics` and emitted to `BlockPowerController` as that tick's hardware output.
- `TrainPhysics` must pass the current `track_voltage` value to `BlockPowerController` as the software-to-hardware output layer for block voltage emission.
- The active mode decides which value is authoritative. In ATO and manual `simulation` mode, `speed_kmh` is the controller/physics source of truth, and `power_percent` / `track_voltage` are derived from that current operating point. In manual `power_percent` mode, `manual.manual_power_percent` is the source of truth, and `speed_kmh` / `track_voltage` are derived from that commanded power level in the same movement update. In this mode, the manual power command sets the absolute calibrated voltage/speed point, instead of being integrated through the normal service acceleration/deceleration rates.
- In manual `power_percent` mode, that authority applies only while no controller braking override is active. There is no handle-based speed-up/speed-down integration in this submode. Without an override, `TrainPhysics` uses the calibrated `power_percent -> speed_kmh` result directly for that tick. With ATC/service-stop/EB intervention, it receives the effective speed/power/voltage produced by the controller's **Canonical power-percent safety-cap ordering**; under ATC or service stopping the stored percentage has already been reset for later ticks, while under EB it stays retained until the driver zeroes it, and either way the current tick may still use a positive capped operating point computed from the sampled command. A zero cap produces the calibrated zero-power, zero-speed outcome.
- In `power_percent` manual mode, `TrainPhysics` must use the profile's voltage/power/speed calibration directly. It must not use scalar target-speed fields, and must not apply the service acceleration/deceleration rates to the manual-power command. The calibration must work in both directions, so physics can convert between the authoritative value for the active mode and the derived outputs it needs to maintain. `SpeedProfile` owns these mappings, and they must be documented exactly this way:
  - `track_voltage <-> power_percent`
  - `power_percent <-> speed_kmh`
- When `TrainPhysics` needs `track_voltage`, it derives it from the current authoritative mode input. In ATO and manual `simulation` mode, that is `speed_kmh -> power_percent -> track_voltage`. In manual `power_percent` mode, it is `manual_power_percent -> track_voltage`, plus the calibrated absolute `speed_kmh` command. In manual `power_percent` mode without a braking override, that calibrated `speed_kmh` is written into the same `PhysicsSpanUpdate.current_speed_kmh` that carries the movement result.
- Do not add extra names for these concepts. The doc speaks in voltage, power percent, and speed, and the three mappings stay distinct.

**Physics invariants**

- `TrainPhysics` must not own manual-mode policy; it must execute controller commands and maintain physical state only.
- Future authority is held by `Block.reserved_by_path` / `Block.reserved_for_train` on the block side and `Switch.locks` on the switch side. `TrainPhysics` holds no path-lock state of its own. Movement is geometry-driven. Once the train enters a resource, switch integrity during traversal is enforced by `Switch.occupied_by` plus any `Switch.train_protection_claims`.

## SpeedProfile

`SpeedProfile` carries motion-rate data plus per-train calibration, in the vocabulary the rest of the system must use directly: voltage, power percent, and speed. The calibration defines two-way mappings: between `track_voltage` and `power_percent`, and between `power_percent` and calibrated `speed_kmh`.
- `SpeedProfile` fields:
  - `profile_id`: unique profile identifier.
  - `name`: display name for UI/debug/profile selection.
  - `max_voltage`: top of the usable `track_voltage` range for the first mapping stage.
  - `voltage_power_curve`: stored calibration mapping `(track_voltage, power_percent)` for the first stage. Its v1 default is linear across the usable range, from `lighting_voltage` (0%) to `max_voltage` (100%). **Speed calibration** does not write it; **Lighting-voltage calibration** is what moves its bottom anchor, by committing a new `lighting_voltage`.
  - `power_speed_curve`: stored calibration mapping `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` for the second stage. Its v1 default is linear from `0%` -> `0 km/h` to `100%` -> the owning train's configured `max_speed`. This default profile is seeded at train creation inside `SimulationController.add_train(...)` when placement imports no profile, anchoring the `100%` point to that train's own `max_speed` so the curve matches the train it belongs to.
  - `lighting_voltage`: the single voltage at which the train is lit but not yet moving. The stopped-but-lit states output it, and it is also the 0%-power point (bottom) of `voltage_power_curve`: at exactly this voltage power is 0 and the train holds, and just above it the train begins to move. It is therefore the highest `track_voltage` at which the train is lit and still holds. Its per-locomotive value is set by **Lighting-voltage calibration**.
  - `acceleration_rate`: service acceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `deceleration_rate`: service braking rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `emergency_brake_rate`: emergency-brake deceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `friction_rate`: passive/coasting deceleration rate in `km/h/s`.
  - `operational_decel_ratio`: comfort-braking fraction used when ATO builds operational braking curves.
  - `air_resistance_enabled`: whether the drag model is active.
  - `drag_coefficient`: drag strength used by the drag model.
  - `reference_speed_kmh`: reference speed used to scale the drag model.
  - `power_multipliers`: per-notch table mapping each power handle position to the fraction of `acceleration_rate` that notch applies, so different train models can have different power-notch response. Stored on the profile and read directly by the physics integration and ATO (not from any module-level default); the v1 construction default is the standard notch table.
  - `brake_multipliers`: per-notch table mapping each brake handle position to the fraction of the operational brake rate (`deceleration_rate × operational_decel_ratio`) that notch applies. Stored on the profile and read directly by the physics integration and ATO; the v1 construction default is the standard notch table.

**SpeedProfile constraint**

- `SpeedProfile` is not a speed-limit object. It holds motion-rate data and per-train calibration (`lighting_voltage`, `max_voltage`, `voltage_power_curve`, `power_speed_curve`), not any kind of speed ceiling. Train maximum speed and block maximum speed are separate constraints, each enforced on its own.

**Speed-profile addition**

- Calibration wording uses three explicit values: `track_voltage`, `power_percent`, and `speed_kmh`.
- The calibration has two bidirectional mappings and both must be stated directly in the doc:
  1. `track_voltage <-> power_percent`
  2. `power_percent <-> speed_kmh`
- `voltage_power_curve` is the stored `(track_voltage, power_percent)` mapping used by the first step. `lighting_voltage` is the highest `track_voltage` at which the train is lit and still holds; just above it the train begins to move (and it is the voltage the stopped-but-lit states output). `max_voltage` is the top of the usable range. In v1 the default `voltage_power_curve` is linear across that usable range, from `lighting_voltage` (0%) to `max_voltage` (100%). So reversing from `power_percent` back to `track_voltage` uses the inverse interpolation of this same stored curve.
- `power_speed_curve` is the stored `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` mapping used by the second step. `power_percent` is the normalized control value between voltage and speed; it is not a second name for either of them. Its v1 default is linear from `0%` -> `0 km/h` to `100%` -> the owning train's configured `max_speed` (seeded at default-profile creation; later per-train tuning replaces the curve (see **Speed calibration**), and the stored curve — not `max_speed` — stays the calibration authority). It is always stored explicitly as profile calibration data, never inferred ad hoc elsewhere. Reverse lookup from `speed_kmh` to `power_percent` uses the inverse interpolation of this same stored curve.
- The motion-rate fields (`acceleration_rate`, `deceleration_rate`) govern ATO, manual `simulation` handle integration, service/emergency braking, and coasting. They do not shape manual `power_percent` response (that submode applies the calibrated speed immediately, see TrainPhysics).
- Every train must have a `SpeedProfile` carrying these calibration fields, so `power_percent` manual mode always has a defined voltage/power/speed mapping, even before per-train tuning. `SimulationController.add_train(...)` guarantees one at creation time: when placement imports a **Train Roster** entry that names a profile, the train uses that profile; otherwise `add_train(...)` creates a default whose `power_speed_curve` `100%` point is anchored to the train's configured `max_speed`. An unidentified train, which the Step-0 detector preamble creates instead (see **Power**, Unidentified trains), gets that same default profile and never uses it.

## Speed calibration

Speed calibration measures one locomotive's real power/speed response and writes it into that locomotive's `SpeedProfile`, replacing the seeded linear `power_speed_curve` with measured points. `SpeedCalibration` is the class that owns and runs this procedure, keyed per train; it holds that train's `CalibrationRun`, the transient working record of the pass in progress. The **SpeedProfile** section is the canonical home for the two stored curves (`voltage_power_curve`, `power_speed_curve`); this section is the canonical home for the procedure that fills the second one. Calibration is the per-train tuning the **SpeedProfile** section names: it is the one way a profile's `power_speed_curve` moves off its seeded linear default.

- **A pass commands a power percent and measures the speed that comes back.** It must work this way round: `power_speed_curve` is the curve that turns a wanted speed into a control value, and it is the very thing the pass is building, so the pass must not ask for a target speed while building it. Each step commands one `power_percent`, `TrainPhysics` drives the rails at the `track_voltage` that percent maps to through `voltage_power_curve`, and the pass reads the resulting `speed_kmh` back from measured motion.
- **The sweep runs in `CALIBRATION_POWER_STEP_PERCENT` increments up to `100%`.** At the v1 increment of `10` that is ten measured steps, `10%` through `100%`. The `0%` point needs no measurement: `0%` power is `lighting_voltage`, the voltage at which the train is lit and holds, so `(0%, 0 km/h)` is the curve's origin by definition and the pass writes it as such. That makes **Lighting-voltage calibration** the pass to run first: every step of this sweep is a percentage of a range whose bottom that one sets.
- **A pass measures the second stage only.** Each step yields one `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` point, and the finished set of points is the new `power_speed_curve`. `voltage_power_curve` is not measured and a pass must not write it. There is nothing about it a timed run can see: `power_percent` is the app's own normalized control value rather than a physical quantity, so a step can only command the voltage that curve already assigns to its percent, and reading that voltage back would restate the curve instead of testing it. The first stage stays the mapping the profile states between `lighting_voltage` and `max_voltage`, and **Lighting-voltage calibration** is the pass that moves its bottom anchor.
- **`CalibrationRun` is the transient working record of one pass**: the ordered power steps, the samples gathered so far, and the train under calibration. Like all runtime movement state it is in-memory only (per **Out of Scope**); only its committed result — the profile curve — persists, through ordinary `SpeedProfile` persistence. When the pass finishes, `SpeedCalibration` writes its samples onto the locomotive's linked `SpeedProfile`, so the calibration stays tied to the locomotive through the profile that train and its **Train Roster** entry reference.

**Auto profiling**

Auto profiling is the automatic measurement mode. It times the locomotive across one block of known length using the block detectors the layout already has, and repeats that over the power sweep. It is the canonical home of the timed run; **Manual marking** below is the fallback for a layout the detectors cannot time.

- **Three consecutive blocks, and the middle one is the measurement.** The run needs three blocks the train can pass through in order — call them A, B and C — where `B.length_cm` is known and greater than zero. The train starts standing in A. The clock starts the moment B is confirmed occupied and stops the moment C is confirmed occupied, so what it times is the head crossing B end to end. `B.length_cm` over that elapsed time, converted through the same model-scale helper physics integrates distance with (see **Units contract**), is the measured `speed_kmh` for that power step. The pass watches only the confirmed entries attribution has credited to the calibration train (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection), so another train moving elsewhere on the layout cannot start or stop its clock.
- **Why the middle block and not the first.** Timing from the moment power was applied to the moment B confirms would measure an unknown distance: the model never knows where inside a block a train sits (see **Power**, The calculated span never runs ahead of the detector), so how far the locomotive stood from A's far seam is not a number the pass has. Timing between two entry confirmations measures exactly one block, whatever the start position was. The two stamps are also the same kind of event — a confirmed OCCUPIED transition — so the detector's fixed lag, the one-tick read lag plus the `OCCUPANCY_ON_THRESHOLD` debounce reads, lands on both of them and cancels in the difference (see **Power**, Block occupancy detection). A one-sided interval would carry that lag as a bias in every sample.
- **A is the run-up, and the first crossing at each step is thrown away.** A measured speed is the speed for that power only if the locomotive had stopped accelerating before it reached B, and A is the room it has to get there. The pass does not trust one standing start to be enough room: at each new power step it discards the first crossing of B and keeps the next `CALIBRATION_LAPS`, whose mean is that step's sample. The discarded crossing is what carries the train back around at this step's power, so every kept crossing enters B already settled, and averaging several also stops one noisy crossing from setting a point. The arrangement therefore has to let the train return to A — a loop, or a shuttle the operator reverses between crossings.
- **A step that does not move the train records `0 km/h`.** The low steps may not overcome the locomotive's stiction at all. A step whose B confirmation does not arrive within `CALIBRATION_STEP_TIMEOUT_S` records `0 km/h` and the pass moves to the next step. The flat bottom this leaves on the curve is that locomotive's real stall band, and physics reading `0 km/h` back for those commands is reading what the locomotive actually does.
- **The three blocks are energized by the ordinary power rules.** Auto profiling adds no power rule and takes no exception from one. The calibration train is driven in manual `power_percent` with no path and no reservations, so it holds the rail under its own span, and the block its head is capped against — B, then C — carries `track voltage` as its pending-entry block (see **Power**, Block power-controller role, and **Only the next reserved block is pre-energized**). A, B and C are live in turn under the rule every train already runs under. That rule is also exactly the protection this pass needs: the whole reason for the run is that the profile does not yet describe this locomotive, so nothing further ahead than the block it is entering may be live.
- **A braking override voids the pass.** ATC, service stopping, and EB outrank the calibration command like any other manual power command (see **TrainController**, Canonical power-percent safety-cap ordering). A tick in which one of them applies is a tick the locomotive was not running at the commanded power, so the sample in progress would describe the override rather than the profile. `SpeedCalibration` abandons the pass, writes `0%`, reports the reason on the command-result stream, and leaves the profile's previous curve in place.
- **The measured curve must stay invertible.** Physics reads `power_speed_curve` in both directions, and the reverse lookup from `speed_kmh` to `power_percent` is an inverse interpolation that needs speed to rise with power (see **SpeedProfile**). On commit the assembled samples must be non-decreasing in `speed_kmh`. A step that measures slower than a lower-power step is measured once more; if it measures slower again the pass fails and reports it, rather than storing a curve half the system cannot read backwards.
- **Commit is all or nothing.** A pass that does not complete every step writes nothing. A partly replaced curve would be part measured and part seeded with nothing on the profile to say which part is which, and no later reading of it could tell.
- **One direction is enough.** The run is made under one cab direction. `track_voltage` is a magnitude and neither stored curve carries a sign (see **TrainPhysics**), so the measured curve is the locomotive's response whichever way it is driven.

**Manual marking**

- Manual marking times the locomotive against an operator marker instead of a detector. The operator runs the train at one fixed commanded `power_percent` and issues a calibration `record` command as the head passes a chosen point each lap. The time between two consecutive `record` commands, over the known marker spacing, is the measured `speed_kmh`. As in auto profiling the laps are averaged, `CALIBRATION_LAPS` of them, so one noisy lap does not set the point. Manual marking is the path when block detection cannot time the run — no three blocks in a row the train can take in order, or a middle block whose length was never recorded.
- Both modes produce the same `(power_percent, speed_kmh)` samples, hold them in the same `CalibrationRun`, and feed the same commit, including its non-decreasing and all-or-nothing rules. `CALIBRATION_POWER_STEP_PERCENT`, `CALIBRATION_LAPS`, and `CALIBRATION_STEP_TIMEOUT_S` are constants in `constants.py`.
- Every calibration interval is measured against `AppState.operating_time` (see **Units contract**, Clocks). Wall time would count an operator's stop as distance covered: the session stops, the locomotive stops, and the interval goes on growing. `operating_time` advances only while the session runs, so a pass paused mid-crossing resumes on the same measurement. It also carries the playback multiplier, so a Simulation pass at four times playback measures the model's own km/h rather than four times it, and in Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware, where `simulation_speed` is `1.0`, it advances at wall rate and the pass is a real-world measurement.

**Where it runs**

- Calibration is a runtime activity, because the locomotive must move to be measured. It is a dedicated controlled pass: it commands the train directly, and it must not run while that train is following an ATO route or has a **Lighting-voltage calibration** run open (that pass states the same exclusion from its side). Calibration must add no new persisted state beyond the one `SpeedProfile` curve it writes and must not open a new event sink; progress and the final result are reported as ordinary command-result events on the one stream owned by `NotificationCenter` (see **Core Runtime Loop**, Runtime notifications).
- In Simulation the measured speed comes from the happy-path train model, so a calibration pass reproduces the model's own curve. In Virtual Hardware it comes from the controllable fake device, which lets the procedure and its fault handling be exercised against a complete Hardware Setup. In Real Hardware it comes from the physical locomotive's true response. The procedure is identical in all three environments; only the source of the measured response differs.

## Lighting-voltage calibration

Lighting-voltage calibration finds the one voltage at which a locomotive is lit and still holds, and writes it to `lighting_voltage` on that locomotive's `SpeedProfile` (**SpeedProfile** is the canonical home for what that field is and what else reads it). It is the first calibration a newly added locomotive gets: `SimulationController.add_train(...)` seeds a placeholder value, and every other calibrated number sits on top of it, because a **Speed calibration** sweep takes its bottom step from `lighting_voltage`. `SpeedCalibration` owns this pass as it owns the speed pass, keyed per train.

**The operator's eye is the measurement**

- This pass commands a voltage and asks the operator whether the locomotive moved. Nothing on the rig can answer that question at this level. The drive is by definition too weak to load a current channel — that is what the lighting zone is (see **Hardware setup**, Occupancy source follows drive state) — and first movement is a creep of a few millimetres, far below what timing a head across two block seams can resolve. A person watching the locomotive is the one instrument that reads it, so the operator's judgement is the sample this pass collects, and there is no measured value to write down.

**The panel**

- The Speed Profile panel's calibration group carries a Calibrate control next to the profile's lighting-voltage field. It opens a small panel holding three things: the candidate voltage as it stands, a step-up and a step-down control, and Save.
- A step changes the candidate by `LIGHTING_VOLTAGE_STEP_V` and puts the new value on the rails at once, so every press is answered by the locomotive in front of the operator. `LIGHTING_VOLTAGE_STEP_V` lives in `constants.py`.
- The value to save is the highest step at which the locomotive still held still, because `lighting_voltage` is the lit-but-not-moving voltage: the operator steps up until it creeps, steps back down one, and saves.
- The step-up control stops at the ceiling in **What a candidate may be**, so the panel cannot show a value Save would refuse; the step-down control stops at `0`.
- Save and closing the panel without saving are the two operator-driven endings: Save commits the candidate and ends the run; closing without saving discards it. Either way the run ends and that train's `track_voltage` returns to what its state ordinarily outputs. See **What the run drives** for the conditions that end a run without operator action.

**What the run drives**

- Opening a run requires the train to be placed, in `manual` control mode, `READY` and stopped, with a cab selected, following no ATO route, and with no **Speed calibration** pass open for it. A cab must be selected because the sign put on each block is the direction that cab picked (see **Power**, A block voltage carries a direction): the operator must know which way the locomotive will creep before it does.
- A run ends the moment any of its opening conditions stops holding, not only on Save or panel-close: `SpeedCalibration.cancel_lighting(train_id)` is called on the same tick the condition breaks, the candidate is discarded, and that train's `track_voltage` returns to what its state ordinarily outputs. A run is never open on a train that is `OFF`, EB-latched, or not in `manual` control mode.
- While a run is open, `TrainPhysics` outputs the run's candidate voltage as that train's `track_voltage`, in place of the stopped-but-lit value its state would otherwise output. Nothing else about emission changes: the value is a magnitude, it reaches that train's ordinary energized set, and each block signs it by the ordinary rule (see **Power**, Block power-controller role and A block voltage carries a direction). A train that may open a run is stopped with nothing reserved ahead of it, so that set is the rail under the locomotive.

**What a candidate may be**

- A candidate is at least `0`, and it stays below both the profile's `max_voltage` and the Hardware Setup's `current_sense_min_drive_v`.
- The second bound is the rule **Hardware setup** states: `current_sense_min_drive_v` sits above the profile's `lighting_voltage`, so a lit but motionless train lands in the lighting zone instead of reading as driven (see **Hardware setup**, Occupancy source follows drive state). This pass is the one thing that moves `lighting_voltage`, so it is where that bound is kept.

**What Save writes**

- Committing writes the candidate to `lighting_voltage` on the train's linked `SpeedProfile`, so the value stays with the locomotive through the profile that train and its **Train Roster** entry reference.
- `lighting_voltage` is also the 0%-power point of `voltage_power_curve` (see **SpeedProfile**), so committing moves that curve's bottom anchor to the saved value. A stored curve point below the new anchor is dropped: it names a voltage that does not move this locomotive. The `0%` anchor point itself is rewritten to the saved value, not dropped.

**Where it lives**

- `LightingCalibrationRun` is the transient working record of one run: the train, its profile, and the candidate voltage. Like `CalibrationRun` it is in-memory only (per **Out of Scope**); only the committed value persists, through ordinary `SpeedProfile` persistence. `SpeedCalibration` exposes `start_lighting(train, profile)`, `step_lighting(train_id, direction)`, `commit_lighting(train_id, profile)`, and `cancel_lighting(train_id)`.
- The run adds no persisted state beyond that one profile field and opens no new event sink. It reports as ordinary command-result events on the one stream owned by `NotificationCenter` (see **Core Runtime Loop**, Runtime notifications), the same way **Speed calibration** reports.
- The value describes a physical motor, so Real Hardware is where a run tells the operator something new. In Simulation and Virtual Hardware the modelled train starts moving exactly at the profile's stored anchor, so a run there returns the value already stored. The procedure is identical in all three environments; only whether the answer is new differs.

## Train Roster

The `TrainRoster` is the catalog of locomotives the operator owns. It is persisted layout data, not runtime state: it is edited with the layout and saved and restored in the layout snapshot (runtime state stays in-memory only per **Out of Scope**; persisted layout data survives, the same split the **Maintenance counters** section draws). Each `RosterEntry` records one owned locomotive and the defaults used to put it on the track.

- `RosterEntry` fields:
  - `entry_id`: unique identifier for the roster record.
  - `name`: display name for the locomotive.
  - `model`: free-text description of the physical item — its manufacturer, product, and box, as written on the packaging.
  - `purchase_date`: the date the locomotive was acquired.
  - `purchase_price`: the acquisition cost.
  - `consist_length_cm`: the physical length used to build the train's span when this entry is placed — the train-side value the pathfinder's own-length feasibility filter reads (see **Train Entity**).
  - `max_speed`: the locomotive's top speed.
  - `min_radius_cm`: the locomotive's minimum curve radius — the train-side value the pathfinder's curve filter reads (see **Train Entity**).
  - `max_slope`: the locomotive's maximum climbable grade as a percent, `0.0` meaning unspecified (see **Train Entity**).
  - `speed_profile_id`: the `SpeedProfile` that holds this locomotive's calibration. **Speed calibration** writes that profile, so the measured curves stay tied to the physical locomotive across placements.
- Placing a train may import a `RosterEntry`. When the placement input names one, `SimulationController.add_train(...)` (the placement home in **Train Entity**) seeds the new `Train`'s persistent fields and its `SpeedProfile` from that entry instead of from blank defaults. A roster entry is a template for placement: the placed `Train` is a separate runtime instance, and editing one after placement does not change the other.
- The roster carries the locomotive's identity, purchase record, placement defaults, and profile link only. Cumulative usage stays on the live `Train` and its block and switch counters per the **Maintenance counters** home; the roster stores no second usage total.

## Power

**Hardware interface boundary**

- The runtime drives and reads the physical-world boundary only through abstract interfaces addressed by logical id (`block_id`, `switch_id`), never by pin or wire. There are three: `TrackOutput` for block track voltage, `SwitchOutput` for switch throw, and `OccupancyInput` for detector reads. Simulation constructs its three happy-path model implementations directly and does not read Hardware Setup. Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware construct the same setup-aware HAL implementations as one complete set; their only device difference is in-memory versus physical. A missing role or interface fails the Virtual/Real build — `None` never asks the runtime to install a simulated fallback. `BlockPowerController` holds `TrackOutput` and `SwitchPowerController` holds `SwitchOutput` (both defined below). They are the only callers of the two output interfaces — including the per-channel zero and de-energize writes that `OutputSafety.enter_safe_state()` drives — and they hold no wiring knowledge themselves.
- **No wiring knowledge above the interface.** No runtime, control, route, physics, or manager code names a GPIO pin, expander chip, shift-register bit, or bus type. A concrete implementation owns the map from logical id to physical channel and is the only place that knows the wiring. Direct GPIO, a 74HC595 / 74HC165 shift-register chain, and an I²C expander such as MCP23017 are interchangeable implementations of the same interfaces. Choosing one changes which implementation is constructed and nothing else. The map itself is data — the binding table in **Hardware setup** below — that the concrete implementation loads and is the only reader of.
- **Fault injection belongs to Virtual Hardware.** Simulation's default interfaces model happy hardware: every write succeeds and every read agrees with the model. Tests may inject a stub directly into the interface seam, but operator-facing hardware faults are modeled by Virtual Hardware's in-memory device, below the real chip and HAL implementations. The canonical injectable fault kinds are exactly `transaction_refusal`, `timeout`, `device_disconnect`, `invalid_reading` (`OccupancyInput` and the block-voltage readback), `input_hold_or_flicker` (`OccupancyInput`), and `output_disagrees_with_command` (`TrackOutput` readback). Every other site names this set rather than restating it, and `PUT /api/virtual-hardware/fault` takes one of these values. That keeps Simulation focused on algorithms while proving the same hardware-failure path Real Hardware uses.
- **Input / sensing boundary.** `OccupancyInput` is the read side for sensors that no software output drives. v1 reads one thing through it: block current detection, via `read_block_currents()`. (The block track-voltage readback is the output interface reading back its own output, `TrackOutput.get_actual_voltage`, not an `OccupancyInput` read; its one v1 consumer is the diagnostic block-voltage read `BlockPowerController.get_block_voltage(block_id)` — see **Block power-controller role**.) Block current detection is the block-occupancy authority. Its read, debounce, attribution, and position reconciliation are defined in **Block occupancy detection** below. `OccupancyInput` also names the blocks it can answer for, through `detected_block_ids()`.
