In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware, activation requires both occupancy bindings for every layout block, so this set is the complete layout block set. For `SimulatedOccupancyDriver` it is empty, because Simulation senses nothing physical and derives occupancy from the model. **Block occupancy detection** reads the set to decide whether detector-gated movement applies. Switch alignment has no sensor read in v1: authority is the commanded `Switch.position`. Treating any sensor as a fault-detection, verification, or recovery source is **Out of Scope** for v1.
- **Safe state / fail-safe.** Each output interface defines a safe state, but the sequence is owned by one session-level entity so track and point outputs cannot disagree about whether the rig is driving. That entity is `OutputSafety`, and it exposes one method: `enter_safe_state()`, which every safe-state caller in this document calls. It performs the whole sequence itself, in order: (1) de-energize every point motor through `SwitchPowerController`, then (2) command every block voltage to `0` through `BlockPowerController`. Point motors go first because a solenoid is the output that destroys itself if left energized; track voltage only keeps a train moving. Every chip is attempted even when one fails, so a single dead chip cannot leave the rest energized, and the failures are reported together. The runtime requests safe state when operation stops, before commissioning takes the outputs, before restart or shutdown, and synchronously before any fatal runtime or hardware failure freezes the session. Virtual Hardware performs the same sequence against the fake device; Simulation only zeroes its stored values.
- **Safe state is a de-energize, not a lock.** Nothing is withheld from the outputs afterwards: the next `start` drives the layout again with no reactivation, because stopping took away the voltage and nothing else. This is the whole of v1's physical safety mechanism, and its limit is written into what it is — reaching the safe state is a write, so it needs a live process and a working transport. A process or host that dies leaves each board holding its last commanded value, which `activation_report` reports as a Real Hardware readiness warning so activating is an informed act.
- **Loss-of-control watchdog.** A real output implementation arms a watchdog that reaches the safe state when the control loop stops refreshing the outputs. A landed output write is the proof the loop is alive; going without one for the watchdog's timeout drops every track voltage and point motor. This covers a tick that stalls, deadlocks, or returns without running its shutdown path while the process still lives — the case where a locomotive is under power and nothing is steering it. It reports the drop and stops there: it protects the layout rather than ending the session, so a loop that recovers drives again on its next write. The report is one `kind = "warning"` event through `NotificationCenter`, with `operation` naming the emitting driver function; it changes no runtime state of its own. `enter_safe_state()` disarms it, because a runtime that deliberately stopped driving is not a runtime that lost control; the next landed output write re-arms it.
- **Hardware I/O failure is fatal to the physical session.** A failed output write, failed detector transaction, lost controller, or inability to complete safe state reports the failure to `SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)`. That method, not the reporting entity, is the only writer of `hardware_failure = True`, mirroring `enter_failed_runtime(...)` in the **Fatal physical-entry conflict procedure**. It calls `OutputSafety.enter_safe_state()` — which attempts every de-energize and zero action even when one of them fails — records every failure, and latches the flag. `SimulationController.update(...)` refuses all further advancement while `hardware_failure` is set, on the same gate as `failed_runtime`. The operation loop stops; it does not log-and-retry the tick and it never replaces the failed interface with a simulated one. `SimulationController.clear_hardware_failure()` is the only writer of `hardware_failure = False`. It is refused unless the environment has been reactivated, because the failure deactivated the environment and only activation makes it this session's to drive again, so reactivation is the whole of the recovery surface. `SimulationController.clear_failed_runtime()` does not clear it. Recovery requires repairing or replacing the device and explicitly reactivating Real Hardware. Virtual Hardware follows the same latch and recovery flow so it can be tested without the rig.

**Hardware setup**

- Hardware Setup is the physical description shared by Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware: a `controller` (the transport bridge), a `chips` list, the binding maps that wire logical ids to chip ports, and detector calibration. It contains no `enabled`, `develop`, or selected-environment flag. Simulation never reads it. The setup is persisted to an app-managed file and inside its layout save file (see **Saved with the layout**); the selected operating environment is stored separately and loading a layout never activates hardware.
- **Completeness is activation policy, not edit policy.** An incomplete setup may be saved and exercised through bounded commissioning while a rig is being built, but it cannot activate Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware operation. Until the setup is complete, the only operating environment that may run the railway is Simulation. Completeness is checked against the loaded layout: every block has one `block_track` binding plus both required occupancy bindings (`block_occupancy` and `block_occupancy_current`); every switch has one `switch_output` binding and one `switch_track_source` binding; every binding names a present compatible chip and unique in-range port; no binding names a deleted entity; and every required device is reachable. Absence of a required item is always a readiness blocker. A missing binding, chip, device, or detector source is never interpreted as a clear input, an unpowered/undetected exception, or a request for a simulated fallback.
- **Controller.** The transport a real session drives through. v1 has one, `ch347` — a USB-HID bridge that carries an I²C bus, an SPI bus, and GPIO. It may carry a `device_port`, but `ch347` opens by USB VID/PID, so the port is optional. The controller list stays open, so another bridge is one added entry.
- **Chips.** Each chip has a `chip_id`, a `kind`, a `bus`, and the parameters that kind needs. v1 has five kinds: `pca9685` (PWM) drives track power, `hc595` (shift-out) drives switch throws, and block occupancy has two sources — `hc165` (shift-in), the digital detector, and a current monitor, which reads block supply current directly. A current monitor is either an `ina3221` (3 channels) or an `ina219` (1 channel); the two differ only in how many blocks one chip covers. A chip's interface `role` is derived from its `kind`, never stored; the occupancy kinds share the `occupancy_input` role and differ by port kind. Its `bus` is user-set from the buses its kind allows, so it is stored, not derived: `pca9685`, `ina3221`, and `ina219` are I²C only, while a shift register (`hc595` / `hc165`) is `spi` or `gpio`. A shift register clocks its data over the SPI peripheral on either bus; its `bus` names where its **latch line** sits — one of the two hardware chip-select pads (`spi`), or a general-purpose pin (`gpio`). The `gpio` bus exists because the bridge carries only two chip-select pads, so a third shift register must latch somewhere else. The rest of a chip's parameters are user-set as well: an `i2c_address` for an I²C chip; a latch line (`spi_select`) plus a cascade count (`chain_length`) for a shift register on either the SPI or GPIO bus. A `pca9685` contributes 16 PWM channels, of which a block's track takes two; a shift register contributes `8 × chain_length` bits; an `ina3221` contributes 3 current-sense channels and an `ina219` 1.
- **A block's track is two PWM channels.** A commanded block voltage is signed — the sign is the block-relative direction the train runs through that block (see **Block power-controller role**, A block voltage carries a direction) — so a block's track is an H-bridge whose two inputs are driven by two `pca9685` channels. The binding names the two `forward` and `reverse`; those are its two slots, and which slot a positive voltage drives is what the binding's `reversed` flag states (see **The rail binding bonds tile order to rail polarity** below). The driver maps voltage magnitude to duty cycle and voltage sign to which of the two channels drives, holding the other at zero, so the two inputs are never driven together. The two are chosen independently, not as a fixed adjacent pair, because a bench build may wire a block's rails to any two channels on the chip.
- **A block at `0` has nothing applied to it.** Zero is a commanded voltage like any other, and the same rule covers it: the channel matching the sign carries the magnitude, and the other rests at zero, so at zero neither channel drives and the H-bridge is given no input at all. That is what an un-energized block is — one the app applies no voltage to. It is not a claim about what a meter reads on those rails. A locomotive's wheelbase bridges an insulated joint as it crosses one, and while it does, the block it is leaving feeds the block it is entering through the locomotive itself; that is a property of the track, not of any voltage the app commanded. Energizing one block ahead is what bounds which block can be fed that way (see **Block power-controller role**, Only the next reserved block is pre-energized).
- **The rail binding bonds tile order to rail polarity.** A block's tile order and its rail polarity are independent facts. The order comes from how the layout was drawn; which rail a channel drives comes from how the rig was wired. Nothing makes the two agree on their own, and a block driven by the wrong one runs its trains backwards. The `reversed` flag on that block's `block_track` binding is what bonds them: it states which of the two bound channels drives the block in its `forward` direction, so a positive `track voltage` drives the binding's `forward` channel when the flag is clear and its `reverse` channel when the flag is set. Neither value is a fault — they are the two ways a block can be wired — and the **track direction sweep** under **Self-test** is the measurement that establishes which one this block is. It is wiring, so it lives with the Hardware Setup like every other binding and persists independently of the layout; an operator never needs a layout save for the bond to survive a restart. The bond must live below the hardware interface: the commanded voltage keeps its block-relative sign everywhere above, so **Occupancy source follows drive state** reads that same value and no layer above the driver carries a wiring branch — the same rule **Detector polarity is resolved in the hardware layer** states for the digital detector. The binding's `forward` and `reverse` channels themselves must not change, because they name two physical rails and one of those rails may carry the block's idle detection feed; rebinding to flip a block would move that feed. A channel edit that does not name the flag keeps its stored value, so re-picking the rails never silently resets a bonded block. The flag is one of the values a driver resolves per write, so correcting it reaches the next track write without rebuilding the session (see **A binding is read per write** below). The flag covers the whole block because a block is fed as one: the binding feeds every member segment, and a segment carries no polarity of its own; a block with no rail binding has no rails to bond. The sweep resolves the flag the same way the driver does — it drives the channel a positive `track voltage` would drive on that block first, which is the channel that must move a locomotive toward that block's higher tile indices. A block whose flag does not match its wiring runs the loco the wrong way on that first leg and names itself; the same block with the flag corrected runs it the right way, which is the operator's check that the bond is right.
- **A switch throw is a pulse on one of two coils.** A solenoid point motor has one coil per side, so a `switch_output` entry names two `hc595` output bits: `port_main` throws toward MAIN and `port_diverge` throws toward DIVERGE. A throw energizes one coil, waits the fixed throw pulse width, and de-energizes it, so both coils rest low between throws and the alignment is held by the points themselves rather than by a driven output. The pulse is short — a coil left energized burns out — and its width is one value in the driver, used unchanged by an operating session and by the commissioning throw test, so a bench test proves the same drive the runtime issues. `port_standby` is the optional bit wired to a motor driver's standby/enable pin, for drivers that ignore their inputs until it is high. A build whose driver needs no such pin leaves `port_standby` unset. Every shift-out bit the commissioning surface drives is driven this same way, because any bit may be wired to a coil.
- **The order of a throw's writes is part of the contract.** The two coil bits are the motor driver's two inputs, so a throw must write them in this order: raise `port_standby`; drive the target coil high; drive the *other* coil low; wait the pulse width; drop `port_standby`; drop the target coil. Two rules in that sequence are what keep a motor alive, and neither is optional. The other coil must be driven low on every throw rather than assumed low, so a throw is correct from any prior bit pattern — including the one a shift-register chain powers up in — and never drives both inputs at once. And `port_standby` must drop *before* the coil, because that one write puts the driver's outputs into high impedance; dropping a coil input first would leave the driver enabled with both inputs low, which is its brake mode and shorts the coil. The final coil write then only settles the resting pattern, except on a driver with no standby bit, where it is the de-energize — which is why it is unconditional. The de-energizing writes must happen even when the throw fails part-way, so a failed throw leaves no coil driven.
- **Latch lines are named by pad.** A shift register latches on a line named by the CH347 board pad, and the line set depends on its bus: the SPI bus offers `cs0` and `cs1`; the GPIO bus offers `gpio0`–`gpio7`. The `ch347api` library names the SPI lines off by one — its `set_CS1` drives pad `cs0`, its `set_CS2` drives pad `cs1` — so the driver holds the pad-to-library map and the correct active polarity per chip kind. No layer above the driver names a pad or a library call.
- **A GPIO-latched chip borrows a chip-select pad.** The bridge clocks SPI data only while a chip-select is asserted, and it releases that select when the transfer ends. A shift register latched on a GPIO pin must therefore borrow one of the two pads for the length of its transfer. The driver borrows a pad that no `hc595` latches on, because releasing a `hc595`'s latch pad would clock stray bits into that chip and may throw a switch; an `hc165`'s load pad is safe to borrow, since a transfer only freezes and reloads its parallel inputs. A build in which every chip-select pad latches an `hc595` must not also carry a GPIO-latched chip.
- **Bindings.** The binding maps carry the logical-id-to-chip-port wiring, each keyed by logical id: `block_track` (`block_id` to the two PWM channels driving its rails, `forward` and `reverse`, plus the `reversed` flag bonding those channels to the block's tile order — see **A block's track is two PWM channels** and **The rail binding bonds tile order to rail polarity** above), `switch_output` (`switch_id` to its two coil ports plus an optional standby port, see **A switch throw is a pulse on one of two coils** below), and two occupancy maps — `block_occupancy` (`block_id` to a digital-detector port on an `hc165`, see **Detector polarity is resolved in the hardware layer** below) and `block_occupancy_current` (`block_id` to a current-sense channel on an `ina3221` or an `ina219`, each entry also carrying that channel's `baseline_ma` and `threshold_ma`). Every layout block must appear exactly once in `block_track` and in both occupancy maps before Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware may activate. One further required map, `switch_track_source`, wires every `switch_id` to the `block_id` whose feed powers that switch's rails; it is the one binding whose value is another logical id rather than a chip port, because a switch's rails have no channel of their own (see **Switch power-controller role** below). It must name the block at the switch's main entrance. `SwitchPowerController` is its only reader; the rest of this binding table is the physical-channel map named under **Hardware interface boundary**, and the concrete driver is its only reader. A binding must name a configured chip whose role matches the map — and, for the two occupancy maps, whose port kind matches (a digital detector bit for `block_occupancy`, a current-sense channel for `block_occupancy_current`) — at a port within that chip's range; a port must not be bound more than once, the two channels of one `block_track` entry must differ, and the two coil ports and the standby port of one `switch_output` entry must all differ. The editor may temporarily save any of these maps incomplete, and commissioning may exercise the entries that exist, but the readiness report keeps hardware-backed operation blocked until the complete cardinality and validity rules pass.
- **A binding is read per write.** A binding names a port; a session is built from chips. Those are different lifetimes, and v1 gives each its own: the chips, their transport, and the maps the chips are constructed and range-checked against are captured when Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware is activated, while the values below are looked up on every write, from the setup as it stands at that moment.
  - a switch's `port_main`, `port_diverge`, and `port_standby` (`switch_output`)
  - a block's `reversed` rail-polarity flag on its `block_track` binding
  - a switch's `switch_track_source` rail feed

  These three are the values a rig being wired gets wrong and corrects, and not one of them changes which chips a session builds — each only re-aims an existing chip, or names a block. An operator who re-picks a coil port must see the next throw land on the new bit: a correction that takes effect only after the environment is activated again reads as a control that does nothing, and the operator changes the wiring instead of the setting. Every other part of the setup — the controller, the chip list, a `block_track` binding's two channels, both occupancy maps and their calibration, and the shared drive-zone levels — is captured at activation, because changing it means building different chips. `GET /api/operation/environment` reports whether the saved setup holds captured changes the running session was not built with; the per-write values never raise that flag, because they are already in use.

- **Occupancy source follows drive state.** A block bound in both occupancy maps is read from whichever source is trustworthy this tick. The magnitude of the block's commanded `track voltage` decides which one, because how hard software is driving the rails is what makes a detector believable; the sign is direction and has no bearing on it. Two configured levels split that range into three zones, and both are one value for the whole Hardware Setup, so a rig sets them once: `idle_deadband_v`, at or below which nothing is driving the rails, and `current_sense_min_drive_v`, at or above which the drive is strong enough to put a readable load on a current channel. `current_sense_min_drive_v` must sit above the profile's `lighting_voltage` (**SpeedProfile**), so a lit but motionless train lands in the middle zone rather than reading as driven. **Lighting-voltage calibration** is where that bound is kept, because it is the one procedure that moves `lighting_voltage`.
  - **Idle** (at or below `idle_deadband_v`): no drive, so there is no current to sense and the block's `hc165` digital detector decides.
  - **Driven** (at or above `current_sense_min_drive_v`): the rail drive swamps the digital detector, but a motor under real drive draws a current the sense channel reads cleanly, so the current channel decides — current is present when `abs(current - baseline_ma)` reaches the channel's `threshold_ma`.
  - **Lighting** (between the two): a stopped-but-lit locomotive. The drive disturbs the digital detector yet is too weak to load the current channel, so neither source may clear the block on its own. Either source reading current present confirms the block on that one read. A clear must be earned: the block is reported clear only after **both** sources have read clear on every one of `LIGHTING_CLEAR_HOLD_READS` consecutive reads, and until then the last value the driver reported holds. Any read where either source sees current resets that count, and so does leaving the zone.

  The lighting zone needs that sustained-agreement rule because occupancy and drive are a loop: a block that reads clear is released, releasing it cuts its voltage, and cutting its voltage changes which zone it is in and therefore which detector answers. A rule that clears on one blind read sends the block around that loop, confirming and vacating several times a second with the track voltage pulsing in step. A rule that never clears breaks the loop but makes the hold a latch — software keeps driving the block because it is occupied, and it stays occupied because software keeps driving it, so nothing physical can ever release it. Requiring both sources to agree for a whole window breaks the loop and still gives the hold an exit: a detector flickering under the drive asserts current somewhere inside the window and resets the count, so it delays a release rather than racing one.

  Both sources are mandatory for every block in Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware. A missing binding blocks activation; a source that becomes unavailable after activation is a hardware I/O failure and enters the fail-closed hardware-failure lifecycle. Missing never counts as clear. This selection lives inside the hardware-backed `OccupancyInput`; the per-block `current present` boolean it returns is the same shape whichever zone answered, so **Block occupancy detection** consumes it without knowing the source.
- **Detector polarity is resolved in the hardware layer.** A shift-in chip carries an `active_low` flag describing how its detector drives the line: a comparator with no inverter stage pulls its output low when a train draws current, so that chain's raw bit reads 0 occupied and 1 clear, while a detector with an inverter reads the other way. Polarity is stated once per chip, because a detector chain is wired one way throughout: every block bound to that chip reads the same way, and a binding carries no polarity of its own. The real `OccupancyInput` applies the chip's polarity as it reads each bit. The boolean it returns must always mean `current present`, whichever way the detector is wired, so polarity is invisible above the interface — no code above it, and no other physical source, carries a polarity branch. Polarity applies to the digital detector only: a current-sense channel is judged on the magnitude `abs(current - baseline_ma)`, which has no polarity to resolve.
- **Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware share one setup.** Both environments build every required chip, binding, interface, occupancy rule, and safety-supervisor contract from the same Hardware Setup. Virtual Hardware supplies an in-memory transport and emulates whatever safety gate the setup maps; Real Hardware supplies the physical transport and external gate. The build is atomic and all-or-nothing. Missing roles, bindings, chips, or required safety facilities are activation blockers, never requests for simulated fallbacks.
- **Simulation is independent of Hardware Setup.** Simulation constructs its model interfaces without opening, validating, pruning, or changing Hardware Setup. A broken or incomplete setup therefore cannot prevent Simulation from running, and running Simulation cannot be mistaken for successfully activating a requested Real Hardware environment.
- **Self-test and commissioning ownership.** Self-test opens the selected device and probes each chip — an I²C chip must ACK, and a shift register's transaction must complete — and the ports of every reachable chip form the pool the binding maps draw from. Commissioning is an explicit setup session, not an operating environment and never a tool that borrows outputs from live operation. It may inspect an incomplete setup so the operator can build and bind the rig, but it cannot run trains or grant ordinary operation ownership. Entering it safely stops operation, drives the outputs to their safe state, and gives one bounded commissioning owner the outputs. It takes the outputs and not the device, so the detectors go on being read the whole time it is open (see **Commissioning owns the writes; the detectors stay shared** below). A port-test then drives one output port or reads one input port so a single wire can be confirmed before binding. A **track direction sweep** drives the block's two bound channels one way, off, then the other way, with a hard duration bound and safe-state cleanup; it resolves the block's `reversed` flag as the driver does (see **The rail binding bonds tile order to rail polarity** under **Power**). A **switch throw test** pulses one coil of a bound switch through the same write order and pulse width an operating throw uses, and then records that alignment on the switch itself, because the points have moved and the app must not go on describing the layout the way it was before the operator watched them move. It writes model state, and only that: it drives no train and grants no operation ownership. Recording it is not a throw command — the coil is already pulsed — so it does not need an operating environment, which is the state every bench throw runs in. A **block occupancy test** drives nothing; when both required bindings exist it reports both sources, the drive zone, and resulting verdict, and otherwise reports the missing source as a readiness blocker rather than synthesizing a result. Leaving, timing out, disconnecting, or failing commissioning completes the safe-state sequence before ordinary operation may reactivate. Virtual Hardware exercises the identical workflow against its fake device.
- **Commissioning owns the writes; the detectors stay shared.** A session opens one device handle and every owner reaches the rig through it. What a commissioning session takes is the exclusive right to **write**: while it holds the outputs it is the only writer of a track channel or a coil, and no other owner may drive one. It does not take the reads. `OccupancyInput.read_block_currents()` is served for the whole commissioning session, so the global read, the debounce, the attribution, and the Phase-3 commit keep running and `Block.occupied_by` stays live (see **Block occupancy detection**, The read does not stop for a commissioning session). This is what a bench is for: an operator opens one to find out why a block reads wrong, and the layout beside it must go on saying what the app currently believes, because that comparison is the diagnosis. No train moves meanwhile — entering commissioning stopped operation, and a stopped hardware-backed session runs the detector pass and nothing else. Reads and writes never collide on the transport, because every device transaction runs on the one runtime thread (see **SimulationController**, the threading model). The zone a block is read in follows the voltage commanded onto that block, whichever owner commanded it (see **Occupancy source follows drive state**): a commissioning owner that drives a block must report that voltage through the same per-block read the zone rule uses, so a rail a bench is driving is read in the `driven` zone and its current channel decides, exactly as it would under operation. Leaving, timing out, disconnecting, or failing commissioning completes the safe-state sequence and returns the writes; the reads never stopped, so occupancy needs nothing reactivated to be visible again.
- **Persisted setup; separate selected environment.** A setup `PUT` writes the whole Hardware Setup to an app-managed JSON file. It may be incomplete while edited, but activation performs full layout-aware validation. Setup changes never rewire a running session in place. The selected environment is stored independently; requesting Simulation remains possible with a malformed setup, while requesting Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware yields a structured blocked state rather than falling back. Activation owns safe stop, rebuild, reconnect, and verification; the operator-facing workflow never requires a raw "restart server" step.
- **Saved with the layout.** Saving a layout writes its Hardware Setup into the layout file because bindings are keyed by that layout's `block_id` and `switch_id`. Loading the layout may adopt that setup after validation, but does not import or change the selected operating environment and never activates physical output. A loaded setup replaces the previous setup as a whole; absent sections become empty/default edit-state values and therefore remain explicit readiness blockers wherever required, dangling bindings are reported, and invalid setup is reported without preventing the geometry from loading.
- **REST surface.**

| Endpoint | Body | Behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `GET /api/hardware/setup` | — | Returns the saved Hardware Setup: `controller`, `chips` (including stored bus plus derived role/direction/port metadata), detector calibration, binding maps, and the layout-aware readiness report. It contains no selected-environment flag. |
| `PUT /api/hardware/setup` | `{ "controller": {…}, "chips": [ … ], "block_track": {…}, "block_occupancy": {…}, "block_occupancy_current": {…}, "switch_output": {…}, "switch_track_source": {…} }` | Validates the fields and bindings that exist and writes the whole setup. Incompleteness is returned in the readiness report and is allowed while editing or commissioning, but it blocks Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware activation; only Simulation may operate until every required binding and device passes readiness. Malformed fields, incompatible chips, out-of-range or duplicate ports, and dangling references are rejected. The running session keeps the chips and maps it was built with until explicit activation; the values named in **A binding is read per write** reach the rig on the next write. |
| `GET /api/operation/environment` | — | Returns `requested_environment`, `active_environment`, lifecycle state (`inactive`, `activating`, `active`, `blocked`, `hardware_failure`, or `commissioning`, where `commissioning` means a commissioning session holds the outputs while the detectors are still read — see **Commissioning owns the writes; the detectors stay shared**), readiness blockers, device health, and whether the saved setup holds changes the running session was not built with (the captured parts only — see **A binding is read per write**). This is the single session truth the header and diagnostics read. |
| `PUT /api/operation/environment` | `{ "environment": "simulation" \| "virtual_hardware" \| "real_hardware" }` | Selects the requested environment but does not silently rewire a running session. Simulation needs no setup. Virtual/Real selection returns the readiness blockers that would prevent activation. |
| `POST /api/operation/activate` | — | Safely stops the current session, validates the requested environment, builds all interfaces atomically, reconnects, and verifies the active environment. A Virtual/Real failure returns the structured blocked state; it never activates Simulation as a fallback. |
| `GET /api/hardware/catalog` | — | Returns the selectable controllers and chip kinds — the buses each kind allows, role, direction, port count, and required parameters — that the setup UI reads. |
| `POST /api/hardware/self-test` | `{ "setup": {…}, "device": "virtual" \| "physical" }` | Probes the selected device and each chip; returns per-chip reachability and ports. It runs only while operation does not own physical outputs. |
| `POST /api/hardware/commission/{enter,output,pulse,inputs,currents,exit}` | per-action payload | `enter` safely stops operation and transfers exclusive **output** ownership to a bounded commissioning session; the occupancy read is shared and keeps running throughout (see **Commissioning owns the writes; the detectors stay shared**). The actions exercise ports with the same drivers and duration limits as operation. `exit`, timeout, disconnect, or failure completes the safe state before releasing ownership. These endpoints change no setup. |
| `GET /api/virtual-hardware` | — | Virtual Hardware only. Returns fake-device connection state, raw inputs, measured/current values, output commands/readback, injected faults, and active high-level scenario. Each block reports which outputs and detectors it is bound to, the raw detector bit alongside that bit resolved through the chain's polarity, and the commanded duty on both of its rails alongside the signed voltage those duties mean once the binding's `reversed` flag is applied. |
| `PUT /api/virtual-hardware/input` | raw detector/current payload | Changes fake device input state only; the ordinary occupancy pipeline observes it on its next read. It writes the one source named in the payload, at the raw level: a digital write sets the register bit itself, so on an `active_low` chain the bit that means `current present` is 0 (see **Detector polarity is resolved in the hardware layer** above). Writing one source leaves the block's other source saying whatever it said before, which is how a deliberate disagreement between the two is set up. |
| `PUT /api/virtual-hardware/input/presence` | `{ "block_id": …, "present": true \| false }` | Puts a virtual object on one block's detectors, or lifts it off. It writes every occupancy source the block is bound to in one call — the digital bit resolved through the chain's polarity, and a current clear of that channel's `threshold_ma` above its `baseline_ma` — so the block reads the same way whichever source **Occupancy source follows drive state** selects. A block bound to no occupancy source is rejected. |
| `PUT /api/virtual-hardware/fault` | device/transaction/readback fault payload | Injects or clears a fake controller, chip, port, response, or readback failure below the real HAL implementation. |
| `POST /api/virtual-hardware/scenario` | place/move/stop/remove train scenario payload | Converts a topology-aware virtual train action into raw fake detector/current transitions, one block at a time through the same write `PUT /api/virtual-hardware/input/presence` performs. A step across a block boundary confirms the entered block's detectors and clears the left block's in the same call. It never writes `Block.occupied_by`, controller span, or another operational state directly. |

**Block occupancy detection**

- Each block has its own current detector and is its own occupancy section; its member segments are geometry only and are not independently detected (see **Block**, Detection isolation is separate from geometry). Current present on a block means a train is standing on it; no current means no train is. This detection is the authority for `Block.occupied_by`: it replaces any guess from the calculated train position. Switch occupancy is not detected this way. Switch-alignment authority stays the commanded `Switch.position` (see **Out of Scope**), and a switch's physical entry and exit still come from the train's calculated traversal.
- **One global read per tick.** At the top of each tick the runtime reads every block's detector at once through `OccupancyInput.read_block_currents()`, which returns a plain `block_id -> current present` map with no train identity. In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware, which physical source produces that boolean — an `hc165` digital detector or a current-sense channel selected by drive state — is defined in **Hardware setup**. In Simulation, `SimulatedOccupancyDriver` derives the same-shaped map from live train spans. The read reflects where trains were at the end of the previous tick, so a block the locomotive just entered is confirmed one tick later; that one-tick lag is the honest sensor latency.
- **The read does not stop for a commissioning session.** A commissioning owner takes the outputs, not the detectors, so this whole pipeline — the global read, the debounce, the attribution, and the Phase-3 commit — keeps running while one is open (see **Hardware setup**, Commissioning owns the writes; the detectors stay shared). What stops is train motion, because entering commissioning stops operation first. A block a locomotive stands on therefore reads OCCUPIED throughout, and the only thing that changed is who may drive the rails.
- **Debounce with on/off thresholds.** Real contacts flicker: a block a train is standing on can briefly read open. `BlockOccupancyDetector` keeps a per-block counter and confirms a block OCCUPIED only after `OCCUPANCY_ON_THRESHOLD` consecutive current reads, and CLEAR only after `OCCUPANCY_OFF_THRESHOLD` consecutive no-current reads. The bias is fail-safe: occupy quickly, clear slowly, so a momentary open circuit inside the train's span does not drop the block. Both thresholds live in `constants.py`. Each tick the detector compares the whole confirmed-occupied set against the previous one, so a fast train that enters or clears several blocks in one tick is reported in one pass.
- **Placement and restore are seeded, not re-detected.** Some blocks become occupied outside the runtime detector path: `place_train` and runtime state restore set `Block.occupied_by` directly when a train is put on the track. This initial placement is the one sanctioned exception to the "runtime entry writes `occupied_by` only through `BlockManager.occupy_blocks(...)`" rule, because it runs before the first tick (the same way `add_train(...)` seeds `current_span` before the first tick). At the top of each tick the detector seeds every already-occupied block as already confirmed, so a block a train was placed on is not reported as a fresh entry. Such a block still clears normally once its current drops for the off-window.
- **Attribution.** The raw read carries no train identity, so each confirmed change is matched to a train from the live authority model.
  - **Match order for a newly OCCUPIED block.** Try in order:
    1. the train the block is reserved for (`Block.reserved_for_train`, the reserved-ahead corridor) — and only when that train is standing next to the block;
    2. the train whose live span currently covers it;
    3. an ordinary train holding a block reachable from it through undetected track.

    Standing next to means the same reachability walk in all three steps, so a train never gains a block with another block between it and everything it holds.
  - **A train is not entering a block it is being driven away from.** Standing next to a block says the two are near each other and nothing about which way either is going, so every one of those matches also asks whether the train could be entering that block at all. `TrainPhysics.could_be_entering(block_id)` is the canonical home of this test.
    - A moving train could only have entered a block in front of it, so current appearing on the side it is driving away from is not that train arriving but something else on the track behind it. In front means the same forward walk the span correction uses, which follows the points as aligned and stops at the first `detected block` the detector has not confirmed (see **Physical detection outranks the calculated position**).
    - The test reads only which way the train is being driven, never how far it got: a `SpeedProfile` describes the locomotive it was calibrated for, and a train reaching a block early is the case this exists for, so a distance would be the one number it could not trust.
    - A train that is not moving is not being driven away from anything, and every block next to it stays plausible: the model never knows where inside a block a train sits, so a stationary locomotive may be bridging an insulated joint the model shows it well clear of, drawing current on the block either side of it.
  - **A reservation on its own is not evidence of where a locomotive is.** A train reserves the corridor in front of it before it gets there, so current on a reserved block with a clear `detected block` still between it and the train must not be read as that train: matching it would mean the train skipped the block in between, which is track no detector confirmed. Such current is a different object, and the block the train can no longer reach is taken out of its authority (see **Path And PathDispatcher**, Authority truncation).
  - **Matching runs until it stops resolving blocks**, rather than in one pass: a train fast enough to cross several blocks between two reads has them all confirmed in the same comparison, and each block a train gains in this pass is evidence for the next one, so the second block across is matched to the train that just gained the first.
  - **The third step keeps one locomotive one record.** A locomotive standing across a block boundary draws current on both blocks, and the far one matches no reservation and no span — but it is this train spanning two blocks, not a second object. Reachability is the same connectivity walk unidentified-train identity uses, so only the first block across undetected track counts: current with a block between it and every train is a different object.
  - **A newly CLEAR block** is matched to its current holder (`Block.occupied_by`).
  - **A confirmed entry that none of those three steps matches** is attributed to an **unidentified train**: a `Train` the Step-0 preamble creates for it, with its own `train_id` and its own `TrainController` (see **Unidentified trains**). From that point it is an ordinary train to every other rule in this document.
    - Step 0 writes its `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` exactly as it does for any other train, its final Phase-2 callback folds them into a progress report, and Phase 3 commits its entries and vacates through the same coordinator/manager path — so no occupant needs a side channel into `Block.occupied_by`.
    - A different train that physically enters a block an unidentified train holds is the ordinary `occupied_by_other_train` fatal conflict; claiming what it is standing on is adoption's job, not a second train's (see **Unidentified trains**, Adoption).
    - Under Simulation no unidentified train ever appears: its current map is built from live train spans, so every confirmed-occupied block already matches a train the app controls. An unidentified train may appear in Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware when the hardware-backed input reports current on a block no controllable train covers.
- **The confirmed change flows through the normal Phase-3 path.** Step 0 writes the affected train's `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks` / `pending_detected_vacated_blocks` and never writes `Block.occupied_by` itself, for every train without exception. The train's final Phase-2 callback folds those sets into its progress report and clears them; Phase 3 commits block entry/vacate through the ordinary coordinator/manager path. Unidentified trains take this same path, which is why a detected occupant the app does not control is modelled as a train rather than as a second kind of occupant. `physics_sync.pending_block_exit_edges` remains the separate direction buffer, holding an entry across ticks when detector clear lags calculated tail exit and holding one within a single tick for an unidentified train.
- **Physical detection outranks the calculated position.** When confirmed occupancy leads the calculated span, Step 0 calls `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(train, controller)` directly for that train; the method itself computes a forward-only corrected span and writes it onto `physics_sync.current_span`, before route ordering/Phase 1. Physics later integrates from that corrected span and its `span_update.new_span` replaces it with the ordinary end-of-tick result. Thus correction and integration compose sequentially rather than competing at one apply site. The correction chases only the confirmed blocks the head can reach by moving forward, and it stops at the first `detected block` the detector has not confirmed occupied — the same line the cap holds the head at (see **The calculated span never runs ahead of the detector**). A block on the far side of one is not somewhere this train can be, because reaching it means having crossed track whose detector says it is clear, so the correction never carries a span over a boundary the cap would not let the train cross under power. A confirmed block behind the span — the locomotive is further back than the calculated position put it, and attribution has just given that block to this train — cannot be reconciled by a forward-only walk, so the span does not move for it and the block is simply held. `TrainPhysics` is the Step-0 span writer; `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)` is the ordinary end-of-tick span writer for continuing integration. Forward-only is the constraint that protects integrated motion from being snapped backwards; a train that integrates no motion of its own has none to protect, so for an unidentified train the corrected span is simply its confirmed-occupied blocks (see **Unidentified trains**).
- **The calculated span never runs ahead of the detector.** The rule above pulls a span forward to where the detector says the train already is. This one is its mirror: it holds a span back from where the detector has not yet said the train is. A train's calculated span must not claim a `detected block` the detector has not confirmed occupied, and must not release a `detected block` the detector still confirms. `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` enforces both by capping the distance it moves the train that tick. The head stops at the entry seam of the next `detected block` and waits there until that block confirms occupied; the tail stops at the exit seam of the rearmost `detected block` the span holds and waits there until that block confirms clear. **A held head always ends up inside a block.** Switch tiles belong to no block and carry no detection, so a run of them between two blocks is track nothing can vouch for: when the next `detected block` lies beyond such a run, the head is held at the exit seam of the block it is in rather than at that block's edge. Holding it on the undetected track would claim a position no detector supports, and a head in no block has no current block to resolve, so `PathDispatcher.create_path` could no longer plan for the train. A head that is already outside every block is left free to run to the next block boundary, where the hold applies again, rather than pinned where nothing can release it. A span is rigid, so the tighter of the two caps decides the whole tick, and a cap never moves a span backwards — it only shortens the step, down to standing still. Each confirmed change is therefore an exact position fix: a confirmed entry puts the head on that block's entry seam, and a confirmed clear puts the tail on the vacated block's exit seam. Between two fixes the position inside the block is integrated from the speed solution and is an estimate, because the speed solution describes the locomotive the profile was calibrated for rather than the one on the track. The cap is what bounds that estimate: the model may be wrong about where a train sits inside a block, and never about which blocks it is on. In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware every layout block is in `detected_block_ids()`; losing a detector after activation is fatal rather than a reason to bypass its cap. Under Simulation the set is empty, so no train is detector-capped and this rule is inert. An unidentified train integrates no motion of its own, so it has nothing to cap (see **Unidentified trains**). The block a capped head is waiting on is that train's `pending-entry block`, and it carries `track voltage` for as long as the cap holds, because the train has to be driven onto the very block whose detector releases the cap (see **Block power-controller role**). Only the detector releases a cap.
- **A large correction is reported.** A correction is the app agreeing the train is further along than it thought, and at one block it is routine and silent: the cap parks the head on the entry seam of the block it is entering, so every ordinary block entry gains exactly that one block once its detector confirms it. That is sensor latency, and reporting it would bury the stream. A correction that gains `OVERRUN_REPORT_MIN_BLOCKS` or more blocks is different. The detector found the train a whole block past where the model had it, which latency does not explain: the locomotive is running faster than its `SpeedProfile` says, so every estimate built on that profile — arrival time, braking distance, where the train sits inside its current block — is wrong in the same direction, and it will be wrong again on the next block. `TrainPhysics` emits one event for such a correction, with `kind = "warning"`, an `ErrorRecord` naming the overrun, and the blocks gained and the distance made up on the payload. The correction itself is unchanged; what this adds is that the app stops making the same correction silently forever. `OVERRUN_REPORT_MIN_BLOCKS` lives in `constants.py`.
- **A held confirmed block behind the span pins the train.** A train can hold a confirmed block its span does not cover and its head cannot reach by going forward: the locomotive is standing behind the calculated position. A moving train is never handed one, because a block behind it is a block it is being driven away from (see **Attribution**). One arrives the other ways a train comes to hold a block — initial placement, adoption, or a block credited to it while it stood still, which its own cab change then puts behind it by reversing which way forward runs. The forward-only correction rule does not move the span for such a block, but the tail rule still applies to it: the span must not run away from a block whose detector says this train is standing there. While the train holds such a block, `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` caps the step to zero, so the whole span stands still with its tail against the detected locomotive. The pin releases through the ordinary vacate path — the locomotive leaves, the block's current stops, the confirmed clear releases `Block.occupied_by` — and the cap lifts on the next tick.
- **Not a latch.** Debounce is a filter; the detector slice is a named within-tick handoff with a final-callback clear point; the span correction is immediate; the distance cap is recomputed from the confirmed set every tick and retains nothing. None waits across ticks on a gating predicate, so none belongs in the Latch Table.
- **Two views of occupancy.** The calculated span's `occupied_blocks` is where the model puts the train. `Block.occupied_by` is what the detector confirms. Over `detected block`s the two agree at the end of every tick, because the rules above make it so: one adds a block the detector has confirmed, and the other refuses to add or drop a block it has not. The one thing `Block.occupied_by` may hold that the span does not is a confirmed block behind the span (see **A held confirmed block behind the span pins the train**), and the pin keeps the span parked against it until it clears. What the span carries on top of that set is where inside those blocks the train sits, which is an estimate between confirmed changes. Every layout block is detected in Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware; in Simulation the detector set is empty and the model span is the sole occupancy source. Block track voltage tracks the calculated span, not the confirmed set, so the rail under the train is never dead while a block is still clearing (see **Block power-controller role**).
- **Ghosted trains.** A train's evidence set is its span's `occupied_blocks` plus the blocks it holds through `Block.occupied_by`, intersected with `detected_block_ids()`. When that set is non-empty and none of it is confirmed occupied, no physical reading backs the calculated position — for example, the locomotive may have been lifted off the rails — so the span represents nothing detected. Such a train is **ghosted**: an identity the app still holds with no current behind it, the mirror image of an **unidentified train** (see **Unidentified trains**). Step 0 recomputes this each read and publishes it as `presence_unknown` on the controller payload; it is computed, never latched, so detection returning anywhere in the evidence set clears it on the next read. On the rising edge Step 0 calls `controller.route_hooks.phase_latches.set_power_off(reason, has_active_path=...)` through `TrainController._apply_power_off_request(...)` — the same general teardown an operator-issued power-off uses (see the **Power-off procedure**) — so the inclusive `pending_path_cancel_request` and `pending_power_off` latches drive the same teardown every other power-off does: future block reservations and switch locks release, the `RouteRun` is removed, and `TrainController.complete_power_off()` eventually zeroes the controller's speed and discards queued commands and progress reports as ordinary OFF cleanup. Queuing every block of its retained span for the post-train drain and excluding the train from the energized set (see **Block power-controller role**) both start on the same rising edge and do not wait for that teardown to commit, because driving the rail under a train no detector can find is the same hazard as driving the rail under an unidentified one. While the teardown is in flight, the per-train loop runs the ghosted train's ordinary transaction, exactly as for any other train with a pending cancel, so the latch commits under the same `is_stopped()` gating every cancellation uses. Full loop suppression — no Phase-1 planning, no operator-command drain, no control tick, no physics integration, no Phase-3 progress — begins only once that teardown has committed and the controller reads `OFF`; the last span stays only as the place to draw the question mark, never as a prediction the app advances. Detection returning clears the flag on the next Step 0 read; once the controller is `OFF`, that lets `power_on_cmd()` through the ordinary Phase-2 drain for an `OFF` train (per **Per-train tick transaction**) and ordinary operation resumes from the retained position. The UI marks a ghosted train with a question mark for as long as `presence_unknown` reads true. Nothing removes it: the absence of current does not prove the locomotive was physically removed, and train removal is out of scope for v1 (see **Out of Scope**). In Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware the evidence set covers every block in the span; in Simulation it is empty because `detected_block_ids()` is empty, so the flag stays off. An unidentified train is never ghosted: it has no identity to outlive its occupancy, so one that loses its blocks is retired instead (see **Unidentified trains**, The gap).

**Unidentified trains**

- An **unidentified train** is a `Train` the runtime creates for current it detects on track that no train the app controls is standing on — a locomotive placed on the rails by hand, a car left behind, a train from before the app started. The app cannot drive it as it stands: driving a train needs a cab, a speed profile, and a route, and this one was never set up with any of them. So the app keeps track of it, which is what keeps every other train clear of it, and it offers the operator the one thing that fixes the situation — saying what the detector found, which turns it into an ordinary train (**Adoption**). It is the mirror image of a **ghosted** train: current on the rails with no identity behind it, rather than an identity with no current behind it (see **Ghosted trains**).
- **Creation and identity.** Step 0 creates one when it confirms a block OCCUPIED that attribution matches to no existing train — either because it is not reachable from any train's blocks through undetected track, or because the one train it is next to is being driven away from it (see **Block occupancy detection**, Attribution). Adjacency is decided first because an unidentified train is never energized: handing a train's own block to one would drive that block to `0` under the locomotive the app is driving. It gets a fresh `train_id` from the runtime, its own `TrainController`, `is_unidentified = True`, and blank persistent train data with the default `SpeedProfile` described in **SpeedProfile**. Identity follows connectivity: one unidentified train per connected group of blocks held by unidentified trains. When two such groups grow together they are one train, and it keeps the older `train_id`; when one group splits in two, the older `train_id` stays with the part holding the block it held longest and the other part becomes a new unidentified train. `SimulationController` keeps a runtime-only **retired-id set**, which is the canonical home of the never-reuse rule: every `train_id` an unidentified train is retired with (**The gap**) or adopted away from (**Adoption**) goes into it, and no later unidentified train is given one. The set is not saved, so a restart starts it empty (**Runtime only**).
- **Span.** Its span is exactly the blocks it holds. Its length, and where inside those blocks it actually sits, are unknown, so its `current_span` end locators sit at the outer boundaries of its end blocks and `occupied_tiles` lists every tile of every block it holds. This claims the most track the detector allows, which is the safe reading for a train nobody is driving.
- **Movement.** `TrainPhysics.tick(...)` integrates nothing for it: it is never powered, so its speed is `0` and it produces no movement of its own. Detection is its only span source, and it arrives through the ordinary Step-0 call to `TrainPhysics.reconcile_to_detected_occupancy(...)`. Everything downstream is the normal per-train chain — Phase-2 `TrainController.on_physics_updated(...)`, `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)`, `ResourceCoordinator`, and the two managers.
- **Direction.** It has no cab and no travel direction when it first appears. Once it moves, its direction is the block it moved into: when it vacates a block while still holding a neighbour, the `direction` passed to `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)` is the vacated block's `exit_edge` toward that neighbour, and the block it left behind takes normal `CAUTION`. Step 0 writes that edge into `physics_sync.pending_block_exit_edges`, the one carrier for a per-block tail-vacate direction, and the final Phase-2 callback consumes it on the same tick — for an unidentified train that entry never waits across ticks, because the edge comes from the detected blocks rather than from a calculated tail exit it does not have.
- **The gap.** Switch tiles carry no detection, so an unidentified train rolling through a switch can hold no blocks at all for a moment. When its last block clears, that block's tail-vacate passes `direction = None` and the block goes straight to `FREE` (see **BlockManager**), but the train itself is held, with an empty span, for `UNIDENTIFIED_GAP_HOLD_S`. Within that window, a confirmed entry no existing train accounts for, on a block reachable from its last held block through undetected track, is that same train continuing its run: it keeps its `train_id`. If more than one held train could account for the entry, the one whose hold began most recently takes it. One that still holds no blocks when the window ends is retired. Retirement is a runtime-owned teardown in this order: clear the train's `physics_sync.pending_detected_entered_blocks`, `pending_detected_vacated_blocks`, and `pending_block_exit_edges`, drop its `TrainController` from the controller registry and its physics instance from the physics registry, drop the `Train` from the train registry, and add the `train_id` to the retired-id set so it is never reused (**Creation and identity**). The train holds no blocks, no reservations, no locks, and no `RouteRun` at this point, so nothing else is released, and nothing about it was ever written to saved state (**Runtime only**). This is the one teardown the runtime performs on a train by itself; removing a train the operator placed is a different thing and is out of scope for v1 (see **Out of Scope**). `UNIDENTIFIED_GAP_HOLD_S` lives in `constants.py`.
- **Never driven while it is unidentified.** For as long as `is_unidentified` is `True` the train is never assigned a route, never given a `RouteRun`, never given a `Path`, never given a cab, and never accepts an operator command; the API rejects every such request for its `train_id`, and `PathDispatcher` never creates a path for it. The one command its `train_id` does accept is adoption, which is not a driving command — it is the operator describing what is on the track. Because it has no `RouteRun` it takes its place in the runnable order like any train without one, per the canonical ordering rule in **Per-train tick transaction**, and its Phase 1 is a no-op. Its controller stays `OFF`, so its Phase-2 command drain always finds an empty queue, and Phase 3 runs for it on the ticks Step 0 attributed a block change to it, which is how its entries and vacates commit (see **Per-train tick transaction**).
- **Adoption.** An unidentified train is a report, not a dead end: the operator has looked at the layout and can say which locomotive it is, how long it is, and which way it is pointing, and that is exactly what it is missing. `SimulationController.adopt_unidentified_train(...)` takes those, plus the `train_id` the train keeps from then on, and turns it into an ordinary train. It is refused for a train that is not unidentified, for a `train_id` another train already holds, for a head tile the train is not standing on, for one holding nothing because it is in the gap, for a consist that would reach into a block reading no current, and when block occupancy is derived from the model rather than read from hardware-backed inputs (**Session mode and operating environment**) — where nothing physical was detected there is nothing to adopt.
  - **The same `Train` record survives, re-keyed.** Adoption does not delete the unidentified train and add a train in its place. `Block.occupied_by` follows the detector, and the detector reports changes rather than levels (**Block occupancy detection**), so releasing a block that is still drawing current would leave it `FREE` under a locomotive standing on it with no future entry to correct it. Instead the `train_id` moves to its new value across every registry that keys on it — the train registry, the controller and physics instances, and the blocks the train holds, whose ownership moves through the ordinary `BlockManager` release-then-occupy writers so no block is ever owned by two ids. `is_unidentified` becomes `False`, and the `train_id` it was adopted away from goes into the retired-id set, so no later unidentified train is handed it (**Creation and identity**).
  - **Placement is the operator's declaration.** An unidentified train claims whole blocks because nobody knows where inside them it sits; the adopted train's span is rebuilt by `RailwayNetwork.place_train(...)` from the declared consist length and facing. With no head tile given, the consist must be centered in the track the detector confirmed: as much unaccounted-for track in front of the train as behind it. That is the whole of what the detector reports — the train is somewhere in these blocks — and standing the train at the first tile of the first block instead would state a position the detector gives no reason to believe. An operator who can see where the train actually stands may give a head tile, which must be one the train claims, and the front then sits at that tile's exit edge in the declared facing. Placement runs before the re-key, so a consist that does not fit the track behind the head leaves nothing half-adopted — the train is still the unidentified train it was.
  - **The body must not reach a block reading no current.** A block the detector reports clear has nothing standing on it, so no adopted train's body can extend into one. A declared consist that would is refused, and the train is left exactly as it was; the operator gives a shorter length, a different facing, a different head tile, or waits until every block the train stands on reads current. Track that carries no detection at all is not such a block: a switch tile has no detector to read clear, and a body may cover it.
  - **What the consist does not reach goes back to being unidentified.** Blocks the rebuilt span does not cover but the train still held are not this train: they are current on track no train the app controls stands on, which is the definition of an unidentified train, so `SimulationController.adopt_unidentified_train(...)` hands them to one through the ordinary Step-0 attribution and reports which blocks it gave back. They must not stay on the adopted train. A block with `occupied_by` set is not an eligible reservation candidate for anyone, its own holder included (see **BlockManager**), and the dispatcher excludes only the train's span blocks from the candidate set — so a train holding track it does not stand on walls itself off from its own route and never acquires a path. They must not simply be released either: they are drawing current, and the detector reports changes rather than levels, so nothing would re-occupy them and another train could be routed straight in. An operator who meant those blocks to be part of the train adopts it again with the full consist length.
  - **It is a real train from that moment.** It takes a `SpeedProfile` — the one the operator named, or a fresh default anchored to the declared `max_speed`, replacing the blank default it carried — accepts every operator command, is energized like any other train, and is written to saved state. Nothing downstream has a branch for an adopted train; `is_unidentified` is `False`, which is the whole of what the other rules test.
- **Never energized.** An unidentified train commands no `track_voltage`, and its span is excluded from the energized set and from the de-energize test (see **Block power-controller role**). A block held only by an unidentified train is driven to `0`. Putting voltage on the rail under a train the app cannot steer or stop is the hazard this rule exists to contain.
- **Runtime only.** Unidentified trains are not written to saved state and not restored. A restart re-detects whatever is still drawing current and creates fresh ones for it, from an empty retired-id set. An adopted train is an ordinary train and is saved like one, so adoption is also how an operator makes a hand-placed locomotive survive a restart.
- **Shown as itself.** An unidentified train appears in the train list and on the layout as one, marked apart from the trains the operator controls and offering none of their controls, so the operator can see there is something on the track. What it does offer is the way out: its detail panel reports which blocks it was detected on and carries the form that adopts it.

**Block power-controller role**

- `BlockPowerController` is focused on the software-to-hardware boundary only: it accepts a voltage number and applies it to block outputs.
- `TrainPhysics` computes `track_voltage` from the current authoritative operating input for the active mode. In ATO and manual `simulation` mode, that is `speed_kmh -> power_percent -> track_voltage`. In manual `power_percent` mode, it is `manual_power_percent -> track_voltage`, plus the calibrated absolute `speed_kmh` command. In manual `power_percent` mode without a braking override, the calibrated speed takes effect immediately in `PhysicsSpanUpdate.current_speed_kmh`. `BlockPowerController` applies that already-computed voltage to the hardware-facing block outputs.
- **A block voltage carries a direction.** `track_voltage` is a magnitude and says nothing about which way a locomotive runs; a DC locomotive's direction is the polarity on its rails. So the value `BlockPowerController` commands on a block is that magnitude given a sign: positive when the train runs the block in its `forward` direction, negative when it runs it in `reverse`, where forward and reverse are the block-relative directions over `Block.ordered_tiles` (see **Block**). The sign is per block, not per train. Each block's tile order is drawn on its own, so two blocks a train holds at the same moment may run opposite ways against the train's line of travel; one sign for the whole train would drive the locomotive backwards on every block whose order was drawn against it. The direction for a held block is the block-relative direction of the train's line of travel through that block, computed by the **canonical block-direction helper** (see **Segment**) — the same value `ReservedBlockTraversal.block_direction` carries for a reserved block (see **BlockManager**). The line of travel is the ordered walk from the train's tail end through its cab end and onward through its pending-entry block and reserved corridor, oriented by `TrainController.travel_direction` (see **TrainController**); a block held through confirmed occupancy that the calculated span does not cover sits at one end of that walk, and extending the walk through it by the connectivity that attributed it gives that block its direction. A train with no cab selected has no `travel_direction`, and every block it holds is driven positive. A cab change recomputes `travel_direction`, so the next emission re-signs every block that train holds — this is how a train reverses. `BlockPowerController.set_block_voltage(...)` records the signed value on the block, so the layout reports which way each block is being driven as well as how hard. Which physical channel a sign reaches is wiring, resolved below the hardware interface (see **Hardware setup**, The rail binding bonds tile order to rail polarity); no layer above the driver names a channel.
- **Energized set: where the train stands, plus one block ahead.** For every train `T` the app controls — that is, every train that is neither unidentified (see **Unidentified trains**) nor ghosted (`presence_unknown`, see **Ghosted trains**) — the exact emission set is `T.controller.physics_sync.current_span.occupied_blocks ∪ {block_id | Block.occupied_by == T.train_id} ∪ {T's `pending-entry block`} ∪ {T's `next reserved block`}`. Each tick `TrainPhysics` drives that train's one computed `track_voltage` onto every block in this set, signed per block by the direction the train runs through that block (see **A block voltage carries a direction**). The calculated-span part, not debounced `Block.occupied_by`, keeps a newly entered unreserved/deviated block powered immediately. The confirmed-occupancy part is there because the two views of occupancy can disagree about where a train is, and the rail must not go dead under a locomotive on either reading. A block attribution gave this train because it neighbours the span (see **Block occupancy detection**, Attribution) is in neither the span nor the reservations, and a forward-only correction cannot bring a span back onto it, so this is the only part that powers it — and it is the part the locomotive standing there needs in order to move at all. The pending-entry part covers the train the detector cap is holding at a block seam: its calculated span stops short of the block it is entering, so without this the rail the locomotive has to roll onto to release its own cap would be dead. Because a block shares one feed across its segments and block exclusion prevents another modeled train from owning the same calculated block, a feed is never asked for two voltages.
- **Only the next reserved block is pre-energized.** A train's **next reserved block** is the first block of its reserved-ahead corridor (`Block.reserved_for_train`) that its head reaches by travelling forward, passing over the blocks it is already standing on. `TrainPhysics` resolves it each tick with the same forward tile walk the seam cap uses, so it follows the points as they are actually aligned rather than as a plan describes them, and it is `None` for a train with nothing reserved ahead of it. The rest of the corridor carries no voltage. One block ahead is the whole of what pre-energizing is for — a locomotive must not cross an insulated boundary into dead rail — and it is also the most that may be live at once. **Rail energized further ahead than the locomotive is authorized to be this instant is rail a train running fast keeps accelerating over.** A speed profile describes the locomotive it was calibrated for, so a real train reaches a seam before the model says it has; with the whole corridor live it runs on through every block of it while the app still believes it is a block back, and the detector confirmations arrive as a correction after the fact. Energizing one block bounds that overrun to one block and puts dead rail immediately past the authority end, so the layout itself enforces the limit the app is computing. A train with no reservations is unaffected: it holds the rail under it through the span and confirmed-occupancy parts, and the block it is entering through the pending-entry part.
- **De-energize against the same source.** A block leaves the energized set two ways, and both queue into `SimulationController.pending_de_energize_block_ids: Set[str]`, drained and cleared after every train has emitted for the tick (see the **Latch Table**). When a calculated span drops a block or a reservation clears, `BlockManager` adds it to that set. When a block stays reserved but stops being its train's `next reserved block` — the train reversed on its corridor, or a replan reordered it — nothing about the block changed, so `BlockManager` cannot see it; `TrainPhysics` adds it instead, from the difference between this tick's emission set and the emission set it stored per train from the previous tick (`TrainPhysics.previous_tick_emission_set_by_train_id`). `SimulationController` drives a block from either source to `0` only when it is absent from every controller's current `current_span.occupied_blocks`, has no `Block.occupied_by` naming that controller's train, is no controller's `pending-entry block`, and is no controller's `next reserved block`. The drain tests the calculated span **in addition to** debounced `Block.occupied_by`, never instead of it, so neither view of occupancy alone can zero a rail and a same-tick re-entry prevents zeroing. A reservation the train has not reached is deliberately not part of the test, because it is not part of the emission set either: holding it would leave a block driven and zeroed on the same tick. Unidentified-train controllers and `presence_unknown` controllers are excluded from all four tests, so a block held only by an unidentified train, or only by a train no detector can find, is driven to `0` (see **Unidentified trains** and **Ghosted trains**). This makes the routine energized set exactly the global union, over the trains the app can steer, of their calculated occupied blocks, confirmed occupancy, pending-entry blocks, and next reserved blocks — the same four terms the emission set carries. It is different from the loss-of-control **safe state**, which zeroes every output at once.
- `BlockPowerController` owns only voltage emission/readback; controller and physics layers supply all manual-mode decisions and the already-computed voltage.
- **A refused write is fatal to a hardware-backed session, and never recorded as voltage.** Simulation's `TrackOutput.set_voltage(...)` is the happy-path model and accepts a valid block command. Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware cannot activate with a missing binding or chip, so a refusal or exception after activation means the device contract has failed at runtime. `BlockPowerController.set_block_voltage(...)` records the commanded voltage only when the write landed; otherwise it commands the global safe state, reports the failure to `SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)`, which latches the halt and emits one event with `kind = "error"` and an `ErrorRecord` naming the failed device; further operating commands are refused until the operator activates a newly validated session. The model never reports a block energized while the rail is dead, and the runtime never continues operating around missing hardware.
- The block-voltage read surface (`BlockPowerController.get_block_voltage(block_id)` over `TrackOutput.get_actual_voltage(block_id)`) reports the voltage on a block. Simulation returns the last value accepted by the happy-path model. Virtual Hardware returns the controllable fake device's readback, and Real Hardware reads the block's bound `block_occupancy_current` channel bus voltage where that chip provides one; neither hardware-backed environment silently substitutes the commanded value for a failed read. It returns `None` where the rig has no measurement facility, and `None` plus one `kind = "warning"` event on a failed readback transaction. This is a diagnostic read: it never enters the hardware-failure lifecycle, which is why the fatal "failed detector transaction" of **Hardware I/O failure** does not cover it.

**Switch power-controller role**

- `SwitchPowerController` is the switch-side counterpart of `BlockPowerController`, and it covers a switch's two separate electricals: the `throw voltage` it drives onto the point motor, and the block feed its rails run on. It holds `SwitchOutput` and is the only caller of it — including the safe-state call — so `SwitchManager` requests a throw through the controller and never touches the interface itself.
- **The two electricals are different wires.** `SwitchOutput` is the software-to-hardware boundary for switch *throw* output. It accepts a target alignment (`0 = MAIN`, `1 = DIVERGE`, matching `Switch.position`) and drives the point motor. It holds no wiring knowledge — how a target alignment reaches the motor is the driver's business, defined under **Hardware setup** (see **A switch throw is a pulse on one of two coils**) — and it owns no routing, lock, or availability policy. The rails are the other wire: they carry the `track voltage` of the switch's feed block, which `BlockPowerController` emits. The two are never compared. Simulation supplies `SimulatedSwitchDriver`; Virtual Hardware and Real Hardware supply the setup-aware driver against their respective device.
- **A switch's rails are fed from one nearby block.** A switch has no track feed of its own. Its rails are wired to one nearby block and carry that block's `track voltage`, so the emission `BlockPowerController` already makes for that block is what drives a train across the switch. The wiring is the `switch_track_source` binding in **Hardware setup** (`switch_id` to `block_id`), and `SwitchPowerController` is that map's only reader.
