The bound block must be the one at the switch's **main entrance**, because every route through a switch enters there: the feeding block is therefore always the block the train is standing on as it crosses the switch, so a switch is live under a train without any emission, reservation, or channel of its own. This holds under the one-block-ahead rule (see **Only the next reserved block is pre-energized**) because a train reaches a switch by leaving the block that feeds it. This is why the energized set stays exactly the block set defined under **Block power-controller role** — switches add nothing to it.
- **Rail voltage is read, never written.** `SwitchPowerController.get_switch_voltage(switch_id)` reports the voltage on a switch's rails by reading its feed block through `BlockPowerController.get_block_voltage(source_block_id)`. While an incomplete setup is being edited or commissioned it returns `None` when that switch has no `switch_track_source` entry; that state is an activation blocker and cannot occur during Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware operation. It is a UI/diagnostic read — the per-switch hardware view shows which block feeds the switch and whether those rails are live — and no control, routing, or physics decision reads it. `SwitchPowerController` drives nothing onto the rails: the bond is physical, so there is no output to write.
- **Why the switch has its own controller.** `BlockPowerController` takes a voltage number and applies it; a switch is the other shape. Its one output is an alignment, not a voltage, and its one voltage is sourced from a block rather than commanded. Holding both facts in `SwitchPowerController` gives every switch-side electrical question a single answer site, and leaves `BlockPowerController` owning exactly one thing: the voltage on a block.
- **Acquisition always throws.** Lock acquisition drives the coil for every switch it acquires, whether or not `Switch.position` needed changing and whether or not that alignment was the last one commanded. A throw is blind, so having driven a coil once is no evidence the points seated: a solenoid can stall, bounce back, or be moved by hand afterwards, and nothing reports it. Points left on the wrong side carry the train's `track voltage` down the other branch, which both denies the train the rail it is about to run on and lights the current detector on a block the train is nowhere near — read as occupancy on track nothing is standing on. One extra pulse removes that whole class of failure, so acquisition re-throws rather than reasoning about whether it needs to. Outside acquisition the throw is emitted whenever the canonical `Switch.position` mutation workflow (see the **Switch entity** section) issues an alignment the output has not last driven.
- **A throw is blind.** A point motor reports no position back — it has no contact, encoder, or sensed alignment — so `Switch.position` is the commanded alignment and nothing confirms the points seated. `SwitchManager` must not infer a physical alignment from any other reading, and must not treat a successful throw as evidence the switch moved. The rail-voltage read is no exception: it reports the feed block's voltage and says nothing about where the points are.
- **An unissued throw is fatal to a hardware-backed session.** `SwitchPowerController.set_throw(switch_id, target_state)` returns `True` when the command reached the device and `False` when it did not. A missing port binding or chip is rejected before Virtual Hardware or Real Hardware activation; a refusal or exception after activation therefore means the device contract has failed at runtime. The controller commands the global safe state, reports the failure to `SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)`, and reports the failed command through `NotificationCenter`. Any partial lock acquisition rolls back its software locks, but operation does not continue and the throw is never auto-retried. `Switch.position` remains the commanded alignment rather than being rewritten as an inferred physical position.
- **Environment behavior.** Simulation accepts every valid throw and records it. Virtual Hardware can inject any of the canonical fault kinds listed in **Hardware interface boundary** to prove the same observable hardware-failure lifecycle without physical equipment. It does not pretend to observe an uninstrumented point motor mechanically sticking after a successful pulse. Real Hardware enters the hardware-failure lifecycle when the physical device refuses or fails. Missing setup is never a runtime throw outcome in either hardware-backed environment because it blocks activation.

## Data Flow To UI

**API payload ownership**

- Each runtime/entity owner must expose its own API payload through its own `to_dict()` (or equivalent read-model method), rather than merging changed live fields back into another entity's payload. `Train.to_dict()` owns persistent train data. `TrainController.to_dict()` owns live onboard, control, and physical diagnostics, including the derived `presence_unknown`. Dispatcher-owned path progress is exposed through `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)`.
- In v1, any composite runtime snapshot that returns the network/train view must expose five sibling payload groups, with this exact ownership split. `trains` is the list of `Train.to_dict()` payloads. `controllers_by_train_id` is a map `train_id -> TrainController.to_dict()`. `dispatcher_progress_by_train_id` is a map `train_id -> {path_id, status, current_resource_index, authority_end_block_id, waiting_for_block_id, resource_sequence}`, resolved from `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (each `resource_sequence` entry carries `sequence_index, resource_type, resource_id, entry_edge, exit_edge`); `PathDispatcher` does not read train/controller mirror fields, and this map carries no requested target, per **Path And PathDispatcher**, Path invariants. `assigned_routes_by_train_id` is a map `train_id -> route_id` (or `None`), resolved from `RouteExecutor.assigned_route_id_for(train_id)`. `route_runs_by_train_id` is a map `train_id -> {route_id, run_number, current_route_status, display_state, requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction}`, where the requested-target pair is the currently-armed `RouteRun.task_runs[walk_position].resolved_target` (`None`/`None` when there is no `RUNNING` run or no walk-position is yet armed) — the one stored copy of the target named under **Path And PathDispatcher**, Path field schema; `TrainController.to_dict()` must not merge route-owned fields into the controller payload for the train's currently-`RUNNING` `RouteRun` produced by `RouteExecutor` (`None` when the train has no `RUNNING` run). The two route-side maps answer different questions, and both are carried. `assigned_routes_by_train_id` is the lightweight "which route is this train on?" map the frontend reads to tell assigned trains from unassigned ones; it is non-`None` even between runs, before Phase 1 has allocated a `RouteRun`. `route_runs_by_train_id` is the live run summary (`None` whenever there is no `RUNNING` run). When a run is live, the `route_id` in both agrees. `waiting_for_block_id` is the live result of the canonical `PathDispatcher.get_waiting_for_block_id(train_id)` helper defined under **Contention snapshot**; the read model does not reimplement that walk. Best-effort retries may change it tick to tick, which is why it is recomputed rather than retained; it feeds the "show stuck" overlay. Other detail endpoints may present the same owned data in a different wrapper, but they must preserve this ownership split and must not merge controller-owned, dispatcher-owned, or route-owned live fields back into `Train.to_dict()`.

**Elapsed values are server-owned**

- A payload that carries a duration carries it as a number the runtime computed. A client renders it and never derives one from its own clock: a browser clock cannot see a stop, a playback multiplier, or the server's own time, so any elapsed figure it derived would be wrong in all three ways (see **Clocks** under the **Units contract**).
- A client may still format a wall instant the payload carries — `started_at`, `finished_at`, `target_time` — as a local clock time, because those are absolute and need no elapsed arithmetic.
- The run payload carries the elapsed figures its panel shows: for each served walk-position, the elapsed time under the route's `timing_basis` (the figure `delay_class` was computed from) and the elapsed wall time (the figure an operator reads against the clock on the wall). Both are carried, because a run that was stopped for ten minutes is honestly described only by both numbers.

**`Train.to_dict()` payload**

- `to_dict()` must expose all canonical fields owned by the `Train` entity for convenience.
- Export only persistent train data from `Train.to_dict()`, because those are the fields owned by `Train`.
- `Train.to_dict()` exports only the persistent train fields owned by `Train`. Live physical/control diagnostics (position, span, speed, state, target speed, ATO info, lookahead, path/destination IDs, reserved blocks) are not in this payload — they live on the controller payload.
- Read live operational diagnostics from `TrainController.to_dict()` instead.

**`TrainController.to_dict()` payload**

- `to_dict()` must expose all canonical fields owned by the `TrainController` entity for convenience.
- Export all live operational state, including `handle_position`, `manual.manual_mode_type`, and `manual.manual_power_percent`.
- Export live physical state from the controller-owned storage, including `current_span` and `current_speed_kmh`, for UI/debug consumers that previously read position or occupied tiles from `Train.to_dict()`.
- Export ATO state using `ato_destination_block_id` derived from `received_path_plan.authority_end_block_id`.
- Export `received_path_plan` when present using the canonical **received_path_plan schema**. This is the controller/UI projection of active dispatcher authority, and its nested authority geometry must continue to follow the canonical **resource_sequence entry schema** and **path_tile entry schema**.
- Do not make `TrainController.to_dict()` the owner of live path-progress state in v1. `PathDispatcher` stays the only owner of `current_resource_index`. If the UI needs that progress projection, expose it through a dispatcher-owned read model such as `PathDispatcher.get_active_path_progress(train_id)` (the full projection is listed above in this section), resolved from the dispatcher's own active-path registry for that `train_id`. Do not copy ownership into `TrainController`, and do not write per-tick sequence-index updates into the controller.
- UI/debug consumers that need active authority geometry must read `received_path_plan.resource_sequence`; consumers that need current physical occupancy must read `TrainController.get_current_span()`.
- Expose the full canonical lookahead payload as the single source of truth for current target speed, ATC limit, speed-limit reason, and forward curve rendering.

**UI data-flow anchor**

- Treat the controller payload as the source of truth for live operational, manual, and physical state. Treat the train payload as the source for persistent train data.
- A UI asking "what is the train's target speed right now?" must call `controller.get_lookahead()` and sample at distance `0`.

**Signals (display-only)**

- Signals are display-only UI state. `SignalRegistry` stores them and owns their lookup surface (`add_signal`, `get_signal`, `get_signal_at`, `get_signal_at_direction`, `remove_signal`, `update_signal`); `RailwayNetwork` reaches signals only through it. `RailwayNetwork.update_signals_from_blocks()` produces aspect values for the UI once per tick, after dispatcher feedback (see the **Per-train tick transaction**). No controller or physics layer uses them as a speed restriction. The CAUTION speed restriction is owned by `TrainController.CAUTION_SPEED_LIMIT_KMH` through the lookahead / ATC path (see **TrainController**, ATC speed limit).

## Event log

The event log is the operator-facing record of what happened during a run. It is the structured stream `NotificationCenter` already owns (see **Core Runtime Loop**, **Runtime notifications**), kept on disk and presented for review, so an operator can scroll back through a run instead of catching events only as they pass. It is not a second sink: every event still flows through `NotificationCenter`, which assigns the order and the schema, and the event log is that one stream persisted and surfaced.

**What it records**

- The log carries every event `NotificationCenter` emits, at every level: command results, the error events defined in **Error events**, the latch writes and consumptions defined by the **Writer-helper rule**, and the operational events that mark what each train did — train placed, departed, arrived, entered station dwell, entered a block, vacated a block, threw a switch, path created / completed / cancelled, and EB activated / released. Each one is a single event with the fields defined by the **Event schema**, and each names the function that produced it in `operation` (`"add_train"`, `"depart"`, `"occupy_blocks"`, `"on_block_tail_vacated"`, `"request_position_change"`, `"create_path"`, `"try_complete_active_path"`, `"cancel_path"`, `"activate"`, `"release"`). Operational events carry `kind = "info"` and no `ErrorRecord`. The increasing `notification_id` is the order the log reads back in.
- Components must not write the log directly. They emit each event through `NotificationCenter` at the points they already report from, the same way as any other notification, so the "no separate sink" rule in **Runtime notifications** still holds. The event log is the persisted projection of that one stream.

**Persistence**

- The event log persists to disk, so a run's record outlives the session and an operator can review an earlier run's incidents later. It holds the same `NOTIFICATION_RETENTION_ROWS` window the in-memory stream holds (see **Runtime notifications**, Retention): one cap governs the whole stream, so a row an operator can no longer see in the viewer is a row no query can reach. This is a deliberate persisted record, separate from live runtime state — occupancy, locks, the train span — which stays in memory per **Out of Scope**. It is runtime-written data that is saved rather than rebuilt each session, alongside the maintenance counters (see **Maintenance counters**).

**Viewer**

- The event-log viewer is a read-only panel in the operator UI. It reads the persisted stream and lets the operator filter it — by entity, by `kind`, by `source`, by `operation`, by `ErrorRecord.origin`, by time — and selecting an entry highlights the entities it names on the canvas. It presents the record only; it never mutates runtime state.

**Replay**

- Replay is a visualization built on the event log. It steps or scrubs through the recorded timeline and draws the state each event establishes — which blocks were occupied, where each train was, how each switch was aligned — at that point in the run. Motion between two events is drawn by interpolating along known track geometry. Replay reconstructs the picture from the recorded events plus the layout; it must not re-run the simulation engine. (Re-running the engine forward or backward — true time-travel of the live model — stays **Out of Scope**.)

## End-To-End ATO Path Run

**End-to-end path-authority projection**

- `PathDispatcher` stores the active dispatcher-owned `Path`. `TrainController.path_projection.received_path_plan` reads the canonical **received_path_plan schema** projection from that active record. `controller.assigned_path_id` is a derived read of that projection's `path_id`.
- `RouteExecutor` must ensure the train's `TrainController` exists before path authority is created or extended in Phase 1.
- `RouteExecutor` must not write `assigned_path_id` or `ato_destination_block_id` directly.

**End-to-end ATO run**

This is a high-level walk-through. The canonical contracts live in the relevant sections (see **Stop-arrival procedure**, **Planning Kernel**, **Route-run state**, **Phase Table**).

1. Phase 1: run the covered-task index advance. Resolve the next task via `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` to get one concrete `(requested_destination_block_id, requested_destination_direction)`. Then call `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` (or `extend_path(...)` if a live path exists), using `acquisition_mode = run.effective_acquisition_mode`. If a resolved pass-through (`waypoint`) target block is already covered, the index advance skips it. A resolved stop target block is finished by the same index advance once the train is stopped with its head in that block and has no active path (the covered-stop rule).
2. Phase 2: controller lookahead / ATC / ATO command generation, then physics integrates and emits boundary-by-boundary `PhysicsSpanUpdate` callbacks.
3. Phase 3: drain and clear the controller-owned progress mailbox (`physics_sync.queued_progress_reports`). A movement-derived deviation report latches `pending_path_cancel_request` with the report's own `ErrorRecord` (`origin = "out_of_sequence"`) here and leaves the live path `ACTIVE`; it commits later in the gated `pending_path_cancel_request` step once the train is stopped, per the **Physical progress / deviation procedure** step `389a`. Run the **Stop-arrival check** — skipped when `pending_path_cancel_request` is set, because a pending cancel outranks arrival (Phase-3 step `9d8b`). On success, call `PathDispatcher.try_complete_active_path(train_id)` per the **Stop-arrival procedure**. Then consume `pending_path_cancel_request`, `pending_route_abort`, `pending_power_off`, and `pending_route_deactivate` in the canonical order. `pending_path_cancel_request` consumption (and any latch that depends on it via the inclusive-latch rule) follows the gating in the **Latch Table** row `c832`. (Dwell-complete, route-deviation cleanup, and terminal route completion are Phase 1 steps on the next eligible tick, not Phase 3.)
4. Extension retries use the same `path_id` until authority reaches the requested target. Every `success=True` extend keeps the existing live path, and `reached_target = False` means retry later; a `no_viable_path` failure latches cancel-and-replan.
5. Stop arrival advances the stop task via `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)`, which writes its `finished_at`. On the next tick, the Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` moves the controller to `RUNNING_AT_STATION` automatically when the route's next task is `dwell`. The Phase 1 dwell-complete step checks `RouteTask.should_depart(...)`, then calls `RouteRun.advance_walk_position(...)` for the dwell row (writing its `finished_at` and advancing the derived `current_waypoint_index`), then calls `TrainController.finish_dwell()`. If `_find_next_task_target(train_id)` then returns `None`, Phase 1 calls `RouteExecutor.finish_route_run(train_id)` per the terminal route completion contract (state `FINISHED`, the run kept as a historical row). Otherwise it leaves the next target to a later Phase 1.

**End-to-end manual-arrival note**

- Manual trains use the same route-committed arrival and the same Phase 1 dwell-entry step inside `RouteExecutor.tick_train_phase1(...)` as ATO trains. The only difference is departure. In manual mode, `TrainController.depart()` marks the current authority ready to go; it does not call `TrainController.ato.go()` or start movement by itself.

**End-to-end post-EB restart note**

- EB recovery follows the canonical **EB workflow state machine** defined in the **TrainController** section. The pre-EB path is never reused. After EB release, the next eligible Phase 1 creates fresh authority and calls `TrainController.depart()` directly, per the canonical depart contract.

## Logic Review Checklist

**Review checklist**

- Is the controller reading the canonical **received_path_plan schema** projection when path-end stopping matters?
- Does each `resource_sequence` entry carry the ordered `path_tiles` slice using the canonical **path_tile entry schema**, with explicit `entry_edge` / `exit_edge` on both the resource entry and each nested tile entry? Controller lookahead and UI geometry need those edges.
- Is controller lookahead the only source of target speed and speed-limit reason?
- Does an **Auto profiling** step time the head between two confirmed block entries — into the middle block and into the one after it — rather than from the moment power was applied? A one-sided interval measures an unknown start position inside the first block and carries the detector's confirmation lag as a bias.
- In manual mode, does ATC enforce the end of the assigned path as a hard stop through the controller lookahead authority-boundary stop?
- Can any path-complete event still fire while the train is braking or creeping toward the terminal authority end? It must not: release waits for the **Stop-arrival check**, which requires `TrainController.is_stopped_at_authority_end()` (state `RUNNING_MOVING` and `TrainController.is_stopped()`).
- Does `controller.ato.compute_handle(delta_seconds)` read only the current tick's canonical lookahead payload, not stale scalar fields?
- Does `RouteExecutor` read controller state rather than mirrored runtime state on `Train`?
- Does `RouteExecutor` resolve waypoint-authored block-relative direction preferences into one concrete `requested_destination_direction` before calling `PathDispatcher.create_path()` or `PathDispatcher.extend_path()`? It must do this without relying on platform-level direction fields.
- Do `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` use the same shared pathfinder/current-span workflow, including the same deterministic tie-break, for start-side and platform-end selection?
- Do `RouteExecutor` and `PathDispatcher` get head/tail/current-span data only through `TrainController.get_current_span()`? `TrainPhysics` must stay the movement-update producer, and `TrainController` must stay the writer for controller-owned span state.
- For a pass-through waypoint whose resolved target block is already physically covered, does Phase 1's covered-task index advance skip it? And does it finish a covered stop once the train is stopped, has no active path, and has its head in the destination block (the head-anchored covered-stop rule), with dwell entry and exit then running normally?
- Does `PathDispatcher.create_path(...)` raise the head-covered-destination precondition only when the requested destination already contains the train head? A block covered by the tail alone remains the explicit turnaround-target carve-out; head-covered stops are left to Phase 1's covered-task index advance.
- Is a resource the path already holds rejected during both `create_path()` and `extend_path()`, while a block the train has passed and released stays available to acquire again?
- In `best_effort` mode, does the system keep one live `path_id`, extend `resource_sequence` in place, and avoid treating a truncated-but-live authority as a creation failure?
- Does `best_effort` creation fail instead of storing a live authority when the head is on a switch and no forward block can be acquired?
- Does `best_effort` truncation hold the live authority one block back, instead of stopping it on a two-way block entered straight off a switch (the junction-fouling exclusion)? One-way blocks, and two-way blocks in `CAUTION`, stay exempt, so trains may pack tight up to them.
- When a best-effort authority must truncate short of its target, does it prefer a holding-point block (`Block.is_holding_point`) as the scale-back target over the switch-based junction-fouling back-off? It falls back to the switch rule only when the acquirable prefix has no holding point, and it must still honor the reversal-trap exclusion as an absolute floor.
- Does a stalled, truncated best-effort hold release in both cases: after the `IDLE_HOLD_RELEASE_TICKS` no-progress timeout (`error.origin = "idle_hold_release"`), and as soon as the tick's **Contention snapshot** shows a strictly-higher-priority run blocked on a block this hold owns but has not entered (`error.origin = "contention_release"`)? Both use the same system-replan mechanics while retaining distinct diagnostics.
- Does **Meet coordination** compute a per-tick advisory meet ceiling (never a reservation)? It caps a contender's **acquired authority** (via `acquisition_cap_block_id`, leaving `requested_destination_block_id` and `resolved_target` at the route target) at a holding point before a contested zone, or at the zone's far entry when capacity allows, so the held train stops at the ceiling without completing its path. All acquisition still runs through the interlocked manager path, and the assignment is re-derived each tick rather than stored.
- Does `PathDispatcher` resolve controller progress reports against active authority by `train_id`, from its own database rather than from physics or train mirrors? `cancel_path(path_id, ...)` is path-keyed instead, because the caller passes the id it just read from `active_path_id_by_train(train_id)`; `try_complete_active_path(train_id)`, the other terminal call, stays train-keyed. Delayed release of a still-occupied block or switch resolves from the resource itself after authority release.
- Are multi-train routes using per-train `RouteRun` objects as the only canonical progress store, with no route-level runtime progress summary fields?
- Does Phase 1's covered-task index advance loop resolve `PlatformTarget` and `StationTarget` task targets as needed, advance past already-covered `task_kind == "waypoint"` tasks, and avoid advancing past `task_kind == "stop"` tasks just because the target block is covered?
- On active-run plan replacement (operator retargeting and route swap use abort + new assignment, never a `route_id` rewrite) and on live-path target re-resolution: does `RouteExecutor` clear the affected `path_cancelled` handoff state through the canonical write contract, and replan from the train's current physical position with no synthetic progress jumps? It must keep target identity in `task_runs[i].resolved_target` rather than in `current_route_status`, and must keep no stored planning cursor outside `_find_next_task_target(train_id)`.
- Does a live authority ever extend past an unserved `stop` or `run` target? It must not, per the **Authority termination rule** — a `waypoint` target is an ordinary terminal, a `stop` or `run` target is not.
- Does Phase 1 arm the next target while the current `waypoint` is still unserved once fewer than `WAYPOINT_LOOKAHEAD_BLOCKS` blocks remain ahead of the head, and does it arm one target at a time so each acquisition is direction-filtered on its own terminal?
- Is "has this authority reached that target?" always asked through `Path.reaches_target(block_id)` rather than by comparing the block against `authority_end_block_id`? An authority that runs past a target has reached it.
- Is the requested target absent from the stored `Path` record — an argument in, an echo in the result, and stored once route-side in `RouteRun.task_runs[i].resolved_target`?
- Does the route decide task completion and the dispatcher only release? No dispatcher-side acceptance test may gate a task being finished.
- Does stop arrival require the **Stop-arrival check**? And do controller deviation cases produce a `cancel_path` event with `deviation = true`, carrying one `ErrorRecord` (`origin = "out_of_sequence"`) on the event payload?
- Does the normal `CAUTION -> FREE` reevaluation require the forward neighbour (reached by the connectivity walk from `block.caution.exit_edge`) to be non-`OCCUPIED`, fire from exactly the three closed triggers (`on_block_tail_vacated()`, `on_switch_position_changed(...)`, and `release_train_occupancy(train_id)`), and read no controller or `travel_direction` state, while still allowing the explicit completion-time `force` exception in `release_path_block_reservations(..., completed=True)` and the **Opposing-traversal caution clear** in `reserve_blocks_for_path(...)` (same end keeps the caution, other end clears it)? (`occupied_by` is always `None` in `CAUTION` — guaranteed by `on_block_tail_vacated()` clearing it — so a separate non-occupied check on the CAUTION block itself is vacuous. `reserved_by_path` is explicitly not a gating condition; if `block.caution` has cleared but `reserved_by_path` remains set, the computed block state is `RESERVED`, not `FREE`.)
- Can any completion or cancellation path still unlock a block or switch that the train physically occupies at full stop?
- After `TrainController.eb.release()`, does a non-dwell EB cycle return to **`READY`** and restart through fresh path creation plus `depart()`, while a dwell EB cycle clears the latch and remains in `RUNNING_AT_STATION` until ordinary dwell completion?
- Does `TrainController.eb.release()` reject a manual `power_percent` driver whose `manual.manual_power_percent` is still non-zero, so no train departs on a percentage retained from before the EB stop? And does `TrainController.commands.set_mode(...)` clear that same retained percentage, so it cannot survive a mode change either?
- Do train-owned flank-protection claims record `source_switch_id` so tail-vacate can release only the protection created from the vacated main switch?
- Is a block's commanded voltage signed per block by the traversal direction the train runs through it, with rail polarity resolved only below the hardware interface through the `block_track` binding's `reversed` flag (see **A block voltage carries a direction**)?
- Is a queued block driven to `0` only when it fails all four emission-set terms — absent from `current_span.occupied_blocks`, no `Block.occupied_by` naming that controller's train, not the `pending-entry block`, not the `next reserved block` — with a still-reserved block that is not the train's `next reserved block` allowed to drop (see **Power**, De-energize against the same source)?
- Are coil ports, rail polarity (`reversed`), and rail-feed (`switch_track_source`) resolved fresh on every write, while the chips, transport, and range-checking maps stay fixed at Virtual Hardware / Real Hardware activation (see **A binding is read per write**)?
- Does block-occupancy attribution refuse to credit a moving train with a block behind it — matching a newly OCCUPIED block only to a train it is physically approaching — while a stopped train's attribution stays permissive (see **Power**, Attribution)?
- Does `SimulationController.enter_hardware_failure(...)` remain the single writer of `hardware_failure = True` and `clear_hardware_failure()` the single clearer, mirroring `enter_failed_runtime(...)` / `clear_failed_runtime()` (see **Hardware I/O failure**)?
- Does an unidentified train's `train_id` retire into the never-reuse set on the gap-hold teardown, and does adoption re-key an existing ghosted or unidentified train in place rather than deleting and recreating it (see **Unidentified trains**, The gap and Adoption)?
- Does `SpeedCalibration` reject opening or continuing a **Lighting-voltage calibration** run whenever the train leaves `READY`, leaves `manual` control mode, loses its cab, is powered off, has EB activated, or is assigned an ATO route, discarding the candidate through `cancel_lighting(train_id)`?
- Does a `task_kind == "run"` task finish only through Phase 1 step `6a0d` — timer from playback-scaled `delta_seconds`, `begin_service_stop()`, a `run_complete = True` release with `error = None`, then `consume_service_stop()` and `advance_walk_position(...)` once stopped with no authority — and never through head entry, coverage, arrival, or the cancellation callback?
- Is a `missing_target` task skipped inline by whichever of steps `c481` / `592a` saw it (writing `finished_at`, `origin = "task_skipped"`), while a `target_unavailable` task is waited on at step `66aa` (`origin = "planning_failed"`, nothing written), and does an all-broken `CIRCULAR` / `ROUND_TRIP` walk set `no_reachable_task` rather than skipping forever?
- Does a fresh `RouteRun` seed its starting walk-position per `RouteStartMode` (`FIRST` / `NEAREST` / `SPECIFIC`), pre-finishing the skipped prefix to `RouteRun.started_at` with `delay_class = None` and no appends, with the per-assignment start-mode input consumed one-shot?

**Notes**

- Ordinary switch release happens when the train tail vacates the switch tile, not on full path completion. `TrainController.on_physics_updated(span_update)` reports the vacated resource in `tail_vacated_resources`, and `PathDispatcher.on_controller_progress(report)` releases it through `ResourceCoordinator.release_tail_vacated(..., route)`: blocks go through `BlockManager.on_block_tail_vacated(...)`, while switches go through `SwitchManager.on_switch_tail_vacated(switch_id, train_id, route)`, which removes the matching `Switch.physical_occupants[route]` entry first and then, only when no entries remain for that train, releases train-owned protection sourced from that vacated switch through `Switch.release_train_protection_from(...)`. The neighbour-side `source_switch_id` filter protects claims created by other occupied main switches. Both completion and cancellation must respect physical occupancy: if a switch is still physically occupied, its train-owned protection stays in place until the later tail-vacate event.
- `assigned_path_id` moves from `Train` to `TrainController` as a read-only derived property of `received_path_plan.path_id`. The underlying `received_path_plan` is itself a read-only projection over `self.simulation_controller.path_dispatcher.get_active_path(train_id)` (which derives over `Path.status == ACTIVE`), so updates and releases surface automatically without any controller-side write step.
